Semitopology: distributed collaborative action via topology, algebra, and logic
Murdoch J. Gabbay and Giuliano Losa

Abstract

Semitopology: distributed collaborative action via topology, algebra, and logic

Preprint version of August 7, 2026.

Chapter 0
Introduction

1
What is a ‘distributed collaborative action’, and what is a semitopology?

One nice side-effect of computing is that it stimulates mathematics by making new things practical, which mathematicians can then build theories about.
This book reports on the outcome of a mathematical investigation into what we call distributed collaborative action, motivated by the ever-increasing power, size, and ubiquity of highly-distributed systems in computing.

So what is
distributed collaborative action
?

Let
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
be the set of all people, which we may call points (as in point-set topology), and assume these come with a local state with just two values: ‘exists’, or ‘will exist’.
Suppose that for two distinct existing people
\{p_{him},p_{her}\}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
there is some set of
p_{child}
people such that the former can (but are not obliged to) collaborate to act together to change the state of
p_{child}
from ‘will exist’ to ‘exists’.

Extensive literatures exist to reflect and advise on the implementational details of this scenario — but this being a
mathematical
text, we work with an idealised model.
Even for this simple model, we can make some observations:

1.

A notion of collaboration is built in to the fact that no single point can act on its own.
Indeed, at least three distinct points — a
p_{him}
, a
p_{her}
, and a suitable
p_{child}
— are required for the local state of the one point
p_{child}
to get updated.

2.

No central authority exists to coordinate or authorise state updates.

3.

The state updates that do occur are local; a set
\{p_{him},p_{her},p_{child}\}
can act to update the existence of
p_{child}
, but this update is local to the participants involved in the particular action.
1
1

1

In the real world, births are supposed to be registered; but this is a
separate action
. People were making babies by distributed collaborative action before central governments and a population census.

4.

Actions may overlap, and (provided they are compatible) they need not be identical: e.g.
\{p_{him},p_{her},p_{child},p_{child}^{\prime}\}
could collaborate to update the states of two children.
Likewise for
\{p_{him},p_{her},p_{child},p_{him}^{\prime},p_{child}^{\prime}\}
or
\{p_{him},p_{her},p_{child},p_{her}^{\prime},p_{child}^{\prime}\}

There are many examples of similar distributed systems.
A swarm of birds or drones, groups of pedestrians, and crowds of sports or music fans can and do communicate locally to make local updates to their speeds and directions.
Note that the updates need not be identical: e.g. if two pedestrians see that they are about to collide, then they had better
not
both dodge in the same direction.

In computing, peer-to-peer filesharing systems locally exchange data (to be fair, this may be mediated by so-called
tracker nodes
, but these are also usually distributed); the
Fediverse
of the distributed social network Mastodon works is in a similar spirit; and so on, and so forth.

Most modern blockchain systems also evolve their state by distributed collaborative action.
The technical details vary, but a typical example is a proof-of-stake system that makes progress such that a group of participants can update their state if they held a majority of the stake at some time in the past (e.g. two weeks ago) — the idea being that all participants have reached agreement on, and learned, the state of the network two weeks in the past, so this can be treated as immutable common knowledge without undermining the distributed nature of the system in the present
[
Goo14
, Subsection 3.2.1, final paragraph]
.
In the XRP Ledger
[
SYB14
]
and the Stellar network
[
LLM+19
]
(which are not proof-of-stake or proof-of-work systems) a notion of ‘actionable coalition’ is represented explicitly in the engineering architecture of the system.

Thus, semitopologies reflect and are motivated by simple and natural intuitive examples as above, and also by the practical realities of more complex systems that at time of writing are up and running and providing services to their users by doing real things.

So at a very high level, what do the examples above have in common?

1.

There is a notion of what we can call an
actionable coalition
— soon, we will just call this an
open set
.
This is a set
O\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
of participants with the capability, though not the obligation, to act collaboratively to advance (= update / transition) the local state of the elements in
O
, possibly but not necessarily in the same way for every
p\in O
(it depends on the context; we will discuss this).

2.

\varnothing
is trivially an actionable coalition.
Also we assume that
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is actionable, since if it were not then literally nothing could ever get done.

3.

A sets union of actionable coalitions, is an actionable coalition.

This leads us to the definition of a semitopology.

Notation 1.1
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set.
Write
\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
for the powerset of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
(the set of subsets of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
); there will be more on this in Notation
2.1
.

Definition 1.2
.

A
semitopological space
, or
semitopology
for short, consists of a pair
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}))
of

•

a (possibly empty) set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
of
points
, and

•

a set
\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\subseteq\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
of
open sets
,

such that:

1.

\varnothing\in\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\in\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.

2.

If
X\subseteq\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
then
\bigcup X\in\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
2
2

2

There is a little overlap between this clause and the first one: if
X=\varnothing
then by convention
\bigcup X=\varnothing
. Thus,
\varnothing\in\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
follows from both clause 1 and clause 2. If desired, the reader can just remove the condition
\varnothing\in\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
from clause 1, and no harm would come of it.

We may write
\mathsf{Open}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
just as
\mathsf{Open}
, if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is irrelevant or understood, and we may write
\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
for the set of nonempty open sets.

The reader will recognise a semitopology as being like a
topology
on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}

[
Eng89
,
Wil70
]
, but without the condition that the intersection of two open sets necessarily be an open set.
This reflects the fact that the intersection of two actionable coalitions need not itself be an actionable coalition.

Armed with this simple definition and bearing in mind its modern relevance as noted above, we introduce and survey semitopologies and their properties.
There is an emphasis (though not an exclusive one) on studying distributed collaborative actions, which (broadly speaking) amounts to studying antiseparation properties of points, and how this interacts with topological continuity of functions out of semitopologies.
The details of what this means are unpacked below.

We will proceed in three parts, by which we hope to give a comprehensive overview of our approach to distributed collaborative action:

point-set semitopologies, algebra, and logic.

The point-set semitopologies are most pertinent to concrete models (i.e. real networks), the algebra is most pertinent to giving a more abstract (algebraic) view of what are the essential structures in play, and the logic is pertinent to specifying properties and — because logic is a portal to computation — computing/checking these properties.

Remark 1.3
.

Traditional notions of consensus and voting can be understood in a semitopological framework.
For instance, a committee may make a decision by two-thirds majority vote; the set of all 2/3 majorities of some
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a semitopology (note that it is not a topology).
Also, concrete algorithms to attain consensus often use a notion of
quorum

[
Lam98
,
LSP82
]
; a set of participants whose unanimous adoption of a value guarantees that other (typically all other) participants will eventually also adopt this value.
Social choice theorists have a similar notion called a
winning coalition

[
Rik62
, Item 5, page 40]
.
If the reader has a background in logic then they may be reminded of a whole field of
generalised quantifiers
(a good survey is in
[
Wes11
]
).

The reader should just note that these examples have a synchronous, centralised flavour.

For instance: a vote in the typical democratic sense is a synchronous, global operation (unless the result is disputed): votes are cast, collected, and then everyone gets together --- e.g. in a vote counting hall --- to count the votes and agree on who won and so certify the outcome.
3
3

3

The first author has seen this happen; votes being tallied up while under supervision by representatives of all parties on the ballot. It is a moving sight. But it is not distributed.

This is certainly a collaborative action, but it is centralised, not distributed.

Our semitopological framework adds to the above by allowing us to study
distributed
collaborative action, which can progress by local state updates on actionable coalitions (which certainly do not need to be simple majorities), and they can so act without
necessarily
having to synchronise step-by-synchronous-step on global state updates.

2
Map of the book

As already mentioned, this book is in three major parts:

1.

We consider point-set semitopologies in Sections
1
to
10
.

2.

We then take an algebraic, point-free, categorical approach by studying semiframes in Sections
11
to
16
.

3.

Finally, we consider logic over semitopologies in Sections
17
to
21
.

In a nutshell, this book studies the topology, algebra, and logic of semiframes, as follows:

1.

Section
Semitopology: distributed collaborative action via topology, algebra, and logic
is the Introduction. You Are Here.

Semitopologies

2.

In Section
1
we show how
continuity corresponds to local agreement
(Definition
1.2
and Lemma
2.4
).

3.

In Section
2
we discuss
transitive sets
,
topens
, and
intertwined points
.
These are all different views on the anti-separation well-behavedness properties that will interest us in this book.
Most of Section
2
is concerned with showing how these different views relate and in what senses they are equivalent (e.g. Theorem
6.8
).
Transitive sets are guaranteed to be in agreement (in a sense made precise in Theorem
2.3
and Corollary
2.5
), and we take a first step to understanding the fine structure of semitopologies by proving that every semitopology partitions into topen sets (Theorem
5.4
), plus other kinds of points which we classify in the next Section.

4.

In Section
3
we start to classify points in more detail, introducing notions of
regularity
for points in Definition
1.4
.
This is the start of a classification of good properties for points in semitopologies, including: regular, weakly regular, indirectly regular, quasiregular, unconflicted, hypertransitive, and more.

5.

In Section
4
we study
closed sets
, and in particular the interaction between intertwined points, topens, and closures.
Typical results are Proposition
4.3
and Theorem
6.2
which characterise sets of intertwined points as minimal closures.
The significance to consensus is discussed in Remarks
5.1
and
5.5
.

6.

In Section
5
we study
unconflicted
and
hypertransitive
points, leading to two useful characterisations of regularity in Theorems
2.2
and
5.8
.

7.

In Section
6
we consider
product semitopologies
.
These are defined just as for topologies (Definition
1.2
) but we study how the semitopological properties we have considered above — like being intertwined, topen, regular, conflicted, and so forth — interact with taking products.
This is also useful for building large complex counterexamples out of smaller simpler ones (examples in Corollary
2.6
or Theorem
3.4
).

8.

In Section
7
we construct a novel theory of computationally tractable semitopologies, based on
witness functions
(Definition
2.2
(
1
)).
We call semitopologies generated by witness functions
witness semitopolgies
.
These display excellent algorithmic behaviour (Remarks
4.6
and
4.14
) and we note deep reasons why this is so by showing that witness functions correspond to Horn clause theories, and that open and closed sets in the witness semitopology are related to answer sets to those theories; see Subsection
5
.

9.

In Section
8
we introduce
(strongly) chain-complete
semitopologies.
We argue in Remark
4.6
that these have properties making them a suitable abstraction of finite semitopologies — finite semitopologies are of particular interest because these are the ones that we can build.
We study their properties and prove a key result that witness semitopologies are chain-complete (Theorem
4.1
), even if they are infinite.
4
4

4

We discuss why infinite semitopologies matter, even in a world of finite implementations, in Remark
4.7
. Note also that in a real system there may be hostile participants who report an unbounded space of ‘phantom’ points, either for denial-of-service or to create ‘extra voters’. So even a system that is physically finite may present itself as infinite.

10.

A key property in a strongly chain-complete semitopology is that the poset of open sets is
atomic
, i.e. minimal nonempty open sets always exist.
In Section
9
we study
kernels
— unions of atomic transitive open sets — especially in strongly chain-complete semitopologies where atoms are guaranteed to exist.
We will see that the kernel dictates behaviour in a sense we make formal (see discussion in Remark
1.1
).

11.

In Section
10
we study notions of
dense subset of
from topology and see that this splits into two notions:
weakly dense in
and
strongly dense in
(Definition
1.2
).
Transitivity turns out to be closely related to denseness (Proposition
4.6
).
We prove a continuous extension result and show that this leads naturally back to the notion of regular point and topen set which we developed to begin with (Remark
5.1
).

Semiframes

12.

In Section
11
we introduce
semiframes
.
These are the algebraic version of semitopologies, and they are to semitopologies as frames are to topologies.
We discover that semiframes are not just join-semilattices;
semiframes are
compatible
semilattices
, which include a
compatibility relation

\ast
to abstract the property of sets intersection
\between
(see Remark
1.2
).

13.

In Section
12
we introduce
semifilters
.
These play a similar role as filters do in topologies, except that semifilters have a
compatibility condition
instead of closure under finite meets.
We develop the notion of abstract points (completely prime semifilters), and show how to build a semitopology out of the abstract points of a semiframe.

14.

In Section
13
we introduce
sober semitopologies
and
spatial semiframes
.
The reader familiar with categorical duality will know these conditions.
Some of the details are significantly different (see for instance the discussion in Subsection
2
) but at a high level
these conditions work in the proofs just as they do for the topological duality.

15.

In Section
14
we consider the
duality
between suitable categories of (sober) semitopologies and (spatial) semiframes.

16.

In Section
15
we
dualise the well-behavedness conditions
from Section
2
to algebraic versions.
The correspondence is good (Proposition
6.2
) but also imperfect in some interesting ways (Remark
8.8
).

17.

In Section
16
we briefly consider alternative
graph-based representations
of semitopologies.

Logic

18.

In Section
17
we introduce a
three-valued modal logic
to describe properties of semitopologies.
Logic is closely related to computation, so what we can describe we can — by passing it to a solver or a prover — also compute.

19.

In Section
18
we use our logic to start
axiomatising
.
In particular, in Definition
1.1
we carry out the most basic task and write down axioms in our logic that correspond to continuity.

20.

In Section
19
we
axiomatise the regularity properties
that we have already investigated.

21.

In Section
20
we look at computing in more detail.
We show how to
convert a witness function into a logical theory
suitable for passing to a SAT solver, we investigate notions of Horn clause programming (both for two-valued and three-valued logic), and we show in Theorem
1.6
that
determining whether two points are intertwined is NP-complete
in general.

22.

In Section
21
we open up a fresh line of inquiry and consider
extremal valuations
, which roughly speaking correspond to final states of a system in which every participant who could return a definite value, has returned a definite value.
This turns up to open a new design space which we put in the context of the maths thus far.

Conclusions

23.

In Section
22
we conclude and discuss related and future work.

Remark 2.1
.

Algebraic topology has been applied to the solvability of distributed-computing tasks in various computational models (e.g. the impossibility of wait-free
k
-set consensus using read-write registers and the Asynchronous Computability Theorem
[
HS93
,
BG93a
,
SZ93
]
; see
[
HKR13
]
for a survey).
Semitopology is not topology, and this is not a paper about algebraic topology applied to the solvability of distributed-computing tasks!

This paper is about the mathematics of actionable coalitions, as made precise by point-set semitopologies; their antiseparation properties; and the implications to partially continuous functions on of them.
If we discuss distributed systems, it is by way of providing motivating examples or noting applicability.

Part 1
Point-set semitopologies

Chapter 1
Semitopology

1
Definitions, examples, and some discussion

1
Definitions

Recall from Definition
1.2
the definition of a semitopology.

Remark 1.1
.

1.

As a sets structure, a semitopology on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is like a
topology
on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, but without the condition that the intersection of two open sets be an open set.

2.

As a lattice structure, a semitopology on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a
bounded complete join-subsemilattice of
\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
1
1

1

Bounded
means closed under empty intersections and unions, i.e. containing the empty and the full set of points.
Complete
means closed under arbitrary (possibly empty, possibly infinite) sets unions.
The reader may know that a complete lattice is also co-complete: if we have all joins, then we also have all meets. However, note that there is no reason for the meets in
\mathsf{Open}
to coincide with the meets in
\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
, i.e. for them to be sets intersections.
Also, note that this does not mean that semitopologies are ‘just’ bounded complete join-subsemilattices. They are in fact
compatible
bounded complete join-semilattices. See Section
11
.

3.

Every semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
gives rise to a topology just by closing opens under intersections.
But, there is more to semitopologies than being subbases for a corresponding topology, because:

(a)

We are explicitly interested in situations where intersections of open sets need
not
be open.

(b)

Completing a semitopology to a topology by closing under intersections, loses information.
For example: the ‘many’, ‘all-but-one’, and ‘more-than-one’ semitopologies in Example
1.4
express three distinct notions of quorum, yet all three yield the discrete semitopology (Definition
1.3
) if we close under intersections and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is infinite.
See also the overview in Subsection
1
.

Semitopologies are not topologies.
We take a moment to spell out one concrete difference:

Lemma 1.2
.

In topologies, if a point
p
has a minimal open neighbourhood then it is least.
In semitopologies, a point may have multiple distinct minimal open neighbourhoods.
2
2

2

We study minimal open neighbourhoods in detail, starting from Definition
5.2
.

Proof.

To see that in a topology every minimal open neighbourhood is least, just note that if
p\in A
and
p\in B
then
p\in A\cap B
.
So if
A
and
B
are two minimal open neighbourhoods then
A\cap B
is contained in both and by minimality is equal to both.

To see that in a semitopology a minimal open neighbourhood need not be least, it suffices to provide an example.
Consider
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
defined as follows, as illustrated in Figure
1
:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}

•

\mathsf{Open}=\bigl\{\varnothing,\ \{0,1\},\ \{1,2\},\ \{0,1,2\}\bigr\}

Note that
1
has two minimal open neighbourhoods:
\{0,1\}
and
\{1,2\}
.
∎

Figure 1
:
An example of a point with two minimal open neighbourhoods (Lemma
1.2
)

2
Examples

As standard, we can make any set
\mathsf{Val}
into a semitopology (indeed, it is also a topology) just by letting open sets be the powerset:

Definition 1.3
.

1.

Call
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}))
the
discrete semitopology on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

We may call a set with the discrete semitopology a
semitopology of values
, and when we do we will usually call it
\mathsf{Val}
.
We may identify
\mathsf{Val}
-the-set and
\mathsf{Val}
-the-discrete-semitopology; meaning will always be clear.

2.

When
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
\mathsf{Val}
is a semitopology of values, we may call a function
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
a
value assignment
.

Note that a value just assigns values to points, and in particular we do not assume
a priori
that it is continuous, where continuity is defined just as for topologies (see Definition
2.1
).

Example 1.4
.

We consider further examples of semitopologies:

1.

Every topology is also a semitopology; intersections of open sets are allowed to be open in a semitopology, they are just not constrained to be open.
In particular, the discrete topology is also a discrete semitopology (Definition
1.3
(
1
)).

2.

The
initial semitopology

(\varnothing,\{\varnothing\})
and the
final semitopology

(\{\ast\},\{\varnothing,\{\ast\}\})
are semitopologies.

3.

An important discrete semitopological space is

\mathbb{B}=\{\bot,\top\}\quad\text{with the discrete semitopology}\quad\mathsf{Open}(\mathbb{B})=\{\varnothing,\{\bot\},\{\top\},\{\bot,\top\}\}.

We may silently treat
\mathbb{B}
as a (discrete) semitopological space henceforth.

4.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to be any nonempty set.
Let the
trivial semitopology
on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
have

\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}.

So (as usual) there are only two open sets: the one containing nothing, and the one containing every point.

The only nonempty open is
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
itself, reflecting a notion of actionable coalition that requires unanimous agreement.

5.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is nonempty and up-closed (so if
P\in\mathcal{F}
and
P\subseteq P^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
then
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
, then
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathcal{F})
is a semitopology.
This is not necessarily a topology, because we do not insist that
\mathcal{F}
is a filter (i.e. is closed under intersections).

We give four sub-examples for different choices of
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
Partly this is to illustrate how varying
\mathcal{F}
can encode different systems, but also, these variations can have substantially different behavior, as Lemmas
3.3
and
3.5
will illustrate later.

(a)

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,\dots,41\}
.
Let the
supermajority semitopology
have

\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing\}\cup\{O\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid\mathit{cardinality}(O)\geq 28\}.

Since
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has 42 elements,
O
is open when it contains at least two-thirds of the points.

Two-thirds is a typical threshold used for making progress in consensus algorithms.

(b)

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to be any nonempty set.
Let the
many semitopology
have

\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing\}\cup\{O\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid\mathit{cardinality}(O)=\mathit{cardinality}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\}.

For example, if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathbb{N}
then open sets include
\mathit{evens}=\{2*n\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
\mathit{odds}=\{2*n{+}1\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

Its notion of open set captures an idea that an actionable coalition is a set that may not be all of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, but does at least biject with it.

(c)

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to be any nonempty set.
Let the
all-but-one semitopology
have

\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\ \bm{\mathsf{P}}\}\cup\{\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus\{p\}\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}.

This semitopology is not a topology.
See also Lemma
3.3
.

The notion of actionable coalition here is that there may be at most one objector (but not two).

(d)

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to be any set with cardinality at least
2
.
Let the
more-than-one semitopology
have

\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing\}\cup\{O\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid\mathit{cardinality}(O)\geq 2\}.

This semitopology is not a topology.
See also Lemma
3.5
.

This notion of actionable coalition reflects a security principle in banking and accounting (and elsewhere) of
separation of duties
, that functional responsibilities be separated such that at least two people are required to complete an action — so that errors (or worse) cannot be made without being discovered by another person.

6.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathbb{R}
(the set of real numbers) and set
O\subseteq\mathbb{R}
to be open when it has the form
[0,r)
or
(-r,0]
for any strictly positive real number
r>0
.

This semitopology is not a topology, since (for example)
(1,0]
and
[0,1)
are open, but their intersection
\{0\}
is not open.

7.

In
[
NW94
]
a notion of
quorum system
is discussed, defined as any collection of pairwise intersecting sets.
Quorum systems are a field of study in their own right, especially in the theory of concrete consensus algorithms.

Every quorum system gives rise naturally to a semitopology, just by closing under arbitrary unions.
We obtain what in this paper we would call an
intertwined space
(Notation
6.4
; a semitopology all of whose nonempty open sets intersect).
3
3

3

A topologist would call this a
hyperconnected space
, but be careful! There are multiple such notions in semitopologies, so intuitions need not transfer over. See the discussion in Subsection
3
.

Going in the other direction is interesting for a different reason, that it is slightly less canonical: of course every intertwined space is already a quorum system; but (for the finite case) we can also map to the set of all open covers of all points (Definition
5.2
(
2
); in the notation of that Definition, we would write this as
\bigcup_{p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}}\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid O\gtrdot p\}
).

To give one specific example of a quorum system from
[
NW94
]
, consider
n\times n
grid of cells with quorums being sets consisting of any full row and a full column; note that any two quorums must intersect in at least two points.
We obtain a semitopology just by closing under arbitrary unions.

Remark 1.5

(Logical models of semitopologies)
.

One class of examples of semitopologies deserves its own discussion.
Consider an arbitrary logical system with predicates
\mathsf{Pred}
and entailment relation
\vdash
.
4
4

4

A validity relation
\vDash
would also work.

Call
\Phi\subseteq\mathsf{Pred}

deductively closed
when
\Phi\vdash\phi
implies
\phi\in\Phi
.
Then take

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathsf{Pred}
, and

•

let
O\in\mathsf{Open}
be
\varnothing
or the complement to a deductively closed set
\Phi
, so
O=\mathsf{Pred}\setminus\Phi
.

Note that an arbitrary union of open sets is open (because an arbitrary intersection of deductively closed sets is deductively closed), but an intersection of open sets need not be open (because the union of deductively closed sets need not be deductively closed).
This is a semitopology.

This example will be important to us and we will return to it in Subsection
5
.

We can note further that:

1.

There is a traditional, simple model of propositional logic whereby we let propositions be denoted by open sets.
Intuitively, these points are ‘worlds’ at which the proposition is ‘true’.
The example of semitopologies given above is
not
this.
For a start, in the model above propositions are points, not sets of points.

2.

Variations on our model above are possible, all with a theme that associates closed sets with consistency and deductive closure, and open sets with inconsistency.
For example:

(a)

Call
\Phi\subseteq\mathsf{Pred}

inconsistent
when
\Phi\vdash
.

Then we can take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathsf{Pred}
, and we can let
\mathsf{Open}
be the set of inconsistent sets of predicates.
Note that an arbitrary union of inconsistent sets of predicates is inconsistent, but an intersection of inconsistent sets of predicates need not be inconsistent.

(b)

We can take open sets to be arbitrary unions of
minimal
inconsistent sets of predicates; then the previous notion of ‘open set’ can be recovered as ‘has a nonempty open interior’.

(c)

We can restrict
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to
atomic predicates
(ones not created by logical connectives or quantifiers, such as
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
, or

\forall

).
5
5

5

In a proposition logic these would be called
propositional constants
, such as
it is raining
; in a predicate logic these might thake the form of predicate-formers applied to closed terms, such as
mortal(Socrates)
or perhaps
1+2=3
.

3
Some discussion

Remark 1.6

(Why the name ‘semitopologies’)
.

When we give a name ‘semitopologies’ to things that are like topologies but without intersections, this is a riff on

•

‘semilattices’, for things that are like lattices with joins but without meets (or vice-versa), and

•

‘semigroups’, for things that are like groups but without inverses.

But, this terminology also reflects a real mathematical connection, because semitopologies
are
semilattices
are
semigroups, in standard ways which we take a moment to spell out:

•

A semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a bounded join subsemilattice of the powerset
\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
, by taking the join
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
to be sets union
\cup
and the bounds
\bot
and
\top
to be
\varnothing
and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
respectively.

•

A semilattice is an idempotent commutative monoid, which is an idempotent commutative semigroup with an identity, by taking the multiplication
\circ
to be
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
and the identity element to be
\bot
(
\top
becomes what is called a
zero
or
absorbing
element, such that
\top\circ x=\top
always).

Figure 2
:
Two nonidentical semitopologies (Remark
1.7
)

Remark 1.7

(Semitopologies are not
just
semilattices)
.

We noted in Remark
1.6
that every semitopology is a semilattice.
This is true, but the reader should not read this statement as reductive: semitopologies are not
just
semilattices.

To see why, consider the following two simple semitopologies, as illustrated in Figure
2
:

1.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
where
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\bigl\{\varnothing,\{0,1\},\{1,2\},\{0,1,2\}\bigr\}
.

2.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
where
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}=\bigl\{\varnothing,\{0\},\{2\},\{0,2\}\bigr\}
.

Note that the semilattices of open sets
\mathsf{Open}
and
\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}
are isomorphic — so, when viewed as semilattices these two semitopologies are the same (up to isomorphism).

However,
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is not the same semitopology as
(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
.
There is more than one way to see this, but perhaps the simplest indication is that there is no inverse pair of continuous injections
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
and
g:(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
(we will define continuity in a moment in Definition
2.1
(
2
) but is just as for topologies, so we take the liberty of using it here).
There are a limited number of possibilities for
f
and
g
, and we can just enumerate them and check:

•

If
f(0)=0
and
f(2)=2
then there is no continuous inverse
g
:
if
g(1)=0
then
g^{\text{-}1}(\{2\})=\{2\}\not\in\mathsf{Open}
, and similarly if
g(1)=1
then
g^{\text{-}1}(\{0\})=\{0\}\not\in\mathsf{Open}
.

•

If
f(0)=0
and
f(2)=1
then there is still no continuous inverse
g
:
if
g(1)=0
then
g^{\text{-}1}(\{2\})=\{1\}\not\in\mathsf{Open}
, and similarly if
g(1)=2
then
g^{\text{-}1}(\{0\})=\{0\}\not\in\mathsf{Open}
.

•

Other possibilites are no harder.

Remark 1.8

(‘Stronger’ does not necessarily equal ‘better’)
.

We conclude with some easy predictions about the theory of semitopologies, made just from general mathematical principles.
Fewer axioms means:

1.

more
models,

2.

finer discrimination
between definitions, and

3.

(because there are more models)
more counterexamples
.

So we can expect a theory with the look-and-feel of topology, but with new models, new distinctions between definitions that in topology may be equivalent, and some new definitions, theorems, and counterexamples — and this indeed will be the case.

Note that fewer axioms does not necessarily mean fewer interesting things to say and prove.
On the contrary: if we can make finer distinctions, there may also be more interesting things to prove; and furthermore, assumptions we make can become
more
impactful in a weaker system, because these assumptions may exclude more models than would have been the case with more powerful axioms.
We see this e.g. in the theory of semigroups, which has its own character that is quite distinct from the theory of groups.
6
6

6

To take this to an extreme, consider the
terminal
theory, which has just one first-order axiom:
\exists x.\forall y.x=y
. This theory ‘subsumes’ groups, lattices, graphs, and much besides, in the sense that every model of the terminal theory
is
a group, a lattice, and a graph, in a natural way.
However, the theory is so strong, and the class of its models is so restricted — just the singleton model with one element — that there is not much left to say about it.
Additional assumptions we may make on elements add literally nothing of value, because there was only one element to begin with!
So a ‘stronger’ set of axioms is not necessarily ‘better’, and conversely a ‘weaker’ set of axioms is not necessarily ‘less useful’. It depends on what we want to do, and on how the maths turns out.

2
Continuity, and its interpretation

We can import the topological notion of continuity and it works fine in semitopologies, and the fact that there are no surprises is a feature.
In Remark
2.5
we explain how these notions matter to us:

Definition 2.1
.

We import standard topological notions of inverse image and continuity:

1.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
are any sets and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
is a function.
Suppose
O^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
.
Then write
f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})
for the
inverse image
or
preimage
of
O^{\prime}
, defined by

f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})=\{p{\in}\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid f(p)\in O^{\prime}\}.

2.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and
(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
are semitopological spaces (Definition
1.2
).
Call a function
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}

continuous
when the inverse image of an open set is open.
In symbols:

\forall O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}.f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}.

3.

Call a function
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}

continuous at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
when

\forall O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}.f(p)\in O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\exists O_{p,O^{\prime}}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O_{p,O^{\prime}}\land O_{p,O^{\prime}}\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime}).

In words:
f
is continuous at
p
when the inverse image of every open neighbourhood of
f(p)
contains an open neighbourhood of
p
.

4.

Call a function
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}

continuous on
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
when
f
is continuous at every
p\in P
.

(It is routine to check that
f
is continous on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
precisely when it is continuous in the sense of part
2
of this Definition.)

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and
(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
are semitopological spaces (Definition
1.2
) and suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
is a function.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is continuous (Definition
2.1
(2)).

2.

f
is continuous at every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
(Definition
2.1
(3)).

Proof.

The top-down implication is immediate, taking
O=f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})
.

For the bottom-up implication, given
p
and an open neighbourhood
O^{\prime}\ni f(p)
, we write

O=\bigcup\{O_{p,O^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}},\ f(p)\in O^{\prime}\}.

Above,
O_{p,O^{\prime}}
is the open neighbourhood of
p
in the preimage of
O^{\prime}
, which we know exists by Definition
2.1
(3).

It is routine to check that
O=f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})
, and since this is a union of open sets, it is open.
∎

Definition 2.3
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and

•

\mathsf{Val}
is a semitopology of values (Definition
1.3
(
1
)) and

•

f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment (Definition
1.3
(
2
); an assignment of a value to each element in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
).

Then:

1.

Call
f

locally constant at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
when there exists
p\in O_{p}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
\forall p^{\prime}{\in}O_{p}.f(p)=f(p^{\prime})
.

So
f
is locally constant at
p
when it is constant on some open neighbourhood
O_{p}
of
p
.

2.

Call
f

locally constant
when it is locally constant at every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
\mathsf{Val}
is a semitopology of values and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

f
is locally constant / locally constant at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
(Definition
2.3
).

•

f
is continuous / continuous at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
(Definition
2.1
).

Proof.

This is just by pushing around definitions, but we spell it out:

•

Suppose
f
is continuous, consider
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and write
v=f(p)
.
By our assumptions we know that
f^{\text{-}1}(v)
is open, and
p\in f^{\text{-}1}(v)
.
This is an open neighbourhood
O_{p}
on which
f
is constant, so we are done.

•

Suppose
f
is locally constant, consider
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and write
v=f(p)
.
By assumption we can find
p\in O_{p}\in\mathsf{Open}
on which
f
is constant, so that
O_{p}\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}(v)
.
∎

Remark 2.5

(Continuity = distributed agreement)
.

Lemma
2.4
tells us that
we can view the problem of attaining agreement across an actionable coalition (as discussed in Subsection
1
) as being the same thing as computing a value assignment that is continuous on that coalition (and possibly elsewhere).

To see why, consider a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and following the intuitions discussed in Subsection
1
view points
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
as
participants
; and view open neighbourhoods
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}
as
actionable coalitions
that include
p
.
Then to say “
f
is a value assignment that is continuous at
p
” is to say that:

•

f
assigns a value or belief to
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and

•

p
is part of a (by Lemma
2.4
continuity) set of peers that agrees with
p
and (being open) can progress to act on this agreement.

Conceptually and mathematically this reduces the general question

How can we model distributed collaborative action?

(which, to be fair, has more than one possible answer!) to a more specific research question

Understand continuous value assignments on semitopologies
.

The rest of this paper is devoted to elaborating (some of) a body of mathematics that we can pull out of this idea.

3
Neighbourhoods of a point

Definition
3.1
is a standard notion from topology, and Lemma
3.2
is a (standard) characterisation of openness, which will be useful later:

Definition 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then call
O
an
open neighbourhood
of
p
when
p\in O
.

In other words: an open set is (by definition) an
open neighbourhood
precisely for the points that it contains.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is any set of points.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

P\in\mathsf{Open}
.

•

Every point
p
in
P
has an open neighbourhood in
P
.

In symbols we can write:

\forall p{\in}P.\exists O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.(p\in O\land O\subseteq P)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad P\in\mathsf{Open}

Proof.

If
P
is open then
P
itself is an open neighbourhood for every point that it contains.

Conversely, if every
p\in P
contains some open neighbourhood
p\in O_{p}\subseteq P
then
P=\bigcup\{O_{p}\mid p\in P\}
and this is open by condition
2
of Definition
1.2
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

An initial inspiration for modelling distributed collaborative action using semitopologies, came from noting that the standard topological property described above in Lemma
3.2
, corresponds to the
quorum sharing
property in
[
LGM19
, Property 1]
; the connection to topological ideas had not been noticed in
[
LGM19
]
.

Chapter 2
Transitive sets & topens

1
Some background on sets intersection

Some notation will be convenient:

Notation 1.1
.

Suppose
X
,
Y
, and
Z
are sets.

1.

Write

X\between Y\quad\text{when}\quad X\cap Y\neq\varnothing.

When
X\between Y
holds then we say (as standard) that
X
and
Y

intersect
.

2.

We may chain the
\between
notation, writing for example

X\between Y\between Z\quad\text{for}\quad X\between Y\ \land\ Y\between Z

3.

We may write
X\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}Y
for
\neg(X\between Y)
, thus
X\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}Y
when
X\cap Y=\varnothing
.

Remark 1.2
.

Note on design in Notation
1.1
:

It is uncontroversial that if
X\neq\varnothing
and
Y\neq\varnothing
then
X\between Y
should hold precisely when
X\cap Y\neq\varnothing
.

But there is an edge case!
What truth-value should
X\between Y
return when
X
or
Y
is empty?

1.

It might be nice if
X\subseteq Y
would imply
X\between Y
.
This argues for setting

(X=\varnothing\lor Y=\varnothing)\Longrightarrow X\between Y.

2.

It might be nice if
X\between Y
were monotone on both arguments (i.e. if
X\between Y
and
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
then
X^{\prime}\between Y
).
This argues for setting

(X=\varnothing\lor Y=\varnothing)\Longrightarrow X\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}Y.

3.

It might be nice if
X\between X
always — after all, should a set
not
intersect itself? — and this argues for setting

\varnothing\between\varnothing,

even if we also set
\varnothing\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}Y
for nonempty
Y
.

All three choices are defensible, and they are consistent with the following nice property:

X\between Y\Longrightarrow(X\between X\lor Y\between Y).

We choose the second — if
X
or
Y
is empty then
X\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}Y
— because it gives the simplest definition that
X\between Y
precisely when
X\cap Y\neq\varnothing
.

We list some elementary properties of
\between
:

Lemma 1.3
.

1.

X\between X
if and only if
X\neq\varnothing
.

2.

X\between Y
if and only if
Y\between X
.

3.

X\between(Y\cup Z)
if and only if
(X\between Y)\lor(X\between Z)
.

4.

If
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
and
X\neq\varnothing
then
X\between X^{\prime}
.

5.

Suppose
X\between Y
.
Then
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
implies
X^{\prime}\between Y
, and
Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}
implies
X\between Y^{\prime}
.

6.

If
X\between Y
then
X\neq\varnothing
and
Y\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

By facts of sets intersection.
∎

2
Transitive open sets and value assignments

Definition 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Suppose
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is any set of points.

1.

Call
T

transitive
when

\forall O,O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.O\between T\between O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}.

2.

Call
T

topen
when
T
is nonempty transitive and open.
1
1

1

The empty set is trivially transitive and open, so it would make sense to admit it as a (degenerate) topen. However, it turns out that we mostly need the notion of ‘topen’ to refer to certain kinds of neighbourhoods of points (we will call them
communities
; see Definition
1.4
). It is therefore convenient to exclude the empty set from being topen, because while it is the neighbourhood of every point that it contains, it is not a neighbourhood of any point.

We may write

\mathsf{Topen}=\{T\in\mathsf{Open}\mid T\text{ is topen}\}.

3.

Call
S
a
maximal topen
when
S
is a topen that is not a subset of any strictly larger topen.
2
2

2

‘Transitive open’
\to
‘topen’, like ‘closed and open’
\to
‘clopen’.

Remark 2.2
.

1.

Notions of
strong
transitivity and topen are in Definition
7.5
.

2.

Transitive sets are of interest because values of continuous functions are strongly correlated on them.
This is Theorem
2.3
.

Theorem 2.3
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.

•

\mathsf{Val}
is a semitopology of values (a nonempty set with the discrete semitopology; see Definition
1.3
(1)).

•

f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment (Definition
1.3
(
2
)).

•

T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a transitive set (Definition
2.1
) — in particular this will hold if
T
is topen — and
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.

Then:

1.

If
f
is continuous at
p
and
p^{\prime}
then
f(p)=f(p^{\prime})
.

2.

As a corollary, if
f
is continuous on
T
, then
f
is constant on
T
.

In words we can say:

Continuous value assignments are constant across transitive sets.

Proof.

Part 2 follows from part 1 since if
f(p)=f(p^{\prime})
for
any

p,p^{\prime}\in T
, then by definition
f
is constant on
T
.
So we now just need to prove part 1 of this result.

Consider
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.
By continuity on
T
, there exist open neighbourhoods
p\in O\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}(f(p))
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}(f(p^{\prime}))
.
By construction
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
(because
p\in O\cap T
and
p^{\prime}\in T\cap O^{\prime}
).
By transitivity of
T
it follows that
O\between O^{\prime}
.
Thus, there exists
p^{\prime\prime}\in O\cap O^{\prime}
, and by construction
f(p)=f(p^{\prime\prime})=f(p^{\prime})
.
∎

A notation will be useful:

Notation 2.4
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a set and
f
is some function on
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
and
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Suppose further that it is known that
f
is constant on
X
.
In symbols:

\exists c.\forall x{\in}X.f(x)=c.

Then we may write
f(X)
for the unique constant value that
f(x)
takes as
x
ranges over
X
.
3
3

3

We feel a bit guilty about this.
A more principled approach might be to define
f(X)=\{f(x)\mid x\in X\}
, and then write
\{c\}
for
f(X)
where
f
is known constant on
X
. The reader is welcome to fill in the “
\exists c.\forall x{\in}X.f(x)=c\land\dots
” as appropriate.

Corollary
2.5
is an easy and useful consequence of Theorem
2.3
:

Corollary 2.5
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.

•

f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment to some set of values
\mathsf{Val}
(Definition
1.3
).

•

f
is continuous on topen sets
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Topen}
.

Then

T\between T^{\prime}\quad\text{implies}\quad f(T)=f(T^{\prime}).

Proof.

By Theorem
2.3

f
is constant on
T
and
T^{\prime}
, so we can write
f(T)
and
f(T^{\prime})
as per Notation
2.4
.
We assumed that
T
and
T^{\prime}
intersect, and the result follows.
∎

A converse to Theorem
2.3
also holds:

Proposition 2.6
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.

•

\mathsf{Val}
is a semitopology of values with at least two elements (to exclude a denegerate case that no functions exist, or they exist but there is only one because there is only one value to map to).

•

T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is any set.

Then

•

if
for every
p,p^{\prime}\in T
and every value assignment
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
,
f
continuous at
p
and
p^{\prime}
implies
f(p)=f(p^{\prime})
,

•

then

T
is transitive.

Proof.

We prove the contrapositive.
Suppose
T
is not transitive, so there exist
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
and yet
O\cap O^{\prime}=\varnothing
.
We choose two distinct values
v\neq v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Val}
and define
f
to map any point in
O
to
v
and any point in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
to
v^{\prime}
.

Choose some
p\in O
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
.
It does not matter which, and some such
p
and
p^{\prime}
exist, because
O
and
O^{\prime}
are nonempty by Lemma
1.3
(
6
), since
O\between T
and
O^{\prime}\between T
).

We note that
f(p)=v
and
f(p^{\prime})=v^{\prime}
and
f
is continuous at
p\in O
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
, yet
f(p)\neq f(p^{\prime})
.
∎

Remark 2.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
\mathsf{Val}
is a semitopology of values with at least two elements.
Say that a value assignment
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}

splits
a set
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
when there exist
p,p^{\prime}\in T
such that
f
is continuous at
p
and
p^{\prime}
and
f(p)\neq f(p^{\prime})
.
Then Theorem
2.3
and Proposition
2.6
together say in words that:

T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is transitive if and only if it cannot be split by a value assignment.

Intuitively, transitive sets characterise areas of guaranteed agreement.

This reminds us of a basic result in topology about
connected spaces

[
Wil70
, section 26, Chapter 8]
.
Call a topology
(\bm{\mathsf{T}},\mathsf{Open})

disconnected
when there exist open sets
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
O\cap O^{\prime}=\varnothing
(in our notation:
O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O^{\prime}
) and
O\cup O^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{T}}
; otherwise call
(\bm{\mathsf{T}},\mathsf{Open})

connected
.
Then
(\bm{\mathsf{T}},\mathsf{Open})
is disconnected if and only if (in our terminology above) it can be split by a value assignment.
Theorem
2.3
and Proposition
2.6
are not identical to that result, but they are in the same spirit.

3
Examples and discussion of transitive sets and topens

We may routinely order sets by subset inclusion; including open sets, topens, closed sets, and so on, and we may talk about maximal, minimal, greatest, and least elements.
We include the (standard) definition for reference:

Notation 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\leq)
is a poset.
Then:

1.

Call
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}

maximal
when
\forall p^{\prime}.p{\leq}p^{\prime}\Longrightarrow p^{\prime}=p
and
minimal
when
\forall p^{\prime}.p^{\prime}{\leq}p\Longrightarrow p^{\prime}=p
.

2.

Call
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}

greatest
when
\forall p.p^{\prime}\leq p
and
least
when
\forall p^{\prime}.p\leq p^{\prime}
.

Example 3.2

(Examples of transitive sets)
.

1.

\{p\}
is transitive, for any single point
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

2.

The empty set
\varnothing
is (trivially) transitive, but not topen because we insist in Definition
2.1
(
2
).

3.

Call a set
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}

topologically indistinguishable
when (using Notation
1.1
) for every open set
O
,

P\between O\Longleftrightarrow P\subseteq O.

It is easy to check that if
P
is topologically indistinguishable, then it is transitive.

Example 3.3

(Examples of topens)
.

1.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
, with open sets
\varnothing
,
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
,
\{0\}
,
\{2\}
, and
\{0,1,2\}
.
This has two maximal topens
\{0\}
and
\{2\}
, and an isolated point
1
, as illustrated in Figure
1
(top-left diagram).

2.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
, with open sets
\varnothing
,
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
,
\{0\}
,
\{0,1\}
,
\{2\}
,
\{1,2\}
, and
\{0,2\}
.
This has two maximal topens
\{0\}
and
\{2\}
, and an isolated point
1
, as illustrated in Figure
1
(top-right diagram).

3.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3,4\}
, with open sets generated by
\{0,1\}
,
\{1\}
,
\{3\}
, and
\{3,4\}
.
This has two maximal topens
\{0,1\}
and
\{2,3\}
, and an isolated point
0
, as illustrated in Figure
1
(lower-left diagram).

4.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,\ast\}
, with open sets generated by
\{0\}
,
\{1\}
,
\{2\}
,
\{0,1,\ast\}
, and
\{1,2,\ast\}
.
This has three maximal topens
\{0\}
,
\{1\}
, and
\{2\}
, and an isolated point
\ast
, as illustrated in Figure
1
(lower-right diagram).

5.

Take the all-but-one semitopology from Example
1.4
(
5c
) on
\mathbb{N}
: so
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathbb{N}
with opens
\varnothing
,
\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{x\}
for every
x\in\mathbb{N}
.
This has a single maximal topen equal to
\mathbb{N}
itself.

6.

The semitopology in Figure
2
has no topen sets at all (
\varnothing
is transitive and open, but by definition in Definition
2.1
(
2
) topens have to be nonempty).

Figure 1
:
Examples of topens (Example
3.3
)

Remark 3.4

(Discussion)
.

We take a moment for a high-level discussion of where we are going.

The semiopologies in Example
3.3
invite us to ask what makes these examples different (especially parts
1
and
2
).
Clearly they are not equal, but that is a superficial answer in the sense that it is valid just in the world of sets, and it ignores semitopological structure.

For comparison: if we ask what makes
0
and
1
different in
\mathbb{N}
, we could just to say that
0\neq 1
, but this ignores what makes them different
as numbers
.
For more insight, we could note that
0
is the additive unit whereas
1
is the multiplicative unit of
\mathbb{N}
as a semiring; or that
0
is a least element and
1
is the unique atom of
\mathbb{N}
as a well-founded poset; or that
1
is the successor of
0
of
\mathbb{N}
as a well-founded inductive structure.
Each of these answers gives us more understanding, not only into
0
and
1
but also into the structures that can be given to
\mathbb{N}
itself.

So we can ask:

What semitopological property or properties on points can identify the essential nature of the differences between the semitopologies in Example
3.3
?

There would be some truth to saying that the rest of this paper is devoted to developing different answers to this question!
In particular, we will shortly define the set of
intertwined points

p_{\between}
in Definition
6.1
.
Example
6.2
will note that
1_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
in Example
3.3
(
1
), whereas
1_{\between}=\{1\}
in Example
3.3
(
2
), and
x_{\between}=\mathbb{N}
for every
x
in Example
3.3
(3).

4
Closure properties of transitive sets

Remark 4.1
.

Transitive sets have some nice closure properties which we treat in this Subsection — here we mean ‘closure’ in the sense of “the set of transitive sets is closed under various operations”, and not in the topological sense of ‘closed sets’.

Topens — nonempty transitive
open
sets — will have even better closure properties, which emanate from the requirement in Lemma
4.3
that at least one of the transitive sets
T
or
T^{\prime}
is open.
See Subsection
5
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
T
is transitive and
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
, then
T^{\prime}
is transitive.

2.

If
T
is topen and
\varnothing\neq T^{\prime}\subseteq T
is nonempty and open, then
T^{\prime}
is topen.

Proof.

1.

By Definition
2.1
it suffices to consider open sets
O
and
O^{\prime}
such that
O\between T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}
, and prove that
O\between O^{\prime}
.
But this is simple: by Lemma
1.3
(5)
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
, so
O\between O^{\prime}
follows by transitivity of
T
.

2.

Direct from part 1 of this result and Definition
2.1
(
2
).
∎

Lemmas
5.2
and
4.5
are required for, and extended by, Lemma
5.2
:

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.

•

T,T^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
are transitive.

•

At least one of
T
and
T^{\prime}
is open.

Then:

1.

\forall O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}.O\between T\between T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}
.

2.

If
T\between T^{\prime}
then
T\cup T^{\prime}
is transitive.

Proof.

1.

We simplify using Definition
2.1
and our assumption that one of
T
and
T^{\prime}
is open.
We consider the case that
T^{\prime}
is open:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l}O\between T\between T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow&O\between T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}&\text{$T$ transitive, $T^{\prime}$ open}\\
\Longrightarrow&O\between O^{\prime}&\text{$T^{\prime}$ transitive}.\end{array}

The argument for when
T
is open, is precisely similar.

2.

Suppose
O\between T\cup T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
1.3
(
3
) (at least) one of the following four possibilities must hold:

O\between T\land T\between O^{\prime},\quad O\between T^{\prime}\land T\between O^{\prime},\quad O\between T\land T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime},\quad\text{or}\quad O\between T^{\prime}\land T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}.

If
O\between T\ \land\ T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}
then by part 1 of this result we have
O\between O^{\prime}
as required.
The other possibilities are no harder.
∎

Definition 4.4

(Ascending/descending chain)
.

A
chain
of sets
\mathcal{X}
is a collection of sets that is totally ordered by subset inclusion
\subseteq
.
4
4

4

A total order is reflexive, transitive, antisymmetric, and total.

We may call a chain
ascending
or
descending
if we want to emphasise that we are thinking of the sets as ‘going up’ or ‘going down’.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a chain of transitive sets.
Then
\bigcup\mathcal{T}
is a transitive set.

Proof.

Suppose
O\between\bigcup\mathcal{T}\between O^{\prime}
.
Then there exist
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
such that
O\between T
and
T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}
.
But
\mathcal{T}
is totally ordered, so either
T\subseteq T^{\prime}
or
T\supseteq T^{\prime}
.
In the former case it follows that
O\between T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}
so that
O\between O^{\prime}
by transitivity of
T^{\prime}
; the latter case is precisely similar.
∎

5
Closure properties of topens

Definition
5.1
will be useful in Lemma
5.2
(2):

Definition 5.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Call a nonempty set of nonempty open sets
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
a
clique
when its elements pairwise intersect.
5
5

5

We call this a
clique
, because if we form the
intersection graph
with nodes elements of
\mathcal{O}
and with an (undirected) edge between
O
and
O^{\prime}
when
O\between O^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{O}
is a clique precisely when its intersection graph is indeed a clique.
See also Definition
7.12
.
We will return to intersection graphs in Subsection
1
.

In symbols:

\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}\ \text{is a clique}\quad\text{when}\quad\forall O,O^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}.O\between O^{\prime}.

Note that if
\mathcal{O}
is a clique then every
O\in\mathcal{O}
is nonempty, since
\varnothing\between O
is impossible (Notation
1.1
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

If
T
and
T^{\prime}
are an intersecting pair of topens (i.e.
T\between T^{\prime}
), then
T\cup T^{\prime}
is topen.

2.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a clique of topens (Definition
5.1
), then
\bigcup\mathcal{T}
is topen.

3.

If
\mathcal{T}
is a chain of topens then
\bigcup\mathcal{T}
is topen.

Proof.

1.

T\cup T^{\prime}
is open because by Definition
1.2
(
2
) open sets are closed under arbitrary unions, and by Lemma
4.3
(2)
T\cup T^{\prime}
is transitive.

2.

\bigcup\mathcal{T}
is open by Definition
1.2
(
2
).
Also, if
O\between\bigcup\mathcal{T}\between O^{\prime}
then there exist
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
such that
O\between T
and
T^{\prime}\between O^{\prime}
.
We assumed
T\between T^{\prime}
, so by Lemma
4.3
(1) (since
T
and
T^{\prime}
are open) we have
O\between O^{\prime}
as required.

3.

Any chain is pairwise intersecting. We use part 2 of this result.
6
6

6

We could also use Lemma
4.5
. The reader might now ask why Lemma
4.5
was not derived directly from Lemma
4.3
(2); this is because (interestingly) Lemma
4.5
does not require openness.

∎

Corollary 5.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then every topen
T
is contained in a unique maximal topen.

Proof.

Consider
\mathcal{T}
defined by

\mathcal{T}=\{T\cup T^{\prime}\mid T^{\prime}\text{ topen}\land T\between T^{\prime}\}.

By Lemma
5.2
(2) this is a set of topens.
By construction they all contain
T
, and by our assumption that
T\neq\varnothing
they pairwise intersect (since they all contain
T
, at least).
By Lemma
5.2
(3) therefore,
\bigcup\mathcal{T}
is a transitive open set.
It is easy to check that this is the unique maximal transitive open set that contains
T
.
∎

Theorem 5.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then any
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and in particular
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
itself, can be partitioned into:

•

Some disjoint collection of maximal topens.

•

A set of other points, which are not contained in any topen.
In Definition
1.4
we will call these points
irregular
.

See also Corollary
3.3
.

Proof.

Routine from Corollary
5.3
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

It may be useful to put Theorem
5.4
in the context of the terminology, results, and examples that will follow below.
We will have Definition
1.4
(
3
) and Theorem
2.6
.
These will allow us to call a point
p
contained in some maximal topen
T

regular
, and to call the maximal topen
T
of a regular point its
community
.
Then Theorem
5.4
says that a semitopology
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
can be partitioned into:

•

Regular points, which partition into disjoint communities — each community is, in a sense made formal in Theorem
2.3
, a coalition of strongly-correlated regular points acting together — and

•

a set of irregular points, which are in no commmunity and so are not members of any such coalition.

We give examples in Example
3.3
and Figure
1
, and we will see more elaborate examples below (see in particular the collection in Example
6.10
).

In the special case that the entire space consists of a single topen community, there are no irregular points and all participants are guaranteed to agree, where algorithms succeed.
For the application of a single blockchain trying to arrive at consensus, this discussion tells us that we want it to consist, semitopologically, of a single topen.

6
Intertwined points

1
The basic definition, and some lemmas

Definition 6.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

Call
p
and
p^{\prime}

intertwined
when
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
is transitive.
Unpacking Definition
2.1
this means:

\forall O,O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.(p\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime})\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}.

By a mild abuse of notation, write

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}\quad\text{when}\quad\text{$p$ and $p^{\prime}$ are intertwined}.

2.

Define
p_{\between}
(read ‘intertwined of
p
’) to be the set of points intertwined with
p
.
In symbols:

p_{\between}=\{p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}\}.

Example 6.2
.

We return to the examples in Example
3.3
.
There we note that:

1.

1_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
and
0_{\between}=\{0\}
and
2_{\between}=\{2\}
.

2.

1_{\between}=\{1\}
and
0_{\between}=\{0\}
and
2_{\between}=\{2\}
.

3.

x_{\between}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
for every
x
.

It might be tempting to suppose that points being intertwined should be transitive.
Lemma
6.3
shows that this is not necessarily the case:

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then the ‘is intertwined’ relation
\between
is not necessarily transitive.
That is:
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime\prime}
does not necessarily imply
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
The semitopology from Example
3.3
(
1
) (illustrated in Figure
1
, top-left diagram) will do.
Take

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\bm{\mathsf{P}},\{0\},\{2\}\}.

Then

0\between 1\ \ \text{and}\ \ 1\between 2,\quad\text{but}\quad\neg(0\between 2).

∎

There is more to be said about Lemma
6.3
but will need more machinery to express it; we will pick up this thread again in Definition
1.1
.

We conclude with an easy observation:

Notation 6.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Call
\bm{\mathsf{P}}

intertwined
when

\forall p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}.p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}.

In words:
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is intertwined when all of its points are pairwise intertwined.

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is an intertwined space.

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a transitive set in the sense of Definition
2.1
(
1
).

3.

All nonempty open sets intersect.

4.

Every nonempty open set is topen.

Proof.

Routine by unpacking the definitions.
∎

Remark 6.6
.

A topologist would call an intertwined space
hyperconnected
(see Definition
7.12
and the following discussion).
This is also — modulo closing under arbitrary unions — what an expert in the classical theory of consensus might call a
quorum system

[
NW94
]
.

2
Pointwise characterisation of transitive sets

Lemma 6.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

T
is transitive.

2.

\{p,p^{\prime}\}
is transitive for every
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.

3.

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
for every
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.

Proof.

The equivalence of parts 2 and 3 above just restates Definition
6.1
.
We now prove equivalence of parts 1 and 2.

•

Suppose
T
is transitive.

By Lemma
4.2
(1),
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
is transitive for every
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.

•

Suppose
\{p,p^{\prime}\}
is transitive for every
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.

Consider open sets
O
and
O^{\prime}
such that
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
.
Choose
p\in O\cap T
and
p^{\prime}\in O\cap T^{\prime}
.
By construction
\{p,p^{\prime}\}\subseteq T
so this is transitive.
It follows that
O\between O^{\prime}
as required.
∎

Theorem 6.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

T
is topen.

2.

T\in\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
and
\forall p,p^{\prime}{\in}T.p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
.

In words we can say:

A topen is a nonempty open set of intertwined points.

Proof.

By Definition
2.1
(
2
),
T
is topen when it is nonempty, open, and transitive.
By Lemma
6.7
this last condition is equivalent to
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
for every
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.
∎

A value assignment is constant on a pair of intertwined points, where it is continuous:

Corollary 6.9
.

Suppose
\mathsf{Val}
is a semitopology of values and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment (Definition
1.3
)
and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p\between p^{\prime}
.
Then if
f
continuous at
p
and
p^{\prime}
then
f(p)=f(p^{\prime})
.

Proof.

\{p,p^{\prime}\}
is transitive by Theorem
6.8
;
we use Theorem
2.3
.
∎

Remark 6.10

(Intertwined as ‘non-Hausdorff’)
.

Recall that we call a topological space
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})

Hausdorff
(or
\mathbf{T_{2}}
) when any two points can be separated by pairwise disjoint open sets.
Using the
\between
symbol from Notation
1.1
, we rephrase the Hausdorff condition as

\forall p,p^{\prime}.p\neq p^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\exists O,O^{\prime}.(p\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\land\neg(O\between O^{\prime})),

and we can then simplify to this:

\neg\exists p,p^{\prime}.p\neq p^{\prime}\land p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}.

Now note that the Hausdorff condition can be compactly written just as

\forall p.p_{\between}=\{p\}.

(1)

Note how distinct
p
and
p^{\prime}
being intertwined is the
opposite
of being Hausdorff:
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
when
p^{\prime}\in p_{\between}
, and they
cannot
be separated by pairwise disjoint open sets.
Thus the assertion
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
in Theorem
6.8
is a negation to the Hausdorff property:

\exists p.p_{\between}\neq\{p\}.

This is useful because for semitopologies as applied to consensus,

•

being Hausdorff means that the space is separated (which is probably a bad thing, if we are looking for a system with lots of points in consensus), whereas

•

being full of intertwined points means
by Theorem
2.3
that the system will (where algorithms succeed) be full of points whose value assignment agrees (which is a good thing).

In the literature this might be called
avoiding forking
.

7
Strong topens: topens that are also subspaces

1
Definition and main result

Let us take stock and recall that:

•

T
is
topen
when it is a nonempty open transitive set (Definition
2.1
).

•

T
is
transitive
when
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
implies
O\between O^{\prime}
for all
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Opens}
(Definition
2.1
).

•

O\between O^{\prime}
means that
O\cap O^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
(Notation
1.1
).

But, note above that if
T
is topen and
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
then
O\cap O^{\prime}
need not intersect
inside
T
.
It could be that
O
and
O^{\prime}
intersect outside of
T
(an example is in the proof Lemma
7.2
below).

Definition
7.1
spells out a standard topological construction in the language of semitopologies:

Definition 7.1

(Subspaces)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set of points.
Write
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
for the semitopology such that:

•

The points are
T
.

•

The open sets have the form
O\cap T
for
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.

We say that
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
is
T
with the
semitopology inherited from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

We may call
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
a
subspace
of
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, and if the open sets are understood then we may omit mention of them and just write:

A subset
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is naturally a
(semitopological) subspace
of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

(a)

A topen that is not strong (Lemma
7.2
)

(b)

A transitive set that is not strongly transitive (Lemma
7.6
(2))

Figure 2
:
Two counterexamples for (strong) transitivity

Lemma 7.2
.

The property of being a (maximal) topen is not necessarily closed under taking subspaces.

Proof.

It suffices to exhibit a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and a subset
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
such that
T
is topen in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
but
T
is not topen in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
.
We set:

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}\qquad\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\ \{0,2\},\ \{1,2\},\ \{0,1\},\ \bm{\mathsf{P}}\}\qquad T=\{0,1\}

as illustrated in Figure
2
(left-hand diagram).
Now:

•

T
is topen in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, because every open neighbourhood of
0
— that is
\{0,2\}
,
\{0,1\}
, and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
— intersects with every open neighbourhood of
1
— that is
\{1,2\}
,
\{0,1\}
, and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

•

T
is not topen in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
, because
\{0\}
is an open neighbourhood of
0
and
\{1\}
is an open neighbourhood of
1
and these do not intersect.
∎

Lemma
7.2
motivates the following definitions:

Definition 7.3
.

Suppose
X
,
Y
, and
Z
are sets.
Write
X\between_{Y}Z
, and say that
X
and
Z

meet
or
intersect in
Y
, when
(X\cap Y)\between(Z\cap Y)
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose
X
,
Y
, and
Z
are sets.
Then:

1.

The following are equivalent:

X\cap Y\cap Z\neq\varnothing\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad X\between_{Y}Z\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad Y\between_{X}Z\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad X\between_{Z}Y.

2.

X\between_{Y}Y
if and only if
X\between Y
.

3.

If
X\between_{Y}Z
then
X\between Z
.

Proof.

From Definition
7.3
, by elementary sets calculations.
∎

Definition 7.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and recall from Definition
2.1
the notions of
transitive set
and
topen
.

1.

Call
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}

strongly transitive
when

\forall O,O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.O\between T\between O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow O\between_{T}O^{\prime}.

2.

Call
T
a
strong topen
when
T
is nonempty open and strongly transitive,

Lemma 7.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
T
is strongly transitive then it is transitive.

2.

The reverse implication need not hold (even if
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology): it is possible for
T
to be transitive but not strongly transitive.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
T
is strongly transitive and suppose
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
7.4
(2)
O\between_{T}T\between_{T}O^{\prime}
.
By strong transitivity
O\between_{T}O^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
7.4
(3)
O\between O^{\prime}
.
Thus
T
is transitive.

2.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
This is illustrated in Figure
2
(right-hand diagram).
We set:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
, and

•

\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\ \{1\},\ \{0,1\},\ \{1,2\},\ \{0,1,2\}\}
.

•

We set
T=\{0,2\}
.

We note that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology, and it is easy to check that
T
is transitive — we just note that
\{0,1\}\between T\between\{1,2\}
and
\{0,1\}\between\{1,2\}
.
However,
T
is not strongly transitive, because
\{0,1\}\cap\{1,2\}=\{1\}\not\subseteq T
.
∎

Proposition 7.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
T\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

T
is a strong topen.

2.

T
is a topen in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
(Definition
7.1
).

Proof.

Suppose
T
is a strong topen; thus
T
is nonempty, open, and strongly transitive in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.
Then by construction
T
is open in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
, and the strong transitivity property of Definition
7.5
asserts precisely that
T
is transitive as a subset of
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
.

Now suppose
T
is a topen in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
; thus
T
is nonempty, open, and transitive in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
.
Then
T
is nonempty and by assumption above
T\in\mathsf{Open}
.
7
7

7

It does not follow from
T
being open in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
that
T
is open in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, which is why we included an assumption that this holds in the statement of the result.

Now suppose
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
and
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
.
Then by Lemma
7.4
(2)
O\between_{T}T\between_{T}O^{\prime}
, so by transitivity of
T
in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
also
O\between_{T}O^{\prime}
, and thus by Lemma
7.4
(3) also
O\between O^{\prime}
.
∎

2
Connection to lattice theory

There is a notion from order-theory of a
join-irreducible
element (see for example in
[
DP02
, Definition 2.42]
), and a dual notion of
meet-irreducible
element:

Definition 7.8
.

Call an element
s
in a lattice
\mathcal{L}

•

join-irreducible
when
x\vee y=s
implies
x=s
or
y=x
, and

•

meet-irreducible
when
x\wedge y=s
implies
x=s
or
y=s
.

Example 7.9
.

1.

Consider the lattice of finite (possibly empty) subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, with
\mathbb{N}
adjoined as a top element.
Then
\mathbb{N}
is join-irreducible; if
x\cup y=\mathbb{N}
then either
x=\mathbb{N}
or
y=\mathbb{N}
.

2.

Consider
\mathbb{N}
with the
final segment semitopology
such that opens are either
\varnothing
or sets
n_{\geq}=\{n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\mid n^{\prime}\geq n\}
.

Then
\varnothing
is meet-irreducible; if
x\cap y=\varnothing
then either
x=\varnothing
or
y=\varnothing
.

3.

Consider the natural numbers with the lattice structure in which meet is minimum and join is maximum.
Then every element is join- and meet-irreducible; if
x\vee y=z
then
x=z
or
y=z
, and similarly for
x\wedge y
.

Thus we see that irreducibility captures a notion that an element cannot be approached by finitely many joins (or meets).

We spell out how this is relates to our notions of transitivity from Definitions
2.1
and
7.5
:

Lemma 7.10
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

T
is strongly transitive if and only if
\varnothing
is meet-irreducible in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
(Definition
7.1
).

2.

T
is transitive if
\varnothing
is meet-irreducible in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
.

3.

If
T
is transitive it does not necessarily follow that
\varnothing
is meet-irreducible in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
.

Proof.

We are abusing terminology talking about meet-irreducibility in the set of open sets
\{O\cap T\mid O\in\mathsf{Open}\}
of the subspace
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
,
because this is not a lattice since
\mathsf{Open}
is not closed under intersections in general.
8
8

8

Unless
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
happens to be a topology and not just a semitopology, of course.

However, some meets may still exist and in particular it makes sense to ask whether two elements have a sets intersection that is equal to
\varnothing
, since
\varnothing
is in
\{O\cap T\mid O\in\mathsf{Open}\}
— if this reader does not approve, they can call the property ‘partial
\cap
-irreducibility’ instead of ‘meet-irreducibility’ and no harm will come of it.
We can now proceed to reason as follows:

1.

\varnothing
is meet-irreducible in
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
means that
(O\cap T)\cap(O^{\prime}\cap T)=\varnothing
implies
O\cap T=\varnothing
or
O\cap T^{\prime}=\varnothing
.

T
is strongly transitive when (taking the contrapositive in Definition
7.5
(1))
(O\cap T)\cap(T\cap O^{\prime})=\varnothing
implies
O\cap T=\varnothing
or
T\cap O^{\prime}=\varnothing
.

That these conditions are equivalent follows by straightforward sets manipulations.

2.

We can use part 1 of this result and Lemma
7.6
(1), or give a direct argument by sets calculations: if
O\cap O^{\prime}=\varnothing
then
(O\cap T)\cap(T\cap O^{\prime})=\varnothing
and by meet-irreducibility
O\cap T=\varnothing
or
T\cap O^{\prime}=\varnothing
as required.

3.

Figure
2
(left-hand diagram) provides a counterexample, taking
T=\{0,1\}
and
O=\{0,2\}
and
O^{\prime}=\{1,2\}
.
Then
(O\cap T)\cap(T\cap O^{\prime})=\varnothing
but it is not the case that
O\cap T=\varnothing
or
O^{\prime}\cap T=\varnothing
.
∎

Remark 7.11
.

The proof of Lemma
7.10
not hard, but the result is interesting for what it says, and also for what it does not say:

1.

The notion of being a strong topen maps naturally to something in order theory; namely that
\varnothing
is meet-irreducible in the induced poset
\{O\cap T\mid O\in\mathsf{Open}\}
which is the set of open sets of the subspace
(T,\mathsf{Open}\cap T)
of
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

2.

However, this mapping is imperfect: the poset is not a lattice, and it is also not a sub-poset of
\mathsf{Open}
— even if
T
is topen.
If
\mathsf{Open}
were a topology and closed under intersections then we would have a lattice — but it is precisely the point of difference between semitopologies vs. topologies that open sets need not be closed under intersections.

3.

Being transitive does not correspond to meet-irreducibility; there is an implication in one direction, but certainly not in the other.

So, Lemma
7.10
says that (strong) transitivity has a flavour of meet-irreducibility, but in a way that also illustrates — as did Proposition
6.6
(2) — how semitopologies are different, because they are not closed under intersections, and have their own behaviour.

See also the characterisation of strong transitivity in Lemma
4.2
and the surrounding discussion.

3
Topens in topologies

We conclude by briefly looking at what ‘being topen’ means if our semitopology is actually a topology.
We recall a standard definition from topology:

Definition 7.12
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Call
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}

hyperconnected
when all nonempty open subsets of
T
intersect.
9
9

9

Calling this
hyperconnected
is a slight abuse of terminology: in topology, ‘hyperconnected’ is typically used to refer to an entire space rather than an open subset of it. It would be more accurate to call
T
a
