hyperconnected open subspace
.

In symbols:

\forall O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}.O,O^{\prime}\subseteq T\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}.

Lemma 7.13
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
T
is transitive then it is hyperconnected.

2.

The reverse implication need not hold; it is possible for
T
to be hyperconnected but not transitive, even if
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology (not just a semitopology).

3.

The reverse implication need not hold; it is possible for
T
to be hyperconnected but not transitive, even if
T
is an open set.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
\varnothing\neq O,O^{\prime}\subseteq T
.
Then
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
and by transitivity
O\between O^{\prime}
as required.

2.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Consider the semitopology illustrated in the lower-left diagram in Figure
1
(which is a topology), and set
T=\{0,4\}
.
This has no nonempty open subsets so it is trivially hyperconnected.
However,
T
is not transitive because
\{0,1\}\between T\between\{3,4\}
yet
\{0,1\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{3,4\}
.

3.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Consider the semitopology illustrated in the top-right diagram in Figure
1
, and set
T=\{0,1\}
.
This has two nonempty open subsets,
\{0\}
and
\{0,1\}
, so it is hyperconnected.
However,
T
is not transitive, because
\{0\}\between T\between\{1,2\}
yet
\{0\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{1,2\}
.
∎

We know from Lemma
7.6
(2) that ‘transitive’ does not imply ‘strongly transitive’ for an arbitrary subset
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, even in a topology.
When read together with Lemma
7.13
, this invites the question of what happens when

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology, and
also

•

T
is an open set.

In this natural special case, strong transitivity, transitivity, and being hyperconnected, all become equivalent:

Lemma 7.14
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology and suppose
T\in\mathsf{Open}
is an open set.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

T
is a strong topen (Definition
7.5
(
2
)).

•

T
is a topen.

•

T
is hyperconnected.

Proof.

We assumed
T
is open, so the equivalence above can also be thought of as

strongly transitive
\Longleftrightarrow
transitive
\Longleftrightarrow
all nonempty open subsets intersect.

We prove a chain of implications:

•

If
T
is a strong topen then it is a topen by Lemma
7.6
(1).

•

If
T
is a topen then we use Lemma
7.13
(1).

•

Suppose
T
is hyperconnected, so every pair of nonempty open subsets of
T
intersect; and
suppose
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
and
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
.
Then also
(O\cap T)\between T\between(O^{\prime}\cap T)
.
Now
O\cap T
and
O^{\prime}\cap T
are open: because
T
is open; and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a topology (not just a semitopology), so intersections of open sets are open.
By transitivity of
T
we have
O\cap T\between O^{\prime}\cap T
.
Since
O
and
O^{\prime}
were arbitrary,
T
is strongly transitive.
∎

Chapter 3
Open interiors, communities & regular points

1
Community of a (regular) point

Definition
1.1
is standard:

Definition 1.1

(Open interior)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Define
\mathit{interior}(P)
the
interior of
P
by

\mathit{interior}(P)=\bigcup\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid O\subseteq P\}.

Lemma 1.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
\mathit{interior}(P)
from Definition
1.1
is the greatest open subset of
P
.

Proof.

Routine by the construction in Definition
1.1
and closure of open sets under unions (Definition
1.2
(
2
)).
∎

Corollary 1.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
P,P^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then if
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
then
\mathit{interior}(P)\subseteq\mathit{interior}(P^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Routine using Lemma
1.2
.
∎

Definition 1.4

(Community of a point, and regularity)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

Define
\mathit{K}(p)
the
community of
p
by

\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between}).

2.

Extend
\mathit{K}
to subsets
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
by taking a sets union:

\mathit{K}(P)=\bigcup\{\mathit{K}(p)\mid p\in P\}.

3.

Call
p
a
regular point
when its community is a topen neighbourhood of
p
.
In symbols:

p\text{ is regular}\quad\text{when}\quad p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}.

4.

Call
p
a
weakly regular point
when its community is an open (but not necessarily topen) neighbourhood of
p
.
In symbols:

p\text{ is weakly regular}\quad\text{when}\quad p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Open}.

5.

Call
p
a
quasiregular point
when its community is nonempty.
In symbols:

p\text{ is quasiregular}\quad\text{when}\quad\varnothing\neq\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Open}.

6.

If
p
is not regular then we may call it an
irregular point
, or just say that it is not regular.

7.

If
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and every
p\in P
is regular/weakly regular/quasiregular/irregular then we may call
P
a
regular/weakly regular/quasiregular/irregular set
respectively (see also Definition
1.1
(2)).

Remark 1.5
.

Note of Definition
1.4
(
1
) that
\mathit{K}(p)
the community of
p
is always an open set by Lemma
1.2
.
Later on we will observe that
\mathit{K}(p)
is also a
regular
open set; see Corollary
7.4
.
However:

•

\mathit{K}(p)
might be empty: see any
x\in\mathbb{R}
in Example
4.1
(
1
).
If the community is not empty, then
p
is quasiregular.

•

\mathit{K}(p)
need not contain
p
: see point
0
in Example
4.1
(
4
).
If the community does contain
p
then
p
is weakly regular.

•

\mathit{K}(p)
need not be topen: see point
1
in Example
4.1
(
2
).
If the community is topen and it contains
p
, then
p
is regular.

Remark 1.6
.

Our development will mostly be concerned with regular and weakly regular points.
The property of being quasiregular is also important.
Two more regularity-flavoured conditions will appear later:
indirect regularity
in Definition
3.2
, and an
MCN
property is mentioned in Remark
6.18
.

Lemma
1.7
gives an initial overview of the relationships between these properties.
A more detailed treatment follows, which repeats these main points and expands on them and puts them in a detailed context.
See also Remark
3.6
.

Lemma 1.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
p
is regular, then
p
is weakly regular.

2.

If
p
is weakly regular, then
p
is quasiregular.

3.

The converse implications need not hold (we sharpen this result in Theorem
2.2
).

4.

Furthermore, it is possible for a point
p
to not be quasiregular.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

If
p
is regular then by Definition
1.4
(
3
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
, so certainly
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
and by Definition
1.4
(
4
)
p
is weakly regular.

2.

If
p
is weakly regular then by Definition
1.4
(
4
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Open}
, so certainly
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
and by Definition
1.4
(
5
)
p
is quasiregular.

3.

To see that the converse implications need not hold, note that:

•

Point
1
in Example
1.8
(
2
) (illustrated in Figure
1
, top-left diagram) is weakly regular (
\mathit{K}(1)=\{0,1,2\}
) but not regular (
\mathit{K}(1)
is open but not topen).

•

Point
\ast
in Example
1.8
(
3
) (illustrated in Figure
1
, lower-right diagram) is quasiregular (
\mathit{K}(\ast)=\{1\}
is nonempty but does not contain
\ast
).

4.

To see that
p
may not even be quasiregular, take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathbb{R}
(real numbers), with its usual topology (which is also a semitopology).
Then
x_{\between}=\{x\}
and
\mathit{K}(x)=\varnothing
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
More on this in Example
4.1
(
1
) and the surrounding discussion.
∎

Example 1.8
.

1.

In Figure
2
(left-hand diagram),
0
,
1
, and
2
are three intertwined points and the entire space
\{0,1,2\}
consists of a single topen set.
It follows that
0
,
1
, and
2
are all regular and their community is
\{0,1,2\}
.

2.

In Figure
1
(top-left diagram),
0
and
2
are regular and
1
is weakly regular but not regular (
1\in\mathit{K}(1)=\{0,1,2\}
but
\{0,1,2\}
is not topen).

3.

In Figure
1
(lower-right diagram),
0
,
1
, and
2
are regular and
\ast
is quasiregular (
\mathit{K}(\ast)=\{1\}
).

4.

In Figure
1
(top-right diagram),
0
and
2
are regular and
1
is neither regular, weakly regular, nor quasiregular (
\mathit{K}(1)=\varnothing
).

5.

In a semitopology of values
(\mathsf{Val},\mathit{pow}(\mathsf{Val}))
(Definition
1.3
) every value
v\in\mathsf{Val}
is regular, weakly regular, and unconflicted.

6.

In
\mathbb{R}
with its usual topology every point is unconflicted because the topology is Hausdorff and by Equation
1
in Remark
6.10
this means precisely that
p_{\between}=\{p\}
so that is intertwined just with itself; and every point is not weakly regular because
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})=\varnothing
.

Example 1.9
.

When we started looking at semitopologies we gave some examples in Example
1.4
.
These may seem quite elementary now, but we run through them commenting on which spaces are regular, weakly regular, or quasiregular:

1.

The initial semitopology is regular: it has no topen neighbourhoods, but also no points.
The final semitopology is regular: it has one topen neighbourhood, containing one point.

2.

\mathbb{B}
with the discrete semitopology, is regular.
It has two topen neighbourhoods:
\{{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\}
and
\{{\boldsymbol{\top}}\}
.

3.

The trivial topology is regular; it has a single topen neighbourhood that is
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
itself.

4.

The supermajority semitopology is regular.
It has one topen neighbourhood containing all of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

5.

The many semitopology is regular if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is finite (because it is equal to the trivial semitopology), and not even quasiregular if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is finite, because (for infinite
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
)
p_{\between}=\varnothing
for every point.
For example, if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathbb{N}
and
p
is even and
p^{\prime}
is odd, then
\mathit{evens}=\{2*n\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
\mathit{odds}=\{2*n{+}1\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
are disjoint open neighbourhoods of
p
and
p^{\prime}
respectively.

6.

The all-but-one semitopology is regular for
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
having cardinality of
3
or more, since all points are intertwined so there is a single topen neighbourhood which is the whole space.
If
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has cardinality
2
or
1
then we have a discrete semitopology (on two points or one point) and these too are regular, with two or one topen neighbourhoods.

7.

The more-than-one semitopology is not even quasiregular for
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
having cardinality of
4
or more.
If
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has cardinality
3
then we get the left-hand topology in Figure
2
, which is regular.
If
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has cardinality
2
then we get the trivial semitopology, which is regular.

8.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathbb{R}
(the set of real numbers) and set
O\subseteq\mathbb{R}
to be open when it has the form
[0,r)
or
(-r,0]
for any strictly positive real number
r>0
.
The reader can check that this semitopology is regular.

9.

For the automaton example we cannot comment, because it depends on the automaton.

Remark 1.10
.

Definition
1.4
is a key definition and we pause to discuss it:

1.

We can ask:

•

It it always the case that the community of
p
exists?

(Yes)

•

Is the community of
p
always open?

(Yes)

•

Is it always topen?

(No)

•

Is it always an open (or a topen) neighbourhood for
p
?

(No)

•

Is it always nonempty?

(No)

A wealth of behaviour is possible and is explored below, including in Lemma
2.3
and in the examples in Subsection
4
.

2.

Why is it interesting to consider
p
such that
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
?
Clearly calling
p
‘regular’ suggests that non-regular behaviour is ‘bad’, and regular behaviour is ‘good’.
But what is this good behaviour that regularity implies?
Theorem
2.3
(continuous value assignments are constant on topens) tells us that a regular
p
is surrounded by a topen neighbourhood of points
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
that must agree with it under continuous value assignments.
Using our terminology
community
and
regular
, we can says that
the community of a regular
p
shares its values
.

3.

We can sum up the above intuitively as follows:

(a)

We care about transitivity because it implies agreement.

(b)

We care about being open, because it implies actionability.

(c)

Thus, a regular point is interesting because it is a participant in a maximal topen neighbourhood and therefore can
i)
come to agreement and
ii)
take action on that agreement.

4.

A mathematical question then arises: how can the community of
p
can be (semi)topologically characterised?
We will explore this theme, notably in Theorem
2.6
, Proposition
4.10
, and Theorem
6.2
; see also Remark
6.1
.

2
Further exploration of (quasi-/weak) regularity and topen sets

Remark 2.1
.

Recall three common separation axioms from topology:

1.

T_{0}
: if
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
then there exists some
O\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
(p_{1}\in O)\mathbin{\mathsf{\small xor}}(p_{2}\in O)
, where
\mathbin{\mathsf{\small xor}}
denotes
exclusive or
.

2.

T_{1}
: if
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
then there exist
O_{1},O_{2}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
p_{i}\in O_{j}\Longleftrightarrow i=j
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
.

3.

T_{2}
, or the
Hausdorff condition
: if
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
then there exist
O_{1},O_{2}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
p_{i}\in O_{j}\Longleftrightarrow i=j
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, and
O_{1}\cap O_{2}=\varnothing
.
Cf. the discussion in Remark
6.10
.

Even the weakest of the well-behavedness property for semitopologies that we consider in Definition
1.4
— quasiregularity — is in some sense strongly opposed to the space being Hausdorff/
T_{2}
(though not to being
T_{1}
), as Lemma
2.2
makes formal.

Lemma 2.2
.

1.

Every quasiregular Hausdorff semitopology is discrete.

In more detail: if
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology that is quasiregular (Definition
1.4
(
5
)) and Hausdorff (equation
1
in Remark
6.10
), then
\mathsf{Open}=\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.

2.

There exists a (quasi)regular
T_{1}
semitopology that is not discrete.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

By the Hausdorff property,
p_{\between}=\{p\}
.
By the quasiregularity property,
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
.
It follows that
\mathit{K}(p)=\{p\}
.
But by construction in Definition
1.4
(
1
),
\mathit{K}(p)
is an open interior.
Thus
\{p\}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
The result follows.

2.

It suffices to provide an example.
We use the left-hand semitopology in Figure
2
.
Thus
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{0,1\}
,
\{1,2\}
, and
\{2,0\}
.
The reader can check that this is regular (since all three points are intertwined) and
T_{1}
.
∎

Lemma
2.3
confirms in a different way that regularity (Definition
1.4
(
3
)) is non-trivially distinct from weak regularity and quasiregularity:

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Open}
.

2.

\mathit{K}(p)
is not necessarily topen; equivalently
\mathit{K}(p)
is not necessarily transitive.
(More on this later in Subsection
4
.)

Proof.

\mathit{K}(p)
is open by construction in Definition
1.4
(
1
), since it is an open interior.

For part 2, it suffices to provide a counterexample.
We consider the semitopology from Example
3.3
(
1
) (illustrated in Figure
1
, top-left diagram).
We calculate that
\mathit{K}(1)=\{0,1,2\}
so that
\mathit{K}(1)
is an open neighbourhood of
1
— but it is not transitive, and thus not topen, since
\{0\}\cap\{2\}=\varnothing
.

Further checking reveals that
\{0\}
and
\{2\}
are two maximal topens within
\mathit{K}(1)
.
∎

So what is
\mathit{K}(p)
?
We start by characterising
\mathit{K}(p)
as the
greatest
topen neighbourhood of
p
, if this exists:

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and recall from Definition
1.4
(
3
) that
p
is regular when
\mathit{K}(p)
is a topen neighbourhood of
p
.

1.

If
\mathit{K}(p)
is a topen neighbourhood of
p
(i.e. if
p
is regular) then
\mathit{K}(p)
is a maximal topen.

2.

If
p\in T\in\mathsf{Topen}
is a maximal topen neighbourhood of
p
then
T=\mathit{K}(p)
.

Proof.

1.

Since
p
is regular, by definition,
\mathit{K}(p)
is topen and is a neighbourhood of
p
.
It remains to show that
\mathit{K}(p)
is a maximal topen.

Suppose
T
is a topen neighbourhood of
p
; we wish to prove
T\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
.
Since
T
is open it would suffice to show that
T\subseteq p_{\between}
.
By Theorem
6.8

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
for every
p^{\prime}\in T
, and it follows immediately that
T\subseteq p_{\between}
.

2.

Suppose
T
is a maximal topen neighbourhood of
p
.

First, note that
T
is open, and by Theorem
6.8

T\subseteq p_{\between}
, so
T\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.

Now consider any open
O\subseteq p_{\between}
.
Note that
T\cup O
is an open subset of
p_{\between}
, so by Theorem
6.8

T\cup O
is topen, and by maximality
T\cup O\subseteq T
and thus
O\subseteq T
.
It follows that
\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq T
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

We can use Lemma
2.4
to characterise regularity in five equivalent ways: see Theorem
2.6
and Corollary
2.8
.
Other characterisations will follow but will require additional machinery to state (the notion of
closed neighbourhood
; see Definition
4.1
).
See Corollary
4.13
and Theorem
6.2
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is regular, or in full:
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
.

2.

\mathit{K}(p)
is a greatest topen neighbourhood of
p
.

3.

\mathit{K}(p)
is a maximal topen neighbourhood of
p
.

4.

p
has a maximal topen neighbourhood.

5.

p
has some topen neighbourhood.

Proof.

We prove a cycle of implications:

1.

If
\mathit{K}(p)
is a topen neighbourhood of
p
then it is maximal by Lemma
2.4
(1).
Furthermore this maximal topen neighbourhood of
p
is necessarily greatest, since if we have two maximal topen neighbourhoods of
p
then their union is a larger topen neighbourhood of
p
by Lemma
5.2
(
1
) (union of intersecting topens is topen).

2.

If
p_{\between}
is a greatest topen neighbourhood of
p
, then certainly it is a maximal topen neighbourhood of
p
.

3.

If
p_{\between}
is a maximal topen neighbourhood of
p
, then certainly
p
has a maximal topen neighbourhood.

4.

If
p
has a maximal topen neighbourhood then certainly
p
has a topen neighbourhood.

5.

Suppose
p
has a topen neighbourhood
T
.
By Corollary
5.3
we may assume without loss of generality that
T
is a maximal topen.
We use Lemma
2.4
(2).
∎

Theorem
2.6
has numerous corollaries:

Corollary 2.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
\{p\}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then
p
is regular.

Proof.

We noted in Example
3.2
(
1
) that a singleton
\{p\}
is always transitive, so if
\{p\}
is also open, then it is topen, so that
p
has a topen neighbourhood and by Theorem
2.6
(
5
)
p
is topen.
1
1

1

It does not follow from
p\in\{p\}\in\mathsf{Topen}
that
\mathit{K}(p)=\{p\}
: consider
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\{0\},\{0,1\}\}
and
p=0
; then
\{p\}\in\mathsf{Topen}
yet
\mathit{K}(p)=\{0,1\}
.

∎

Corollary 2.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is regular.

2.

p
is weakly regular and
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
for every
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.

It might be useful to look at Example
3.3
(
2
) and Figure
1
(top-right diagram).
In that example the point
1
is
not
regular, and its community
\{0,1,2\}
is not a community for
0
or
2
.

Proof.

We prove two implications, using Theorem
2.6
:

•

Suppose
p
is regular.
By Lemma
1.7
(
1
)
p
is weakly regular.
Now consider
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Theorem
2.6

\mathit{K}(p)
is topen, so it is a topen neighbourhood of
p^{\prime}
.
By Theorem
2.6

\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
is a greatest topen neighbourhood of
p^{\prime}
.
But by Theorem
2.6

\mathit{K}(p)
is also a greatest topen neighbourhood of
p
, and
\mathit{K}(p)\between\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
since they both contain
p^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
5.2
(
1
) and maximality, they are equal.

•

Suppose
p
is weakly regular and suppose
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
for every
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
, and consider
p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
Then
p^{\prime}\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime\prime}
holds, since
p^{\prime\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})=\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Theorem
6.8

\mathit{K}(p)
is topen, and by weak regularity
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
, so by Theorem
2.6

p
is regular as required.
∎

Corollary 2.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then if
p
is regular and
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
then
p^{\prime}
is regular and has the same community.

Proof.

Suppose
p
is regular — so by Definition
1.4
(
3
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
— and suppose
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
Then by Corollary
2.8

\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
, so
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Topen}
and by Theorem
2.6

p^{\prime}
is regular.
∎

Corollary 2.10
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then the following are equivalent for
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
:

•

T
is a maximal topen.

•

T\neq\varnothing
and
T=\mathit{K}(p)
for every
p\in T
.

Proof.

If
T
is a maximal topen and
p\in T
then by Theorem
2.6
(
3
)
T=\mathit{K}(p)
.

If
T\neq\varnothing
and
T=\mathit{K}(p)
for every
p\in T
,
then
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
for every
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
and by Corollary
2.8

p
is regular, so that by
Definition
1.4
(
3
)
T=\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
as required.
∎

3
Intersection and partition properties of regular spaces

Proposition
3.1
is useful for consensus in practice.
Suppose we are a regular point
q
and we have reached consensus with some topen neighbourhood
O\ni q
.
Suppose further that our topen neighbourhood
O
intersects with the maximal topen neighbourhood
\mathit{K}(p)
of some other regular point
p
.
Then Proposition
3.1
tells us that were inside
\mathit{K}(p)
all along.
See also Remark
3.3
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.
Suppose
O\in\mathsf{Topen}
is a topen.
Then

O\between\mathit{K}(p)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad O\subseteq\mathit{K}(p).

Proof.

The right-to-left implication is immediate from Notation
1.1
(
1
), given that
topens are nonempty by Definition
2.1
(
2
).

For the left-to-right implication, suppose
O\between\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Theorem
2.6

\mathit{K}(p)
is a maximal topen, and by Lemma
5.2
(
1
)
O\cup\mathit{K}(p)
is topen.
Then
O\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
follows by maximality.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
are regular.
Then

\mathit{K}(p)\between\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})

(See also Corollary
4.6
, which considers similar properties for
p
and
p^{\prime}
that are not necessarily regular.)

Proof.

We prove two implications.

•

Suppose there exists
p^{\prime\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)\cap\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
.
By Corollary
2.9
(
p^{\prime\prime}
is regular and)
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime\prime})=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
.

•

Suppose
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
.
By assumption
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
, so
p\in\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
.
Thus
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\cap\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
.
∎

We obtain a simple characterisation of regular semitopological spaces:

Corollary 3.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is regular.

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}
partitions into topen sets: there exists some set of topen sets
\mathcal{T}
such that
T\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}T^{\prime}
for every
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}
and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\bigcup\mathcal{T}
.

3.

Every
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has a cover of topen sets: there exists some set of topen sets
\mathcal{T}
such that
X\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

The proof is routine from the machinery that we already have.
We prove equivalence of parts 1 and 2:

1.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is regular, meaning by Definition
1.4
(
7
&
3
) that
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
We set
\mathcal{T}=\{\mathit{K}(p)\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}
.
By assumption this covers
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
in topens, and by Proposition
3.2
the cover is a partition.

2.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a topen partition of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
By definition for every point
p
there exists
T\in\mathcal{T}
such that
p\in T
and so
p
has a topen neighbourhood.
By Theorem
2.6
(
5
&
1
)
p
is regular.

We prove equivalence of parts 2 and 3:

1.

Suppose
\mathcal{T}
is a topen partition of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
X\subseteq\mathcal{P}
.
Then trivially
X\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{T}
.

2.

Suppose every
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has a cover of topen sets.
Then
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has a cover of topen sets; write it
\mathcal{T}
.
By Lemma
5.2
(
1
) we may assume without loss of generality that
\mathcal{T}
is a partition, and we are done.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

The moral we take from the results and examples above (and those to follow) is that the world we are entering has rather different well-behavedness criteria than those familiar from the study of typical Hausdorff topologies like
\mathbb{R}
.
Put crudely:

1.

‘Bad’ spaces are spaces that are not regular.

\mathbb{R}
with its usual topology (which is also a semitopology) is an example of a ‘bad’ semitopology; it is not even quasiregular.

2.

‘Good’ spaces are spaces that are regular.

The supermajority and all-but-one semitopologies from Example
1.4
(
5a
&
5c
) are typical examples of ‘good’ semitopologies; both are intertwined spaces (Notation
6.4
).

3.

Corollary
3.3
shows that the ‘good’ spaces are just the (disjoint, possibly infinite) unions of intertwined spaces.

So to sum this up: the study of successful distributed collaboration is the study of (unions of) intertwined semitopological spaces — i.e. spaces consisting of a single topen set of points that are all intertwined with one another.

4
Examples of communities and (ir)regular points

By Definition
1.4
a point
p
is regular when its community is a topen neighbourhood.
Then a point is
not
regular when its community is
not
a topen neighbourhood of
p
.
We saw one example of this in Lemma
2.3
.
In this subsection we take a moment to investigate the possible behaviour in more detail.

Example 4.1
.

1.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to be
\mathbb{R}
the real numbers, with its usual topology (which is also a semitopology).
Then

x_{\between}=\{x\}
and
\mathit{K}(x)=\varnothing
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

In particular, no
x\in\mathbb{R}
is regular.

2.

We continue the semitopology from Example
3.3
(
1
) (illustrated in Figure
1
, top-left diagram), as used in Lemma
2.3
:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{0\}
and
\{2\}
.

Then:

•

0_{\between}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathit{K}(0)=\mathit{interior}(0_{\between})=\{0\}
.

•

2_{\between}=\{1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(2)=\mathit{interior}(2_{\between})=\{2\}
.

•

1_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(1)=\{0,1,2\}
.

3.

We take, as illustrated in Figure
1
(left-hand diagram):

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3,4\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{1,2\}
,
\{0,1,3\}
,
\{0,2,4\}
,
\{3\}
, and
\{4\}
.

Then:

•

x_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(x)=\mathit{interior}(x_{\between})=\{1,2\}
for
x\in\{0,1,2\}
.

•

x_{\between}=\{x\}=\mathit{K}(x)
for
x\in\{3,4\}
.

(We return to this example in Example
1.3
(
3
), and we will also use it in the proof of Lemma
2.4
.)

4.

We take, as illustrated in Figure
1
(right-hand diagram):

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3,4\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{1\}
,
\{2\}
,
\{3\}
,
\{4\}
,
\{0,1,2,3\}
, and
\{0,1,2,4\}
.

Then:

•

0_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(0)=\{1,2\}
.

•

\mathit{K}(0)
is not transitive and consists of two distinct topens
\{1\}
and
\{2\}
.

•

0\not\in\mathit{K}(0)
.

See Remark
6.3
for further discussion of this example.

5.

The reader can also look ahead to Example
6.10
.
In Example
6.10
(1), every point
p
is regular and
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathbb{Q}^{2}
.
In Example
6.10
(2), no point
p
is regular and
\mathit{K}(p)=\varnothing\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{2}
.

Figure 1
:
Illustration of Example
4.1
(
3
&
4
)

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then precisely one of the following possibilities must hold, and each is possible:

1.

p
is regular:
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
and
\mathit{K}(p)
is topen (nonempty, open, and transitive).

2.

\mathit{K}(p)
is topen, but
p\not\in\mathit{K}(p)
.

3.

\mathit{K}(p)=\varnothing
.

4.

\mathit{K}(p)
is open but not transitive.
(Both
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
and
p\not\in\mathit{K}(p)
are possible.)

Proof.

1.

To see that
p
can be regular, consider
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0\}
with the discrete topology.
Then
p\in\mathit{K}(p)=\{0\}
.

2.

To see that it is possible for
\mathit{K}(p)
to be topen but
p
is not in it, consider Example
4.1
(
3
).
There,
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3,4\}
and
1_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(1)=\{0,2\}
.
Then
\mathit{K}(1)
is topen, but
1\not\in\mathit{K}(1)
.

3.

To see that
\mathit{K}(p)=\varnothing
is possible, consider Example
4.1
(
1
) (the real numbers
\mathbb{R}
with its usual topology).
Then by Remark
6.10

r_{\between}=\{r\}
and so
\mathit{K}(x)=\mathit{interior}(\{r\})=\varnothing
.
(See also Example
6.10
(2) for a more elaborate example.)

4.

To see that it is possible for
\mathit{K}(p)
to be an open neighbourhood of
p
but not transitive, see Example
4.1
(
2
).
There,
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and
1\in 1_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}=\mathit{K}(1)
, but
\{0,1,2\}
is not transitive (it contains two disjoint topens:
\{0\}
and
\{2\}
).

To see that it is possible for
\mathit{K}(p)
to be open and nonempty yet not contain
p
and not be transitive, see Example
4.1
(
4
) for
p=0
, and see also Remark
6.3
for a discussion of the connection with minimal closed neighbourhoods.

The possibilities above are clearly mutually exclusive and exhaustive.
∎

Chapter 4
Closed sets

1
Closed sets

Remark 1.1
.

In Subsection
1
we check that some familiar properties of closures carry over from topologies to semitopologies.
There are no technical surprises, but this in itself is a mathematical result that needs checked.
From Subsection
3
and the following Subsections we will study the close relation between closures and sets of intertwined points.

First, we spare a few words on why closures are particularly interesting in semitopologies:

1.

A participant may wish to compute a quorum that it can be confident of remaining in agreement with, where algorithms succeed.
The notion of maximal topen from Definition
2.1
(
3
) provides this, as discussed in Remark
2.7
— but computing maximal topens is hard since the definition involves quantifications over all open sets and there may be many of them.

2.

However, computing
closures
is quite tractable in the right circumstances (see Section
7
and Remark
4.14
), so a characterisation of maximal topens using closed sets and closures is of practical interest.

3.

Closures are significant for other reasons too: see the discussions in Remarks
5.1
and
5.5
, and see the later material in Section
10
that considers
dense sets
.

Thus, and just as is the case in topology, closures are an interesting object of study.

Definition 1.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

Define
|P|\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
the
closure of
P
to be the set of points
p
such that every open neighbourhood of
p
intersects
P
.
In symbols using Notation
1.1
:

|P|=\{p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p^{\prime}\in O\Longrightarrow P\between O\}.

2.

As is standard, we may write
|p|
for
|\{p\}|
.
Unpacking definitions for reference:

|p|=\{p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p^{\prime}\in O\Longrightarrow p\in O\}.

Lemma 1.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
P,P^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then taking the closure of a set is:

1.

Monotone:
If
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
then
|P|\subseteq|P^{\prime}|
.

2.

Increasing:

P\subseteq|P|
.

3.

Idempotent:

|P|=||P||
.

Proof.

By routine calculations from Definition
1.2
.
∎

Lemma 1.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then

P\between O\quad\text{if and only if}\quad|P|\between O.

Proof.

Suppose
P\between O
.
Then
|P|\between O
using Lemma
1.3
(
2
).

Suppose
|P|\between O
.
Pick
p\in|P|\cap O
.
By construction of
|P|
in Definition
1.2

p\in O\Longrightarrow P\between O
.
It follows that
P\between O
as required.
∎

Definition 1.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
C\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

Call
C
a
closed set
when
C=|C|
.

2.

Call
C
a
clopen set
when
C
is closed and open.

3.

Write
\mathsf{Closed}
for the set of
closed sets
(as we wrote
\mathsf{Open}
for the open sets; the ambient semitopology will always be clear or understood).

Lemma 1.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
|P|
is closed and contains
P
.
In symbols:

P\subseteq|P|\in\mathsf{Closed}.

Proof.

From Definition
1.5
(1) and Lemma
1.3
(
2
&
3
).
∎

Example 1.7
.

1.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\{0\},\{0,1\}\}
.
Then the reader can verify that:

•

\{0\}
is open.

•

The closure of
\{1\}
is
\{1\}
and
\{1\}
is closed.

•

The closure of
\{0\}
is
\{0,1\}
.

•

\varnothing
and
\{0,1\}
are the only clopen sets.

2.

Now take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\{0\},\{1\},\{0,1\}\}
.
1
1

1

Following Definition
1.3
and Example
1.4
(
3
), this is just
\{0,1\}
with the
discrete semitopology
.

Then the reader can verify that:

•

Every set is clopen.

•

The closure of every set is itself.

Remark 1.8
.

There are two standard definitions for when a set is closed: when it is equal to its closure (as per Definition
1.5
(1)), and when it is the complement of an open set.
In topology these are equivalent.
We do need to check that the same holds in semitopology, but as it turns out the proof is routine:

Lemma 1.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

Suppose
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
is closed (by Definition
1.5
:
C=|C|
).
Then
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C
is open.

2.

Suppose
O\in\mathsf{Open}
is open.
Then
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
is closed (by Definition
1.5
:
|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O|=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
).

Proof.

1.

Suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C
.
Since
C=|C|
, we have
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|C|
.
Unpacking Definition
1.2
, this means precisely that there exists
O_{p}\in\mathsf{Open}
with
p\in O_{p}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}C
.
We use Lemma
3.2
.

2.

Suppose
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Combining Lemma
3.2
with Definition
1.2

it follows that
O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O|
so that
|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O|\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.
Furthermore, by Lemma
1.3
(
2
)
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O\subseteq|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O|
.
∎

Corollary 1.10
.

If
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
then
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C=\bigcup_{O\in\mathsf{Open}}O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}C
.

Proof.

By Lemma
1.9
(1)
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C\subseteq\bigcup_{O\in\mathsf{Open}}O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}C
.
Conversely, if
O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}C
then
O\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C
by Definition
1.2
(
1
).
∎

Corollary 1.11
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
Then:

1.

\varnothing
and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
are closed.

2.

If every
C\in\mathcal{C}
is closed, then
\bigcap\mathcal{C}
is closed.
Or succinctly in symbols:

\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathsf{Closed}\Longrightarrow\bigcap\mathcal{C}\in\mathsf{Closed}.

3.

|P|
is equal to the intersection of all the closed sets that contain it.
In symbols:

|P|=\bigcap\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid P\subseteq C\}.

Proof.

1.

Immediate from Lemma
1.9
(2).

2.

From Lemma
1.9
and Definition
1.2
(1&2).

3.

By Lemma
1.6

\bigcap\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid P\subseteq C\}\subseteq|P|
.
By construction
P\subseteq\bigcap\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid P\subseteq C\}
, and using Lemma
1.3
(
1
) and part 2 of this result we have

|P|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.closure.monotone}(\ref{closure.monotone})}}{{\subseteq}}|\bigcap\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid P\subseteq C\}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pt.2}}{{=}}\bigcap\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid P\subseteq C\}.

∎

The usual characterisation of continuity in terms of inverse images of closed sets being closed, remains valid:

Corollary 1.12
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and
(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
are semitopological spaces (Definition
1.2
) and suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
is a function.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is continuous, meaning by Definition
2.1
(
2
) that
f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}
for every
O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}
.

2.

f^{\text{-}1}(C^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Closed}
for every
C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By routine calculations as for topologies, using Lemma
1.9
and the fact that the inverse image of a complement is the complement of the inverse image; see
[
Wil70
, Theorem 7.2, page 44]
or
[
Eng89
, Proposition 1.4.1(iv), page 28]
.
∎

2
Duality between closure and interior

The usual dualities between closures and interiors remain valid in semitopologies.
There are no surprises but this still needs checked so we spell out the details:

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
and
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
.
Then:

1.

O\subseteq\mathit{interior}(|O|)
. The inclusion may be strict.

2.

|\mathit{interior}(C)|\subseteq C
. The inclusion may be strict.

3.

\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|O|
.

4.

|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C|=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus\mathit{interior}(C)
.

Proof.

The reasoning is just as for topologies, but we spell out the details:

1.

By Lemma
1.3
(
2
)
O\subseteq|O|
.
By Corollary
1.3

\mathit{interior}(O)\subseteq\mathit{interior}(|O|)
.
By Lemma
1.2
(2)
O=\mathit{interior}(O)
, so we are done.

For an example of the strict inclusion, consider
\mathbb{R}
with the usual topology (which is also a semitopology) and take
O=(0,1)\cup(1,2)
.
Then
O\subsetneq\mathit{interior}(|O|)=(0,2)
.

2.

By Lemma
1.2
(1)
\mathit{interior}(C)\subseteq C
.
By Lemma
1.3
(
1
)
|\mathit{interior}(C)|\subseteq|C|
.
By Definition
1.5
(
1
) (since we assumed
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
)
|C|=C
, so we are done.

For an example of the strict inclusion, consider
\mathbb{R}
with the usual topology and take
C=\{0\}
.
Then
|\mathit{interior}(C)|=\varnothing\subsetneq C
.

3.

Consider some
p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
By Definition
1.1

p^{\prime}\in\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
when there exists some
O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O
.
By definition in Definition
1.2
(
1
) this happens precisely when
p^{\prime}\not\in|O|
.

4.

By Definition
1.2
(
1
),
p^{\prime}\not\in|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C|
precisely when there exists some
O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C
.
By facts of sets this means precisely that
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\subseteq C
.
By Definition
1.1
this means precisely that
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{interior}(C)
.
∎

Corollary 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and

O\in\mathsf{Open}
and
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
.
Then:

1.

|O|=|\mathit{interior}(|O|)|
.

2.

\mathit{interior}(C)=\mathit{interior}(|\mathit{interior}(C)|)
.

Proof.

We use Lemma
2.1
(
1
&
3
) along with Lemma
1.3
(
1
) and Corollary
1.3
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }c@{\ }c@{\ }ll}|O|&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.closure.interior}(\ref{item.closure.interior.open})\&L\ref{lemm.closure.monotone}(\ref{closure.monotone})}}{{\subseteq}}&|\mathit{interior}(|O|)|&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.closure.interior}(\ref{item.closure.interior.closed})}}{{\subseteq}}&\mathit{interior}(|O|)\\
\mathit{interior}(C)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.closure.interior}(\ref{item.closure.interior.open})}}{{\subseteq}}&\mathit{interior}(|\mathit{interior}(C)|)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.closure.interior}(\ref{item.closure.interior.closed})\&C\ref{corr.interior.monotone}}}{{\subseteq}}&\mathit{interior}(C)\end{array}

∎

3
Transitivity and closure

We explore how the topological closure operation interacts with taking transitive sets.

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is transitive and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then

T\between O\quad\text{implies}\quad|T|\subseteq|O|.

Proof.

Unpacking Definition
1.2

we have:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}p^{\prime}\in|T|\Longleftrightarrow&\forall O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow O^{\prime}\between T\qquad\text{and}\\
p^{\prime}\in|O|\Longleftrightarrow&\forall O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow O^{\prime}\between O.\end{array}

It would suffice to prove
O^{\prime}\between T\Longrightarrow O^{\prime}\between O
for any
O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.

So suppose
O^{\prime}\between T
.
By assumption
T\between O
and by transitivity of
T
(Definition
2.1
)
O^{\prime}\between O
.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\in\mathsf{Topen}
and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

T\between O\quad\text{if and only if}\quad T\subseteq|T|\subseteq|O|.

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
T\between O
.
By Lemma
3.1

|T|\subseteq|O|
.
By Lemma
1.3
(
2
) (as standard)
T\subseteq|T|
.

•

Suppose
T\subseteq|T|\subseteq|O|
.
Then
T\between|O|
and by Lemma
1.4
(since
T
is nonempty (and transitive) and open) also
T\between O
.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

In retrospect we can see the imprint of topens (Definition
2.1
) in previous work, if we look at things in a certain way.
Many consensus algorithms have the property that once consensus is established in a quorum
O
, it propagates to
|O|
.

This is apparent (for example) in the Grade-Cast algorithm
[
FM88
]
, in which participants assign a confidence grade of 0, 1 or 2 to their output and must ensure that if any participant outputs
v
with grade 2 then all must output
v
with grade at least 1.
In this algorithm, if a participant finds that all its quorums intersect some set
S
that unanimously supports value
v
, then the participant assigns grade at least 1 to
v
.
From the view of our paper, this is just taking a closure in the style we discussed in Remark
1.1
.
If
T
unanimously supports
v
and participants communicate enough, then eventually every member of
|T|
assigns grade at least 1 to
v
.
Thus, Proposition
3.2
suggests that, to convince a topen to agree on a value, we can first convince an open neighbourhood that intersects the topen, and then use Grade-Cast to convince the closure of that open set and thus in particular the topen which we know must be contained in that closure.

Remark 3.4
.

Later on we will revisit these ideas and fit them into a nice general framework having to do with dense subsets.
See Lemma
4.5
and Proposition
4.6
.

We conclude with an easy observation which will be useful later.
Recall from Notation
6.4
and Lemma
6.5
the notion of an intertwined space being one such that all nonempty open sets intersect.
Then we have:

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
T\in\mathsf{Topen}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is intertwined.

2.

|T|=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
|T|=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and consider any
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Unpacking Definition
1.2
(
1
) it follows that
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
.
By transitivity of
T
(Definition
2.1
(
1
))
O\between O^{\prime}
as required.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is intertwined.
By Lemma
6.5
every nonempty open set is topen, thus
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is topen, and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=|T|
follows by Lemma
3.1
.
∎

4
Closed neighbourhoods and intertwined points

1
Definition and basic properties

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
We generalise Definition
3.1
as follows:

1.

Call
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
a
neighbourhood
when it contains an open set (i.e. when
\mathit{interior}(P)\neq\varnothing
), and call
P
a
neighbourhood of
p
when
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
P
contains an open neighbourhood of
p
(i.e. when
p\in\mathit{interior}(P)
).
In particular:

2.

C\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a
closed neighbourhood of
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
when
C
is closed and
p\in\mathit{interior}(C)
.

3.

C\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a
closed neighbourhood
when
C
is closed and
\mathit{interior}(C)\neq\varnothing
.

Remark 4.2
.

1.

If
C
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
in the sense of Definition
4.1
(
2
) then
C
is a closed neighbourhood in the sense of Definition
4.1
(
3
), just because if
p\in\mathit{interior}(C)
then
\mathit{interior}(C)\neq\varnothing
.

2.

For
C
to be a closed neighbourhood of
p
it is not enough for
p\in C
.
We require
p\in\mathit{interior}(C)
, which is a stronger condition.

For instance take the semitopology
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\bm{\mathsf{P}},\{0\},\{2\}\}
from Figure
1
(top-left diagram), and consider
p=1
and
C=\{0,1\}
.
Then
p\in C
but
p\not\in\mathit{interior}(C)=\{0\}
, so that
C
is not a closed neighbourhood of
p
.

Recall from Definition
6.1
the notions of
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
and
p_{\between}
.
Proposition
4.3
packages up our material for convenient use in later results.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

We can characterise when
p^{\prime}
is intertwined with
p
as follows:

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\Longrightarrow p^{\prime}\in|O|.

2.

As a corollary,

p_{\between}=\bigcap\{|O|\mid p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}\}.

3.

Equivalently:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l}p_{\between}=&\bigcap\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid p\in\mathit{interior}(C)\}\\
=&\bigcap\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid C\text{ a closed neighbourhood of }p\}&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.cn}}.\end{array}

Thus in particular, if
C
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
then
p_{\between}\subseteq C
.

4.

p_{\between}
is closed and (by Lemma
1.9
(1))
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus p_{\between}
is open.

Proof.

1.

We just rearrange Definition
6.1
.
So
2
2

2

The proof relies on pushing around bracketed scopes, so we bracket everywhere for extra clarity.

\forall O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}.((p\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime})\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime})

rearranges to

\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}.(p\in O\Longrightarrow\forall O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}.(p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime})).

We now observe from Definition
1.2
that this is precisely

\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}.(p\in O\Longrightarrow p^{\prime}\in|O|).

2.

We just rephrase part 1 of this result.

3.

Using part
2
of this result it would suffice to prove

\bigcap\{|O|\mid p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}\}=\bigcap\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid p\in\mathit{interior}(C)\}.

We will do this by proving that for each
O
-component on the left there is a
C
on the right with
C\subseteq|O|
; and for each
C
-component on the right there is an
O
on the left with
|O|\subseteq C
:

•

Consider some
O\in\mathsf{Open}
with
p\in O
.

We set
C=|O|
, so that trivially
C\subseteq|O|
.
By Lemma
2.1
(1)
O\subseteq\mathit{interior}(|O|)
, so
p\in\mathit{interior}(C)
.

•

Consider some
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
such that
p\in\mathit{interior}(C)
.

We set
O=\mathit{interior}(C)
.
Then
p\in O
, and by Lemma
2.1
(2)
|O|\subseteq C
.

4.

We combine part
2
of this result with Corollary
1.11
(
2
).
∎

Definition 4.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

Write
\mathit{nbhd}(p)=\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in\mathsf{Open}\}
and call this the
open neighbourhood system
of
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

2.

Write
\mathit{nbhd}^{c}(p)=\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid p\in\mathsf{Closed}\}
and call this the
closed neighbourhood system
of
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Remark 4.5
.

As standard, we can use Definition
4.4
to rewrite the definition of
f
being continuous at
p
(Definition
2.1
(
3
)) as

\forall O^{\prime}{\in}\mathit{nbhd}(f(p)).\exists O{\in}\mathit{nbhd}(p).O\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime}).

Remark 4.6
.

If
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology, then
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is a filter (a nonempty up-closed down-directed set) and this is often called the
neighbourhood filter
of
p
.

We are working with semitopologies, so
\mathsf{Open}
is not necessarily closed under intersections, and
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is not necessarily a filter.
Figure
1
illustrates examples of this: e.g. in the left-hand example
\{0,1\},\{0,2\}\in\mathit{nbhd}(0)
but
\{0\}\not\in\mathit{nbhd}(0)
, since
\{0\}
is not an open set.

Figure 1
:
Examples of open neighbourhoods (Remarks
4.6
and
1.3
)

Remark 4.7
.

We can relate Proposition
4.3
to concepts from topology.
Then:

•

Proposition
4.3
(
2
) identifies
p_{\between}
as the the set of
cluster points
of
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
; see
[
Bou98
, Definition 2, page 69]
,
[
Eng89
, page 52]
, or
Wikipedia
(
permalink
).

•

Proposition
4.3
(
3
) identifies
p_{\between}
as the set of
convergence points
of
\mathit{nbhd}^{c}(p)
.

2
Application to characterise (quasi/weak) regularity

Remark 4.8
.

Recall that Theorem
2.6
characterised regularity in multiple ways, including as the existence of a greatest topen neighbourhood.
Proposition
4.10
below does something similar, for quasiregularity and weak regularity and the existence of closed neighbourhoods (Definition
4.1
), and Theorem
6.2
is a result in the same style, for regularity.

Here, for the reader’s convenience, is a summary of the relevant results:

1.

Proposition
4.9
:
p
is quasiregular when
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood.

2.

Proposition
4.10
:
p
is weakly regular when
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
.

3.

Theorem
6.2
:
p
is regular when
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
and is a minimal closed neighbourhood.

Proposition 4.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is quasiregular, or in full:
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
(Definition
1.4
(
5
)).

2.

p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood (Definition
4.1
(
3
)).

Proof.

By construction in Definition
1.4
(
1
),
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
.
So
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
means precisely that
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood.
∎

Proposition 4.10
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is weakly regular, or in full:
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
(Definition
1.4
(
4
)).

2.

p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
(Definition
4.1
(
2
)).

3.

The poset of closed neighbourhoods of
p
ordered by subset inclusion, has a least element.

4.

p_{\between}
is least in the poset of closed neighbourhoods of
p
ordered by subset inclusion.

Proof.

We prove a cycle of implications:

•

Suppose

p\in\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
.
By Proposition
4.3
(
4
)
p_{\between}
is closed, so this makes it a closed neighbourhood of
p
as per Definition
4.1
.

•

Suppose
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
.
By Proposition
4.3
(
3
)

p_{\between}
is the intersection of
all
closed neighbourhoods of
p
, and it follows that this poset has
p_{\between}
as a least element.

•

Assume the poset of closed neighbourhoods of
p
has a least element; write it
C
.
So
C=\bigcap\{C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid C^{\prime}\text{ is a closed neighbourhood of }p\}
and thus by Proposition
4.3
(
3
)
C=p_{\between}
.

•

If
p_{\between}
is least in the poset of closed neighbourhoods of
p
ordered by subset inclusion, then in particular
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
and it follows from Definition
4.1
that
p\in\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
.
∎

Recall from Definition
1.4
that
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
:

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
|\mathit{K}(p)|\subseteq p_{\between}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.3
(
4
)
p_{\between}
is closed; we use Lemma
2.1
(2).
∎

Theorem 4.12
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
p
weakly regular then
|\mathit{K}(p)|=p_{\between}
.
In symbols:

p\in\mathit{K}(p)\quad\text{implies}\quad|\mathit{K}(p)|=p_{\between}.

2.

As an immediately corollary, if
p
is regular then
|\mathit{K}(p)|=p_{\between}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

If
p\in\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
then
|\mathit{K}(p)|
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
, so by Proposition
4.3
(
3
)
p_{\between}\subseteq|\mathit{K}(p)|
.
By Lemma
4.11

|\mathit{K}(p)|\subseteq p_{\between}
.

2.

By Lemma
1.7
(
1
) if
p
is regular then it is weakly regular.
We use part 1 of this result.
∎

We can combine Theorem
4.12
with Corollary
2.8
:

Corollary 4.13
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is regular.

2.

p
is weakly regular and
p_{\between}=p^{\prime}_{\between}
for every
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.

Proof.

Suppose
p
is regular and
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
Then
p
is weakly regular by Lemma
1.7
(
1
), and
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
by Corollary
2.8
, and
p_{\between}=p^{\prime}_{\between}
by Theorem
4.12
.

Suppose
p
is weakly regular and
p_{\between}=p^{\prime}_{\between}
for every
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Definition
1.4
(
1
) also
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})=\mathit{interior}(p^{\prime}_{\between})=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
for every
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
, and by Corollary
2.8

p
is regular.
∎

Remark 4.14
.

Note a subtlety to Corollary
4.13
: it is possible for
p
to be regular, yet it is not the case that
p_{\between}=p^{\prime}_{\between}
for every
p^{\prime}\in p_{\between}
(rather than for every
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
).
For an example consider the top-left semitopology in Figure
1
, taking
p=0
and
p^{\prime}=1
; then
1\in 0_{\between}
but
0_{\between}=\{0,1\}
and
1_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
.

To understand why this happens the interested reader can look ahead to Subsection
1
: in the terminology of that Subsection,
p^{\prime}
needs to be
unconflicted
in Corollaries
2.8
and
4.13
.

5
Intersections of communities with open sets

Remark 5.1

(An observation about consensus)
.

Proposition
5.3
and Lemma
5.2
tell us some interesting and useful things about distributed consensus from a semitopological perspective:

•

Suppose a weakly regular
p
wants to convince its community
\mathit{K}(p)
of some belief.
How might it proceed?

By Proposition
5.3
it would suffice to seed one of the open neighbourhoods in its community with that belief, and then compute a
topological closure
of that open set; in Remark
5.5
we discuss why topological closures are particularly interesting.

•

Suppose
p
is regular, so it is a member of a transitive open neighbourhood, and
p
wants to convince its community
\mathit{K}(p)
of some belief.

By Lemma
5.2

p
need only convince
some
open set that intersects its community (this open set need not even contain
p
), and then compute a topological closure as in the previous point.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular (so
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
).
Suppose
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then

p\in O\between\mathit{K}(p)\quad\text{implies}\quad\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq p_{\between}\subseteq|O|.

In word:

If an open set intersects the community of a regular point, then that community is included in the closure of the open set.

Proof.

Suppose
p
is regular, so
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
, and suppose
p\in O\between\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Proposition
3.2

\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq|\mathit{K}(p)|\subseteq|O|
.
By Theorem
4.12

|\mathit{K}(p)|=p_{\between}
, and putting this together we get

\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq p_{\between}\subseteq|O|

as required.
∎

Proposition
5.3
generalises Theorem
4.12
, and is proved using it.
We regain Theorem
4.12
as the special case where
O=\mathit{K}(p)
:

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is weakly regular (so
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Open}
).
Suppose
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then:

1.

p\in O\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
implies

p_{\between}=|O|
.

2.

As a corollary,
p\in O\subseteq p_{\between}
implies

p_{\between}=|O|
.

Proof.

If
p\in O\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
then
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
and using Theorem
4.12

|\mathit{K}(p)|\subseteq p_{\between}
.
Since
O\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
also
|O|\subseteq p_{\between}
.
Also, by Proposition
4.3
(
2
) (since
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}
)
p_{\between}\subseteq|O|
.

For the corollary, we note that if
O
is open then
O\subseteq\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})=\mathit{K}(p)
if and only if
O\subseteq p_{\between}
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

Note in Proposition
5.3
that it really matters that
p\in O
— that is, that
O
is an open neighbourhood
of
p
and not just an open set in
p_{\between}
.

To see why, consider the example in Lemma
2.3
(illustrated in Figure
1
, top-left diagram): so
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\bm{\mathsf{P}},\{0\},\{2\}\}
.
Note that:

•

1_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
.

•

If we set
O=\{0\}\subseteq\{0,1,2\}
then this is open, but
|O|=\{0,1\}\neq\{0,1,2\}
.

•

If we set
O=\{0,1,2\}\subseteq\{0,1,2\}
then
|O|=\{0,1,2\}
.

Remark 5.5
.

Topological closures will matter because we will develop a theory of computable semitopologies which will (amongst other things) deliver a distributed algorithm to compute closures (see Remark
4.14
).

Thus, putting together the results above with the witness semitopology machinery to come in Definition
2.5
onwards, we can say that from the point of view of a regular participant
p
, Proposition
5.3
and Lemma
5.2
reduce the problem of

p
wishes to progress with value
v

to the simpler problem of

p
wishes to find an open set that intersects with the community of
p
, and work with this open set to agree on
v
(which open set does not matter;
p
can try several until one works).

Once this is done, the distributed algorithm will safely propagate the belief across the network.

Note that no forking is possible above; all the action is in finding and convincing the
O\between\mathit{K}(p)
, and then the rest is automatic.

More discussion of this when we develop the notion of a
kernel
in Section
9
.

6
Regularity, maximal topens, and minimal closed neighbourhoods

Remark 6.1
.

Recall we have seen an arc of results which

•

started with Theorem
2.6
and Corollary
2.8
— characterisations of regularity
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
in terms of maximal topens — and

•

passed through Proposition
4.10
— characterisation of weak regularity
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Open}
in terms of minimal closed neighbourhoods.

We are now ready to complete this arc by stating and proving Theorem
6.2
.
This establishes a pleasing — and not-at-all-obvious — duality between ‘has a maximal topen neighbourhood’ and ‘has a minimal closed neighbourhood’.

Theorem 6.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is regular.

2.

\mathit{K}(p)
is a maximal/greatest topen neighbourhood of
p
.

3.

p
is weakly regular (meaning that
p\in\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
) and
p_{\between}
is a minimal closed neighbourhood (Definition
4.1
).
3
3

3

We really do mean “
p_{\between}
is minimal amongst closed neighbourhoods” and
not
the weaker condition “
p_{\between}
is minimal amongst closed neighbourhoods of
p
”! That weaker condition is treated in Proposition
4.10
. See Remark
6.4
.

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 is just Theorem
2.6
(2).

For equivalence of parts 2 and 3 we prove two implications:

•

Suppose
p
is regular.
By Lemma
1.7
(
1
)
p
is weakly regular.
Now consider a closed neighbourhood
C^{\prime}\subseteq p_{\between}
.
Note that
C^{\prime}
has a nonempty interior by Definition
4.1
(
3
), so pick any
p^{\prime}
such that

p^{\prime}\in\mathit{interior}(C^{\prime})\subseteq C^{\prime}\subseteq p_{\between}.

It follows that
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
, and
p
is regular, so by Corollary
4.13

p^{\prime}_{\between}=p_{\between}
,
and then by Proposition
4.10
(
3
) (since
p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
)
p^{\prime}_{\between}\subseteq C^{\prime}
.
Putting this all together we have

p_{\between}=p^{\prime}_{\between}\subseteq C^{\prime}\subseteq p_{\between},

so that
C^{\prime}=p_{\between}
as required.

•

Suppose
p
is weakly regular and suppose
p_{\between}
is minimal in the poset of closed neighbourhoods ordered by subset inclusion.

Consider some
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Proposition
4.3
(
3
)
p^{\prime}_{\between}\subseteq p_{\between}
, and by minimality it follows that
p^{\prime}_{\between}=p_{\between}
.
Thus also
\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})=\mathit{K}(p)
.

Now
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
was arbitrary, so by Corollary
2.8

p
is regular as required.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

Recall Example
4.1
(
4
), as illustrated in Figure
1
(right-hand diagram).
This has a point
0
whose community
\mathit{K}(0)=\{1,2\}
is not a single topen (it contains two topens:
\{1\}
and
\{2\}
).

A corollary of Theorem
6.2
is that
0_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
cannot be a minimal closed neighbourhood, because if it were then
0
would be regular and
\mathit{K}(0)
would be a maximal topen neighbourhood of
0
.

We check, and see that indeed,
0_{\between}
contains
two
distinct minimal closed neighbourhoods:
\{0,1\}
and
\{0,2\}
.

Remark 6.4
.

Theorem
6.2
(3) looks like Proposition
4.10
(4), but

•

Proposition
4.10
(4) regards the
poset of closed neighbourhoods of
p
(closed sets with a nonempty open interior that contains
p
),

•

Theorem
6.2
(3) regards the
poset of all closed neighbourhoods
(closed sets with a nonempty open interior, not necessarily including
p
).

So the condition used in Theorem
6.2
(3) is strictly stronger than the condition used in Proposition
4.10
(4).
Correspondingly, the regularity condition in Theorem
6.2
(1) can be written as
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
, and (as noted in Lemma
1.7
and Example
1.8
(
2
)) this is strictly stronger than the condition
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
used in Proposition
4.10
(1).

Corollary
6.5
makes Remark
6.10
(intertwined is the opposite of Hausdorff) a little more precise:

Corollary 6.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a Hausdorff semitopology (so every two points have a pair of disjoint neighbourhoods).
Then if
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular, then
\{p\}
is clopen.

Proof.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is Hausdorff and consider
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
By Remark
6.10

p_{\between}=\{p\}
.
From Theorem
6.2
(3)
\{p\}
is closed and has a nonempty open interior which must therefore also be equal to
\{p\}
.
By Corollary
2.7
(or from Theorem
6.2
(2)) this interior is transitive.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

Every maximal topen is equal to the interior of a minimal closed neighbourhood.

2.

The converse implication holds if
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology, but need not hold in the more general case that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology: there may exist a minimal closed neighbourhood whose interior is not topen.

Proof.

1.

Suppose
T
is a maximal topen.
By Definition
2.1
(
2
)
T
is nonempty, so choose
p\in T
.
By Proposition
4.3
(
4
)
p_{\between}
is closed, and using Theorem
2.6

p\in T=\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})\subseteq p_{\between}.

Thus
p
is weakly regular and by Proposition
4.10
(1&4)
p_{\between}
is a least closed neighbourhood of
p
.

2.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
This is Example
6.8
below.
However, we also provide here a breaking ‘proof’, which throws light on precisely what Example
6.8
is breaking, and illustrates what the difference between semitopology and topology can mean in practical proof.

Suppose
T=\mathit{interior}(C)
is the nonempty open interior of some minimal closed neighbourhood
C
: we will try (and fail) to show that this is transitive.
By Theorem
6.8
it suffices to prove that
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
for every
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.

So suppose
p\in O
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
and
O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O^{\prime}
.
By Definition
1.2
(
1
)
p^{\prime}\not\in|O|
, so that
|O|\cap C\subseteq C
is a strictly smaller closed set.
Also,
O\cap C
is nonempty because it contains
p
.

If
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology then we are done, because
O\cap T=\mathit{interior}(O\cap C)
would be necessarily be open, contradicting our assumption that
C
is a minimal closed neighbourhood.

However, if
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology then this does not necessarily follow:
O\cap T
need not be open, and we cannot proceed.
∎

Figure 2
:
An unconflicted, irregular space (Proposition
2.1
) in which every minimal closed neighbourhood has a non-transitive open interior (Example
6.8
)

Lemma 6.7
.

Consider the semitopology illustrated in Figure
2
.
So:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{A,B,C,D\}
where:

A=\{3,0\},\quad B=\{0,1\},\quad C=\{1,2\},\quad\text{and}\quad D=\{2,3\}.

Then for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
we have:

1.

p
is intertwined only with itself.

2.

\mathit{K}(p)=\varnothing
.

Proof.

Part 1 is by routine calculations from Definition
6.1
(
2
).
Part 2 follows, noting that
\mathit{interior}(\{p\})=\varnothing
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
∎

Example 6.8
.

The semitopology illustrated in Figure
2
, and specified in Lemma
6.7
,
contains sets that are minimal amongst closed sets with a nonempty interior, yet that interior is not topen:

•

A
,
B
,
C
, and
D
are clopen, because
C
is the complement of
A
and
D
is the complement of
B
, so they are their own interior.

•

A
is a minimal closed neighbourhood (which is also open, being
A
itself), because

–

A=\{3,0\}
is closed because it is the complement of
C
, and it is its own interior, and

–

its two nonempty subsets
\{3\}
and
\{0\}
are closed (being the complement of
B\cup C
and
C\cup D
respectively) but they have empty open interior because
\{3\}
and
\{0\}
are not open.

•

A
is not transitive because
3
and
0
are not intertwined:
3\in D
and
0\in B
and
B\cap D=\varnothing
.

•

Similarly
B
,
C
, and
D
are minimal closed neighbourhoods, which are also open, and they are not transitive.

We further note that:

1.

|0|=\{0\}
, because its complement is equal to
C\cup D
(Definition
1.2
; Lemma
1.9
).
Similarly for every other point in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

2.

0_{\between}=\{0\}
, as noted in Lemma
6.7
.
Similarly for every other point in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

3.

\mathit{K}(0)=\mathit{interior}(0_{\between})=\varnothing
as noted in Lemma
6.7
,
so that
0
is not regular (Definition
1.4
(
1
)), and
0
is not even weakly regular or quasiregular.
Similarly for every other point in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

4.

0
has
two
minimal closed neighbourhoods:
A
and
B
.
Similarly for every other point in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

This illustrates that
p_{\between}\subsetneq C
is possible, where
C
is a minimal closed neighbourhood of
p
.

Remark 6.9
.

The results and discussions above tell us something interesting above and beyond the specific mathematical facts which they express.

They demonstrate that points being intertwined (the
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
from Definition
6.1
) is a distinct
semitopological
notion.
A reader familiar with topology might be tempted to identify maximal topens with interiors of minimal closed neighbourhood (so that in view of Theorem
6.8
, being intertwined would be topologically characterised just as two points being in the interior of the same minimal closed neighbourhood).

This works in topologies, but we see from Example
6.8
that in semitopologies being intertwined has its own distinct identity.

We conclude with one more example, showing how an (apparently?) slight changes to a semitopology can make a big difference to its intertwinedness:

Example 6.10
.

1.

\mathbb{Q}^{2}
with open sets generated by any covering collection of pairwise non-parallel
rational lines
— meaning a set of solutions to a linear equation
a.x{+}b.y=c
for
a
,
b
, and
c
integers — is a semitopology.

This consists of a single (maximal) topen: lines are pairwise non-parallel, so any two lines intersect and (looking to Theorem
6.8
) all points are intertwined.
There is only one closed set with a nonempty open interior, which is the whole space.

2.

\mathbb{Q}^{2}
with open sets generated by all (possibly parallel) rational lines, is a semitopology.
It has no topen sets and (looking to Theorem
6.8
) no two distinct points are intertwined.

For any line
l
, its complement
\mathbb{Q}^{2}\setminus l
is a closed set, given by the union of all the lines parallel to
l
.
Thus every closed set is also an open set, and vice versa, and every line
l
is an example of a minimal closed neighbourhood (itself), whose interior is not a topen.

7
More on minimal closed neighbourhoods

We make good use of closed neighbourhoods, and in particular minimal closed neighbourhoods, in Subsection
6
and elsewhere.
We take a moment to give a pleasing alternative characterisation of this useful concept.

1
Regular open/closed sets

Remark 7.1
.

The terminology ‘regular open/closed set’ is from the topological literature.
It is not directly related to terminology ‘regular point’ from Definition
1.4
(
3
), which comes from semitopologies.
However, it turns out that a mathematical connection does exist between these two notions.
We outline some theory of regular open/closed sets, and then demonstrate the connections to what we have seen in our semitopological world.

Definition 7.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Recall some standard terminology from topology
[
Wil70
, Exercise 3D, page 29]
:

1.

We call an open set
O\in\mathsf{Open}
a
regular open set
when
O=\mathit{interior}(|O|)
.

2.

We call a closed set
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
a
regular closed set
when
C=|\mathit{interior}(C)|
.

3.

Write
\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}
and
\mathsf{Closed}_{\mathit{reg}}
for the sets of regular open and regular closed sets respectively.

Lemma 7.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
and
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
.
Then:

1.

\mathit{interior}(C)
is a regular open set.

2.

|O|
is a regular closed set.

Proof.

Direct from Definition
7.2
and Corollary
2.2
.
∎

Corollary 7.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}
.

Proof.

We just combine Lemma
7.3
(1) with Proposition
4.3
(
4
).
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then
\mathit{interior}(|O|)
is a regular open set.

Proof.

By Lemma
1.6

|O|
is closed, and by Lemma
7.3

\mathit{interior}(|O|)
is regular open.
∎

The regular open and the regular closed sets are the same thing, up to an easy and natural bijection:

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then

•

the topological closure map
|\text{-}|
and

•

the topological interior map
\mathit{interior}(\text{-})

define a bijection of posets between
\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}
and
\mathsf{Closed}_{\mathit{reg}}
ordered by subset inclusion.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.3
,
|\text{-}|
and
\mathit{interior}(\text{-})
map between
\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}
to
\mathsf{Closed}_{\mathit{reg}}
.
Now we note that the regularity property from Definition
7.2
, which states that
\mathit{interior}(|O|)=O
when
O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}
and
|\mathit{interior}(C)|=C
when
C\in\mathsf{Closed}_{\mathit{reg}}
, expresses precisely that these maps are inverse.

They are maps of posets by Corollary
1.3
and Lemma
1.3
(
2
).
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
and
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
.
Then:

1.

O
is a regular open set if and only if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
is a regular closed set if and only if
|O|
is a regular closed set.

2.

C
is a regular closed set if and only if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C
is a regular open set if and only if
\mathit{interior}(C)
is a regular open set.

Proof.

By routine calculations from the definitions using parts
3
and
4
of Lemma
2.1
.
∎

2
Intersections of regular open sets

An easy observation about open sets will be useful:
4
4

4

Lemma
7.8
is true in topologies as well, but not prominent in the literature. We could not find it in two standard reference works
[
Eng89
,
Wil70
]
. It appears as equation 10 in Theorem 1.37 of
[
Kop89
]
, and as
a lemma in
\pi
-base
(
permalink
) — we thank the mathematics StackExchange community for the pointers.
We mention this because it illustrates an interesting contrast: this result is just as true in topologies as it is in semitopologies, but somehow, it
matters
more in the latter than the former.

Lemma 7.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

O\between O^{\prime}
.

2.

O\between\mathit{interior}(|O^{\prime}|)
.

3.

\mathit{interior}(|O|)\between\mathit{interior}(|O^{\prime}|)
.

Proof.

First we prove the equivalence of parts 1 and 2:

1.

Suppose
O\between O^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
2.1
(
1
)
O\between\mathit{interior}(|O^{\prime}|)
.

2.

Suppose there is some
p\in O\cap\mathit{interior}(|O^{\prime}|)
.
Then
O
is an open neighbourhood of
p
and
p\in|O^{\prime}|
, so by Lemma
5.3
(or just direct from Definition
1.2
(
1
))
O\between O^{\prime}
as required.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 3 then follows easily by two applications of the equivalence of parts 1 and 2.
∎

Corollary 7.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

p
and
p^{\prime}
have a nonintersecting pair of open neighbourhoods.

2.

p
and
p^{\prime}
have a nonintersecting pair of regular open neighbourhoods.

Proof.

Part 2 clearly implies part 1, since a regular open set is an open set.
Part 1 implies part 2 using Lemma
7.8
and Corollary
7.5
.
∎

Remark 7.10
.

In Definition
6.1
(
1
) we defined
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
in terms of open neighbourhoods of
p
and
p^{\prime}
as follows:

\forall O,O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.(p\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime})\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}.

In the light of Corollary
7.9
, we could just as well have defined it just in terms of regular open neighbourhoods:

\forall O,O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}.(p\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime})\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}.

Mathematically, for what we have needed so far, this latter characterisation is not needed.
However, it is easy to think of scenarios in which it might be useful.
In particular,
computationally
it could make sense to restrict to the regular open sets, simply because there are fewer of them.

3
Minimal nonempty regular closed sets are precisely the minimal closed neighbourhoods

Lemma 7.11
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
.
Then:

1.

If
C
is a minimal closed neighbourhood (a closed set with a nonempty open interior), then
C
is a nonempty regular closed set (Definition
7.2
).

2.

If
C
is a nonempty regular closed set then
C
is a closed neighbourhood (Definition
4.1
).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
C
is a minimal closed neighbourhood.

Write
O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(C)
and
C^{\prime}=|O^{\prime}|=|\mathit{interior}(C)|
.
Because
C
is a closed neighbourhood, by Definition
4.1

O^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
.
By Lemma
1.6

C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
.
Using Corollary
2.2

\mathit{interior}(C^{\prime})=\mathit{interior}(|\mathit{interior}(C)|)=\mathit{interior}(C)=O^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
, so that
C^{\prime}
is a closed neighbourhood, and by minimality
C^{\prime}=C
.
But then
C=|\mathit{interior}(C)|
so
C
is regular, as required.

2.

Suppose
C
is a nonempty regular closed set,
so that
\varnothing\neq C=|\mathit{interior}(C)|
.

It follows that
\mathit{interior}(C)\neq\varnothing
and this means precisely that
C
is a closed neighbourhood.
∎

In Theorem
6.2
we characterised the point
p
being regular in terms of minimal closed neighbourhoods.
We can now characterise the minimal closed neighbourhoods in terms of something topologically familiar:

Proposition 7.12
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

C
is a minimal nonempty regular closed set.

2.

C
is a minimal closed neighbourhood.

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
C
is a minimal closed neighbourhood.

By Lemma
7.11
(1)
C
is a nonempty regular closed set.
Furthermore by Lemma
7.11
(2) if
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
is any other nonempty regular closed set contained in
C
, then it is a closed neighbourhood, and by minimality it is equal to
C
.
Thus,
C
is minimal.

•

Suppose
C
is a minimal nonempty regular closed set.

By Lemma
7.11
(2)
C
is a closed neighbourhood.
Furthermore by Lemma
7.11
(1) if
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
is any other closed neighourhood then it is a nonempty regular closed set, and by minimality it is equal to
C
.
∎

8
How are
p_{\between}
and
|p|
related?

Remark 8.1
.

Recall the definitions of
p_{\between}
and
|p|
:

•

The set
|p|
is the
closure
of
p
.

By Definition
1.2
this is the set of
p^{\prime}
such that every open neighbourhood
O^{\prime}\ni p^{\prime}
intersects with
\{p\}
.
By Definition
1.5

|p|
is closed.

•

The set
p_{\between}
is the set of points
intertwined
with
p
.

By Definition
6.1
(
2
) this is the set of
p^{\prime}
such that every open neighbourhood
O^{\prime}\ni p^{\prime}
intersects with every open neighbourhood
O\ni p
.
By Proposition
4.3
(
4
)
p_{\between}
is closed.

So we see that
|p|
and
p_{\between}
give us two canonical ways of generating a closed set from a point
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
This invites a question:

How are
p_{\between}
and
|p|
related?

In this section we develop answers.

Lemma
8.2
rephrases Remark
8.1
more precisely by looking at it through sets complements.
We will use it in Lemma
4.4
(
2
):

Lemma 8.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|p|=\bigcup\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\not\in O\}\in\mathsf{Open}
.

In words:
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|p|
is the union of the
open
sets that avoid
p
.

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus p_{\between}=\bigcup\{C\in\mathsf{Closed}\mid p\not\in C\}\in\mathsf{Open}
.

In words:
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus p_{\between}
is the union of the
closed
sets that avoid
p
.

3.
