But this contradicts finiteness of
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(n)
.
∎

4
Computing open and closed sets in witness semitopologies

1
Computing open sets:
X
is open when
X\prec X

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set (Definition
2.2
) and
X,X^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Define the
witness ordering
X\prec X^{\prime}
by

X\prec X^{\prime}\quad\text{when}\quad X\subseteq X^{\prime}\ \land\ \forall p{\in}X.\exists w{\in}{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p).w\subseteq X^{\prime}.

If
X\prec X
then call
X
a
\prec
-fixedpoint
.

Remark 4.2
.

Intuitively,
X\prec X^{\prime}
when
X^{\prime}
extends
X
with (at least) one witness-set for every element
p\in X
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set, and recall the witness ordering
\prec
from Definition
4.1
.
Then:

1.

If
X\prec X^{\prime}
then
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
, or in symbols:
{\prec}\subseteq{\subseteq}
.

2.

\prec
is a transitive (
X\prec X^{\prime}\prec X^{\prime\prime}
implies
X\prec X^{\prime\prime}
) and antisymmetric (
X\prec X^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime}\prec X
implies
X=X^{\prime}
) relation on
\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.

Proof.

By routine calculations from Definition
4.1
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

O
is open in the witness semitopology (Definition
2.5
).

•

O
is a
\prec
-fixedpoint (Definition
4.1
).

In symbols:

\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})=\{X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid X\prec X\}.

Proof.

Being a
\prec
-fixedpoint in Definition
4.1
— every point in
O
is witnessed by a subset of
O
— simply reformulates the openness condition from Definition
2.5
.
∎

Proposition 4.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set and suppose
\mathcal{X}=(X_{0}\prec X_{1}\prec\dots)
is a countably ascending
\prec
-chain.
Write
\bigcup\mathcal{X}
for the union
\bigcup_{i}X_{i}
of the elements in
\mathcal{X}
.
Then:

1.

\bigcup\mathcal{X}
is a
\prec
-limit for
\mathcal{X}
.
In symbols:
\forall i.X_{i}\prec\bigcup\mathcal{X}
.

2.

\bigcup\mathcal{X}
is a
\prec
-fixedpoint and so (by Lemma
4.4
) is open.
In symbols:
\bigcup\mathcal{X}\prec\bigcup\mathcal{X}\in\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
.

Proof.

1.

We must show that if
p\in X_{i}
then
w\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{X}
for some
w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.
But this is automatic from the fact that
X_{i}\prec X_{i{+}1}\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{X}
.

2.

From part 1 noting that if
p\in\bigcup\mathcal{X}
then
p\in X_{i}
for some
i
.
∎

Remark 4.6

(Computing open sets)
.

Proposition
4.5
and Lemma
4.4
above are not complicated
5
5

5

This is a feature and did not happen by accident: it required design effort.

and they say something important: in the
witness
semitopology, open sets can be computed with a simple loop that accumulates a set of points; and for each point in the set so far, add some choice of witness-set of that point to the set (if one is not already present); repeat until we reach a fixed point; then return the result.

In more detail, to compute an open set in the witness semitopology:

1.

Nondeterministically choose an initial
R_{0}
— in particular, to compute an open neighbourhood of
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
we can set
R_{0}=\{p\}
.

2.

Given
R_{i}
, for each
p\in R_{i}
nondeterministically pick some witness-set
w(p)\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
and set
R_{i{+}1}=R_{i}\cup\bigcup_{p\in R_{i}}w(p)
.

3.

If
R_{i{+}1}=R_{i}
then terminate with result
R_{i}
; otherwise loop back to 2.

This algorithm is nondeterministic and could run forever if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is infinite, but it is an algorithm and it is local in the sense of Remark
1.1
.
We continue this thread in Remarks
4.14
and
5.1
.

2
Computing closed sets using limit points:
|P|=\mathit{lim}(P)

Definition 4.7
.

Suppose
P
is a set and
\mathcal{W}
is a set (or a sequence) of sets.
Define
P\between\mathcal{W}
by

P\between\mathcal{W}\quad\text{when}\quad\forall W{\in}\mathcal{W}.P\between W.

In words:
P\between\mathcal{W}
when
P
intersects with every
W\in\mathcal{W}
.

Definition 4.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Define
\mathit{lim}_{w}(P)
by

\mathit{lim}_{w}(P)=P\cup\{p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid P\between{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\}.

In words:
\mathit{lim}_{w}(P)
is the set of points
p
whose every witness-set contains a
P
-element.

We iterate this:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}\mathit{lim}_{0}(P)=&P\\
\mathit{lim}_{i{+}1}(P)=&\mathit{lim}_{w}(\mathit{lim}_{i}(P))\\
\mathit{lim}(P)=&\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathit{lim}_{n}(P)\end{array}

We call
\mathit{lim}(P)
the set of
limit points of
P
.

Remark 4.9
.

In Definition
2.2
(
1
) we insisted that
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
is nonempty for every point
p
.
This avoids a degenerate situation in the definition of
\mathit{lim}_{w}(P)
in Definition
4.8
above in which the condition
P\between{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
is vacuously satisfied by a
p
with empty
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
(i.e. by a
p
with no witness sets).
Definition
2.2
(
1
) excludes this by insisting that
p
has to have at least one witness, even if it is just
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=\{\{p\}\}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then

P\subseteq\mathit{lim}(P).

Proof.

It is a fact of Definition
4.8
that
P=\mathit{lim}_{0}(P)\subseteq\mathit{lim}_{1}(P)\subseteq\mathit{lim}(P)
.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
\mathit{lim}(P)\between{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
(Definition
4.7
) then
p\in\mathit{lim}(P)
.

2.

By the contrapositive and expanding Definition
4.7
,

p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus\mathit{lim}(P)\quad\text{implies}\quad\exists w{\in}{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p).w\cap\mathit{lim}(P)=\varnothing.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathit{lim}(P)\between{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.
Unpacking Definitions
4.7
and
4.8
it follows that for every
w{\in}{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
there exists
n_{w}\geq 0
such that
\mathit{lim}_{n_{w}}(P)\between w
.
Now by Definition
2.2
(
1
)
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
— the set of witness-sets to
p
— is finite, and
it follows that for some/any
n
greater than the maximum of all the
n_{w}
, we have
\mathit{lim}_{n}(P)\between{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.
Thus
p\in\mathit{lim}_{w}(\mathit{lim}_{n}(P))\subseteq\mathit{lim}(P)
as required.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
O\in\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
.
Then:

1.

If
O\between\mathit{lim}_{w}(P)
then
O\between P
.

2.

If
O\between\mathit{lim}(P)
then
O\between P
.

3.

As a corollary, if
O\cap P=\varnothing
then
O\cap\mathit{lim}(P)=\varnothing
.

Proof.

1.

Consider
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
such that
p\in O
and
p\in\mathit{lim}_{w}(P)
.
By assumption there exists
w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
such that
w\subseteq O
.
Also by assumption
w\between P
.
It follows that
O\between P
as required.

2.

If
O\between\mathit{lim}(P)
then
O\between\mathit{lim}_{n}(P)
for some finite
n\geq 0
.
By a routine induction using part 1 of this result, it follows that
O\between P
as required.

3.

This is just the contrapositive of part 2 of this result, noting that
O\between P
when
O\cap P=\varnothing
by Notation
1.1
, and similarly for
O\between\mathit{lim}(P)
.
∎

Proposition 4.13
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set and suppose
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

\mathit{lim}(P)=|P|.

In words: the set of limit points of
P
from Definition
4.8
is equal to the topological closure of
P
from Definition
1.2
.

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
p\not\in|P|
.

Then there exists some
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
such that
O\cap P=\varnothing
.
Thus by Lemma
4.12
(3) also
O\cap\mathit{lim}(P)=\varnothing
.

•

Suppose
p\not\in\mathit{lim}(P)
.

By Definition
1.2
we need to exhibit an
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
that is disjoint from
P
, and since
P\subseteq\mathit{lim}(P)
by Lemma
4.10
,
it would suffice to exhibit
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
that is disjoint from
\mathit{lim}(P)
.
We set

O=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus\mathit{lim}(P).

Lemma
4.11
(2) expresses precisely that this is an open set in the witness semitopology, and by construction it is disjoint from
\mathit{lim}(P)
.
∎

Remark 4.14

(Computing closed sets)
.

As in Remark
4.6
we see that in the
witness
semitopology, closed sets can be computed with a simple loop that accumulates a set of points so far: and for each point in the space, if all of its witness-sets intersect with the set of points so far, add that point to the set so far; repeat until we reach a fixed point; return the result.

In more detail, to compute a closed set in the witness semitopology:

1.

Nondeterministically choose an initial
P_{0}
— in particular, to compute a closed set containing
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
we can set
P_{0}=\{p\}
.

2.

Given
P_{i}
, for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
check if
w\between P_{i}
for every witness-set
w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
and collect these
p
into a set
B_{i}
.
Set
P_{i{+}1}=P_{i}\cup B_{i}
.

3.

If
P_{i{+}1}=P_{i}
then terminate with result
P_{i}
; otherwise loop back to 2.

This algorithm could run forever if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is infinite, but it is an algorithm and it is local in the sense of Remark
1.1
.
Note that quantification over every point is local in the sense of Remark
1.1
, in spite of the quantification over all
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
in step 2 above: participants would listen for queries from peers on the channel “I am trying to compute an open set; here is my
R_{i}
; do you want to join it?”.

Remark 4.15

(Summing up)
.

From a distributed-computing perspective, it might at first appear that working with semitopologies would require some form of prior coordination: e.g. for participants to at least have common knowledge of their shared, minimal open neighbourhoods.
For, consider a new participant
p
joining a system based on semitopology: how is
p
supposed to know which are the open sets?

Suprisingly, we have seen that
witness semitopologies can be built without any coordination
.
Each participant just unilateraly chooses a set of witness-sets.
As discussed in Remarks
4.6
and
4.14
, and even in an infinite semitopology,
a participant can compute open and closed sets — they do not have to, but they can if they wish to spend the bandwidth — by exploring witness-sets using nondeterministic algorithms.

We make no claims to efficiency (we have not even set up machinery in this paper to measure what that would mean) but what matters is that for witness semitopologies such procedures exist, in contrast e.g. to the uncomputable semitopology from Example
1.2
.

In the next subsection we offer an interpretation of witness functions that in some sense explains why this should be so,
and gives a new intuition of why witness semitopologies are amenable to a distributed, local, uncoordinated computation in the style that we require.

5
Declarative content of witness semitopologies

1
Witnessed sets and Horn clause theories

Remark 5.1
.

Recall that a
sequential space
is one in which the sets closed under convergent sequences, are precisely the closed sets.
Proposition
4.13
(
\mathit{lim}(P)=|P|
) looks, just a bit, like a sequential space closure result.
Looking more closely, we see that the similarity comes from the fact that the definition uses an
\omega
-iteration that is, just a little, reminiscent of a converging
\omega
-sequence of points.
Perhaps surprisingly, we can make this resemblance into something much more precise, as follows:

Definition 5.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a finite witnessed set (so
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a finite set).

1.

Let the
derived logic
\mathsf{Prop}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
be a propositional syntax with connectives
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\top}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
over a set of
atomic proposition symbols

\bar{\bm{\mathsf{P}}}=\{\bar{p}\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}
.

Note that
\bar{p}
is just a symbol in our formal syntax; there is one such for each point
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

2.

For each
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
define an
axiom
\bar{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
by
6
6

6

Below,
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
denote a finite list of
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
connectives. We use this instead of
\bigwedge
and
\bigvee
to emphasise that this is formal syntax in
\mathsf{Prop}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
.

\bar{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}\bar{q}\bigr){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\bar{p}

and collect these axioms into a set

\mathsf{Ax}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})=\{\bar{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}.

3.

A
sequent
\Phi\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\displaystyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-2.08334pt\cr\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\textstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-2.08334pt\cr\hbox{$\textstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\scriptstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-1.45834pt\cr\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-1.04167pt\cr\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}}\Psi
is a pair of finite sets of propositions in the syntax of
\mathsf{Prop}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
.

4.

Call
\Phi\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\displaystyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-2.08334pt\cr\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\textstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-2.08334pt\cr\hbox{$\textstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\scriptstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-1.45834pt\cr\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-1.04167pt\cr\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}}\Psi
a
derivable sequent
when
\Phi,\mathsf{Ax}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vdash\Psi
is derivable in propositional logic.

5.

If
S\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
write
\bar{S}=\{\bar{p}\mid p\in S\}
.
Call
\bar{S}
a
model
or
answer set
for
\mathsf{Ax}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
when

\forall p{\in}\bm{\mathsf{P}}.(\bar{S}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\displaystyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-2.08334pt\cr\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\textstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-2.08334pt\cr\hbox{$\textstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\scriptstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-1.45834pt\cr\hbox{$\scriptstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}{\vbox{\halign{\hfil#\hfil\cr$\scriptscriptstyle{}_{{\shortminus}}\kern-0.5pt$\cr\kern-1.04167pt\cr\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\vdash$}\cr}}}}\bar{p}\Longrightarrow\bar{p}\in\bar{S}).

Proposition 5.3

(Declarative interpretation)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a finite witnessed set and
C\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

C
is closed in the witness semitopology (Definition
2.5
).

•

\bar{C}
is a model (Definition
5.2
(
5
)).

Proof.

By Definitions
2.5
(
2
) and
5.2
(
2
),
the condition in Definition
5.2
(
5
) for
\bar{C}
to be a model precisely expresses the property that
C
is closed.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Every finite semitopology can be exhibited as the set of (set complements of) models of a propositional Horn clause theory.

Proof.

Lemma
3.2
shows how to exhibit a finite semitopology as the witness semitopology of a witnessed set, and Proposition
5.3
shows how to interpret that witnessed set as a Horn clause theory in a propositional logic.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

An axiom
\bar{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
consists of a propositional goal implied by a conjunction of disjunctions of (unnegated) propositional goals.
This fits the Horn clause syntax from Section 3 of
[
MNPS91
]
, and it can be translated into a more restricted Prolog-like syntax if required, just by expanding the disjuncts into multiple clauses using the
{\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\vee}}L)}}
rule.
7
7

7

An example makes the point:
((\bar{q}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\bar{q}^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\bar{p}
is equivalent to two simpler clauses
(\bar{q}{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\bar{p}
and
(\bar{q}^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\bar{q}^{\prime\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\bar{p}
; for more details see
[
MNPS91
]
.

Thus closed sets — and so also open sets, which are their complements — can be computed from the axioms
\mathsf{Ax}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
by asking a suitable propositional solver to compute
models
.
Answer Set Programming (ASP) tool is one such tool
[
Lif08
,
Lif19
]
.
Thus:

•

We can view the algorithm for computing closed sets described in Remark
4.14
as ‘just’ (see next Remark) a distributed ASP solver for the Horn clause theory
\mathsf{Ax}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
in the logic
\mathsf{Prop}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
.

•

Conversely, we can view this Subsection as observing that the set of all solutions to a finite Horn clause theory has a semitopological structure, via witnessed sets.

Remark 5.6
.

Proposition
5.3

is not a ‘proof’ that we should, or even could, actually use an ASP solver to do this.

Proposition
5.3
assumes complete and up-to-date information on the witness function.
Mathematically this is fine, just as writing ‘consider an uncomputable subset of
\mathbb{N}
’ is mathematically fine — we can prove that this exists.
As a
computational
statement about possible implementations, this is more problematic, because it is precisely the point of a network being distributed, that we do not suppose that a participant could ever collect a global snapshot of the network state; and if they somehow did, it could become out-of-date; and in any case, in the presence of failing or adversarial participants it could be inaccurate.
So just because there
is
a network state at some point in time, does not mean we have access to it.

Even mathematically, Proposition
5.3
is not the full story of (witness) semitopologies:

•

it concerns
finite
semitopologies, whereas we are also interested in
infinite
ones (see Remark
4.7
); and

•

the questions we ask in the mathematics — especially the second-order ones such as “Are these two points intertwined?” or “Find a maximal topen neighbourhood of this point, or confirm that none such exists.” — have not been considered in declarative programming, so far as we know.

So it is important to appreciate that while Proposition
5.3
characterises closed and open sets in a witness semitopology in terms of solutions to Horn clause theories, and so helps us to understand what these sets really are at a mathematical level, this is not in and of itself automatically useful to actually turning such a semitopology into working network — for that, we need algorithms like that described in Remark
4.14
— nor is it a full mathematical account of all the facts of interest about semitopologies.

One practical use case where the correspondence with declarative programming might be immediately useful would be a monitoring tool, especially one testing mathematical properties to detect leading indicators of network malfunction.
Thus, for a network that is operating well and not changing too quickly, it would be feasible to traverse the network collecting information, and then use something like an ASP solver as part of a monitoring tool to compute the closed and open sets and so monitor properties such as the current intertwinedness of the network.
This is fine, so long as the reader is clear that a (centralised) network monitor that works in good conditions is not the same thing as the robust distributed network itself.

2
Witnessed sets and topologies

If finite semitopologies can be thought of as sets of solutions to Horn clause theories via witnessed sets, as outlined in Corollary
5.4
, what do finite topologies correspond to?
We will find answers just by unrolling definitions and doing some simple reasoning, but the results are perhaps illuminating and a little bit surprising:

Definition 5.7
.

1.

Call a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
a
deterministic
when each point
p
has a unique least open neighbourhood
p\in M_{p}\in\mathsf{Open}
.

2.

Call a Horn clause theory (in the sense used in Definition
5.2
(
2
))
deterministic
when for each propositional atom
\bar{p}\in\bar{\bm{\mathsf{P}}}
there exists at most one axiom in which
\bar{p}
appears in its head.
8
8

8

The
head
of the axiom is its final propositional atom, written to the right-hand side of the
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
in Definition
5.2
(
2
).

3.

Call a witnessed set
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
(Definition
2.2
(
1
))
deterministic
when for each point
p
,
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
is a singleton set; thus
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=\{W_{p}\}
.
9
9

9

W_{p}
(the witness-set to
p
) is not necessarily equal to
M_{p}
(the least open set containing
p
). The witness function
generates
a witness semitopology, but is not necessarily
equal
to it.

In words:
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
is deterministic when
every point has precisely one (possibly empty) witness-set
.

Remark 5.8
.

Recall the algorithms for computing open and closed sets from witness functions from Remarks
4.6
and
4.14
.
When
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
is deterministic, the algorithms simplify: there is precisely one witness-set to each point, and this removes the
nondeterminism
from the algorithms and they become deterministic — as our choice of name in Definition
5.7
suggests.

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a finite semitopology.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology (intersections of open sets are open).

2.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a deterministic semitopology (every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has a unique least open neighbourhood
M_{p}\in\mathsf{Open}
).

Proof.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology and consider some
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
We must find a least open neighbourhood
p\in M_{p}
.
We just set
M_{p}=\bigcap\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in O\}
.
Open sets in topologies are closed under finite unions, so
M_{p}
is an open neighbourhood of
p
, and by construction it is least.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is deterministic and consider
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
We must show that
O\cap O^{\prime}
is open.
We just note that
O\cap O^{\prime}=\bigcup\{M_{p}\mid p\in O\cap O^{\prime}\}
.
This is a union of open sets and so an open set, and by construction it contains
O\cap O^{\prime}
.
But also it is contained in
O\cap O^{\prime}
, since if
p\in O
then
M_{p}\subseteq O
, and similarly for
O^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

Returning to the terminology
deterministic
in Definition
5.7
above: when we are doing resolution in the Horn clause theory, and when we are building an open set using the algorithm in Remark
4.6
, there is only ever one witness/clause for each point.
Thus resolution never has to backtrack; and building the open set never has to make any choices.

Lemma 5.11
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a finite semitopology.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology.

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
for some deterministic witness function
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology.
We just modify the construction from Lemma
3.2
and set
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=\{M_{p}\}
.
The reader can check that
\mathsf{Open}=\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
.

Conversely, suppose
\mathsf{Open}=\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
for deterministic
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
, and write
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=\{W_{p}\}
.
Now consider
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
; we need to show that
O\cap O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
By the construction of the witness semitopology in Definition
2.5
(
3
) it would suffice to show that if
p\in O\cap O^{\prime}
then
W_{p}\subseteq O\cap O^{\prime}
.
But this is immediate, since
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
so that if
p\in O
then
W_{p}\subseteq O
, and similarly for
O^{\prime}
.
∎

Proposition 5.12
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a finite semitopology.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology.

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a deterministic semitopology.

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is the witness semitopology of a deterministic witness function.

Proof.

We combine Lemmas
5.9
and
5.11
.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

The definitions and proofs in this Subsection are quite easy, but they capture a nice intuition which is not immediately obvious from just looking at the definitions:

•

Finite semitopologies correspond to computation with nondeterminism and backtracking.

•

Finite topologies correspond to computation that does not require backtracking.

Proposition
5.12
makes this intuition formal up to a point, but it is not the full story.
What is missing is that a semitopology may have more than one presentation as the witness semitopology of a witnessed set.
10
10

10

Let’s spell that out: it is possible for
\mathsf{Open}=\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})=\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\prime})
for distinct
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
and
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\prime}
.

In particular, it is possible to create a non-deterministic witness function that generates a topology; intuitively, just because there might be a choice of witness-set, does not mean that the choice makes any difference to the final result.
Put another way:
determinism
ensures that backtracking is impossible, but nondeterminism not necessarily imply that it is
required
.
11
11

11

Think of reducing a simply-typed
\lambda
-calculus term; there are many reduction paths, but they all lead to the same normal form.

We speculate that Proposition
5.12
could be strengthened to show that topologies correspond to Horn clause theories that (may not be deterministic in the sense of Definition
5.7
, but that) do not require backtracking.
We leave this for future work.

Chapter 8
(Strongly) chain-complete semitopologies

1
Definition and discussion

Remark 1.1
.

Just as for topologies, in semitopologies it is not true in general that the intersection of a descending chain of open sets is open.

Consider
\mathbb{N}
with the semitopology generated by
O\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
\{0\}\subsetneq O
.
Then
(\{0\}\cup i_{\geq}\mid i\geq 1)
where
i_{\geq}=\{i^{\prime}\mid i^{\prime}\geq i\}
is a descending chain of open sets, but its intersection
\{0\}
is not open.

For the special case of
witness
semitopologies, we can say something considerably stronger, as we shall see in Definition
1.2
and Theorem
4.1
.

Recall from Definition
4.4
the notion of an ascending/descending
chain of sets
:

Definition 1.2
.

1.

Call a semitopology
chain-complete
when for every descending chain of open sets
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}
(Definition
4.4
), its intersection
\bigcap\mathcal{O}
is open.

2.

Call a semitopology
strongly chain-complete
when for every nonempty descending chain of nonempty open sets
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
, its intersection
\bigcap\mathcal{O}
is open and nonempty.
1
1

1

We insist the chain is nonempty to exclude the pathological case of an empty chain over the semitopology
(\varnothing,\{\varnothing\})
.

Remark 1.3

(Chain-completeness in context)
.

We make a few general observations about Definition
1.2
in the context of topology:

1.

The strong chain-completeness condition (every descending chain of nonempty open sets is nonempty and open) is reminiscent of, though different from, a standard
compactness
condition on metric spaces, that every descending chain of nonempty closed sets should be nonempty and closed.

2.

Call a topological space
Alexandrov
when its open sets are closed under arbitrary (and not just finite) intersections.

In the case that a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology (so open sets are closed under finite intersections), and assuming that open sets can be well-ordered, being chain-complete is equivalent to being Alexandrov.
Clearly, an Alexandrov space is chain-complete; and conversely if we have an infinite collection of open sets in a chain-complete topology then (assuming that this collection can be well-ordered) we obtain their intersection by a transfinite induction taking limits of infinite descending chains of intersections.

The Alexandrov condition is unnatural in semitopologies in the sense that we do not assume even that finite intersections exist, so there is no finite-intersections condition to strengthen to the infinite case.
However, the chain-completeness condition
is
natural in semitopologies, so in the light of the previous paragraph we could argue that chain-completeness is to semitopologies as being Alexandrov is to topologies.

This is an intuitive observation, not a mathematical one, but it may help to guide the reader’s intuitions.

3.

Strong chain-completeness has a much stronger flavour of finiteness than chain-completeness.

For example: a strongly chain-complete space can contain only finitely many disjoint open sets — since otherwise it would be easy to form an infinite descending chain of open sets with an empty intersection — so, in the light of the topen partitioning result in Theorem
5.4
, we see that the topen partition of a strongly chain-complete semitopology is
actually finite
.

Remark 1.4
.

Definition
1.2
abstracts two useful properties of two important classes of semitopologies:

1.

Every finite semitopology is strongly chain-complete, because a strictly descending chain of finite sets is finite.
2
2

2

For the record, it is easy to come up with other conditions. For instance, an even stronger condition is that a descending chain of open sets strictly above some
O\in\mathsf{Open}
has an open intersection that is also strictly above
O
(we recover the strong chain-completeness condition just by restricting
O
to be equal to
\varnothing
). This is a very reasonable thing to say: it is in a footnote and not the main text just because we have not (yet) found a direct use for it. In contract, strong chain-completeness turns out to be natural and very useful, so we focus on that.

2.

Every witness semitopology is chain-complete; we will prove this shortly, in Theorem
4.1
.

More discussion of these points is in Remark
4.6
.
The main mathematical/technical properties that come out of a semitopology being chain-complete and strongly chain-complete are respectively:

•

Lemma
5.3
and Corollary
5.4
(existence of open covers), and

•

Lemma
5.7
and Corollary
5.8
(existence of open atoms) respectively.

However, before we come to that, we will set up some machinery and check some useful properties.

2
Elementary properties of the definition

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

If
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is strongly chain-complete, then it is chain-complete.

2.

The reverse implication need not hold: it is possible for a semitopology to be chain-complete but not strongly chain-complete.

3.

Not every semitopology is chain-complete.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Consider a descending chain of open sets
\mathcal{O}
.
If one of the elements in
\mathcal{O}
is empty then
\bigcap\mathcal{O}=\varnothing
and
\varnothing\in\mathsf{Open}
so we are done.
If all of the elements in
\mathcal{O}
are nonempty then by chain-completeness
\bigcap\mathcal{O}
is nonempty and open, and thus in particular it is open.

2.

A counterexample is
(\mathbb{N},\mathit{pow}(\mathbb{N}))
(the discrete semitopology on the infinite set of natural numbers).
Then
i_{\geq}=\{i^{\prime}\mid i^{\prime}\geq i\}
for
i\geq 0
is a descending chain of nonempty open sets whose intersection
\varnothing
is open but notnonempty.

3.

This just repeats Remark
1.1
, which gives an easy counterexample.
∎

Example 2.2
.

1.

The
all-but-one
and
more-than-one
semitopologies (see Examples
1.4
(
5c
&
5d
)) are (strongly) chain-complete.

2.

The closed interval
[{\shortminus}1,1]
with its usual topology is not chain-complete (and not strongly chain-complete): e.g.
\{({\shortminus}1/i,1/i)\mid i\geq 1\}
is a descending chain of open sets but its intersection
\{0\}
is not open.
Similarly for the two semitopologies on
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
in Example
6.10
.

(Looking ahead just for a moment to Theorem
4.1
, this tells us that these semitopologies cannot be generated by witness functions.)

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is chain-complete if and only if the union of any ascending chain of closed sets, is closed.

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is strongly chain-complete if and only if the union of any ascending chain of closed sets that are not equal to
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, is closed and not equal to
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

Direct from Definition
1.2
using Lemma
1.9
, which notes that closed sets are the complements of open sets (just as for topologies).
∎

3
Consequences of being strongly chain-complete

Being strongly chain-complete is a useful well-behavedness condition.
We consider some of its consequences.

1
Strongly chain-complete implies
\mathrel{\between}
-complete

We saw a chain-completeness condition before:
\mathrel{\between}
-completeness from Definition
4.2
(
2
).
As promised in Remark
4.3
(
2
), we now note that strongly chain-complete semitopologies are also
\mathrel{\between}
-complete:

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a quasiregular semitopology.
Then if
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is strongly chain-complete then it is
\mathrel{\between}
-complete (Definition
4.2
(
2
)).

Proof.

Suppose we have a
\geq_{\hskip-0.7pt\mathrel{\between}}
-descending chain of points
p_{1}\geq_{\hskip-0.7pt\mathrel{\between}}p_{2}\geq_{\hskip-0.7pt\mathrel{\between}}\dots
.

Since
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is quasiregular,
\mathit{K}(p_{i})\in\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
for every
i
.
Write
I=\bigcap_{i}\mathit{K}(p_{i})
.

Since
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is strongly chain-complete (Definition
1.2
(
2
)),
I\in\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
(we need
strong
chain-completeness here to know that
I
is not just open but also nonempty).
Choose some
p\in I
.

It follows from Lemma
4.5
(1) that
p\leq_{\hskip-0.7pt\mathrel{\between}}p_{i}
for every
i
, thus
p
is a
\leq_{\hskip-0.7pt\mathrel{\between}}
-lower bound for the chain.
∎

2
Indirectly regular points: inherent properties

In Definition
1.4
we saw three regularity conditions on points: quasiregular, weakly regular, and regular.
We now add a fourth condition to this mix:
indirect regularity
.
A point is indirectly regular when it is intertwined with a regular point; intuitively, if regular points are ‘nice’ then an indirectly regular point is a point that is not necessarily nice itself, but it is intertwined with a point that
is
nice.
3
3

3

Like in the movies: where a gangster falls in love with a nice person; the gangster may not stop being a gangster, but they now have a moral compass, if only indirectly.

It is not at all obvious that this should have anything to do with strong chain-completeness, but it does: a punchline of this Subsection will come in Remark
3.6
, where we note that if a semitopology in strongly chain-complete then indirect regularity slots in particularly nicely with the three regularity conditions from Definition
1.4
.
We now set about building the machinery we need to tell this story:

Definition 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Call
p
an
indirectly regular point
when
p\mathrel{\between}q
for some regular
q
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
p
is indirectly regular if and only if
p
is in the closure of a topen set.

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
p
is indirectly regular; so
p\mathrel{\between}q
for some regular
q\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

By Definition
1.4
(
3
)
q\in\mathit{K}(q)\in\mathsf{Topen}
.
By Definition
6.1
(
2
) (since
p\mathrel{\between}q
)
p\in q_{\between}
, and by Theorem
4.12
(
2
)
q_{\between}=|\mathit{K}(q)|
.
Thus
p
is in the closure of the topen set
\mathit{K}(q)
.

•

Suppose
p\in|T|
for some
T\in\mathsf{Topen}
.

Choose any
q\in T
.
Note by Theorem
2.6
(
5
) that
q
is regular; we will now show that
p\mathrel{\between}q
.

Consider a pair of open neighbourhoods
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}
and
q\in O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then
O\between T
(because
p\in O
and
p\in|T|
), and
T\between O^{\prime}
(because
q\in T\cap O^{\prime}
).
By transitivity of
T
,
O\between O^{\prime}
.
It follows that
p\mathrel{\between}q
as required.
∎

We will need Lemma
3.4
below.
We can think of this as a version of Lemma
4.5
where we know that one of the points is regular:

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,q\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
q
is regular.
Then:

1.

If
q\mathrel{\between}p
then
\mathit{K}(q)\subseteq p_{\between}
.

2.

As a corollary, if
p\in q_{\between}
or
q\in p_{\between}
, then
\mathit{K}(q)\subseteq p_{\between}
.

Proof.

The corollary follows because by Definition
6.1
(
2
),
p\in q_{\between}
and
q\in p_{\between}
are both equivalent to
p\mathrel{\between}q
.

So suppose
q
is regular and
q\in p_{\between}
.
By Definition
1.4
(
3
)
q\in\mathit{K}(q)\in\mathsf{Topen}
and by Theorem
2.2

q
is unconflicted.
Consider any other
q^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(q)
; unpacking Definition
1.4
(
1
) and
6.1
(
2
)
p\mathrel{\between}q\mathrel{\between}q^{\prime}
and so by Definition
1.1
(
2
) (since
q
is unconflicted)
p\mathrel{\between}q^{\prime}
.
Thus
\mathit{K}(q)\subseteq p_{\between}
as required.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is indirectly regular.

2.

p_{\between}
contains a topen set.

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose there exists
T\in\mathsf{Topen}
such that
T\subseteq p_{\between}
.

Take any
q\in T
.
By Theorem
2.6
(
5
)
q
is regular, and by Definition
6.1
(
2
)
q\mathrel{\between}p
.

•

Suppose
p
is indirectly regular.

By Definition
3.2

p\mathrel{\between}q
for some regular
q\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
By Lemma
3.4

\mathit{K}(q)\subseteq p_{\between}
.
By Definition
1.4
(
3
)
\mathit{K}(q)\in\mathsf{Topen}
.
∎

3
Indirectly regular points in the context of other regularity properties

We can now continue the observations made in Remark
1.6
:

Remark 3.6
.

This Subsection develops a sequence of results that are interesting in themselves, but also taken together with Lemma
1.7
they indicate that in a strongly chain-complete semitopology, our regularity conditions organise into a nice list ordered by increasing strength as follows:

•

Being quasiregular (having a nonempty community).

•

Being indirectly regular (intertwined with a regular point / being on the boundary of a topen set).

•

Being weakly regular (being an element of your community).

•

Being regular (being an element of your
topen
community).

As a diagram, in strongly chain-complete semitopologies we get this chain of implications:

\text{quasiregular}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\text{indirectly regular}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\text{weakly regular}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\text{regular}.

If the semitopology is not strongly chain-complete, then (by Lemma
1.7
we still have the other implications, but) being indirectly regular does not fall so neatly in line.

We read this as evidence that the
strongly chain-complete semitopologies
are a particularly natural class of semitopologies for us to study, and they are a useful abstraction of the finite semitopologies (much as e.g. Alexandrov topologies, or compact topologies, capture aspects of finiteness for topologies).

Proposition 3.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology (by Remark
1.4
this holds in particular if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is finite).
Then:

1.

If
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is weakly regular then
p
is indirectly regular.

2.

The converse implication need not hold: it is possible for
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to be strongly chain-complete, and even actually
finite
, and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is indirectly regular yet not weakly regular.

3.

If
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is not strongly chain-complete then the implication in part 1 might fail: it is possible for
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to be weakly regular but not indirectly regular.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is weakly regular.
From Proposition
4.10

p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood (a closed set with a nonempty open interior).
Using strong chain-completeness and Zorn’s lemma on
\supseteq
, the set of closed neighbourhoods that are subsets of
p_{\between}
contains a minimal closed neighbourhood
C\subseteq p_{\between}
(we need
strong
chain-completeness to ensure that
C
has a
nonempty
open interior).
Take
q\in\mathit{interior}(C)
; By Theorem
6.2

q
is regular.

2.

For a counterexample consider point
\ast
in Figure
1
. Then
\mathit{K}(\ast)=\{1\}
and
1
is regular, but
\ast\not\in\mathit{K}(\ast)
so
\ast
is not weakly regular.

(It
does
follow from existence of a regular
q\in\mathit{K}(p)
that
p
is quasiregular, but only because existence of
any
(not necessarily regular)
q\in\mathit{K}(p)
means precisely that
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
.
and
\mathit{K}(q)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.)

3.

A counterexample is in Figure
2
.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

Proposition
3.7
is just an easy corollary of Theorem
6.2
.
We can think of this as another version of the ‘hairy ball’ result that we saw in Theorem
4.7
, but for the case of a weakly regular point, instead of for a quasiregular space.

Recall that we care about regular points because these are (for our purposes) well-behaved: they have a topen neighbourhood (Theorem
2.6
), by which fact local consensus is guaranteed where algorithms succeed (Remark
2.7
).
Thus the interest of Proposition
3.7
is that it provides certain guarantees of progress; a weakly regular point may not be able to progress (even if algorithms succeed), but it guaranteed to be intertwined with some well-behaved regular point.

Figure 1
:
Lemma
3.9
(2): a point
\ast
that is quasiregular but not indirectly regular

Lemma 3.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
p
is indirectly regular then
p
is quasiregular.

2.

The converse implication need not hold: it is possible for
p
to be quasiregular but not indirectly regular.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
p
is indirectly regular.
By Definition
3.2

p\in q_{\between}
for some regular
q
.
By Lemma
1.7
(
1
&
2
)
q
is quasiregular, meaning that
\varnothing\neq\mathit{K}(q)
.
By Lemma
3.4

\mathit{K}(q)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
,
so that
\mathit{K}(p)
is nonempty and
p
is quasiregular.

2.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Consider the point
\ast
in the semitopology illustrated in Figure
1
:
4
4

4

This is an elaboration of the semitopology we have already seen in Figure
2
).

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{\ast,0,1,2,3\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{\{3,0\},\ \{0,1\},\ \{1,2\},\ \{2,3\}\}
;
note that the only open neighbourhood of
\ast
is all of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

The reader can check that
\ast_{\between}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, so
\mathit{K}(\ast)=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\neq\varnothing
so
\ast
is quasiregular.
However, the reader can also check that no point in this space is regular, so
\ast
is not intertwined with any regular point.
∎

4
Witness semitopologies are chain-complete

Theorem 4.1
.

Suppose that

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},{w\hskip-1.8ptf})
is a witnessed set.
Then the witness semitopology
\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
from Definition
2.5
is chain-complete.

Unpacking this we can say:

In a witness semitopology, intersections of descending chains of open sets are open, and unions of ascending chains of closed sets are closed.

Proof.

Consider a chain of open sets
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}
.
There are three cases:

•

Suppose
\bigcap\mathcal{O}=\varnothing
.

We note that
\varnothing\in\mathsf{Open}
(Definition
1.2
(
1
)) and we are done.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{O}
has a least element
O
.

Then
O=\bigcap\mathcal{O}
and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
and we are done.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{O}\neq\varnothing
and
\mathcal{O}
has no least element.

Then note that
\mathcal{O}
is infinite.
Consider some
p\in\bigcap\mathcal{O}
.
By construction of the witness semitopology (Definition
2.5
) for each
O\in\mathcal{O}
there exists a witness-set
w_{O}\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
such that
w_{O}\subseteq O
.
Now by Definition
2.2
(
1
)
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
is finite, so by the pigeonhole principle, there exists some
w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
such that
w\subseteq O
for for every
O\in\mathcal{O}
,
and thus
w\subseteq\bigcap\mathcal{O}
.

Now
p
in the previous paragraph was arbitrary, so we have shown that if
p\in\bigcap\mathcal{O}
then also there exists
w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
such that
w\subseteq\bigcap\mathcal{O}
.
It follows by construction of the witness semitopology in Definition
2.5
that
\bigcap\mathcal{O}
is open as required.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

The reverse implication in Theorem
4.1
does not hold: there exists a chain-complete semitopology (indeed, it is also strongly chain-complete) that is not generated as the witness semitopology of a witnessed set.

Proof.

It is a fact that the more-than-one semitopology on
\mathbb{N}
(having open sets generated by distinct pairs
\{i,i^{\prime}\}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
; see Example
1.4
(
5d
)) is strongly chain-complete, but by Lemma
3.5
is is not generated by a witness function.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

Elaborating further on Lemma
4.2
, suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a chain-complete semitopology.
Then to every
p
we can assign a nonempty set
\mathcal{O}_{p}
of
covers
(minimal open sets containing
p
; see Definition
5.2
).

Can we obtain a witness function just by setting
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=\mathcal{O}_{p}
?
No: because
p
need not have finitely many covers, and Definition
2.2
insists on a
finite
set of (possibly infinite) nonempty witness-sets.
5
5

5

See Example
1.4
(
5c
) for an example of a semitopology containing points with infinitely many covers, though interestingly, this
can
be generated by a witness function, as noted in Lemma
3.3
.

We could allow an infinite set of witness-sets in Definition
2.2
, but at a price:

•

The proof of Theorem
4.1
depends on the pigeonhole principle, which uses finiteness of the set of witness-sets.

•

The proof of Lemma
4.11
depends on the set of witness-sets being finite, and this is required for Proposition
4.13
.

Remark 4.4
.

Theorem
4.1
shows that witness semitopologies are chain-complete, but Lemma
4.2
demonstrates that this cannot precisely characterise witness semitopologies.
Might there be another way?

We might look at Corollary
5.4
(open covers exist), cross-reference with Definition
2.2
(
1
) (every
p
has only finitely many witness-sets), and ask if we might characterise witness semitopologies as those topologies that are chain-complete
and
every
p
has finitely many open covers (Definition
5.2
(
2
)).

No: by Lemma
3.3
, the all-but-one semitopology from Example
1.4
(
5c
) is a witness semitopology, and if the underlying set of points is infinite then every point has infinitely many covers.
See also Remark
5.12
(1).

Proposition 4.5
.

1.

Not every witness semitopology (Definition
2.5
) is strongly chain-complete (Definition
1.2
(
2
)).

2.

Part 1 holds even if we restrict the witness function

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathit{fin}_{\neq\varnothing}(\mathit{pow}_{\neq\varnothing}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}))
in Definition
2.2
(
1
) to return a finite set of finite witness-sets, so that

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathit{fin}_{\neq\varnothing}(\mathit{fin}_{\neq\varnothing}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}))
.

3.

Every
finite
semitopology (this includes every finite witness semitopology) is strongly chain-complete.

Proof.

1.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Consider
\mathbb{N}
with witness function
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(i)=\{\{i{+}1\}\}
.
This generates a semitopology with open sets generated by
i_{\geq}=\{i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\mid i^{\prime}\geq i\}
.
Then
(i_{\geq}\mid i\in\mathbb{N})
is a descending chain of open sets with an open, but empty, intersection.

2.

We just use the counterexample in part 1.

3.

We noted already in Remark
1.4
that if the semitopology is finite then every descending chain of open sets is eventually stationary; so we just take the final element in the chain.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

We are particularly interested in the concrete example of finite witnessed semitopologies, since these are the ones that we can actually implement.
But we can ask what it is about this class of examples that makes them mathematically well-behaved; what essential algebraic features might we identify here?
Proposition
4.5
suggests that being strongly chain-complete may be a suitable mathematical abstraction:

•

by Proposition
4.5
the abstraction is both non-trivial and sound (not every witness semitopology is strongly chain-complete, but every
finite
witness semitopology is), and

•

Theorem
4.1
asserts that for a (possibly infinite)
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, any convergence using a descending sequence of open sets has a flavour of being ‘locally finite’ in the sense of being guaranteed to have a nonempty open intersection.
6
6

6

There is also a computational interpretation to (strong) chain-completeness: think of a descending chain of open sets as a computation that computes to narrow down possibilities to smaller and smaller nonempty open sets, then this possibly infinite computation does deliver a final answer that is a (nonempty) open set.

So strongly chain-complete semitopologies are a plausible abstraction of finite witness semitopologies.
7
7

7

There may be more than one such abstraction; identifying one candidate does not mean there may not be others. For example, both
rings
and
models of first-order arithmetic
are valid abstractions of the notion of ‘number’. Which of these mathematical structures we work with, depends on which aspects of the concrete thing we are interested in studying.

The test is now to explore the theory of strongly chain-complete semitopologies and see if they preserve enough structure, and enough of the right kind of structure, to model how finite witness semitopologies are applied to distributed collaborative action.

Key results in this direction are Corollaries
5.4
and
5.8
, which ensure that in a strongly chain-complete semitopology, open covers and atoms always exist, and we will build from there.

Remark 4.7

(Why infinity?)
.

Following on from Remark
4.6
,
we sometimes get asked, especially by engineers, why we care about infinite models when all practical computer networks are finite.

A simple answer is that we do this for the same reason that Python (and many other programming languages) have a datatype for infinite precision integers.
Any given execution will only compute numbers in a finite subset this infinity, but since we may not be able to predict how large this subset is, it is natural to support the notion of an infinite datatype.
Note this this holds for data, not just datatypes: e.g. Python accommodates values for
\pi
,
e
, and
j
even though these are not rational numbers, and for infinite streams and may other ‘infinite’ objects.
8
8

8

The first author once spent a while trying to convince a Computer Science undergraduate student that
1/3
is finite. The blockage was that the student only believed in the
float
datatype, and the decimal expansion of
1/3
as
0.333\dots
is infinite. This deadlock was broken by inviting the student to implement a base-3 float type. The deeper point here is that what we consider ‘infinite’ may depend on what representation we assume as primitive. We see something similar in model theory, where we may distinguish between
internal
and
external
notions of size in a model. The bottom line is: obsessing about size can become a dead end; we also need to pay attention to what seems elegant and natural, i.e. to what our brains want — and then model
that
.

However there is another reason: participants cannot depend on an exhaustive search of the full network ever terminating (nor that even an attempt at this would be cost-effective), so this requires a theory and algorithms that make sense on at least countably infinitely many points.

In fact, arguably the natural cardinality for semitopology is at least
uncountable
, since for a participant on a system with network latency, the system is not just unbounded, but also unenumerable.
This is another reason that Theorem
4.1
is remarkable.
See also Remark
3.6
.

5
Minimal sets: open covers and atoms

1
Open covers (minimal open neighbourhoods)

First, some useful notation:

Notation 5.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Write

O\gtrdot P\quad\text{and synonymously}\quad P\lessdot O

when
O
is a minimal nonempty open set containing
P
.
In symbols:

O\gtrdot P\quad\text{when}\quad O\neq\varnothing\land P\subseteq O\land\forall O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}.(P\subseteq O^{\prime}\subseteq O\Longrightarrow O^{\prime}=O).

We may combine
\gtrdot
with other relations for compactness.
For example:

•

p\in O\gtrdot P
is shorthand for
p\in O\land O\gtrdot P
; and

•

P\supseteq O\gtrdot Q
is shorthand for
O\subseteq P\land O\gtrdot Q
.

Definition
5.2
collects some (standard) terminology.

Definition 5.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

Call
O\in\mathsf{Open}
an
(open) neighbourhood of
p
when
p\in O
.

2.

Call
O\in\mathsf{Open}
an
(open) cover of
p
, write

O\gtrdot p\quad\text{and/or}\quad p\lessdot O,

and say that
O

covers

p
, when
O\gtrdot\{p\}
(Notation
5.1
).

In words using the terminology of part 1:
O\gtrdot p
when
O
is a minimal open neighbourhood of
p
.

3.

Write
\mathit{Covers}(p)
for the set of
open covers of
p
.
In symbols:

\mathit{Covers}(p)=\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\lessdot O\}.

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology and suppose
\varnothing\neq\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
is a nonempty set of nonempty open sets that is
\subseteq
-down-closed (meaning that if
\varnothing\neq O^{\prime}\subseteq O\in\mathcal{O}
then
O^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
).

Then
\mathcal{O}
contains a
\subseteq
-minimal element.

Proof.

A straightforward application of Zorn’s Lemma
[
Jec73
,
Cam78
]
:
By strong chain-completeness,
\mathcal{O}
ordered by the
superset
relation (the reverse of the subset inclusion relation), contains limits, and so upper bounds, of ascending chains.
By Zorn’s Lemma,
\mathcal{O}
contains a
\supseteq
-maximal element.
This is the required
\subseteq
-minimal element.
∎

Corollary 5.4

(Existence of open covers)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a chain-complete semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
9
9

9

Note that we only require
chain-completeness
here, not strong chain-completeness.

Then
p
has at least one open cover.
In symbols:

\exists O\in\mathsf{Open}.p\lessdot O\quad\text{and equivalently}\quad\mathit{Covers}(p)\neq\varnothing.

Furthermore, if
p\in O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
then
O^{\prime}
contains an open cover of
p
.
In symbols:

\exists O\in\mathsf{Open}.p\lessdot O\subseteq O^{\prime}.

Proof.

Direct from Lemma
5.3
, considering

\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in O\}
(nonempty because it contains
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
) and
\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in O\subseteq O^{\prime}\}
(nonempty because it contains
O^{\prime}
).
∎

Remark 5.5
.

Recall that our semitopological analysis of consensus is all about continuity and value assignments being locally constant — as per Definitions
2.1
(
3
) and
1.3
and results like Lemma
2.4
— and these discussions are about the open neighbourhoods of
p
.
Thus, to understand consensus at
p
we need to understand its open neighbourhoods.

Corollary
5.4
tells us that in a witness semitopology,
we can simplify and just consider the open covers of
p
.
This is because if a continuous function
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
such that
f(p)=p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
is continuous at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, then using continuity and Corollary
5.4
there exists some open cover
p\lessdot P\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})
.

Turning this around, if we want to
create
consensus around
p
— perhaps as part of a consensus algorithm — it suffices to find some open cover of
p
, and convince that cover.
This fact is all the more powerful because Corollary
5.4
does not assume that
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is finite: it is a fact of witness semitopologies of any cardinality.

2
Atoms (minimal nonempty open sets)

Definition 5.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.

1.

Call
A\in\mathsf{Open}
an
(open) atom
when
A
is a minimal nonempty open set.
10
10

10

An open atom covers every point that it contains, but an open cover for a point
p
need not be an atom, since it may contain a smaller open set — just not one that contains
p
. See Example
5.11
(
3
).

In symbols using Notation
5.1
this is:

A\gtrdot\varnothing\quad\text{and synonymously}\quad\varnothing\lessdot A.

2.

If
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
then write
\mathit{Atoms}(P)
for the atoms that are subsets of
P
.
In symbols:

\mathit{Atoms}(P)=\{A\in\mathsf{Open}\mid\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq P\}.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology and suppose
\varnothing\neq\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
is a nonempty set of nonempty open sets that is
\subseteq
-down-closed (meaning that if
\varnothing\neq O^{\prime}\subseteq O\in\mathcal{O}
then
O^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}
).

Then
\mathcal{O}
contains an atom.

Proof.

Just from Lemma
5.3
, noting that an atom is precisely a
\subseteq
-minimal nonempty open set.
∎

Corollary 5.8

(Existence of atoms)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology and
O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
is a nonempty open set.
Then
O
contains an atom.
In symbols:

\mathit{Atoms}(O)\neq\varnothing.

Proof.

From Lemma
5.7
, considering
\{O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}\mid\varnothing\neq O^{\prime}\subseteq O\}
(which is nonempty because it contains
O
).
∎

Remark 5.9
.

A simple observation is that if
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete topology — thus, a strongly chain-complete semitopology whose opens are closed under finite intersections — then the atom that exists by Corollary
5.8
is unique, simply because if we have atoms
A
and
A^{\prime}
then
A\cap A^{\prime}
is less than both and so by minimality must be equal to both.
See also Lemma
1.5
.

3
Discussion

Remark 5.10

(Origin of terminology)
.

1.

The terminology “
O
covers
p
” in Definition
5.2
(
2
)
is adapted from order theory (see e.g.
[
DP02
, §1.14]
), where we say that
y
covers
x
when
y>x
and there exists no
z
such that
y>z>x
.

2.

The terminology “
A
is an atom” in Definition
5.6
(1)
is also adapted from order theory (see e.g.
[
DP02
, §5.2]
), where we call
x
an atom when it is a least element not equal to
\bot
(i.e. when
x
covers
\bot
).

Example 5.11

((Counter)examples of atoms and open covers)
.

1.

p
can be in multiple distinct atoms (minimal nonempty open sets), and/or open covers (minimal open sets that contain
p
).

For instance, consider
\mathbb{N}
with the semitopology generated by
1_{\leq}=\{0,1\}
and
1_{\geq}=\{1,2,3,\dots\}
.
Then
1\in 1_{\leq}
and
1\in 1_{\geq}
, and
1_{\leq}
and
1_{\geq}
are distinct minimal open sets (and also open covers of
1
).

A topology would compress this example down to nothing: if
\{0,1\}
is open and
\{1,2,3,\dots\}
is open then their intersection
\{1\}
would be open, and this would be the unique least open set containing
1
.
Because open sets in semitopologies are not necessarily closed under intersection, semitopologies permit richer structure.

2.

An open cover
O
of
p
is a minimal open set that contains
p
— but
O
need not be an atom (a minimal nonempty open set).

Consider
\mathbb{N}
with the semitopology generated by
i_{\geq}=\{i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\mid i^{\prime}\geq i\}
.
Then
\mathit{Covers}(i)=\{i_{\geq}\}
but (with this semitopology)
\mathit{Atoms}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})=\varnothing
; there are no least nonempty open sets.

3.

An atom
A\in\mathit{atoms}(p)
is a minimal nonempty open set that is a subset of a minimal open set that contains
p
— but
A
need not contain
p
.

For instance, consider
\mathbb{N}
with the semitopology generated by
i_{\leq}=\{i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\mid i^{\prime}\leq i\}
.
Then
\mathit{atoms}(i)=\{\{0\}\}
for every
i
, because with this semitopology
\mathit{Atoms}(\mathbb{N})=\{\{0\}\}
and each
i
is covered by
i_{\leq}
, and
\{0\}\subseteq i_{\leq}
.
However, we only have
i\in\{0\}
when
i=0
.

Remark 5.12

(Two open problems)
.

1.

Topological characterisation of witness semitopologies.

Following on from Remark
4.4
, we have seen that witness semitopologies are chain-complete, but that this does not precisely characterise witness semitopologies.
A topological characterisation of witness semitopologies, or a proof that such a characterisation is impossible, remains an open problem.
To this end, the material in Subsection
5
may be relevant, which relates witness semitopologies to a Turing-complete model of computation.

2.

Conditions on witness functions to guaranteee (quasi)regularity.

It remains an open problem to investigate conditions on witness functions to guarantee that every point is quasiregular.
In view of Proposition
4.9
and Corollary
4.10
, such conditions would suffice to guarantee the existence of a regular point in the finite case.
Regular points are well-behaved, so a system with at least one regular point is a system that is in some sense ‘somewhere sensible’.

Chapter 9
Kernels: the atoms in a community

1
Definition and examples

Remark 1.1
.

We have studied
\mathit{K}(p)
the community of a point and have seen that is has a rich mathematics.
We also know from results (like Theorem
2.3
) and discussions (like Remark
5.1
) that to understand consensus in a semitopology, we have to understand its communities.

It is now interesting to look at the atoms in a community (Definition
5.6
; minimal nonempty open sets).
As we shall see later, the atoms in a community dictate its ability to act — see e.g. Corollary
, which is reminiscent of
Arrow’s theorem
from social choice theory — so that understanding
\mathit{K}(p)
is, in a sense we will make formal, much the same thing as understanding the atoms in
\mathit{K}(p)
(see e.g. Proposition
3.2
).

Kernels are also interesting in and of themselves, so we start in this Section by studying them (culminating, out of several results, with Propositions
2.7
and
3.2
).

Definition 1.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

Define
\mathit{ker}(p)
the
kernel of
p
to be the union of the atoms in its community.
We give equivalent formulations which we may use as convenient:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\mathit{ker}(p)=&\bigcup\{A\in\mathit{Atoms}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\mid A\subseteq p_{\between}\}\\
=&\bigcup\{A\in\mathit{Atoms}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\mid A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)\}\\
=&\bigcup\{A\in\mathsf{Open}\mid\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)\}.\end{array}

Above,
\varnothing\lessdot A
is just another way of saying that
A
is an atom (minimal nonempty open set; see Definition
5.6
), and
A\subseteq p_{\between}
if and only if
A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
because
A
is open and
\mathit{K}(p)
is just the open interior of
p_{\between}
(Definition
1.4
(
1
)).

2.

If
A
is an atom that is a subset of
\mathit{ker}(p)
(in symbols:
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
) then we might call
A
a
kernel atom of
p
.

3.

Extend
\mathit{ker}
to subsets
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
by taking a sets union:

\mathit{ker}(P)=\bigcup\{\mathit{ker}(p)\mid p\in P\}.

We return to and extend Example
4.1
, and we include details of the kernels:

Example 1.3
.

1.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to be
\mathbb{R}
the real numbers, with its usual topology (which is also a semitopology), as per Example
4.1
(
1
).
Then:

•

x_{\between}=\{x\}
and
\mathit{K}(x)=\varnothing
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(x)=\varnothing
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(\mathbb{R})=\varnothing
.

2.

We take, as per Example
4.1
(
2
) and as illustrated in Figure
1
, top-left diagram:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{0\}
and
\{2\}
.

Then:

•

0_{\between}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathit{K}(0)=\mathit{interior}(0_{\between})=\{0\}=\mathit{ker}(0)
.

•

2_{\between}=\{1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(2)=\mathit{interior}(2_{\between})=\{2\}=\mathit{ker}(2)
.

•

1_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(1)=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathit{ker}(1)=\{0,2\}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})=\{0,2\}
.

3.

We take, as per Example
4.1
(
3
), as illustrated in Figure
1
, and as reproduced for convenience here in Figure
1
(left-hand diagram):

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3,4\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{1,2\}
,
\{0,1,3\}
,
\{0,2,4\}
,
\{3\}
, and
\{4\}
.

Then:

•

x_{\between}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(x)=\mathit{interior}(x_{\between})=\{1,2\}
for
x\in\{0,1,2\}
.

•

x_{\between}=\{x\}=\mathit{K}(x)
for
x\in\{3,4\}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(x)=\{1,2\}
for
x\in\{0,1,2\}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(x)=\{x\}
for
x\in\{3,4\}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})=\{1,2,3,4\}
.

•

By construction
\mathit{ker}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\subseteq\bigcup\mathit{Atoms}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
, but but we see here that the inclusion may be strict, since e.g.
\{0,1,3\}
is an atom in this example but
0\not\in\mathit{ker}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.

4.

We add one point to part
3
of this example,
{\shortminus}1
, which is intertwined with
0
,
1
, and
2
but is not in a minimal nonempty open set, as illustrated in Figure
1
(right-hand diagram):

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{{\shortminus}1,0,1,2,3,4\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{{\shortminus}1,1,2\}
,
\{1,2\}
,
\{0,1,3\}
,
\{0,2,4\}
,
\{3\}
, and
\{4\}
.

Then:

•

x_{\between}=\{{\shortminus}1,0,1,2\}
and
\mathit{K}(x)=\mathit{interior}(x_{\between})=\{{\shortminus}1,1,2\}
for
x\in\{{\shortminus}1,0,1,2\}
.

•

x_{\between}=\{x\}=\mathit{K}(x)
for
x\in\{3,4\}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(x)=\{1,2\}
for
x\in\{{\shortminus}1,0,1,2\}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(x)=\{x\}
for
x\in\{3,4\}
.

•

\mathit{ker}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})=\{1,2,3,4\}
.

•

By construction
\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq p_{\between}
, but the inclusions may be strict.
For instance:

\mathit{ker}(0)=\mathit{ker}({\shortminus}1)=\{1,2\}\subsetneq\mathit{K}(0)=\mathit{K}({\shortminus}1)=\{{\shortminus}1,1,2\}\subsetneq 0_{\between}={\shortminus}1_{\between}=\{{\shortminus}1,0,1,2\}.

5.

We take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathbb{N}
, with the semitopology (also a topology) generated by final subsets
n_{\geq}=\{n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\mid n^{\prime}\geq n\}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
n_{\between}=\mathbb{N}=\mathit{K}(n)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathit{ker}(n)=\varnothing
(because there is no minimal nonempty open set).

Figure 1
:
Illustration of Example
1.3
(3&4)

We warm up with a couple of simple lemmas:

Lemma 1.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a regular point.

Then all kernel atoms of
p
intersect, or in symbols:

\varnothing\lessdot A,A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)\quad\text{implies}\quad A\between A^{\prime}.

Proof.

By construction in Definition
1.2
(
1
)
A,A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.
By regularity (Definition
1.4
(
3
))
\mathit{K}(p)
is transitive.
Then
A\between\mathit{K}(p)\between A^{\prime}
and by transitivity (Definition
2.1
) it follows that
A\between A^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 1.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a
topology
— thus: a semitopology whose open sets are closed under intersections — and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.
Then one of the following holds:

•

\mathit{ker}(p)=\varnothing
.

•

\mathit{ker}(p)=A
for some atom
\varnothing\lessdot A
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
, and suppose there exist two atoms
A,A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
.
Then (just as already noted in Remark
5.9
)
A\cap A^{\prime}
is an open set.
It is not empty because
A\between A^{\prime}
by Lemma
1.4
.
By minimality,
A=A\cap A^{\prime}=A^{\prime}
.
Thus, being a topology crushes Definition
1.2
down to be at most a single atom.
∎

2
Characterisations of the kernel

We open with a non-implication:

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular (so
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
).
Then it is not necessarily the case that
\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
.

Proof.

A counterexample is Example
1.3
(
5
).
In full: we consider
\mathbb{N}
with the semitopology generated by
n_{\geq}=\{n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\mid n^{\prime}\geq n\}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then
n_{\between}=\mathbb{N}=\mathit{K}(n)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, but
\mathit{ker}(n)=\varnothing
because there is no minimal nonempty open set.
∎

We can exclude the case noted in the proof of Lemma
2.1
by restricting to strongly chain-complete semitopologies.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology — in particular, this holds if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is finite by Proposition
4.5
— and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

\mathit{K}(p)=\varnothing
if and only if
\mathit{ker}(p)=\varnothing
, and equivalently
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
if and only if
\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
.

In words:
p
has a nonempty community if and only if it has a nonempty kernel.

2.

If
p
is regular then
\mathit{ker}(p)
is a topen subset of
\mathit{K}(p)
(nonempty transitive and open, see Definition
2.1
(
2
)).

(See also Lemma
2.5
, which proves a stronger version of this property for the kernel atoms of a regular
p
.)

Proof.

1.

Suppose
\varnothing\neq\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
.
Then by Corollary
5.8
(since
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is strongly chain-complete)
\mathit{K}(p)
contains at least one atom
A
, which is a subset of
\mathit{K}(p)
by construction, and so
A\in\mathit{ker}(p)
.

Conversely, if there exists an atom
A\in\mathit{ker}(p)
then (since an atom is by assumption a nonempty set) we have
\varnothing\neq A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.

2.

Suppose
p
is regular.
Unpacking Definition
1.4
(
3
) this means that
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
Thus in particular
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
, and by part 1 of this result
\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
.

So
\mathit{ker}(p)
is a nonempty subset of
\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Theorem
2.6

\mathit{K}(p)
is a (maximal) topen, and by Lemma
4.2
(2)
\varnothing\neq\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
is topen as required.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

Note in Lemma
2.2
(
2
) that
\mathit{ker}(p)
need not be a topen neighbourhood of
p
, simply because
p
(even if it is regular) might generate a topen community
\mathit{K}(p)
but need not necessarily be in an atom in that community. See Example
1.3
(
4
) taking
p=0
or
p={\shortminus}1
, or Lemma
2.4
(3).

We complement Lemma
2.2
with some non-implications:

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
does not imply that
p
is regular.

2.

\mathit{ker}(p)
topen does not imply that
p
is regular.

Proof.

1.

See Example
4.1
(2):
then
\mathit{ker}(1)=\{0,2\}\neq\varnothing
but (as noted in Lemma
4.2
)
1
is not regular.

2.

See Example
4.1
(
3
): so
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3,4\}
and
\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{1,2\}
,
\{0,1,3\}
,
\{0,2,4\}
,
\{3\}
, and
\{4\}
and
\mathit{ker}(0)=\mathit{K}(0)=\{1,2\}
, and this is (nonempty and) topen, but
0
is not regular since
0\not\in\mathit{K}(0)=\{1,2\}
.

3.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1\}
and set
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\{0\},\{0,1\}\}
.
Then
1
is regular but
1\not\in\mathit{ker}(p)=\{0\}
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a regular point and
A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

A
is a kernel atom of
p
(
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
).

2.

A
is a minimal topen in
\mathit{K}(p)
.

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
A
is a kernel atom of
p
.
By assumption in Definition
1.2
(
2
) it is an atom (a minimal nonempty open set) in
\mathit{K}(p)
, and by Lemma
4.2
(2) it is topen; so it is necessarily a minimal topen.

•

Suppose
A
is a minimal topen in
\mathit{K}(p)
and suppose
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
is any nonempty open set.
By Lemma
4.2
(2)
A^{\prime}
is topen, so by minimality
A=A^{\prime}
.
Thus,
A
is an atom in
\mathit{K}(p)
, and so is a kernel atom of
p
.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

The proof of Lemma
2.5
above is elementary given our results so far, but it makes a useful observation.
Recall from Theorem
2.6
that if
p
is regular (so
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
) then
\mathit{K}(p)
is a maximal topen, and recall from Definition
1.2
that a kernel atom is an atom (i.e. a minimal nonempty open set) in
\mathit{K}(p)
.
So we can read Lemma
2.5
as follows:

A kernel atom is a minimal topen inside a maximal topen.

Thus for regular
p
,
\mathit{ker}(p)
and
\mathit{K}(p)
are in some sense dual: the community of
p
is the maximal topen containing
p
, and the kernel of
p
is the union of the minimal topens inside that maximal topen.

So Lemma
2.5
tells us that for regular
p
, the kernel atoms of
p
are the minimal topens that are subsets of the community of
p
.
Proposition
2.7
strengthens this to show that in fact, the kernel atoms of regular
p
are also the minimal topens that even
intersect
with the community of
p
(the significance of this to distributed consensus is discussed in Remark
5.1
):

Proposition 2.7
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
1
1

1

We do not seem to need
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to be (strongly) chain-complete here. This is simply because we normally use strong chain-completeness to ensure that atoms and open covers exist, but in this result this is assumed.

•

A\in\mathit{Atoms}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is an atom.

•

p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a regular point (so by Definition
1.4
(
1
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
).

•

O\in\mathsf{Topen}
and
O\between\mathit{K}(p)
, so
O
is any topen set that intersects the community of
p
(at least one such exists, by regularity, namely
\mathit{K}(p)
itself).
2
2

2

By Proposition
3.1
, this is equivalent to
O\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
. We use
O\between\mathit{K}(p)
because it yields a stronger form of the result.

Then the following are all equivalent:

1.

A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
.

In words:
A
is a kernel atom of
p
.

2.

A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.

In words:
A
is an atom in the community of
p
.

3.

A
is topen and
A\between O
.

In words:
A
is topen and intersects
O
.

In particular, if
A
is a topen atom
3
3

3

An atom is a minimal nonempty open set, and a topen is a nonempty open transitive set; so saying ‘topen atom’ is just the same as saying ‘transitive atom’.
then we have:

A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad A\between O\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad A\between\mathit{ker}(p)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad A\between\mathit{K}(p).

Proof.

We consider a cycle of implications:

•

Suppose
A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
.
By construction in Definition
1.2
(
1
)
\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
, so
A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.

•

Suppose
A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Definition
5.6
(1) (since
A
is an atom)
A
is nonempty.
Then
A\between\mathit{K}(p)\between O
.
By regularity
\mathit{K}(p)
is topen, so by transitivity (Definition
2.1
)
A\between O
as required.

•

Suppose
A
is topen and
A\between O
.
By assumption
A\between O\between\mathit{K}(p)
so by transitivity of
O
,
A\between\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Proposition
3.1

A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
and it follows from Definition
1.2
(
2
) that
A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
as required.

The equivalence

A\between O\Longleftrightarrow A\between\mathit{ker}(p)\iff A\between\mathit{K}(p)

then follows routinely from the above, noting the equivalence
A\between O\Longleftrightarrow A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
and choosing
O=\mathit{ker}(p)
or
O=\mathit{K}(p)
.
∎

Corollary 2.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
O\in\mathsf{Topen}
and
p\in O
(so
p
is regular and
O
is some topen neighbourhood of
p
).
Then

\mathit{ker}(p)=\bigcup\{A{\in}\mathsf{Topen}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\mid\varnothing\lessdot A\between O\}.

In words: for any topen neighbourhood
O
of
p
,
\mathit{ker}(p)
is equal to the union of the topen atoms that intersect that neighbourhood.

Proof.

Unpacking Definition
1.2
(
1
),
\mathit{ker}(p)
is the union of atoms
A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.
We use Proposition
2.7
.
∎

Lemma
2.9
explicitly characterises the union of all kernels as the union of all transitive atoms, which (given the results above) is what one might expect:

Lemma 2.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

If
A\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a transitive atom then
A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
for every
p\in A
.

In words we can say: a transitive atom is a kernel atom for any points that it contains.

2.

\mathit{ker}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is the union of the transitive atoms in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

1.

If
p\in A\in\mathsf{Topen}
then
A
is a topen neighbourhood for
p
.
By Theorem
2.6

p\in A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.
But then by construction
A
is an atom in
\mathit{K}(p)
so by Definition
1.2
(
1
)
A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
.

2.

It follows from Lemma
2.5
and Definition
1.2
(
3
) that every atom in
\mathit{ker}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is (topen and so) transitive.
Conversely by part 1 of this result every transitive atom is in the kernel of the community of its points.
∎

3
Further properties of kernels

1
Intersections between the kernel of
p
and its open neighbourhoods

Lemma
3.1
is quite easy to prove by following definitions and applying transitivity properties, but it makes a useful point:

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.

•

p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a regular point.

•

A
is a kernel atom for
p
. In symbols:
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
.

Then

\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\Longrightarrow A\between O.

In words:

If
p
is regular then every open neighbourhood of
p
intersects every kernel atom of
p
.
4
4

4

This property is a bit subtle, because it is not necessarily the case that
p\in\mathit{ker}(p)
(cf. Remark
2.3
). So a kernel atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
is is not itself necessarily a neighbourhood of
p
, but it still has a property of ‘oversight’ over
p
in the sense that it intersects with every quorum (open neighbourhood) that
p
has.

Proof.

By our assumption that
p
is regular we have
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
(Definition
1.4
(
3
)), and we assumed
p\in O
, so
O\between\mathit{K}(p)
.

Also by assumption
A\between\mathit{K}(p)
, since
A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
by Definition
1.2
(
2
&
1
).
Thus
O\between\mathit{K}(p)\between A
.
Now
\mathit{K}(p)
is topen, thus it is transitive (Definition
2.1
(
1
)) and so
O\between A
as required.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p
is regular and
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then:

1.

The kernel of
p
is a subset of the union of the atoms intersecting
O
.
In symbols:
