\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\bigcup\{A{\in}\mathsf{Open}\mid\varnothing\lessdot A\between O\}=\bigcup\{A{\in}\mathit{Atoms}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\mid A\between O\}.

2.

The inclusion may be strict, even if
O
is an open cover of
p
(in symbols:
O\gtrdot p
).

3.

The inclusion may be strict, even if
O\gtrdot p
is a topen (transitive open) cover of
p
.

4.

If
O
is a topen cover of
p
, then the kernel of
p
is precisely equal to the union of the transitive atoms intersecting
O
.
In symbols:

\mathit{ker}(p)=\bigcup\{A{\in}\mathsf{Topen}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\mid\varnothing\lessdot A\between O\}.

Proof.

1.

For the inclusion we just combine Lemma
3.1
with Definition
1.2
(
1
).

2.

To see how the inclusion may be strict, see Example
3.3
(1).

3.

To see how the inclusion may be strict, even for transitive
O
, see Example
3.3
(2).

4.

This just repeats Corollary
2.8
.
∎

Example 3.3
.

1.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and let opens be generated by
\{0,1\}
and
\{0,2\}
and
\{1,2\}
and
\{2\}
, as illustrated in Figure
2
(left-hand diagram).

Set
p=1
and
O=\{1,2\}
.
Then we can calculate that:

•

0
,
1
, and
2
are all regular.

•

The community and kernel of
1
and
0
are equal to
\{0,1\}
—
2
is not intertwined with
0
or
1
because
\{2\}\cap\{0,1\}=\varnothing
.

•

The community and kernel of
2
are equal to
\{2\}
.

•

\{1,2\}
is an open cover of
1
.

•

The union of the atoms that intersect with
\{1,2\}
is the whole space
\{0,1,2\}
.

Thus
\mathit{ker}(1)=\{0,1\}\subsetneq\bigcup\{A{\in}\mathit{Atoms}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\mid A\between\{1,2\}\}=\{0,1\}\cup\{2\}=\{0,1,2\}
.

2.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3\}
and let opens be generated by
\{0,1\}
and
\{1,2\}
and
\{2,3\}
, as illustrated in Figure
2
(right-hand diagram).

Set
p=1
and
O=\{0,1\}
.
Then we can calculate that:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}
splits into two disjoint topen sets:
\{0,1\}
and
\{2,3\}
.
So
O
is topen.

•

The community and kernel of
0
and
1
are equal to
\{0,1\}
—
2
is not intertwined with
0
or
1
because
\{2,3\}\cap\{0,1\}=\varnothing
.
So
\{1,2\}
is an atom, but it is not transitive.

•

The community and kernel of
2
and
3
are equal to
\{2,3\}
.

•

\{0,1\}
is an open cover of
1
.

Thus
\mathit{ker}(1)=\{0,1\}\subsetneq\bigcup\{A{\in}\mathit{Atoms}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\mid A\between\{0,1\}\}=\{0,1\}\cup\{1,2\}=\{0,1,2\}
.

:

3.3

Remark 3.4

(Algorithmic content of Proposition
3.2
)
.

Proposition
3.2

reduces the problem of computing kernels to the problem of identifying transitive sets.
5
5

5

We considered
that
question in results including Proposition
4.10
and Theorem
6.2
.

Once we have this, an algorithm for computing
\mathit{ker}(p)
for regular
p
follows:

•

Compute a transitive open neighbourhood
O
of
p
— for example using the algorithm outlined in
Remark
4.6
to compute open neighbourhoods of
p
, and testing until we find one that is transitive.
At least one transitive cover of
p
exists, by our assumption that
p
is regular.

•

For each
p^{\prime}\in O
, compute all the atoms that contain
p^{\prime}
— for example by computing the open neighbourhoods of
p^{\prime}
and checking which are atoms, and are transitive.

By Proposition
3.2
(4), this collection of transitive atoms that intersect with
O
, will return the kernel atoms of
p
.

We conclude by noting a non-result:

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.
Recall from Theorem
6.2
that
\mathit{K}(p)
the community of
p
is the greatest transitive open neighbourhood of
p
, so that any
transitive
open neighbourhood of
p
is contained in the community of
p
.

However, there may still exist a non-transitive open cover of
p
that is not contained in the community of
p
.

Proof.

It suffices to provide a counterexample, and as it happens we have just considered one.
Consider Example
3.3
(2), as illustrated in Figure
2
(right-hand diagram).
Then
1_{\between}=\mathit{K}(1)=\{0,1\}
and
\{1,2\}
is an open cover of
1
and
\{1,2\}\not\subseteq\{0,1\}
.
∎

2
Idempotence properties of the kernel and community

Remark 3.6
.

In Definitions
1.4
(
2
) and
1.2
(
3
) we extend the notions of community and kernel of a set of points, using sets union.
This allows us to take the community of a community
\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))
, then kernel of a kernel
\mathit{ker}(\mathit{ker}(p))
, and so forth.
Does doing this add any information?
One would hope not — but this needs checked.

In this Subsection we take check this for regular points, and see that they display good behaviour (e.g.: the community of the community is just the community, and so forth).
The proofs also illuminate how regularity condition ensures good behaviour.

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.
Then

\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))=\mathit{K}(p).

Proof.

We prove two subset inclusions:

•

Suppose
q\in\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))
, so unpacking Definition
1.4
(
2
) there exists
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
such that
q\in\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
.
By Corollary
2.8
(since
p
is regular)
\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})=\mathit{K}(p)
, so
q\in\mathit{K}(p)
.

q
was arbitrary, and it follows that
\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.

•

Suppose
q\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
Then by Corollary
2.8
(since
p
is regular)
\mathit{K}(q)=\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
, so in particular
q\in\mathit{K}(q)
.

q
was arbitrary, and it follows that
\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))
.
∎

Corollary 3.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.
Suppose further that
\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
(if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is strongly chain-complete or finite then by Lemma
2.2
(
2
) and Proposition
4.5

\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
is guaranteed).
Then

\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(\mathit{ker}(p)).

Proof.

Suppose
q\in\mathit{K}(p)
and pick any
k\in\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.
Then
k\in\mathit{K}(p)
so by Corollary
2.8

\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(k)
so
q\in\mathit{K}(k)
.
Thus
\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(\mathit{ker}(p))
.

Furthermore
\mathit{K}(\mathit{ker}(p))\subseteq\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))
is a structural fact of Definition
1.4
(
2
) and the fact, noted above, that
\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)
.

We finish with Lemma
3.7
:

\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq\mathit{K}(\mathit{ker}(p))\subseteq\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.double.community}}}{{=}}\mathit{K}(p).\qed

Lemma 3.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.
Then

\mathit{ker}(\mathit{K}(p))=\mathit{ker}(p).

Proof.

Unpacking Definition
1.2
,
\mathit{ker}(\mathit{K}(p))=\bigcup\{\mathit{ker}(p^{\prime})\mid p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)\}
and for each
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
we have
\mathit{ker}(p^{\prime})=\bigcup\{A\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})\}
.
By Corollary
2.8
,
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(p^{\prime})
for every
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
, and threading this equality through the definitions above, we obtain the result.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.
Then

\mathit{ker}(p)=\mathit{ker}(\mathit{ker}(p)).

Proof.

If
\mathit{ker}(p)=\varnothing
then the result is immediate.
So suppose
\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
.
We show two subset inclusions.

•

To prove
\mathit{ker}(p)\subseteq\mathit{ker}(\mathit{ker}(p))
we can reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}\mathit{ker}(\mathit{ker}(p))\subseteq&\mathit{ker}(\mathit{K}(p))&\mathit{ker}(p){\subseteq}\mathit{K}(p),\text{Def.~\ref{defn.kernel}(\ref{item.kernel.P})}\\
=&\mathit{ker}(p)&\text{Lemma~\ref{lemm.kernel.community}}\end{array}

•

To prove
\mathit{ker}(\mathit{ker}(p))\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
we note that a kernel is a union of atoms in Definition
1.2
(
1
), and we reason as follows, for an atom
\varnothing\lessdot A
(which exists because
\mathit{ker}(p)
is a union of atoms and we assumed
\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
):

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)\Longleftrightarrow&A\subseteq\mathit{K}(p)&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.kernel}(\ref{item.kernel})}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&A\subseteq\mathit{K}(\mathit{ker}(p))&\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(\mathit{ker}(p))=\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))\\
\Longleftrightarrow&A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(\mathit{ker}(p))&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.kernel}(\ref{item.kernel})}.\end{array}

Above,
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{K}(\mathit{ker}(p))=\mathit{K}(\mathit{K}(p))
follows from Lemma
3.7
and Corollary
3.8
(since we assumed
\mathit{ker}(p)\neq\varnothing
).
∎

Chapter 10
Dense subsets & continuous extensions

1
Definition and basic properties

Remark 1.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
\varnothing\neq D\subseteq P\in\mathsf{Open}
(
D
need not be open).
The following four standard definitions of what it means for
D
to be
dense
in
P
are equivalent in topology:

1.

Every nonempty open subset of
P
intersects
D
.

2.

The interior of
P\setminus D
is empty.

3.

Every open subset that intersects
P
, intersects
D
.

4.

|D|=|P|
.

We shall see that in semitopologies, these definitions split into two groups.

Definition 1.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
\varnothing\neq D\subseteq P\in\mathsf{Open}
(
D
need not be open).
Then:

1.

Call
D

weakly dense in
P
when

\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}.\varnothing\neq O\subseteq P\Longrightarrow D\between O.

In words:

D
is weakly dense in
P
when every nonempty open subset of
P
intersects
D
.

2.

Call
D

strongly dense in
P
when

\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}.P\between O\Longrightarrow D\between O.

In words:

D
is strongly dense in
P
when every open set that intersects
P
, intersects
D
.
1
1

1

We do not need to explicitly state that
O
is nonempty because if
O
is empty then
O\between P
is false.

3.

If
D
is strongly dense in
P
and
\mathit{interior}(D)\neq\varnothing
then, following Definition
4.1
(
1
), we may call
D
a
strongly dense neighbourhood in
P
.

In a topology, the two notions of being
dense
described in Definition
1.2
above are equivalent.
A semitopology permits richer structure, because we do not insist that intersections of open sets be open, and thus it discriminates more finely between the definitions:

Lemma 1.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
\varnothing\neq D\subseteq P\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then:

1.

If
D
is strongly dense in
P
then
D
is weakly dense in
P
.

2.

In a topology, the reverse implication holds; but

3.

in a semitopology the reverse implication need not hold: it may be that
D
is weakly dense but not strongly dense in
P
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

If a nonempty open set is a subset of
P
then it intersects with
P
.
It follows that if
D
intersects every nonempty open set that intersects
P
, then it certainly intersects every nonempty open set that is a subset of
P
.

2.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a topology and suppose
D
is weakly dense in
P
and
O\between P
.
Then
\varnothing\neq O\cap P\between P
, and because (this being a topology)
O\cap P\in\mathsf{Open}
we have that
O\cap P\between D
and so
O\between D
as required.

3.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Consider the top-right semitopology in Figure
1
and take
D=\{0\}
and
P=\{0,1\}
.
Then
D
is weakly dense in
P
(because
D
intersects
\{0\}
and
\{0,1\}
) but
D
is not strongly dense in
P
(because
D
does not intersect
\{1,2\}
).
∎

We can rearrange the definitions to obtain more abstract characterisations of weakly and strongly dense:

Proposition 1.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
\varnothing\neq D\subseteq P\in\mathsf{Open}
(
D
need not be open).
Then:

1.

D
is weakly dense in
P
if and only if
\mathit{interior}(P\setminus D)=\varnothing
.

2.

D
is strongly dense in
P
if and only if
|D|=|P|
.

Proof.

For each part we prove two implications:

1.

\mathit{interior}(P\setminus D)=\varnothing
means precisely that there is no nonempty open subset of
P\setminus D
, i.e. that every nonempty subset of
P
intersects
D
.
But this is just the definition of
D
being weakly dense in
P
from Definition
1.2
(
1
).

2.

Since
D\subseteq P
, also
|D|\subseteq|P|
.

To prove
|P|\subseteq|D|
it suffices to prove
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|D|\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|P|
.
By Corollary
1.10

\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|D|
is the union of the open sets that do not intersect
D
, and
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|P|
is the union of the open sets that do not intersect
P
.
So
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|D|\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|P|
when for every open set
O\in\mathsf{Open}
, if
O
does not intersect
D
then
O
does not intersect
P
.
This is just the contrapositive of the property of
D
being strongly dense in
P
from Definition
1.2
(
2
).
∎

Corollary 1.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology and
\varnothing\neq D\subseteq P\in\mathsf{Open}
(
D
need not be open).
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

D
is weakly dense in
P
.

2.

D
intersects every atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq P
in
P
.

In symbols using Definitions
5.6
and
4.7
we can write:

\text{$D$ weakly dense in $P$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad D\between\mathit{Atoms}(P).

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
D
is weakly dense in
P
.
By Definition
1.2
(
1
) this means that
D
intersects every open
O\subseteq P
.
In particular,
D
intersects every atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq P
.

•

Conversely, suppose
D
intersects every atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq P
and suppose
O\subseteq P
is open.
By Corollary
5.8
(since
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is strongly chain-complete) there exists an atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq O
and by assumption
D\between A
, thus also
D\between O
as required.
∎

2
Dense subsets of topen sets

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
T\in\mathsf{Topen}
and
O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
and
O\subseteq T
.
Then
O
is strongly dense in
T
.

In words: any nonempty open subset of a topen set is strongly dense.

Proof.

Suppose
T\between O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Thus
O\between T\between O^{\prime}
and by transitivity of
T
(since
T
is topen; see Definition
2.1
) we have
O\between O^{\prime}
as required.
∎

Corollary 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
\varnothing\neq D\subseteq T\in\mathsf{Topen}
.
Then
precisely one
of the following holds:

•

D
is weakly dense in
T
.

•

T\setminus D
is a strongly dense neighbourhood in
T
(Definition
1.2
(
3
)).

As a corollary,
precisely one
of the following holds:

•

D
is a strongly dense neighbourhood in
T
.

•

T\setminus D
is weakly dense in
T
.

Proof.

For the first part we reason as follows:

•

If
D
is weakly dense in
T
then by Proposition
1.4
(1)
\mathit{interior}(T\setminus D)=\varnothing
, so following Definition
1.2
(
3
)
T\setminus D
is not a strongly dense neighbourhood.

•

If
D
is not weakly dense in
T
then by Proposition
1.4
(1)
\mathit{interior}(T\setminus D)\neq\varnothing
.
By Lemma
2.1
(since
T
is topen)

\mathit{interior}(T\setminus D)
is strongly dense in
T
, thus so is
T\setminus D
.
It follows from Definition
1.2
(
3
) that
T\setminus D
is a strongly dense neighbourhood in
T
, as required.

For the corollary, write
D=T\setminus D^{\prime}
, so that
D^{\prime}=T\setminus D
.
We use the first part of this result, for
D^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 2.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology and suppose
\varnothing\neq D\subseteq T\in\mathsf{Topen}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

D
is a strongly dense neighbourhood in
T
.

•

\mathit{interior}(D)\neq\varnothing
.

•

D
contains an atom, or in symbols:
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq D
.

Proof.

We prove a cycle of implications:

•

If
D
is a strongly dense neighbourhood in
T
then
\mathit{interior}(D)\neq\varnothing
direct from Definition
1.2
(
3
).

•

If
\mathit{interior}(D)\neq\varnothing
then there exists an atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{interior}(D)\subseteq D
by Corollary
5.8
(since
T
is strongly chain-complete).

•

If
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq D
then using Lemma
2.1
(since
T
is topen)
D
is strongly dense in
T
.
∎

3
Explaining kernels

Notation 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
\varnothing\neq D\subseteq P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

Call
D

minimally weakly dense in
P
when:

•

D
is weakly dense in
P
, and

•

if
\varnothing\neq D^{\prime}\subseteq D
and
D^{\prime}
is weakly dense in
P
, then
D^{\prime}=D
.

2.

Call
D
a
minimally strongly dense open subset of
P
when:

•

D\in\mathsf{Open}
,

•

D
is a strongly dense subset of
P
, and

•

if
\varnothing\neq D^{\prime}\subseteq D
and
D^{\prime}
is a strongly dense open subset of
P
, then
D^{\prime}=D
.

Recall from Definition
5.6
(
2
) that
\mathit{Atoms}(P)=\{A\in\mathsf{Open}\mid\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq P\}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology and
P\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then:

1.

\bigcup\mathit{Atoms}(P)
is equal to the sets union of the minimal weakly dense subsets of
P
.
2
2

2

This sentence is potentially confusing, because
\bigcup\mathit{Atoms}(P)
is itself a sets union of the atoms in
P
. So what is being asserted is that the sets union of the atoms in
P
, is equal to the sets union of the minimal weakly dense subsets of
P
. However, these minimal weakly dense subsets are not necessarily atoms, and the atoms are not necessarily minimal weakly dense subsets.

2.

If furthermore
P
is transitive (so that
P\in\mathsf{Topen}
) then
\bigcup\mathit{Atoms}(P)
is equal to the sets union of the minimal strongly dense neighbourhoods in
P
.

3.

If
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular then
\mathit{ker}(p)
is equal to the union of the minimal weakly dense subsets of
\mathit{K}(p)
and also to the union of the minimal strongly dense neighbourhoods in
\mathit{K}(p)
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

For brevity, write
\mathcal{A}=\mathit{Atoms}(P)
.
By Corollary
1.5
(since
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is strongly chain-complete), if
D
is weakly dense in
P
then so is
D\cap\bigcup\mathcal{A}
.
Thus, the
minimal
weakly dense subsets of
P
are all contained in
\bigcup\mathcal{A}
.

It remains to show that every
p\in S
is contained in
some
minimal weakly dense subset of
P
.
Fix one such
p
.
We need some notation: if
X\subseteq P
then write
{\langle X\rangle}_{P}
for the union of the set of atoms in
P
that intersect
X
.
We now argue transfinitely to generate a minimal weakly dense
D\subseteq P
that contains
p
:

•

Set
p_{0}=p
and set
A_{0}
to be some atom in
P
that contains
p
; one such exists, since we chose
p\in\bigcup\mathcal{A}
.

•

Suppose
p_{\alpha^{\prime}}
and
A_{\alpha^{\prime}}
are defined for all
\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha
.
If
\bigcup\mathcal{A}\subseteq{\langle\{p_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mid\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha\}\rangle}_{P}
— so that
\bigcup\mathcal{A}={\langle\{p_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mid\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha\}\rangle}_{P}
— then stop.
Otherwise, pick an atom
A_{\alpha}
of
P
that is not a subset, and choose some
p_{\alpha}\in A_{\alpha}\setminus{\langle\{p_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mid\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha\}\rangle}_{P}
.

Note that
A_{\alpha}\subseteq{\langle\{p_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mid\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha\}\rangle}_{P}
.

•

Continue transfinitely until we stop, and set
D=\{p_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mid\alpha^{\prime}<\alpha\}
.
By construction,
{\langle D\rangle}_{P}=\bigcup\mathcal{A}
.

Then
D
is weakly dense in
P
by Corollary
1.5
, and it is minimal because if we remove any
p_{\alpha^{\prime}}
from
D
to obtain a smaller
D^{\prime}=D\setminus\{p_{\alpha^{\prime}}\}
then by construction
A_{\alpha^{\prime}}\not\subseteq{\langle D^{\prime}\rangle}_{P}
.

2.

It follows from Corollary
2.3
that
D
is a minimal strongly dense neighbourhood in
P
precisely when
D
is equal to an atom in
P
.
The result follows.

3.

By Definition
1.4
(
3
)
\mathit{K}(p)
is transitive.
By Definition
1.2
(
1
)
\mathit{ker}(p)=\bigcup\mathit{Atoms}(\mathit{K}(p))
.
We use parts 1 and 2 of this result.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Proposition
3.2
(3) gives some independent explanation for why
\mathit{ker}(p)
— the atoms in the community of
p
, as studied in Section
9
— is interesting.

\mathit{ker}(p)
identifies where the minimal weakly dense and strongly dense subsets of
\mathit{K}(p)
are located.

4
Unifying is-transitive and is-strongly-dense-in

It turns out that transitivity and denseness are closely related:
in this Subsection we explore their relationship.

Remark 4.1
.

Consider the following three notions:

1.

D
is strongly dense in
P
from Definition
1.2
(
2
).

2.

P
is transitive
from Definition
2.1
(
1
).

3.

P
is strongly transitive
from Definition
7.5
(
1
).

Notice that while the definitions are different, they share a ‘family resemblance’.
Can we identify a common ancestor for them; some definition that naturally subsumes them into a most general principle?

Yes: it is easy to see that item 1 and 3 above are very closely related — see Lemma
4.2
— and then we will prove that all three definitions listed above are special instances of a general definition — see Definition
4.3
and Proposition
4.6
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:
3
3

3

Cf. also Lemma
7.10
.

•

P
is strongly transitive.

•

O\cap P
is strongly dense in
P
, for every
O{\in}\mathsf{Open}
such that
O\between P
(meaning that
O\cap P\neq\varnothing
).

In words we can say:

P
is strongly transitive when every nontrivial open intersection with
P
is strongly dense.

Proof.

Unpacking Definition
7.5
(
1
),
P
is strongly transitive when
O\between P\between O^{\prime}
implies
O\cap P\between O^{\prime}\cap P
.
Unpacking Definition
1.2
(
2
),
O\cap P
is strongly dense in
P
when
P\between O^{\prime}
implies
O\cap P\between O^{\prime}
— and this is clearly equivalent to
O\cap P\between O^{\prime}\cap P
.
The result now follows by routine reasoning.
∎

We can generalise the notion of strongly dense from Definition
1.2
(
2
) from
D\subseteq P
to
any

D
.

Definition 4.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
D,P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Call
D

strongly dense for
P
when

\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.P\between O\Longrightarrow D\between O.

We state the obvious:

Lemma 4.4
.

\varnothing\neq D\subseteq P
is strongly dense
in

P
in the sense of Definition
1.2
(
2
) if and only if it is strongly dense
for

P
in the sense of Definition
4.3
.

Proof.

The definitions are identical where they overlap.
The only difference is that Definition
1.2
(
2
) assumes a nonempty subset of
P
, whereas Definition
4.3
assumes a nonempty set that intersects (but is not necessarily a subset of)
P
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
D,P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

D
is strongly dense for
P
.

•

|P|\subseteq|D|
.
4
4

4

Compare with Proposition
3.2
.

Proof.

Suppose
D
is strongly dense for
P
.
Then
O\in\mathsf{Open}
does not intersect with
P
if and only if
O
does not intersect with
D
, and it follows (just as in the proof of Proposition
1.4
(2)) that
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|D|\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|P|
, and so that
|P|\subseteq|D|
.

Conversely, if
|P|\subseteq|D|
then
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|D|\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|P|
and it follows that if
O\in\mathsf{Open}
does not intersect with
P
then
O
does not intersect with
D
, and thus that
D
is strongly dense for
P
.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
T\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

T
is transitive.

•

O
is strongly dense for
T
, for every
T\between O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
5
5

5

Compare with Lemma
4.2
.

Proof.

We unpack Definition
4.3
and see that a condition that
T
is transitive with respect to every
T\between O\in\mathsf{Open}
is precisely what Definition
2.1
(
1
) asserts, namely: for every
O\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
O\between T
,

\forall O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.T\between O^{\prime}\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}.\qed

Remark 4.7
.

In topology it makes less sense to talk about
D
being dense in
P
for
D\not\subseteq P
, since we can just consider
D\cap P
— and if
D
and
P
are open then so is
D\cap P
.
In semitopology the following happens:

•

The notion of
dense in
splits into two distinct concepts (
weakly dense in
and
strongly dense in
), as we saw in Definition
1.2
and the subsequent discussion.

•

The notion of
strongly dense in
generalises to a notion that we call
strongly dense for
, which has the same definition but just weakens a precondition that
D\subseteq P
.

Given the above, we then see from Lemma
4.2
and Proposition
4.6

that the notions of
transitive
and
strongly transitive
from Definitions
2.1
(
1
) and
7.5
(
1
) lend themselves to being naturally expressed in terms of strongly-dense-for.

5
Towards a continuous extension result

Remark 5.1
.

Topology has a family of results on
continuous extensions
of functions: a nice historical survey is in
[
Gut22
]
.
Here is an example, adapted from
[
Erd18
, Theorem 24.1.15]
:
6
6

6

Available online (
permalink
).

Suppose
f:B\to\mathbb{R}
is uniformly continuous and suppose
B
is a dense subset of
A
.
Then
f
has a unique extension to a continuous function
g:A\to\mathbb{R}
.

This is true in the world of topologies: but what might correspond to this in the semitopological world?

A direct translation to semitopologies seems unlikely,
7
7

7

Except trivially that we can restrict to those semitopologies that are also topologies (i.e. for which intersections of open sets are open).

because we have seen from Definition
1.2
and Lemma
1.3
and the subsequent discussion and results how the notion of ‘is dense in’ behaves differently for semitopologies in general, so that the very premise of the topological result above is now up for interpretation.
8
8

8

There are other differences. For instance we care a lot in this paper about value assignments — maps to discrete semitopologies — rather than maps to
\mathbb{R}
.
Of course we could try to generalise from value assignments to more general examples, but as we shall see, even this ‘simple’ case of value assignments is more than rich enough to raise some canonical questions.

But, the impossibility of a direct translation only opens up an even more interesting question: whether we can find definitions and well-behavedness conditions on semitopological spaces that reflect the spirit of the corresponding topological results, but without assuming that intersections of open sets are open.

We shall see that this is possible and we propose a suitable result below in Definition
5.2
.
However, before we come to that, we will sketch a design space of
failing
definitions and counterexamples — and so put our working definition in its proper design context.

We map to semitopologies of values, so (the spirit of) uniform continuity is automatic, and we concentrate (to begin with) on being strongly dense in rather than weakly dense in, since by Lemma
1.3
(1) the former implies the latter:

1.

Candidate definition 1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment that is continuous on
D\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
D
is a strongly dense subset of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
f
has a unique extension to a continuous function
g:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
.

This does not work:

•

Take
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to be the top-left example in Figure
1
and

•

\mathsf{Val}=\{0,1\}
with the discrete semitopology.

•

Define
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
such that
f(0)=0
and
f(1)=1
and
f(2)=1
and

•

set
D=\{0,2\}
and
P=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Note that
D
is a strongly dense open subset of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and
f
is continuous on
D
.

However,
f
cannot be continuously extended to a
g
that is continuous at
1
.
We note that
1
is conflicted and intertwined with two distinct topens,
\{0\}
and
\{1\}
.
Looking at this example we see that Candidate definition 1 is unreasonable:
of course
we cannot extend
f
continuously to
1
, because
1
is intertwined with two distinct topen sets on which
f
takes distinct values.
The natural solution is just to exclude conflicted points since they may be, as the terminology suggests, conflicted:

2.

Candidate definition 2.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment that is continuous on
D\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
D
is a strongly dense subset of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
f
has a unique extension to a function
g:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
that is continuous at all
unconflicted
points.

This does not work:

•

Take
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to be the semitopology in Figure
1
and

•

\mathsf{Val}=\{0,1,2\}
with the discrete semitopology.

•

Define
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
such that
f(0)=0
and
f(1)=1
and
f(2)=2
and
f(\ast)=0
and

•

set
D=\{0,1,2\}
and
P=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Note that
D
is a strongly dense open subset of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
f
is continuous on
D
.

Note that
\ast
is unconflicted (because
\ast_{\between}=\{\ast,1\}
).
However,
f
cannot be continuously extended to a
g
that is continuous at
\ast
.

3.

Candidate definition 2 is even more telling than it might appear.
Note that
\ast
is unconflicted and quasiregular (Definition
1.4
(
5
)), because
\mathit{K}(\ast)=\{1\}\neq\varnothing
.

Thus we could not even rescue Candidate definition 2 above by insisting that points be not only unconflicted but also quasiregular (i.e. unconflicted and having a nonempty community).
The next natural step up from this is to be unconflicted and weakly regular, which by Corollary
4.13
leads us to regular points.

We can now state a definition and result that work:

Definition 5.2
.

Suppose
f,g:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to\mathsf{Val}
are value assignments (Definition
1.3
(2)) and suppose
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

Say that
g

continuously extends

f
to regular points in
P
when:

•

If
f
is continuous at
p\in P
then
f(p)=g(p)
.

•

g
is continuous on every regular
p\in P
(Definition
1.4
(
3
)).

2.

Say that
g
is a
unique continuous extension
of
f
to regular points in
P
when for any other continuous extension
g^{\prime}
of
f
to
P
, we have
g(p)=g^{\prime}(p)
for every regular
p\in P
.

Remark 5.3

(Justification for regular points)
.

Note that ‘continuously extends’ and ‘uniquely’ in Definition
5.2
both apply to to
regular
points in
P
only.
By the examples in Remark
5.1
it would not be reasonable to expect unique continuous extensions on non-regular points.
This gives a retrospective justification for the theories of topens and regular points that we develop in this paper (see Definitions
2.1
and
1.4
): regularity arises as a natural condition for a semitopological continuous extension result.
9
9

9

This is not an exclusive claim. Other reasonable conditions or generalisations might also exist, for example the
hypertwined
points discussed starting from Definition
6.6
(
3
). But being hypertwined is a more complex notion, and note by Remark
6.20
that intertwined and hypertwined amount to the same thing on regular points. So it seems likely that Definition
5.2
is a canonical point in the design space. Investigating this further is future work.

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment and suppose
D,P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
f
is continuous on
D
then
f
can be continuously extended to all regular points in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

2.

If
D
is strongly dense for
P
(Definition
4.3
) then this extension is unique on
P
in the sense of Definition
5.2
(2).

Proof.

Choose some fixed but arbitrary
default value

v\in\mathsf{Val}
and for this choice of
v
define
g
by cases as follows:

•

Suppose
f
is continuous at
p
.

We set
g(p)=f(p)
.

•

Suppose
f
is not continuous at
p
and
\mathit{K}(p)\between D
.

Choose some
d\in\mathit{K}(p)\cap D
and set
g(p)=f(d)
.

If
p
is intertwined with two points
d
and
d^{\prime}
then (because
p
is regular and so unconflicted by Theorem
2.2
)
d\mathrel{\between}d^{\prime}
and their open neighbourhoods of continuity intersect, so that
f(d)=f(d^{\prime})
.

•

Suppose
f
is not continuous at
p
and
\mathit{K}(p)\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}D
.

If
D
is strongly dense for
P
then this case cannot happen because
P\between\mathit{K}(p)
so by the strong dense property
D\between\mathit{K}(p)
(see Definition
1.2
(
2
)).

Otherwise, we set
g(p)=v
(so
g(p)
is the fixed but arbitrary default value).

We now show that if
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular, then
g
is continuous at
p
.
The proof is again by cases:

•

If
f
is continuous at
p
then
g(p)=f(p)
and so
g
is continuous at
p
.

•

If
f
is not continous at
p
and
d\in\mathit{K}(p)\cap D
then
g(p)=f(d)
.
Thus (since we assumed that
p
is regular)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq g^{\text{-}1}(g(p))
, so that
g
is continuous at
p
.

•

If
f
is not continous at
p
and
\mathit{K}(p)\cap D=\varnothing
then
g(p)=v
.
Thus (since we assumed that
p
is regular)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\subseteq g^{\text{-}1}(v)
, so that
g
is continuous at
p
.

If
D
is strongly dense for
P
then uniqueness follows by routine reasoning from the above, using Theorem
2.3
.
∎

In view of Lemma
4.5
we can more succinctly rephrase Proposition
5.4
as follows:

Corollary 5.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment.
Then if
f
is continuous on
D\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, then
f
can be continuously extended to all regular points in
|D|
.

Proof.

Direct from Proposition
5.4
taking
P=|D|
and using Lemma
4.5
.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

1.

There are a few subtleties to Corollary
5.5
.
The result actually tells us that there exists an open set
O\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
D\subseteq|D|\subseteq O
, and
f
continuously extends to some
g:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
that is continuous at
O
.
This is because if
g
is continuous at
p\in|D|
, then it is by definition continuous on some open neighbourhood of
p
.

2.

Similarly, the condition that
f
be continuous on
D
is equivalent to insisting that
f
be continuous on an
open

D
.

3.

The condition of
D
being strongly dense in
P
is required for uniqueness in Proposition
5.4
(2).
Being weakly dense is not enough.
For, consider the semitopology illustrated in Figure
1
, such that:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3\}
.

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
D=\{0\}
,
\{0,1\}
,
P=\{0,1,2\}
, and
\{2,3\}
.

Then we have
D\subseteq P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and we even have that
D,P\in\mathsf{Open}
and every point in the space is regular, making this is a particularly well-behaved example.
This semitopology is
not
is a topology, because
P\cap\{2,3\}\not\in\mathsf{Open}
; we will exploit this fact in a moment.
The reader can check that

•

D
is weakly dense in
P
(because
D
intersects every open wholly contained in
P
) but

•

D
is not strongly dense in
P
(because
D
does not intersect
\{2,3\}
), and

the value assignment
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathbb{B}
mapping
0
to
\bot
and every other point to
\top
has two continuous extensions to all of
P
:
g
mapping all points to
\bot
, and
g^{\prime}
mapping
0
and
1
to
\bot
and
2
and
3
to
\top
.

Figure 1
:
A weakly dense subset is not enough for uniqueness (Remark
5.6
(2))

6
Kernels determine values of continuous extensions

Remark 6.1
.

In Subsection
5
we considered continuous extensions in a semitopological context.
We concluded with Corollary
5.5
, which showed how to extend a value assignment
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
that is continuous on some
D
, to a
g
that is continuous on
D
and on the regular points in
|D|
.

We also discussed why this result is designed as it is and why it seems likely to be optimal within a certain design space as outlined in Remark
5.1
.

However, our study of semitopologies is motivated by distributed systems.
This means that we also care about intermediate continuous extensions of
f
; i.e. about
g
that continuously extend
f
but not necessarily on all of
|D|
.

The mathematics in this subsection is in some moral sense a ‘pointwise’ dual to the ‘setwise’ mathematics in Subsection
5
.
Perhaps surprisingly, we shall see that when developed pointwise, the details are different: contrast Theorem
6.7
and Corollary
with Proposition
5.4
and Corollary
5.5
; they are similar, but they are not the same.

Recall that:

•

A point
p
is
regular
when its community (which is the interior of its intertwined points) is a topen neighbourhood of
p
; see Definition
1.4
(
3
).

•

\mathit{ker}(p)
is the union of the kernel atoms of
p
(minimal nonempty open sets in the community of
p
); see Definition
1.2
(
1
).

•

A value assignment
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a mapping from
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to some set of values
\mathsf{Val}
having the discrete semitopology; see Definition
1.3
.

We now consider how the value of
f
on kernel atoms influences the value of
f
at regular points.

Definition 6.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f,g:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
are value assignments (Definition
1.3
).
10
10

10

This definition makes sense for
f
mapping
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to any semitopology
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
, but (for now) we will only care about the case when
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
is a discrete semitopology so that
f
is a value assignment.

1.

Call
f

confident at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
when
f
is continuous on some atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
.

2.

Call
f

unanimous at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
when
f
is continuous on all of
\mathit{K}(p)
.

3.

We generalise Definition
5.2
and write
f\leq g
, and call
g
a
(partial) continuous extension of
f
, when for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, if
f
is continuous at
p
then
g
is continuous at
p
and
f(p)=g(p)
.
11
11

11

Definition
5.2
is interested in a
g
that continuously extends
f
all at once from
D
to
|D|
, which is fine mathematically but less helpful computationally. The definition here refines this concept and is interested in the space of all possible
g
such that
f\leq g
, which more accurately reflects how
g
might be computed, in stages, on a network.

It is routine to check that
\leq
is a partial order (transitive, reflexive, antisymmetric).

Remark 6.3
.

Intuitively,
f
is ‘confident’ at
p
when the value we obtain if we continuously extend
f
to
p
, is already determined.
In the context of a distributed system, it may be that a result has been determined by some part of the system, but not yet fully propagated to the whole system.
12
12

12

For instance:
f
may know the results of an election, but not yet have told point
p
; whereas some
g\geq f
may represent a state in which this result has been correctly propagated to
p
. Similarly, technology allows us to determine the weather tomorrow based on weather data that was collected this morning, but that is not the same thing as knowing what the weather will be: a supercomputer needs to run calculations, and the data needs to be broadcast, and put on a webpage and sent down a cable and rendered to a computer screen, and so on.

We make this formal in Theorem
6.7
(
3
).

We start with an easy lemma:

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a strongly chain-complete semitopology and
f,g:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
are value assignments.
Then:

1.

If
f\leq g
and
f
is confident/unanimous at
p
then
g
is also confident/unanimous at
p
.

2.

If
f
is unanimous at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
then it is confident at
p
.

Proof.

By simple arguments from the definitions:

1.

Just unpacking definitions: if
f
is continuous on some
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
then so is
g
; and if
f
is continuous on
\mathit{K}(p)
then so is
g
.

2.

Suppose
f
is unanimous at
p
, meaning that
f
is continuous on
\mathit{K}(p)
.
Then
f
is also continuous on some kernel atom in
\mathit{K}(p)
— at least one such kernel atom exists by Corollary
5.8
, since
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is strongly chain-complete.
It follows that
f
is confident at
p
as required.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment.
Then:

1.

Suppose
f
is confident at
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

By Definition
6.2

f
is continuous on some kernel atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
.
By Lemma
2.5

A
is transitive, so by Theorem
2.3
(since
f
is continuous on
A
)
f
is constant on
A
, and thus it makes sense to use Notation
2.4
and write
f(A)
to denote the (unique) value of
f
on
A
.

2.

Likewise if
f
is unanimous at
p
then we can sensibly write

f(\mathit{K}(p))
.

3.

Just for this paragraph call
f

doubly confident
at
p
when
f
is continuous on two distinct kernel atoms
\varnothing\lessdot A\neq A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
of
p
.
Suppose
f
is doubly confident at
p
and suppose
p
is regular; so by the previous paragraph
f(A)
and
f(A^{\prime})
are both well-defined.
Now
A\between A^{\prime}
by Lemma
1.4
, so
f(A)=f(A^{\prime})
.

Thus being doubly confident at
p
is the same as just being confident, provided that
p
is regular so that all of its kernel atoms intersect.

Remark
6.5
brings us to a notation:

Definition 6.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
is a value assignment and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a regular point.
Then define
\lim_{p}f
the
limit of
f
at
p
by

\lim_{p}f=f(A)

where
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
is some/any (by Remark
6.5
(3) writing ‘some/any’ makes sense) kernel atom of
p
on which
f
is continuous.
The justification for calling this value the
limit
of
f
at
p
is below in Theorem
6.7
, culminating with part
3
of that result.

Recall that Theorem
2.3
asserted that continuous value assignments are constant on transitive sets.
We can now prove a more general result along the same lines:

Theorem 6.7
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.

•

p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.

•

f,g:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Val}
are value assignments to some set of values
\mathsf{Val}
.

Then:

1.

If
f
is confident at
p
(Definition
6.2
(1)) then

f\leq g\quad\text{implies}\quad\lim_{p}f=\lim_{p}g.

2.

If
f
is confident at
p
and also
f
is continuous at
p
(Definition
2.1
) then

f(p)=\lim_{p}f.

3.

Combining parts 1 and 2 of this result, if
f
is confident at
p
and
g
is continuous at
p
then

f\leq g\quad\text{implies}\quad g(p)=\lim_{p}f.

In words: the limit value of an
f
confident at
p
, is the value of any sufficiently continuous extension of
f
— where ‘sufficiently continuous’ means ‘continuous at
p
’.

4.

As a corollary using Lemma
6.4
, if
f
is unanimous at
p
(Definition
6.2
(2)) and
g
is continuous at
p
, then

f\leq g\quad\text{implies}\quad g(p)=\lim_{p}f=f(\mathit{K}(p)).

Proof.

We reason as follows:

1.

Since
f
is confident at
p
, there exists some kernel atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)

on which
f
is continuous (by Remark
6.5
(3) it does not matter which one).
Since
f\leq g
,
g
is also continuous at
A
.
It follows from Definition
6.6
that
\lim_{p}f=\lim_{p}g
.

2.

Since
f
is confident at
p
,
f
is continuous on some kernel atom
\varnothing\lessdot A\subseteq\mathit{ker}(p)
.
Since
f
is continuous at
p
,
f
is continuous on some open neighbourhood
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
By Lemma
3.1

O\between A
, and using Corollary
2.5
we have that
f(p)=f(O)=f(A)=\lim_{p}f
as required.

3.

Direct from parts 1 and 2 of this result, using Lemma
6.4
(1) to note that
g
is confident at
p
because
f
is and
f\leq g
.

4.

Suppose
f
is unanimous at
p
.
Then by Lemma
6.4

f
is confident at
p
, and we use part
3
of this result.
∎

Notation 6.8
.

Suppose
f
is a function on sets, and
X
is a set.
Recall that we write
f|_{X}
for the function obtained by
restricting
f
to
X
(sothat

(f—_X)=

(f)∩X
).\end{nttn}\par\par\begin{rmrk}We can use Theorem~\ref{thrm.p.kernel.p} to obtain a result that seems to us similar in spirit to Arrow's theorem~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{fey:strpat}{}{}]} from social choice theory, in the sense that $\mathit{ker}(p)$ is identified as a `dictator set' for $\mathit{K}(p)$ (the technical details seem to be different):
\end{rmrk}\par\par\begin{corr}\it Suppose that:
\itemize\itemize@item@$(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})$ is a

\enditemize Then
$$f|_{\mathit{ker}(p)}=f^{\prime}|_{\mathit{ker}(p)}\quad\text{implies}\quad f(p)=f^{\prime}(p).$$In words we can say:
\begin{quote}Confident continuous values at regular points are determined by their kernel.
\end{quote}Note that we assume that $f$ and $f^{\prime}$ are equal on $\mathit{ker}(p)$, but they do not need to be continuous on all of $\mathit{ker}(p)$; they only need to be continuous (and confident) at $p$.

13

If we only had
f|_{O}=f^{\prime}|_{O}
for some open set
O
that intersects the kernel (so
O\between\mathit{ker}(p)
), then the reasoning would break down at this point.
We would still know that
A\between A^{\prime}
but we would not necessarily know that
O\between(A\cap A^{\prime})
so that
f(A)=f(A^{\prime})
and
\lim_{p}f=\lim_{p}f^{\prime}
. (Remember that we have not assumed continuity on all of
\mathit{ker}(p)
, so
f
and
f^{\prime}
might not be constant on
\mathit{ker}(p)
.)

Part 2
Semiframes: algebra and duality

Chapter 11
Semiframes: compatible complete semilattices

1
Complete join-semilattices, and morphisms between them

Remark 1.1

(Setting the scene)
.

We have studied point-set semitopologies; now the challenge is to give an algebraic account of them.

A straightforward reading of the definition of a semitopology in Definition
1.2
is that a semitopology is a complete semilattice (under sets unions) in a powerset, so the algebraic version of this should be just a complete semilattice.
This turns out to be wrong: what we need is a
compatible
complete semilattice, which we call a
semiframe
.
Precise details are in Definition
3.1
.

We proceed in three steps:

1.

Between here and Section
14
we develop a duality between semitopologies and semiframes, which deliberately echoes the classic duality between topologies and frames.

2.

Then in Section
15
we give algebraic versions of the antiseparation properties.

3.

Finally, in Section
16
we (briefly) consider alternative representations, using graphs.

Taken together, this gives a fairly comprehensive algebraic treatment of the material we have seen thus far, complementing the point-set approach taken until now.

Remark 1.2
.

Something amazing will happen below.
We have the compatibility relation
\ast
in Subsection
2
.
This arises naturally in two ways: is is a natural algebraic abstraction of
\between
in semitopologies (sets intersection) which we have used to express well-behavedness properties such as intertwinedness and regularity, but it will also be key to our categorical duality result.

These motivations for
\ast
are independent: the categorical duality does not require regularity, and the regularity properties do not require a duality — and yet when we study both well-behavedness and duality, the same structures emerge.

We recall some (mostly standard) definitions and facts:

Definition 1.3
.

1.

A
poset

(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a set
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
of
elements
and a relation
{\leq}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}\times\bm{\mathsf{X}}
that is transitive, reflexive, and antisymmetric.

2.

A poset
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a
complete join-semilattice
when every
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
(
X
may be empty or equal to all of
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
) has a least upper bound — or
join
—
\bigvee X\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

All semilattices in this paper will be join (rather than meet) semilattices, so we may omit the word ‘join’ and just call this a
complete semilattice
henceforth.

3.

If
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a complete semilattice then we may write

{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}=\bigvee\varnothing.

By the least upper bound property,
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\leq x
for every
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

4.

If
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a complete semilattice then we may write

{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}=\bigvee\bm{\mathsf{X}}.

By the least upper bound property,
x\leq{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
for every
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

Definition 1.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})
and
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
are complete join-semilattices.
Define a
morphism

g:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
to be a function
\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}\to\bm{\mathsf{X}}
that commutes with joins, and sends
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}}
to
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.
That is:

1.

If
X^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
then
g(\bigvee X^{\prime})=\bigvee_{x^{\prime}{\in}\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}}g(x^{\prime})
.

2.

g({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}})={\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.

Remark 1.5
.

In Definition
1.3
(
2
) we insist that
g({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}})={\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
; i.e. we want our notion of morphism to preserve the top element.

This is does not follow from Definition
1.3
(
1
), because
g
need not be surjective onto
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
, so we need to add this as a separate condition.
Contrast with
g({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})={\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}}
, which does follow from Definition
1.3
(
1
), because
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
is the least upper bound of
\varnothing
.

We want
g({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}})={\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
because our intended model is that
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)=(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq)
is the semilattice of open sets of a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, and similarly for
(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})
, and
g
is equal to
f^{\text{-}1}
where
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
is a continuous function.

We recall a standard result:

Lemma 1.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a complete join-semilattice.
Then:

1.

If
x_{1},x_{2}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
then
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
if and only if
x_{1}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}x_{2}=x_{2}
.

2.

If
f:(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})
is a semilattice morphism
(Definition
1.4
)
then
f
is
monotone morphism
: if
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
then
f(x_{1})\leq f(x_{2})
, for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
.
By the definition of a least upper bound, this means precisely that
x_{2}
is a least upper bound for
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
.
It follows that
x_{1}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}x_{2}=x_{2}
.
The converse implication follows just by reversing this reasoning.

2.

Suppose
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
.
By part 1 of this result
x_{1}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}x_{2}=x_{2}
, so
f(x_{1}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}x_{2})=f(x_{2})
.
By Definition
1.4

f(x_{1}){\boldsymbol{\vee}}f(x_{2})=f(x_{2})
.
By part 1 of this result
f(x_{1})\leq f(x_{2})
.
∎

2
The compatibility relation

Definition
2.1
is a simple idea, but so far as we are aware it is novel:

Definition 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a complete semilattice.
A
compatibility relation
{\ast}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}\times\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a relation on
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
such that:

1.

\ast
is
commutative
, so if
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
then

x\ast x^{\prime}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad x^{\prime}\ast x.

2.

\ast
is a
properly reflexive relation
,
1
1

1

‘Properly reflexive’ is a loose riff on terminologies like ‘proper subset of’ or ‘proper ideal of a ring’. We might also call this ‘non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
reflexive’, which is descriptive, but perhaps a bit of a mouthful.

by which we mean

\forall x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}{\setminus}\{{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\}.x\ast x.

Note that it will follow from the axioms of a compatibility relation that
x\ast x\Longleftrightarrow x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
; see Lemma
3.7
(
2
).

3.

\ast
satisfies a
distributive law
, that
if
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
and
X^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
then

x\ast\bigvee X^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow\exists x^{\prime}{\in}X^{\prime}.x\ast x^{\prime}.

Thus we can say:

a compatibility relation
{\ast}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}\times\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a commutative properly reflexive completely distributive relation on
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

When
x\ast x^{\prime}
holds, we may call
x
and
x^{\prime}

compatible elements
.

Remark 2.2
.

The compatibility relation
\ast
is what it is and we will study it in this paper.
But we take a moment to discuss some intuitions, and to put it in the context of some natural generalisations:

1.

We can think of
\ast
as an
abstract intersection
.

It lets us observe whether
x
and
x^{\prime}
intersect — but without having to explicitly represent this intersection as a join
x{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}x^{\prime}
in the semilattice itself.

We call
\ast
a
compatibility relation
following an intuition of
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
as observations, and
x\ast x^{\prime}
holds when there is some possible world at which it is possible to observe
x
and
x^{\prime}
together.
More on this in Example
3.3
.

2.

We can think of
\ast
as a
generalised intersection
; so a semiframe is an instance of a frame with a
generalised
join.

In this paper we concentrate on the case where
x\ast x^{\prime}
measures whether
x
and
x^{\prime}
intersect, but there are other possibilities.
Here are some natural ways to proceed:

(a)

(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a complete join-semilattice and
{\ast}:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}\times\bm{\mathsf{X}})\to\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is any commutative distributive map.
In this paper, we can set
x\ast x^{\prime}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}},{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

(b)

(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a complete join-semilattice and
{\ast}:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}\times\bm{\mathsf{X}})\to\mathbb{N}
is any commutative distributive map.
We think of
x\ast x^{\prime}
as returning the
size
of the intersection of
x
and
x^{\prime}
.

(c)

Any complete join-semilattice
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is of course a (generalised) semiframe by taking
x\ast x^{\prime}=\bigvee\{x^{\prime\prime}\mid x^{\prime\prime}\leq x,\ x^{\prime\prime}\leq x^{\prime}\}
.

(d)

We can generalise further, in more than one direction.
We would take
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
and
(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime})
to be complete join-semilattices and
{\ast}:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}\times\bm{\mathsf{X}})\to\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
to be any commutative distributive map (which generalises the above).
We could also take
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
to be a cocomplete symmetric monoidal category
[
Mac71
, Section VII]
: a category with all colimits and with a (symmetric) monoid action
\ast
that distributes over (commutes with) colimits.

See also Remark
3.7
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a complete semilattice and suppose
{\ast}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}\times\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a compatibility relation on
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then:

1.

\ast
is monotone on both arguments.

That is: if
x_{1}\ast x_{2}
and
x_{1}\leq x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}\leq x_{2}^{\prime}
, then
x_{2}\ast x_{2}^{\prime}
.

2.

If
x_{1},x_{2}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
have a non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
lower bound
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\lneq x\leq x_{1},x_{2}
, then
x_{1}\ast x_{2}
.

In words we can write:
\ast
reflects non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
lower bounds.

3.

The converse implication to part 2 need not hold: it may be that
x_{1}\ast x_{2}
(
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are compatible) but the greatest lower bound of
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}
is
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

We argue much as for Lemma
1.6
(1).
Suppose
x_{1}\ast x_{2}
and
x_{1}\leq x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}\leq x_{2}^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
1.6

x_{1}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}x_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}^{\prime}
and
x_{2}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}x_{2}^{\prime}=x_{2}^{\prime}
.
It follows using distributivity and commutativity (Definition
2.1
(
3
&
1
)) that
x_{1}\ast x_{2}
implies that
(x_{1}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}x_{1}^{\prime})\ast(x_{2}\ast x_{2}^{\prime})
, and thus that
x_{1}^{\prime}\ast x_{2}^{\prime}
as required.

2.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\neq x\leq x_{1},x_{2}
, so
x
is a non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
lower bound.
By assumption
\ast
is properly reflexive (Definition
2.1
(
2
)) so (since
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
)
x\ast x
.
By part 1 of this result it follows that
x_{1}\ast x_{2}
as required.

3.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Define
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
by:

•

\bm{\mathsf{X}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\bot}},0,1,{\boldsymbol{\top}}\}
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\leq 0,1\leq{\boldsymbol{\top}}
and
\neg(0\leq 1)
and
\neg(1\leq 0)
.

•

x\ast x^{\prime}
for every
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\neq x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

We note that
0\ast 1
but the greatest lower bound of
\{0,1\}
is
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
.
∎

3
The definition of a semiframe

Definition 3.1
.

A
semiframe
is a tuple
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
such that

1.

(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a complete semilattice (Definition
1.3
), and

2.

\ast
is a compatibility relation on it (Definition
2.1
).

Slightly abusing terminology, we can say that

semiframe =
compatible complete semilattice
.

Semiframes are new, so far as we know, but they are a natural idea.
We consider some elementary ways to generate examples, starting with arguably the simplest possible instance:

Example 3.2

(The empty semiframe)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.

1.

If
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a singleton set, so that
\bm{\mathsf{X}}=\{\bullet\}
for some element
\bullet
, then we call
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\bullet)
the
empty semiframe
or
singleton semiframe
.

Then necessarily
\bullet={\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}={\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
and
\bullet\leq\bullet
and
\neg(\bullet\ast\bullet)
.

2.

If
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
has more than one element then we call
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\bullet)
a
nonempty semiframe
.
Then necessarily
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\neq{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.

Thus,
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is nonempty if and only if
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\neq{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.
We call a singleton semiframe
empty
, because this corresponds to the semiframe of open sets of the empty topology, which has no points and one open set,
\varnothing
.

Example
3.3
continues Remark
2.2
:

Example 3.3
.

1.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then the reader can check that the
semiframe of open sets

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\subseteq,\between)
is a semiframe.
We will study this example in detail; see Definition
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
.

2.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,{\boldsymbol{\bot}},{\boldsymbol{\top}})
is a bounded lattice with finite meets and all joins.
Then
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe, where
x\ast x^{\prime}
when
x{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}x^{\prime}\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
.

3.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
is a poset with all joins.
Then
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe, where
x\ast x^{\prime}
when
\bigvee\{x^{\prime\prime}\mid x^{\prime\prime}\leq x,\ x^{\prime\prime}\leq x^{\prime}\}\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
.

4.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{X}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\bot}},0,1,{\boldsymbol{\top}}\}
with
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\leq 0\leq{\boldsymbol{\top}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\leq 1\leq{\boldsymbol{\top}}
(so
0
and
1
are incomparable).
There are two possible semiframe structures on this, characterised by choosing
0\ast 1
or
\neg(0\ast 1)
.

5.

See also the semiframes used in Lemmas
2.7
.

Definition
3.4
is just an example of semiframes for now, though we will see much more of it later:

Definition 3.4

(
Semitopology
\to
semiframe)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Define the
semiframe of open sets
\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
(cf. Example
3.3
(
1
))
by:

•

\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
has elements open sets
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.

•

\leq
is subset inclusion.

•

\ast
is
\between
(sets intersection).

We may write

(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)\quad\text{as a synonym for}\quad\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}).

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
is indeed a semiframe.

Proof.

As per Definition
3.1
we must show that
\mathsf{Open}
is a complete semilattice (Definition
1.3
)
and
\between
is a compatibility relation (Definition
2.1
)
— commutative, properly reflexive, and distributive and satisfies a distributive law that if
O\between\bigcup\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
then
O\between O^{\prime}
for some
O^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.
These are all facts of sets.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Definition
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
are the start of our development.
Once we have built more machinery, we will have a pair of translations:

•

Definition
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
go from semitopologies to semiframes.

•

Definition
4.3
and Lemma
4.4
go from semiframes to semitopologies.

These translations are part of a dual pair of functors between categories of semitopologies and semiframes, as described in Definitions
1.1
and
2.1
and Proposition
3.7
.

Semitopologies are (relatively) concrete: we have concrete points and open sets that are sets of points.
Semiframes are more abstract: we have a join-complete semilattice, and a compatibility relation.
The duality we are about to build will show how these two worlds interact and reflect each other.

We conclude with a simple technical lemma:

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe (a complete semilattice with a compatibility relation) and
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then:

1.

\neg(x\ast{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})
and in particular
\neg({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\ast{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})
.

2.

x\ast x
if and only if
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.

3.

x\ast{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
if and only if
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.

4.

{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\ast{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
holds precisely if
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is nonempty (Example
3.2
).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Recall from Definition
1.3
(
3
) that
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}=\bigvee\varnothing
.
By distributivity (Definition
2.1
(
3
))

x\ast{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\Longleftrightarrow\exists x^{\prime}\in\varnothing.x\ast x^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow\bot.

2.

We just combine part
1
of this result with
Definition
2.1
(
2
).

3.

Suppose
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.
Then
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\lneq x\leq x\leq{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
, and by Lemma
2.3
(
2
)
x\ast{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.

Suppose
x={\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.
Then
\neg(x\ast{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})
by combining commutativity of
\ast
(Definition
2.1
(
1
)) with part 1 of this result.

4.

If
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is nonempty then by Example
3.2

{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\neq{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
and so
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\ast{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
holds by part 2 of this result.
However, in the degenerate case that
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
has one element then
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}={\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\ast{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
does not hold.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

We note in passing that a semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
naturally supports a notion of ‘complement’ for an element
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
, given by
x^{c}=\bigvee\{x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid\neg(x^{\prime}\ast x)\}
.
We put ‘complement’ in scare quotes because it does not follow that
\neg(x\ast x^{c})
, nor that
(x^{c})^{c}=x
(the latter equality is unsurprising; the former perhaps is).
For example, in the semiframe illustrated in Figure
1

0^{c}=\bigvee\{1,2,3\}={\boldsymbol{\top}}
and
(0^{c})^{c}={\boldsymbol{\bot}}
.
This and related constructions will be useful later, in Definition
4.2
and Lemma
4.4
.

Chapter 12
Semifilters & abstract points

1
The basic definition, and discussion

Definition 1.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and suppose
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then:

1.

Call
F

prime
when for every
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
,

x{\boldsymbol{\vee}}x^{\prime}\in F\quad\text{implies}\quad x\in F\lor x^{\prime}\in F.

2.

Call
F

completely prime
when for every (possibly empty)
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
,

\bigvee X\in F\quad\text{implies}\quad\exists x{\in}X.x\in F.

(This condition is used in Lemma
3.2
, which is needed for Lemma
4.2
.)

3.

Call
F

up-closed
when
x\in F
and
x\leq x^{\prime}
implies
x^{\prime}\in F
.

4.

Call
F

compatible
when its elements are
pairwise compatible
, by which we mean that
x\ast x^{\prime}
for every
x,x^{\prime}\in F
.

5.

A
semifilter
is a nonempty, up-closed, compatible subset
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

6.

Call
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
a
maximal semifilter
when it is a semifilter and is contained in no strictly greater semifilter.

7.

An
abstract point
is a completely prime semifilter.

8.

Write

\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)

for the set of abstract points of
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.

Notation 1.2
.

We will generally write
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
for a subset of
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
that is intended to be a semifilter, or for which in most cases of interest
F
is a semifilter.
We will generally write
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
when the subset is intended to be an abstract point, or when in most cases of interest
P
is an abstract point.

Remark 1.3
.

Note on design:

The notion of semifilter from Definition
1.1
is, obviously, based on the standard notion of filter
[
Joh86
, I.2.2, page 12]
.
We just replace the join-directedness condition

‘if
x,x^{\prime}\in F
then
x{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}x^{\prime}\in F
’

with a weaker compatibility condition

‘if
x,x^{\prime}\in F
then
x\ast x^{\prime}
’.

This is in keeping with our move from frames to semiframes, which weakens from joins
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
to the compatibility relation
\ast
.

Note that a semifilter or abstract point need not be join-directed;

1.

Consider
\mathit{nbhd}(0)
in the (semiframes of open sets of the) semitopologies in the left-hand and middle examples in Figure
1
.
In both cases,
\{0,1\},\{0,2\}\in\mathit{nbhd}(0)
but
\{0\}\not\in\mathit{nbhd}(0)
because
\{0\}
is not an open set.

2.

Consider
\{0,1,2\}
with the discrete semitopology (so every set is open).
Then the set of all two- or three-element subsets
\{\{0,1\},\{1,2\},\{2,0\},\{0,1,2\}\}
is a semifilter, but it is not closed under joins because it does not contain
\{0\}
,
\{1\}
, or
\{2\}
.

This second example is particularly interesting.
As the reader may know, the intuition of a filter in topology is a set of
approximations
.
But this example is clearly not approximating anything — after all, we are in the discrete semitopology and there is no need to approximate anything since we can just take a singleton set!
This suggests that a better intuition for semiframe is a set of
collaborations
; in this case, of
0
with
1
,
1
with
2
, and
2
with
0
.

Thus in particular, the standard result in frames that a finite filter has a non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
least element (obtained as the join of all the elements in the filter), does not hold for semifilters in semiframes.
See also Remark
1.6
and Proposition
2.8
(
1
).

Example 1.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
We recall some (standard) facts about abstract points, which carry over from topologies and frames:

1.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and set
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)=(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
.
The reader can check that
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is indeed a semiframe (see Definition
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
).

If
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
then the
neighbourhood system

\mathit{nbhd}(p)=\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in O\}

from Definition
4.4
is an abstract point: see Proposition
2.1
.
Intuitively,
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
abstractly represents
p
as the set of all of its open approximations in
\mathsf{Open}
.

2.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
could contain an abstract point that is not the neighbourhood semifilter
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
of a point
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Set
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)=(\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbb{Q}},\subseteq,\between)
, where
(\mathbb{Q},\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbb{Q}})
is the rational numbers with the usual open set topology.
Set
P_{\pi}
to be the set of all open sets
O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbb{Q}}
such that there exist
q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that
q_{1}<\pi<q_{2}
and
(q_{1},q_{2})\subseteq O
.
Note that
\pi\not\in\mathbb{Q}
, but
P
is the set of open sets ‘approximating’
\pi
.

3.

We mention one more (standard) example.
Consider
\mathbb{N}
with the
final segment semitopology
such that opens are either
\varnothing
or sets
n_{\geq}=\{n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\mid n^{\prime}\geq n\}
.
Then
\{n_{\geq}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is an abstract point.
Intuitively, this approximates a point at infinity, which we can understand as
\omega
.

Lemma 1.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and suppose
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is compatible.
Then
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\not\in F
.

Proof.

By compatibility,
x\ast x
for every
x\in F
.
We use Lemma
3.7
(
1
).
∎

Remark 1.6
.

We continue Remark
1.3
.

As the reader may know, a semiframe still has greatest lower bounds, because we can build them as
x{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}x^{\prime}=\bigvee\{x^{\prime\prime}\mid x^{\prime\prime}\leq x,\ x^{\prime\prime}\leq x^{\prime}\}
.
It is just that this greatest lower bound may be unhelpful.
To see why, consider again the examples in Figure
1
.
In the left-hand and middle examples in Figure
1
, the greatest lower bound of
\{0,1\}
and
\{0,2\}
exists in the semiframe of open sets: but it is
\varnothing
the emptyset in the left-hand and middle example, not
\{0\}
.
In the right-hand example, the greatest lower bound of
\{0,\ast,1\}
and
\{0,\ast,2\}
is
\{0\}
, not
\{0,\ast\}
.

So the reader could ask whether perhaps we should add the following weakened directedness condition to the definition of semifilters (and thus to abstract points):

If
x,x^{\prime}\in F
and
x{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}x^{\prime}\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
then
x{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}x^{\prime}\in F
.

Intuitively, this insists that semifilters are closed under
non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
greatest lower bounds.
However, there are two problems with this:

•

It would break our categorical duality proof in the construction of
g^{\circ}
in Lemma
3.3
; see the discussion in Remark
3.4
.
This technical difficulty may be superable, but …

•

…the condition is probably not what we want anyway.
It would mean that the set of open neighbourhoods of
\ast
in the right-hand example of Figure
1
, would not be a semifilter, because it contains
\{0,\ast,1\}
and
\{0,\ast,2\}
but not its (non-
\varnothing
) greatest lower bound
\{0\}
.

2
Properties of semifilters

1
Things that are familiar from filters

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then:

1.

{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\in F
.

2.

{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\not\in F
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

By nonemptiness (Definition
1.1
(
7
))
F
is nonempty, so there exists some
x\in F
.
By definition
x\leq{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.
By up-closure (Definition
1.1
(
3
))
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\in F
follows.

2.

By assumption in Definition
1.1
(
4
) elements in
F
are pairwise compatible (so
x\ast x
for every
x\in F
).
We use Lemma
1.5
.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
It is possible for a semifilter
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
to be completely prime but not maximal.

Proof.

We give a standard example (which also works for frames and filters).
Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\{0\},\{0,1\}\}
.
Then
P^{\prime}=\{\{0,1\}\}
is an abstract point, but it is not a maximal semifilter (it is not even a maximal abstract point) since
P^{\prime}
is contained in the strictly larger semifilter
\{\{0\},\{0,1\}\}
(which is itself also a strictly larger abstract point).
∎

Lemma 2.3
.

If
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a finite semiframe (meaning that
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is finite) then the properties of

•

being a prime semifilter (Definition
1.1
(
1
)) and

•

being a completely prime semifilter (Definition
1.1
(
2
)),

coincide.

Proof.

This is almost trivial, except that if
X=\varnothing
in the condition for being completely prime then we get that
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\not\in P
— but we know that anyway from Lemma
2.1
(
2
), from the compatibility condition on semifilters.
∎

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then:

1.

The union of a countably ascending chain of semifilters in
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
, is a semifilter in
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

2.

As a corollary, every semifilter
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is contained in some maximal semifilter
F^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
(assuming Zorn’s lemma).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

By a straightforward verification of the conditions of being a semifilter from Definition
1.1
(
5
).

2.

Direct application of Zorn’s lemma.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

1.

Lemma
2.1
(
2
) has a small twist to it.
In the theory of
filters
, it does not follow from the property of being nonempty, up-closed, and closed under all joins and finite meets, that
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\not\in F
; this must be added as a distinct condition if required.

In contrast, we see in the proof of Lemma
2.1
(
2
) that for semifilters,
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\not\in F
follows from the compatibility condition.

2.

Lemma
2.3
matters in particular to us here, because we are particularly interested in abstracting the behaviours of finite semitopologies, because our original motivation for looking at both of these structures comes from looking at real networks, which are finite.
1
1

1

This is carefully worded. We care about
abstracting
properties of finite semitopologies, but we should not restrict to considering
only
semitopologies and semiframes that are actually finite! See Remark
4.7
.

2
Things that are different from filters

Remark 2.6
.

Obviously, by definition semifilters are necessarily compatible but not necessarily closed under meets.
But aside from this fact, we have so far seen semiframes and semifilters behave more-or-less like frames and filters, modulo small details like that mentioned in Remark
2.5
(1).

But there are also differences, as we will now briefly explore.
In the theory of (finite) frames, the following facts hold:

1.

Every filter
F
has a greatest lower bound
x
, and
F=x^{\leq}=\{x^{\prime}\mid x\leq x^{\prime}\}
.

Just take
x=\bigwedge F
the join of all of its (finitely many) elements.
This is not
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
, by the filter’s finite intersection property.

2.

Every filter can be extended to a maximal filter.

Just extend the filter to a maximal filter using Zorn’s lemma (as in Lemma
2.4
).

3.

Every maximal filter is completely prime.

It is a fact of finite frames that a maximal filter is prime,
2
2

2

A succinct proof is in
Wikipedia
(
permalink
).
and since we assume the frame is finite, it is also completely prime.

4.

Every non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
element
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
in a finite frame is contained in some abstract point.

Just form
\{x^{\prime}\mid x\leq x^{\prime}\}
, observe it is a filter, form a maximal filter above it, and we get an abstract point.

5.

As a corollary, if the frame is nonempty (so
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\neq{\boldsymbol{\top}}
; see Example
3.2
) then it has at least one abstract point.

In Lemma
2.7
and Proposition
2.8
we consider some corresponding
non-properties
of (finite) semiframes.

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
It is possible for
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
to be empty, even if
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is nonempty (Example
3.2
(
2
)).
This is possible even if
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is finite, and even if
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is infinite.

Proof.

It suffices to provide an example.
We define a semiframe as below, and as illustrated in Figure
1
:

•

\bm{\mathsf{X}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\bot}},0,1,2,3,{\boldsymbol{\top}}\}
.

•

Let
x\leq x^{\prime}
when
x=x^{\prime}
or
x={\boldsymbol{\bot}}
or
x^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\top}}
.

•

Let
x\ast x^{\prime}
when
x{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}x^{\prime}\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
.
3
3

3

Unpacking what that means, we obtain this:
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\land x=x^{\prime}
or
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\land x^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\top}}
or
x^{\prime}\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\land x={\boldsymbol{\top}}
.
This definition for
\ast
is what we need for our counterexample, but other choices for
\ast
also yield valid semiframes. For example, we can set
x\ast x^{\prime}
when
x,x^{\prime}\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
.

Then
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
has no abstract points.

For suppose
P
is one such.
By Lemma
2.1

{\boldsymbol{\top}}\in P
.
Note that
{\boldsymbol{\top}}=0{\boldsymbol{\vee}}1=2{\boldsymbol{\vee}}3
.
By assumption
P
is completely prime, we know that
0\in P\lor 1\in P
, and also
2\in P\lor 3\in P
.
But this is impossible because
0
,
1
,
2
, and
3
are not compatible.

For the infinite case, we just increase the width of the semiframe by taking
\bm{\mathsf{X}}=\{{\boldsymbol{\bot}}\}\cup\mathbb{N}\cup\{{\boldsymbol{\top}}\}
.
∎

Proposition 2.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then:

1.

It is not necessarily the case that
F
has a non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
greatest lower bound (even if
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is finite).

2.

Every semifilter can be extended to a maximal semifilter, but …

3.

…this maximal semifilter is not necessarily prime (even if
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is finite).

4.

There may exist a non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
element
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
that is contained in no abstract point.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Consider
(\mathit{pow}(\{0,1,2\}),\subseteq,\between)
and take

F=\{\{0,1\},\ \{1,2\},\ \{0,2\},\ \{0,1,2\}\}.

The greatest lower bound of
F
is
\varnothing
.

2.

This is Lemma
2.4
.

3.

F
from part 1 of this result is maximal, and it cannot be extended to a point
P\supseteq F
.

For suppose such a
P
exists; so
P
is a semifilter that contains
F
and is (completely) prime.
Since
\{0,1\}\in P
we must by primeness have
\{0\}\in P
or
\{1\}\in P
.
Suppose
\{0\}\in P
.
Since
\{1,2\}\in P
we must by primeness have
\{1\}\in P
or
\{2\}\in P
.
In either case we lose compatibility.
4
4

4

See also a discussion of the design of the notion of semifilter, in Remarks
1.6
and
3.4
.
Figure
1
gives another counterexample, and in a rather interesting way: the semitopology has four maximal semifilters
\{i,{\boldsymbol{\top}}\}
for

, but by

it has no prime semifilters at all.

4.

We just take
x=0\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
from the example in
Lemma

(see Figure

).
Since this semiframe has no abstract points at all, there is no abstract point that contains

.
∎
