Remark 2.9
.

For now, we will just read Proposition
2.8
as a caution not to assume that semiframes and semifilters behave like frames and filters.
Sometimes they do, and sometimes they don’t; we have to check.

We now proceed to build our categorical duality, culminating with Theorem
4.1
.
Once that machinery is constructed,
we will continue our study of the fine structure of semifilters in Section
15
.

3
Sets of abstract points

Definition 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and recall
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
from Definition
1.1
(
7
).
Define a map
\mathit{Op}:\bm{\mathsf{X}}\to\mathit{pow}(\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast))
by

\mathit{Op}(x)=\{P\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\mid x\in P\}.

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then

\mathit{Op}(\bigvee X)=\bigcup_{x\in X}\mathit{Op}(x).

In words: we can say that
\mathit{Op}
commutes with joins, and that
\mathit{Op}
commutes with taking least upper bounds.

Proof.

Suppose
P\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
There are two sub-cases:

•

Suppose
X\neq\varnothing
.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l}P\in\mathit{Op}(\bigvee X)\Longleftrightarrow&\bigvee X\in P&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.Op}}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\exists x{\in}X.x\in P&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.point}(\ref{item.completely.prime})}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&P\in\bigcup_{x\in X}\mathit{Op}(x)&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.Op}}.\end{array}

•

Suppose
X=\varnothing
.

By the least upper bound property we have
\bigvee X={\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
, and we need to show that
P\in\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})
if and only if
\exists x{\in}\varnothing.x\in P
, i.e. we need to show that
\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})=\varnothing
.
This follows by Lemma
2.1
(
2
) which proves that
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\in P
is impossible.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then:

1.

If
x\leq x^{\prime}
then
\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

2.

If
\mathit{Op}(x)\between\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
then
x\ast x^{\prime}
.

3.

\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})=\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)

and

\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})=\varnothing
.

4.

\mathit{Op}(\bigvee X)=\bigcup_{x\in X}\mathit{Op}(x)
for
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

We prove that
x\leq x^{\prime}
implies
\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

Suppose
x\leq x^{\prime}
, and consider some abstract point
P\in\mathit{Op}(x)
.
By Definition
3.1

x\in P
, and by up-closure of
P
(Definition
1.1
(
3
))
x^{\prime}\in P
, so by Definition
3.1

P\in\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

P
was arbitrary, and it follows that
\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

2.

We prove that
\mathit{Op}(x)\between\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
implies
x\ast x^{\prime}
.

Suppose there exists an abstract point
P\in\mathit{Op}(x)\cap\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.
By Definition
3.1

x,x^{\prime}\in P
, and by compatibility of
P
(Definition
1.1
(
4
))
x\ast x^{\prime}
.

3.

Unpacking Definition
3.1
, it suffices to show that
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\in P
and
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\not\in P
, for every abstract point
P\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
This is from Lemma
2.1
(
1
).

4.

This is just Lemma
3.2
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Proposition
3.3
carries a clear suggestion that

(\{\mathit{Op}(x)\mid x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\},\subseteq,\between)
is trying, in some sense, to be an isomorphic copy of
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
Lemm
3.5
notes that it may not quite manage this, because there may not be enough points (indeed, there may not be any abstract points at all).
This will (just as for topologies and frames) lead us to the notion of a
spatial
semiframe in Definition
1.2
and Proposition
1.4
.

Lemma 3.5
.

The converse implications in Proposition
3.3
(
1
&
2
) need not hold.
That is:

1.

There exists a semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
such that
\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
yet
x\not\leq x^{\prime}
.

2.

There exists a semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
and
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
such that
x\ast x^{\prime}
yet
\mathit{Op}(x)\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The example from Lemma
2.7
(as illustrated in Figure
1
) is a counterexample for both cases:

•

\mathit{Op}(0)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(1)
because both are equal to the empty set, yet
0\not\leq 1
; and

•

{\boldsymbol{\top}}\ast{\boldsymbol{\top}}
yet
\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\top}})\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\top}})
.
∎

4
The semitopology of abstract points

Recall from Definition
1.1
(
7
) that an abstract point in a semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a nonempty up-closed compatible completely prime subset of
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
, and recall from Definition
3.1
that

Op
(x) = {P∈
Points
(
X
,≤,∗) ∣x∈P},

or in words:
\mathit{Op}(x)
is the set of abstract points that contain
x
.

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then define
\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
by

\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)=\{\mathit{Op}(x)\mid x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\}.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then:

1.

\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
from Definition
4.1
is closed under arbitrary sets union.

2.

As a corollary,
(\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast),\subseteq)
(in words:
\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
ordered by subset inclusion) is a complete join-semilattice.

Proof.

Part 1 is just Lemma
3.2
.
The corollary part 2 is then just a fact, since
\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}})\subseteq\mathit{pow}(\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast))
, and sets union is the join (least upper bound) in the powerset lattice.
∎

Recall from Definition
3.4
and Lemma
3.5
that we showed how to go from a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to a semiframe
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
.
We now show how to go in the other direction:

Definition 4.3

(
Semiframe
\to
semitopology)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Define the
semitopology of abstract points
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
by

\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)=\bigl(\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast),\mathsf{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\bigr).

Unpacking this a little:

1.

The set of points of
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is the set of abstract points
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
from Definition
1.1
(
7
) — namely, the completely prime nonempty up-closed compatible subsets of
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
5
5

5

There are no guarantees in general about
how many
abstract points exist; e.g. Lemma
2.7
gives an example of a semiframe that has no abstract points at all and so maps to the empty semitopology. Later on in Definition
1.2
we consider conditions to ensure the existence of abstract points.

2.

Open sets
\mathsf{Opens}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
are the
\mathit{Op}(x)
from Definition
3.1
:

\mathit{Op}(x)=\{P\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\mid x\in P\}.

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
from Definition
4.3
is indeed a semitopology.

Proof.

From conditions
1
and
2
of Definition
1.2
, we need to check that

\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
contains
\varnothing
and
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
and is closed under arbitrary unions.
This is from Proposition
3.3
(
3
&
4
).
∎

Recall from Definitions
4.3
and
3.4
that
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semitopology, and
\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe each of whose elements is the set of abstract points of
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
that contain some
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
:

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then

\mathit{Op}:(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\to\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is surjective.

Proof.

Direct from Definition
4.3
(
2
).
∎

We conclude with Definition
4.6
and Proposition
4.7
, which are standard properties of the construction in Definition
4.3
.

Definition 4.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Define
p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}
by

p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}\quad\text{when}\quad\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\Longleftrightarrow p^{\prime}\in O.

We recall some standard terminology from topology:

•

Call
p
and
p^{\prime}

topologically indistinguishable
when
p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}
.

•

Call
p
and
p^{\prime}

topologically distinguishable
when
\neg(p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime})
(so there exists some
O\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
p\in O\land p^{\prime}\not\in O
or
p\not\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O
).

•

Call
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
a
T_{0}
space
when points are topologically indistinguishable precisely when they are equal, or in symbols:
{\mathrel{\mathring{=}}}={=}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
(Definition
4.3
) is a
T_{0}
space.

Proof.

Suppose
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
Unpacking Definition
1.1
(
7
), this means that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are completely prime nonempty up-closed compatible subsets of
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

It is immediate that
P=P^{\prime}
implies
P\mathrel{\mathring{=}}P^{\prime}
.

Now suppose
P\mathrel{\mathring{=}}P^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
; to prove
P=P^{\prime}
it would suffice to show that
x\in P\Longleftrightarrow x\in P^{\prime}
, for arbitrary
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
By Definition
4.3
(
2
), every open set in
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
has the form
\mathit{Op}(x)
for some
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
We reason as follows:

x∈P ⟺

P∈
Op
(x)

Definition
3.1

⟺

P’∈
Op
(x)

P
,
P^{\prime}
top. indisting.

⟺

x∈P’

Definition
3.1

Since
x
was arbitrary and
P,P^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}
, it follows that
P=P^{\prime}
as required.
∎

Chapter 13
Spatial semiframes & sober semitopologies

1
Definition of spatial semiframes

Remark 1.1
.

We continue Remark
3.4
.
We saw in Example
1.4
(2&3) that there may be
more
abstract points than there are concrete points, and in Remark
3.4
that there may also be
fewer
.

In the theory of frames, the condition of being
spatial
means that the abstract points and concrete points correspond.
We imitate this terminology for a corresponding definition on semiframes:

Definition 1.2

(
Spatial semiframe)
.

Call a semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)

spatial
when:

1.

\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
implies
x\leq x^{\prime}
, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

2.

x\ast x^{\prime}
implies
\mathit{Op}(x)\between\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

Remark 1.3
.

Not every semiframe is spatial, just as not every frame is spatial.
Lemma
2.7
gives an example of a semiframe that is not spatial because it has no points at all, as illustrated in Figure
1
.

We check that the conditions in Definition
1.2
correctly strengthen the implications in Proposition
3.3
to become logical equivalences:

Proposition 1.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a spatial semiframe and
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then:

1.

x\leq x^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

2.

x\ast x^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)\between\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

3.

x=x^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)=\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

4.

\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})=\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)

and

\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})=\varnothing
.

5.

\mathit{Op}(\bigvee X)=\bigcup_{x\in X}\mathit{Op}(x)
for
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

We prove that
x\leq x^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

The right-to-left implication is direct from Definition
1.2
(
1
).
The left-to-right implication is Proposition
3.3
(
1
).

2.

We prove that
x\ast x^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)\between\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

The left-to-right implication is direct from Definition
1.2
(
2
).
The right-to-left implication is Proposition
3.3
(
2
).

3.

We prove that
x=x^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)=\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
.

If
x=x^{\prime}
then
\mathit{Op}(x)=\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
is immediate.
If
\mathit{Op}(x)=\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
then
\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
and
\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})\subseteq\mathit{Op}(x)
.
By part 1 of this result (or direct from Definition
1.2
(
1
))
x\leq x^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}\leq x
.
By antisymmetry of
\leq
it follows that
x=x^{\prime}
.

4.

This is just Proposition
3.3
(
3
)

5.

This is just Lemma
3.2
.
∎

Definition
1.5
will be useful in a moment:
1
1

1

More on this topic later on in Definition
2.1
, when we build the category of semiframes.

Definition 1.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
and
(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
are semiframes.
Then an
isomorphism
between them is a function
g:\bm{\mathsf{X}}\to\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
such that:

1.

g
is a bijection between
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
.

2.

x_{1}\leq x_{2}
if and only if
g(x_{1})\leq g(x_{2})
.

3.

x_{1}\ast x_{2}
if and only if
g(x_{1})\ast g(x_{2})
.

Lemma 1.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
and
(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
are semiframes and
g:\bm{\mathsf{X}}\to\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
is an isomorphism between them.
Then
g({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})=g({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}})
and
g({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})={\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By construction
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\leq x
for every
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
It follows from Definition
1.5
(2) that
g({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})\leq g(x)
for every
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
; but
g
is a bijection, so
g({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})\leq x^{\prime}
for every
x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
.
It follows that
g({\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})={\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}}
.

By similar reasoning we conclude that
g({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})={\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}}
.
∎

Remark 1.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Just for this Remark, define

\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}})=\{\mathit{Op}(x)\mid x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\}.

Then the intuitive content of Proposition
1.4
is that a semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is spatial when

(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is isomorphic (in the sense made formal by Definition
1.5
) to

(\mathit{Op}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}),\subseteq,\between)
.

And, because
\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})=\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
we can write a slogan:

A semiframe is spatial when it is (up to isomorphism) generated by its abstract points.

We will go on to prove in Proposition
2.4
that every semitopology generates a spatial semiframe — and in Theorem
4.1
we will tighten and extend the slogan above to a full categorical duality.

2
The neighbourhood semifilter
\mathit{nbhd}(p)

1
The definition and basic lemma

Recall from Definition
4.4
that
\mathit{nbhd}(p)=\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in O\}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then:

1.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
(Definition
4.4
) is an abstract point (a completely prime semifilter) in the semiframe
\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
(Definition
3.4
).
In symbols:

\mathit{nbhd}(p)\in\mathsf{Points}(\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})).

2.

The following are equivalent:

\mathit{nbhd}(p)\in\mathit{Op}(O)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad O\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad p\in O.

3.

We have an equality:

\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(O))=O.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

From Definition
1.1
(
7
), we must check that
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is a nonempty, completely prime, up-closed, and compatible subset of
\mathsf{Open}
when considered as a semiframe as per Definition
3.4
.
All properties are by facts of sets; we give brief details:

•

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is nonempty because
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\in\mathsf{Open}
.

•

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is completely prime because it is a fact of sets that if
P\subseteq\mathsf{Open}
and
p\in\bigcup P
then
p\in O
for some
O\in P
.

•

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is up-closed because it is a fact of sets that if
p\in O
and
O\subseteq O^{\prime}
then
p\in O^{\prime}
.

•

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is compatible because if
p\in O
and
p\in O^{\prime}
then
O\between O^{\prime}
.

2.

By Definition
3.1
,
\mathit{Op}(O)
is precisely the set of abstract points
P
that contain
O
, and by part 1 of this result
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is one of those points.
By Definition
4.4
,
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is precisely the set of open sets that contain
p
.
The equivalence follows.

3.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[b]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}p\in\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(O))\Longleftrightarrow&\mathit{nbhd}(p)\in\mathit{Op}(O)&\text{Fact of function inverse}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&p\in O&\text{Part~2 of this result}\end{array}\qed

Corollary 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then:

1.

\mathit{Op}(O)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
if and only if
O\subseteq O^{\prime}
.

2.

\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
if and only if
O\between O^{\prime}
.

3.

As a corollary,
\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}(\varnothing)=\varnothing
and
\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}(\mathsf{Points}(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between))=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
; i.e.
\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}
maps the bottom/top element to the bottom/top element.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

If
\mathit{Op}(O)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
then
\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(O))\subseteq\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime}))
by facts of inverse images, and
O\subseteq O^{\prime}
follows by Proposition
2.1
(
3
).

If
O\subseteq O^{\prime}
then
\mathit{Op}(O)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
by Proposition
3.3
(
1
).

2.

If
O\between O^{\prime}
then there exists some point
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
with
p\in O\cap O^{\prime}
.
By Proposition
2.1
(
1
)
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is an abstract point, and by Proposition
2.1
(
2
)
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\in\mathit{Op}(O)\cap\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
; thus
\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
.

If
\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
then
O\between O^{\prime}
by Proposition
3.3
(
2
).

3.

Routine from Proposition
3.3
(
3
) (or from Lemma
1.6
).
∎

2
Application to semiframes of open sets

Proposition 2.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}
bijects open sets of
\mathsf{St}(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
(as defined in Definition
4.3
(
2
)), with open sets of
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, taking
\mathit{Op}(O)
to
O
.

2.

\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}
is an isomorphism between the semiframe of open sets of
\mathsf{St}(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
, and the semiframe of open sets of
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
(Definition
1.5
).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Unpacking Definition
4.3
(
2
), an open set in
\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
has the form
\mathit{Op}(O)
for some
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
By Proposition
2.1
(
3
)
\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(O))=O
, and so
\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}
is surjective and injective.

2.

Unpacking Definition
1.5
it suffices to check that:

•

\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}
is a bijection, and maps
\mathit{Op}(O)
to
O
.

•

\mathit{Op}(O)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
if and only if
O\subseteq O^{\prime}
.

•

\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
if and only if
O\between O^{\prime}
.

The first condition is part 1 of this result; the second and third are from Corollary
2.2
.
∎

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then the semiframe
\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
from Definition
3.4
is spatial.

Proof.

The properties required by Definition
1.2
are that
\mathit{Op}(O)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
implies
O\subseteq O^{\prime}
, and
O\between O^{\prime}
implies
\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
.
Both of these are immediate from Proposition
2.3
(
2
).
∎

3
Application to characterise
T_{0}
spaces

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)=\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
(cf. also Lemma
7.1
)

2.

\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\Longleftrightarrow p\in O^{\prime}

3.

p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}
(
p
and
p^{\prime}
are topologically indistinguishable in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
).

4.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)\mathrel{\mathring{=}}\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
(
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
and
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
are topologically indistinguishable as — by Proposition
2.1
(
1
) — abstract points in
\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
).

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 is direct from Definition
4.4
.
Equivalence of parts 2 and 3 is just Definition
4.6
(1).
Equivalence of parts 1 and 4 is from Proposition
4.7
.
∎

Corollary 2.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is
T_{0}
(Definition
4.6
(
•
‣
4.6
)).

2.

\mathit{nbhd}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Points}(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
is injective.

Proof.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is
T_{0}
, and suppose
\mathit{nbhd}(p)=\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
.
By Lemma
2.5
(1&3)
p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}
.
By Definition
4.6
(2)
p=p^{\prime}
.
Since
p
and
p^{\prime}
were arbitrary,
\mathit{nbhd}
is injective.

Suppose
\mathit{nbhd}
is injective.
Reversing the reasoning of the previous paragraph, we deduce that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is
T_{0}
.
∎

3
Sober semitopologies

Recall from Proposition
2.4
that if we go from a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to a semiframe
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
, then the result is not just any old semiframe — it is a
spatial
one.

We now investigate what happens when we go from a semiframe to a semitopology using Definition
4.3
.

1
The definition and a key result

Definition 3.1
.

Call a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})

sober
when every abstract point
P
of
\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
— i.e. every completely prime nonempty up-closed compatible set of open sets — is equal to the neighbourhood semifilter
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
of some unique
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Equivalently:
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is sober when
\mathit{nbhd}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Points}(\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}))
(Definition
1.1
(
7
)) is a bijection.

Remark 3.2
.

A bijection is a map that is injective and a surjective.
We noted in Corollary
2.6
that a space is
T_{0}
when
\mathit{nbhd}
is injective.
So the sobriety condition can be thought of as having two parts:

•

\mathit{nbhd}
is injective and the space is
T_{0}
, so it intuitively contains no ‘unnecessary’ duplicates of points;

•

\mathit{nbhd}
is surjective, so the space contains ‘enough’ points that there is (precisely) one concrete point for every abstract point.
2
2

2

‘Unnecessary’ and ‘enough’ are in scare quotes here because these are subjective terms. For example, if points represent computer servers on a network then we might consider it a
feature
to not be
T_{0}
by having multiple points that are topologically indistinguishable — e.g. for backup, or to reduce latency — and likewise, we might consider it a feature to not have one concrete point for every abstract point, if this avoids redundancies. There is no contradiction here: a computer network based on a small non-sober space with multiple backups of what it has, may be a more efficient and reliable system than one based on a larger non-sober space that does not back up its servers but is full of redundant points.
And, this smaller non-sober space may present itself to the user abstractly as the larger, sober space.
Users may even forget about the computation that goes on under the hood of this abstraction, as illustrated by the following
true story:
The authors had a paper presenting an efficient algorithm rejected because it ‘lacked motivation’. Why? Because the algorithm was unnecessary: the reviewer claimed, apparently with a straight face, that guessing the answer until you got it right was computationally equivalent.

Example 3.3
.

We give some examples of sober and non-sober semitopologies.

1.

\mathbb{R}
with its usual topology (which is also a semitopology) is sober.

2.

\mathbb{Q}
with its usual topology (which is also a semitopology) is not sober: the set of open neighbourhoods of
\pi
is a completely prime semifilter, but is not the neighbourhood semifilter of a unique point in
\mathbb{Q}
.

3.

Any nonempty set with the discrete semitopology, is sober.

4.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\{0,1\}\}
.
This has one abstract point
P=\{\{0,1\}\}
but two concrete points
0
and
1
.
It is therefore not sober.

5.

Take
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\mathbb{N}
with the final topology; so
O\in\mathsf{Open}
when
O=\varnothing
or
O=n_{\geq}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
n_{\geq}=\{n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\mid n^{\prime}\geq n\}
.
Take
P=\{n_{\geq}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
The reader can check that this is an abstract point (up-closed, completely prime, compatible); however
P
is not the neighbourhood semifilter of any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus this space is not sober.

Proposition 3.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
from Definition
4.3
is a sober semitopology.

Proof.

We know from Lemma
4.4
that
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semitopology.
The issue is whether it is sober; thus by Definition
3.1
(1) we wish to exhibit every abstract point
P
of
\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
as a neighbourhood semifilter
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
for some unique abstract point
p
of
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
The calculations to do so are routine, but we give details.

Fix some abstract point
P
of
\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
By Definition
1.1
(
7
),

P

is a completely prime nonempty up-closed set of intersecting open sets in the semitopology
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
, and by Definition
4.3
(
2
) each open set in
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
has the form
\mathit{Op}(x)=\{p\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\mid x\in p\}
for some
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

We define
p\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
as follows:

p={x∈
X
∣
Op
(x)∈P} ⊆
X
.

By construction we have that
x\in p
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)\in P
, and so

nbhd
(p)
=

{
Op
(x) ∣p∈
Op
(x)}

Definition
4.4

=

{
Op
(x) ∣x∈p}

Definition
3.1

=

{
Op
(x) ∣
Op
(x)∈P}

Construction of
p

=

P

Fact

.

Now
P
is completely prime, nonempty, up-closed, and compatible and it follows by elementary calculations using Proposition
1.4
that
p
is also completely prime, nonempty, up-closed, and compatible — so
p
is an abstract point of
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.

So we have that

p∈
Point
(
X
,≤,∗)

and
P =
nbhd
(p) .

To prove uniqueness of
p
, suppose
p^{\prime}
is any other abstract point such that
P=\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
.
We follow the definitions:
\mathit{Op}(x)\in\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})\Longleftrightarrow\mathit{Op}(x)\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
, and thus by Definition
4.4

p^{\prime}\in\mathit{Op}(x)\Longleftrightarrow p\in\mathit{Op}(x)
, and thus by Definition
3.1

x\in p^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow x\in p
, and thus
p^{\prime}=p
.
∎

2
Sober topologies contrasted with sober semitopologies

(a)

Finite
T_{0}
(and also
T_{1}
) semitopology that is not sober (Lemma
3.7
)

(b)

Hausdorff semitopology that is not sober (Lemma
3.9
)

Figure 1
:
Two counterexamples for sobriety

(a)

(b)

Soberification of right-hand example in Figure

Figure 2
:
Example soberifications (Remark
3.11
)

We will need Notation
3.5
for Remark
3.6
:

Notation 3.5
.

Call a closed set
irreducible
when it cannot be written as the union of two proper closed subsets.

Remark 3.6
.

Topology has a wealth of separation actions.
Three of them are:
T_{0}
(distinct points have distinct neighbourhood (semi)filters);
T_{1}
(distinct points have distinct open neighbourhoods); and
T_{2}
, also known has the Hausdorff condition (distinct points have disjoint open neighbourhoods) — see Remark
2.1
for formal statements.
In the case of topologies, the following is known about sobriety:

1.

Every finite
T_{0}
(and thus
T_{1}
) topological space is sober.

2.

Every
T_{2}
/Hausdorff space (including infinite ones) is sober
[
MM92
, page 475, Theorem 3]
.

3.

A topological space is sober if and only if every nonempty irreducible closed set is the closure of a unique point
[
MM92
, page 475]
.

The situation for semitopologies is different, as we explore in the rest of this Subsection.

Lemma 3.7
.

1.

It is not necessarily the case that a finite
T_{0}
semitopology (or even a finite
T_{1}
semitopology) is sober (Definition
3.1
(1)).

2.

It is not necessarily the case that if every nonempty irreducible closed set is the closure of a unique point, then a semitopology is sober.

These non-implications hold even if the semitopology is regular (so
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
for every
p
; see Definition
1.4
(
3
)).

Proof.

We provide a semitopology that is a counterexample for both parts.

Consider the left-hand semitopology illustrated in Figure
1
, so that:
3
3

3

Recall that we used this example, for different purposes, in Lemma
7.2
and Figure
2
.

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
, and

•

\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\{0,1\},\{1,2\},\{0,2\},\{0,1,2\}\}
.

We note that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
.

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is regular because all points are intertwined, so that
\mathit{K}(p)=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

•

The nonempty irreducible closed sets are
\{0\}
(which is the complement of
\{1,2\}
),
\{1\}
, and
\{2\}
.
Since these are singleton sets, they are certainly the closures of unique points.

So
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is
T_{0}
, regular, and irreducible closed sets are the closures of unique points.

We take as our filter
P=\mathsf{Open}\setminus\{\varnothing\}
.
The reader can check that
P
is completely prime (Definition
1.1
(
2
)), nonempty, up-closed, and compatible (
P
is also the greatest filter); but,
P
is not the neighbourhood semifilter of
0
,
1
, or
2
in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Thus,
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is not sober.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

The counterexample used in Lemma
3.7
generalises, as follows: the reader can check that the
all-but-one
semitopology from Example
1.4
(
5c
) on three or more points (so open sets are generated by
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus\{p\}
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
) has similar behaviour.

In topology, every Hausdorff space is sober.
In semitopologies, this implication does not hold, and in a rather strong sense:

Lemma 3.9
.

1.

It is not necessarily the case that if a semitopology is Hausdorff, then it is sober.

2.

Every quasiregular Hausdorff semitopology is sober.

3.

Every quasiregular Hausdorff semitopology is discrete (the open sets are the full powerset).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

It suffices to give a counterexample.
Consider the right-hand semitopology illustrated in Figure
1
(which we also used, for different purposes, in Figure
2
), so that:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3\}
, and

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
X=\{\{3,0\},\{0,1\},\{1,2\},\{2,3\}\}
.

This is Hausdorff, but it is not sober: the reader can check that the up-closure
\{3,0\}^{\leq}\subseteq\mathsf{Open}

is nonempty, up-closed, compatible, and completely prime, but it is not the neighbourhood filter of any
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

2.

By Lemma
2.2
, a quasiregular Hausdorff semitopology is discrete.
The reader can easily check that a discrete semitopology is sober.

3.

As part 2 of this result.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

A bit more discussion of Lemma
3.9
.

1.

The space used in the counterexample for part 1 is Hausdorff,
T_{1}
, and unconflicted (Definition
1.1
(
2
)).
It is not quasiregular (Definition
1.4
(
5
)) because the community of every point is empty; see Proposition
2.1
.

2.

The implication holds if we add quasiregularity as a condition: every quasiregular Hausdorff space is sober.
But, this holds for very bad reasons, because by Lemma
2.2
every quasiregular Hausdorff space is discrete.

3.

Thus, the non-implication discussed in Lemma
3.9
is informative and tells us something interesting about semitopological sobriety.
Semitopological sobriety is not just a weak form of topological sobriety.
Indeed, if anything it is a rather strong condition, and it has its own distinct personality — in particular, it does not like the
T_{2}
/Hausdorff separation axiom and refuses to coexist with it outside of the (trivial) discrete semitopology.

So the examples above suggest that, in contrast the the situation in topologies where separation axioms tend to induce sobriety, in a semitopological context separation axioms (and especially Hausdorff separation) seem to be quite antithetical to sobriety.

Remark 3.11
.

We can inject the examples illustrated in Figure
1
(used in Lemmas
3.7
and
3.9
) into
soberified
versions of the spaces that are sober and have an isomorphic lattice of open sets, as illustrated in Figure
2
:

1.

The left-hand semitopology has abstract points (completely prime semifilters; see Definition
1.1
(
2
)) generated as the
\subseteq
-up-closures of the following sets:
\{A\}
,
\{B\}
,
\{C\}
,
\{A,B\}
,
\{B,C\}
,
\{C,A\}
, and
\{A,B,C\}
.
Of these,
\{A,B\}^{\subseteq}=\mathit{nbhd}(0)
,
\{B,C\}^{\subseteq}=\mathit{nbhd}(1)
, and
\{C,A\}^{\subseteq}=\mathit{nbhd}(2)
.
The other completely prime semifilters are not generated as the neighbourhood semifilters of any point in the original space, so we add points as illustrated using
\bullet
in
the left-hand diagram in Figure
2
.
This semitopology is sober, and has the same semiframe of open sets.

2.

For the right-hand example, we again add a
\bullet
point for every abstract point in the original space that is not already the neighbourhood semifilter of a point in the original space.
These abstract points are generated as the
\subseteq
-up-closures of
\{A\}
,
\{B\}
,
\{C\}
, and
\{D\}
.

There is no need to add a
\bullet
for the abstract point generated as the
\subseteq
-up-closure of
\{A,B\}
, because
\{A,B\}^{\subseteq}=\mathit{nbhd}(0)
.
Similarly
\{B,C\}^{\subseteq}=\mathit{nbhd}(1)
,
\{C,D\}^{\subseteq}=\mathit{nbhd}(2)
, and
\{D,A\}^{\subseteq}=\mathit{nbhd}(3)
.
Note that
\{A,B,C\}
does not generate an abstract point because it is not compatible:
A\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}C
.
Similarly for
\{B,C,D\}
,
\{C,D,A\}
,
\{D,A,C\}
, and
\{A,B,C,D\}
.

These soberified spaces are instances of a general construction described in Theorem
1.4
.
And, continuing the observation made in Remark
3.10
, note that neither of these spaces, with their extra points, are Hausdorff.

Chapter 14
Four categories & functors between them

1
The categories
\mathsf{SemiTop}/\mathsf{Sober}
of semitopologies/sober semitopologies

Definition 1.1
.

1.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and
(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
are semitopologies and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
is any function.
Then call
f
a
morphism of semitopologies
when

f
is continuous, by which we mean (as standard) that

O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}\quad\text{implies}\quad f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}.

2.

Define
\mathsf{SemiTop}
the
category of semitopologies
such that:

•

objects are semitopologies, and

•

arrows are morphisms of semitopologies (continuous maps on points).
1
1

1

A discussion of possible alternatives, for future work, is in Remark
3.3
. See also Remarks
1.3
and
1.6
.

3.

Write
\mathsf{Sober}
for the
category of sober semitopologies
and continuous functions between them.
By construction,
\mathsf{Sober}
is the full subcategory of
\mathsf{SemiTop}
, on its sober semitopologies.

Remark 1.2
.

For convenience reading Theorem
1.4
we recall some facts:

1.

The
semiframe

\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)

from Definition
3.4
has elements open sets
O\in\mathsf{Open}
, preordered by subset inclusion and with a compatibility relation given by sets intersection.

It is spatial, by Proposition
2.4
.

2.

An abstract point
P
in
\mathsf{Points}(\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}))
is a completely prime nonempty up-closed compatible subset of
\mathsf{Open}
.

3.

\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is by Definition
4.3
a semitopology such that:

(a)

Its set of points is
\mathsf{Points}(\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}))
; the set of abstract points in
\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
, the semilattice of open sets of
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, and

(b)

Its open sets are given by the
\mathit{Op}(O)
, for
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.

It is sober, by Proposition
3.4
.

Continuing Remark
1.2
, a notation will be useful:

Notation 1.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then define

\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}).

We may use
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and
\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
interchangeably, depending on whether we want to emphasise “this is a sober semitopology obtained from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
” or “this is
\mathsf{St}
acting on
\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
”.

Theorem 1.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then

1.

\mathit{nbhd}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Points}(\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}))
is a morphism of semitopologies from

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to
\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})

2.

taking
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to a sober semitopology
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, such that

3.

\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}
induces a bijection on open sets by mapping
\mathit{Op}(O)
to
O
, and furthermore this is an isomorphism of the semiframes of open sets, in the sense of Definition
1.5
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Following Definition
1.1

we must show that
\mathit{nbhd}
is continuous (inverse images of open sets are open) from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.
So following Definition
4.3
(
2
), consider
\mathit{Op}(O)\in\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}))
.
By Proposition
2.1
(
3
)

\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(O))=O\in\mathsf{Open}.

Continuity follows.

2.

\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is sober by Proposition
3.4
.

3.

This is Proposition
2.3
.
∎

Remark 1.5
.

We can summarise Theorem
1.4
as follows:

1.

By construction, the kernel of the
\mathit{nbhd}
function (the relation determined by which points it maps to equal elements) is topological indistinguishability
\mathrel{\mathring{=}}
.

2.

We can think of
\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
as being obtained from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
by

(a)

quotienting topologically equivalent points to obtain a
T_{0}
space, and then

(b)

adding extra points to make it sober.

See also the discussion in Remark
3.2
about what it means to have ‘enough’ points.

3.

This is done without affecting the semiframe of open sets (up to isomorphism), with the semiframe bijection given by
\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}
.

In this sense, we can view
\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
as a
soberification
of
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

2
The categories
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
/
\mathsf{Spatial}
of semiframes/spatial semiframes

Definition 2.1
.

1.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
and
(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
are semiframes (Definition
3.1
) and
g:\bm{\mathsf{X}}\to\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
is any function.
Then call
g
a
morphism of semiframes
when:

(a)

g
is a morphism of complete semilattices (Definition
1.4
).

(b)

g
is
compatible
,
by which we mean that
g(x^{\prime})\ast g(x^{\prime\prime})
implies
x^{\prime}\ast x^{\prime\prime}
for every
x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
.

2.

We define
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
the
category of semiframes
such that:

•

objects are semiframes, and

•

arrows are morphisms of semiframes.

3.

Write
\mathsf{Spatial}
for the
category of spatial semiframes
and semiframe morphisms between them.
By construction,
\mathsf{Spatial}
is the full subcategory of
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
, on its spatial semiframes (Definition
1.2
).

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then
\mathit{Op}:(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\to\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a morphism of semiframes and is surjective on underlying sets.

Proof.

Following Definition
2.1
(
1
)
we must show that

•

\mathit{Op}
is a semilattice morphism (Definition
1.4
) — commutes with joins and maps
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
to
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
) — and

•

is compatible with the compatibility relation
\ast
, and

•

we must show that
\mathit{Op}
is surjective.

We consider each property in turn:

•

\mathit{Op}
is a semilattice morphism.

\mathit{Op}(\bigvee X)=\bigvee_{x\in X}\mathit{Op}(x)
by Lemma
3.2
, and
\mathit{Op}({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}})=\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
by Proposition
3.3
(
3
).

•

\mathit{Op}
is compatible with
\ast
.

Unpacking Definition
2.1
(
1b
), we must show that
\mathit{Op}(x)\between\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
implies
x\ast x^{\prime}
.
We use Proposition
3.3
(
2
).

•

\mathit{Op}
is surjective

… by Lemma
4.5
.
∎

3
Functoriality of the maps

Definition 3.1
.

Suppose
g:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is an arrow in
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
.
Define a mapping
g^{\circ}:\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\to\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
by

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}c@{\quad}l}g^{\circ}:&\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)&\longrightarrow&\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\\
&P&\longmapsto&P^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}\mid g(x^{\prime})\in P\}.\end{array}

Remark 3.2
.

We will show that
g^{\circ}
from Definition
3.1
is an arrow in
\mathsf{SemiTop}
.
We will need to prove the following:

•

If
P\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
then
g^{\circ}(P)\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
.

•

g^{\circ}
is a morphism of semitopologies.

We do this in Lemmas
3.3
and
3.6
respectively.

Lemma 3.3

(
g^{\circ}
well-defined)
.

Suppose
g:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is an arrow in
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
and suppose
P\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
Then
g^{\circ}(P)
from Definition
3.1
is indeed in
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
— and thus
g^{\circ}
is well-defined function from
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
to
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
.

Proof.

For brevity write

P’={x’∈
X
’∣g(x’)∈P} .

We must check that
P^{\prime}
is a completely prime nonempty up-closed compatible subset of
\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
.
We consider each property in turn:

1.

P^{\prime}
is completely prime.

Consider some
X^{\prime}\subseteq P^{\prime}
and suppose
g(\bigvee X^{\prime})\in P
.
By Definition
2.1
(
1a
)
g
is a semilattice homomorphism, so by Definition
1.3
(
2
)
g(\bigvee X^{\prime})=\bigvee_{x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}}g(x^{\prime})
.
Thus
\bigvee_{x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}}g(x^{\prime})\in P
.
By assumption
P
is completely prime, so
g(x^{\prime})\in P
for some
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
.
Thus
x^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}
for that
x^{\prime}
.
Since
X^{\prime}
was arbitrary, it follows that
P^{\prime}
is completely prime.

2.

P^{\prime}
is nonempty.

By assumption
g
is an arrow in
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
(i.e. a semiframe morphism) and unpacking Definition
2.1
(
1a
) it follows that it is a semilattice homomorphism.
In particular by Definition
1.3
(
2
)
g({\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}})={\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
, and
by Lemma
2.1
(
1
)
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\in P
.
Thus
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}}\in P^{\prime}
, so
P^{\prime}
is nonempty.

3.

P^{\prime}
is up-closed.

Suppose
x^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}\leq x^{\prime\prime}
.
By construction
g(x^{\prime})\in P
.
By Lemma
1.6
(because
g
is a semilattice morphism by Definition
2.1
(
1a
))
g
is monotone, so
g(x^{\prime})\leq g(x^{\prime\prime})
.
By assumption in Definition
1.1
(
3
)
P
is up-closed, so that
g(x^{\prime\prime})\in P
and thus
x^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}
as required.

4.

P^{\prime}
is compatible.

Suppose
x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime}
.
Thus
g(x^{\prime}),g(x^{\prime\prime})\in P
.
By assumption in Definition
1.1
(
4
)
P
is compatible, so
g(x^{\prime})\ast g(x^{\prime\prime})
.
By compatibility of
g
(Definition
2.1
(
1b
)) it follows that
x^{\prime}\ast x^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus
P^{\prime}
is compatible.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Note on design:

If we want to impose further conditions on being an abstract point (such as those discussed in Remark
1.6
) then
Lemma
3.3
would need to be extended to show that these further conditions are preserved by the
g^{\circ}
operation, so that for
P\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
an abstract point in
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
,
g^{\circ}(P)=\{x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}\mid g(x^{\prime})\in P\}
is an abstract point in
(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
.

For example: consider what would happen if we add the extra condition on semifilters from Remark
1.6
.
Then the
P^{\prime}
defined in the proof of Lemma
3.3
above might not be closed under this additional condition (it will be if
g
is surjective).
This could be mended by closing
P^{\prime}
under greatest lower bounds that are not
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
, but that in turn might compromise the property of being completely prime.
These comments are not a proof that the problems would be insuperable; but suggest that complexity would be added.
For this initial paper, we prefer to keep things simple!

Lemma 3.5
.

Suppose
g:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is an arrow in
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
, and suppose
x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
.
Then

(g^{\circ})^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime}))=\mathit{Op}(g(x^{\prime})).

Proof.

Consider an abstract point
P\in\mathsf{Point}(\mathsf{Gr}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime}))
.
We just chase definitions:

P∈(g^∘)^
-
1(
Op
(x’))
⟺

g^∘(P)∈
Op
(x’)

Fact of inverse image

⟺

x’∈g^∘(P)

Definition
3.1

⟺

g(x’)∈P

Definition
3.1

⟺

P∈
Op
(g(x’)).

Definition
3.1

The choice of
P
was arbitrary, so
(g^{\circ})^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime}))=\mathit{Op}(g(x^{\prime}))
as required.
∎

Lemma 3.6

(
g^{\circ}
continuous)
.

Suppose
g:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is an arrow in
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
.
Then
g^{\circ}:\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\to\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
is continuous:

(g^{\circ})^{\text{-}1}(\mathcal{O}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast))

for every
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.3
,
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime})
for some
x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
3.5

(g^{\circ})^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(x^{\prime}))=\mathit{Op}(g(x^{\prime}))
.
By Definition
4.3
(
2
)
\mathit{Op}(g(x^{\prime}))\in\mathsf{Open}(\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast))
.
∎

Proposition 3.7

(Functoriality)
.

1.

Suppose
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
is an arrow in
\mathsf{SemiTop}
(thus: a continuous map on underlying points).

Then
f^{\text{-}1}
is an arrow
\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
in
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
.

2.

Suppose
g:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is an arrow in
\mathsf{SemiFrame}
.

Then
g^{\circ}
from Definition
3.1
is an arrow
\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\to\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{SemiTop}
.

3.

The assignments
f\mapsto f^{\text{-}1}
and
g\mapsto g^{\circ}
are
functorial
— they map identity maps to identity maps, and commute with function composition.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Following Definition
2.1
, we must check that
f^{\text{-}1}
is a morphism of semiframes.
We just unpack what this means and see that the required properties are just facts of taking inverse images:

•

f^{\text{-}1}
commutes with joins, i.e. with
\bigcup
.

This is a fact of inverse images.

•

f^{\text{-}1}
maps
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
to
{\boldsymbol{\top}}_{\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

This is a fact of inverse images.

•

f^{\text{-}1}
is compatible, meaning that
f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})\between f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime\prime})
implies
O^{\prime}\between O^{\prime\prime}
.

This is a fact of inverse images.

2.

We must check that
g^{\circ}
is continuous.
This is Lemma
3.6
.

3.

Checking functoriality is entirely routine, but we sketch the reasoning anyway:

•

Consider the identity function
\mathit{id}
on some semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.
Then
\mathit{id}^{\text{-}1}
should be the identity function on
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
.
It is.

•

Consider maps
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
and
f^{\prime}:(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime\prime})
.
Then
(f^{\prime}\circ f)^{\text{-}1}
should be equal to
f^{\text{-}1}\circ(f^{\prime})^{\text{-}1}
.
It is.

•

Consider the identity function
\mathit{id}
on
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
Then
\mathit{id}^{\circ}
should be the identity function on
\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
.
We look at Definition
3.1
and see that this amounts to checking that
P=\{x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid\mathit{id}(x)\in P\}
.
It is.

•

Consider maps
g:(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
and
g^{\prime}:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime\prime},\leq^{\prime\prime},\ast^{\prime\prime})
and consider some
P^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{Points}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime\prime},\leq^{\prime\prime},\ast^{\prime\prime})
.
Then
(g^{\prime}\circ g)^{\circ}(P^{\prime\prime})
should be equal to
(g^{\circ}\circ(g^{\prime})^{\circ})(P^{\prime\prime})
.
We look at Definition
3.1
and see that this amounts to checking that

\{x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid g^{\prime}(g(x))\in P^{\prime\prime}\}=\{x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid g(x)\in P^{\prime}\}

where

P^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime}\mid g^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\in P^{\prime\prime}\}
.
Unpacking these definitions, we see that the equality does indeed hold.
∎

4
Sober semitopologies are categorically dual to spatial semiframes

We can now state the duality result between
\mathsf{Sober}
and
\mathsf{Spatial}
:

Theorem 4.1
.

The maps
\mathsf{St}
(Definition
4.3
) and
\mathsf{Fr}
(Definition
3.4
), with actions on arrows as described in Proposition
3.7
, form a categorical duality between:

•

the category
\mathsf{Sober}
of sober semitopologies (Definition
3.1
), and all continuous compatible morphisms between them; and

•

the category
\mathsf{Spatial}
of spatial semiframes and morphisms between them (Definition
2.1
(
3
)).

Proof.

There are various things to check:

•

Proposition
3.4
shows that
\mathsf{St}
maps spatial semiframes to sober semitopologies.

•

Proposition
2.4
shows that
\mathsf{Fr}
maps sober semitopologies to spatial semiframes.

•

By Proposition
3.7
the maps
f\mapsto f^{\text{-}1}
(inverse image) and
g\mapsto g^{\circ}
(Definition
3.1
) are functorial.

•

The equivalence morphisms are given by the bijections
\mathit{Op}
and
\mathit{nbhd}
:

–

\mathit{Op}
is from Definition
3.1
.
By Lemma
2.2

\mathit{Op}
is a morphism
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\to\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
in
\mathsf{Spatial}
that is surjective on underlying sets.
Injectivity is from Proposition
1.4
(
3
).

–

\mathit{nbhd}
is from Definition
4.4
.
By Theorem
1.4

\mathit{nbhd}
is a morphism
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
in
\mathsf{Sober}
.
It is a bijection on underlying sets by the sobriety condition in Definition
3.1
.

Finally, we must check naturality of
\mathit{Op}
and
\mathit{nbhd}
, which means (as standard) checking commutativity of the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{nbhd}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{\text{-}1})^{\circ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{nbhd}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{Op}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g^{\circ})^{\text{-}1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathit{Op}}

We proceed as follows:

•

Suppose
g:(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\to(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
in
\mathsf{Spatial}
, so that

g^{\circ}:\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)\to\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{Sober}
and

(g^{\circ})^{\text{-}1}:\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}}^{\prime},\leq^{\prime},\ast^{\prime})\to\mathsf{Fr}\,\mathsf{St}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
in
\mathsf{Spatial}
.
To prove naturality we must check that

(g^{\circ})^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{Op}(x))=\mathit{Op}(g(x))

for every
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
This is just Lemma
3.5
.

•

Suppose
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{SemiTop}
, so that

f^{\text{-}1}:\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})\to\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
in
\mathsf{Spatial}
and

(f^{\text{-}1})^{\circ}:\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{SemiTop}
.
To prove naturality we must check that

(f^{\text{-}1})^{\circ}(\mathit{nbhd}(p))=\mathit{nbhd}(f(p)).

We just chase definitions, for an open set
O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l}O^{\prime}\in(f^{\text{-}1})^{\circ}(\mathit{nbhd}(p))\Longleftrightarrow&f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.g.circ}}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&p\in f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.nbhd}}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&f(p)\in O^{\prime}&\text{Inverse image}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&O^{\prime}\in\mathit{nbhd}(f(p))&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.nbhd}}.\end{array}

∎

Remark 4.2
.

We review the background to Theorem
4.1
:

1.

A semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a set of points
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and a set of open sets
\mathsf{Open}\subseteq\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
that is closed under arbitrary (possibly empty) unions (Definition
1.2
).

2.

A morphism between semitopologies is a continuous function, just as for topologies (Definition
1.1
(
1
)).

3.

A semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a complete join-semilattice
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq)
with a properly reflexive distributive
compatibility relation

\ast
(Definition
3.1
).

4.

A morphism between semiframes is a morphism of complete join-semilattices with
{\boldsymbol{\top}}
that is compatible with the compatibility relation (Definition
2.1
(
1
)).

5.

An
abstract point
of a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a nonempty up-closed compatible subset
P\subseteq\mathsf{Open}
(Definition
1.1
(
7
)).

6.

A semitopology is
sober
when the neighbourhood semifilter map
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mapsto\mathit{nbhd}(p)=\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in O\}
is injective and surjective between the points of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and the abstract points of
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
(Definition
3.1
).

7.

By Theorem
1.4
, and as discussed in Remark
1.5
, every (possibly non-sober) semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
maps into its
soberification

\mathsf{St}\,\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, which has an isomorphic semiframe of open sets.
So even if our semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is not sober, there is a standard recipe to make it so.

8.

A semiframe is
spatial
when
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mapsto\mathit{Op}(x)=\{P\in\mathsf{Point}\mid x\in P\}
respects
\leq
and
\ast
in senses make formal in
Definition
1.2
and Proposition
1.4
.

9.

Sober semitopologies and continuous functions between them, and spatial semiframes and semiframe morhphisms between them, are categorically dual (Theorem
4.1
).

Remark 4.3
.

A
categorical duality
between two categories
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
is an equivalence between
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}^{op}
; this is an adjoint pair of functors whose unit and counit are natural isomorphisms.
See
[
Mac71
, IV.4]
.
2
2

2

The
Wikipedia page
(
permalink
) is also exceptionally clear.

There are many duality results in the literature.
The duality between topologies and frames is described (for example) in
[
MM92
, page 479, Corollary 4]
.
A duality between distributive lattices and coherent spaces is in
[
Joh86
, page 66]
.
There is the classic duality by Stone between Boolean algebras and compact Hausdorff spaces with a basis of clopen sets
[
Sto36
,
Joh86
]
.
An encyclopaedic treatment is in
[
Car11
]
, with a rather good overview in Example 2.9 on page 17.

Theorem
4.1
appends another item to this extensive canon.
It also constructively moves us forward in studying semitopologies, because it gives us an algebraic treatment of semitopologies, and a formal framework for studying morphisms between semitopologies.
For instance: taking morphisms to be continuous functions is sensible not just because this is also how things work for topologies, but also because this is what is categorically dual to the
{\leq}/{\ast}
-homomorphisms between semiframes (Definition
2.1
).
And of course, if we become interested in different notions of semitopology morphism (a flavour of these is given in Remark
3.3
) then the algebraic framework gives us a distinct mathematical light with which to inspect and evaluate them.

Note what Theorem
4.1
does
not
do: it does not give a duality between all semitopologies and all semiframes; it gives a duality between sober semitopologies and spatial semiframes.
This in itself is nothing new — the topological duality is just the same —
but what is interesting is that our motivation in this paper for studying semitopologies comes from practical network systems.
These tend to be (finite) non-sober semitopologies — non-sober, because a guarantee of sobriety cannot be enforced, and anyway it is precisely the point of the exercise to achieve coordination,
without
explicitly representing every possible constellation of cooperating agents with its own point.
After all, this is part of what it means to be a
permissionless
and
distributed
system.

It is true that by Theorem
1.4
every non-sober semitopology can be embedded into a sober one without affecting the semiframe of open sets, but this makes the system to which it corresponds larger, by adding points.

So, the duality that Theorem
4.1
packages up is a mathematical statement, but not necessarily a directly practical one — and this is as expected, because we knew from the start that this is an abstract result.

\mathit{nbhd}
maps a point to a set of (open) sets; and
\mathit{Op}
maps an (open) set of points to a set of sets of (open) sets.
Of course these need not necessarily be computationally optimal.

Remark 4.4
.

We have constructed an algebraic representation of semitopologies — but this is not the last word on representing semitopologies.
Other methodologies are also illuminating, and because our motivation comes from distributed systems, which are networks, we are particularly interested in representations based on ideas from graphs.
We will investigate these in Section
16
.

Chapter 15
Well-behavedness conditions, dually

We want to understand semifilters better, and in particular we want to understand how properties of semifilters and abstract points correspond to the well-behavedness properties which we found useful in studying semitopologies — for example
topens
,
regularity
, and being
unconflicted
(Definitions
2.1
,
1.4
and
1.1
).

1
(Maximal) semifilters and transitive elements

Remark 1.1

(Semifilters are not filters)
.

We know that semifilters do not necessarily behave like filters.
For instance:

1.

It is possible for a finite semifilter to have more than one minimal element, because the join-directedness condition of filters is replaced by a weaker compatibility condition (see also Remarks
1.3
and
1.6
).

2.

There are more semifilters than filters — even if the underlying space is a topology.
For example, the discrete semitopology on
\{0,1,2\}
(whose open sets are all subsets of the space) is a topology.
Every filter in this space is a semifilter, but it also has a semifilter
\bigl\{\{0,1\},\{1,2\},\{2,0\},\{0,1,2\}\bigr\}
, which is not a filter.

More on this in Subsection
2
.

In summary: semifilters are different and we cannot necessarily take their behaviour for granted without checking it.
We now examine them in more detail.

We start with some easy definitions and results:

Lemma 1.2

(Characterisation of maximal semifilters)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

F
is maximal.

2.

For every
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
,
x\ast F
if and only if
x\in F
.

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
F
is a maximal semifilter.

Suppose
x\in F
.
Then
x\ast F
is immediate from
Notation
2.3
(
1
) and semifilter compatibility (Definition
1.1
(
4
)).

Suppose
x\ast F
; thus by Notation
2.3
(
1
)
x
is compatible with (every element of)
F
.
We note that the
\leq
-up-closure of
\{x\}\cup F
is a semifilter (nonempty, up-closed, compatible).
By maximality,
x\in F
.

•

Suppose
x\ast F
if and only if
x\in F
, for every
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

Suppose
F^{\prime}
is a semifilter and
F\subseteq F^{\prime}
.
Consider
x^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
.
Then
x\ast F
by compatibility of
F^{\prime}
, and so
x\in F
.
Thus,
F^{\prime}\subseteq F
.
∎

Definition 1.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Call
x

transitive
when:

1.

x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.

2.

x^{\prime}\ast x\ast x^{\prime\prime}
implies
x^{\prime}\ast x^{\prime\prime}
, for every
x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.

‘Being topen’ in semitopologies (Definition
2.1
(
2
)) corresponds to ‘being transitive’ in semiframes (Definition
1.3
):

Lemma 1.4

(Characterisation of topen sets)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

O
is topen in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
in the sense of Definition
2.1
(
2
).

2.

O
is transitive in
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
in the sense of Definition
1.3
.
1
1

1

Confusing terminology alert:
Definition
2.1
(
1
) also has a notion of
transitive set
. The notion of transitive set is well-defined for a set that may not be open. In the world of semiframes, we just have elements of the semiframe (which correspond, intuitively, to open sets). Thus
transitive
semiframe elements correspond to (nonempty) transitive open sets of a semitopology, which are called
topens
.

Proof.

We unpack the definitions and note that the condition for being topen — being a nonempty open set that is transitive for
\between
— is identical to the condition for being transitive in
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
— being a non-
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\mathsf{Open}}
element that is transitive for
{\ast}={\between}
.
∎

2
The compatibility system
x^{\ast}

Definition 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then define
x^{\ast}
the
compatibility system
of
x
by

x^{\ast}=\{x^{\prime}\mid x^{\prime}\ast x\}.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then
(\bigvee X)^{\ast}=\bigcup_{x{\in}X}x^{\ast}
.

Proof.

We just follow the definitions:

y∈(⋁X)^∗⟺

y∗⋁X

Definition
2.1

⟺

∃x∈X.y∗x

Definition
2.1
(
3
)

⟺

∃x∈X.y∈x^∗

Definition
2.1

⟺

y∈⋃_x∈X x^∗

Fact of sets

∎

Notation 2.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
X,Y\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
and
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then we generalise
x\ast y
to
x\ast Y
,
X\ast y
, and
X\ast Y
as follows:

1.

Write
x\ast Y
for
\forall y{\in}Y.x\ast y
.

2.

Write
X\ast y
for
\forall x{\in}X.x\ast y
.

3.

Write
X\ast Y
for
\forall x{\in}X.\forall y{\in}Y.x\ast y
.

We read
x\ast Y
as ‘
x
is
compatible
with
Y
’, and similarly for
X\ast y
and
X\ast Y
.

Remark 2.4
.

We will see later on in Lemma
7.1
that

X\ast X^{\prime}
generalises
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
, in the sense that if
X=\mathit{nbhd}(p)
and
X^{\prime}=\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
, then
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\ast\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is transitive.
Then the following are equivalent for every
y\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
:

y\ast x\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad y\in x^{\ast}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad y\ast x^{\ast}.

Proof.

We prove a cycle of implications:

•

Suppose
y\ast x
.
Then
y\in x^{\ast}
is direct from Definition
2.1
.

•

Suppose
y\in x^{\ast}
.
Then
y\ast x^{\ast}
— meaning by Notation
2.3
(
1
) that
y\ast x^{\prime}
for every
x^{\prime}\in x^{\ast}
— follows by transitivity of
x
.

•

Suppose
y\ast x^{\ast}
.
By proper reflexivity of
\ast
(Definition
2.1
(
2
); since
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
)
x\in x^{\ast}
, and
y\ast x
follows.
∎

Proposition 2.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and suppose
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\neq x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

x
is transitive.

2.

x^{\ast}
is a completely prime semifilter (i.e. an abstract point).

3.

x^{\ast}
is a semifilter.

4.

x^{\ast}
is compatible.

5.

x^{\ast}
is a maximal semifilter.

Proof.

We first prove a cycle of implications betweeen parts 1 and 3:

1.

Suppose
x
is transitive.
We need to check that
x^{\ast}
is nonempty, up-closed, compatible, and completely prime.
We consider each property in turn:

•

x\ast x
by proper reflexivity of
\ast
(Definition
2.1
(
2
); since
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
), so
x\in x^{\ast}
.

•

It follows from monotonicity of
\ast
(Lemma
2.3
(
1
)) that if
x^{\prime}\leq x^{\prime\prime}
and
x\ast x^{\prime}
then
x\ast x^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Suppose
x^{\prime}\ast x\ast x^{\prime\prime}
.
By transitivity of
x
(Definition
1.3
),
x^{\prime}\ast x^{\prime\prime}
.

•

Suppose
x\ast\bigvee X^{\prime}
; then by distributivity of
\ast
(Definition
2.1
(
3
))

x\ast x^{\prime}
for some
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
.

2.

If
x^{\ast}
is a completely prime semifilter, then it is certainly a semifilter.

3.

If
x^{\ast}
is a semifilter, then it is compatible (Definition
1.1
(
5
)&
4
).

4.

Suppose
x^{\ast}
is compatible (Definition
1.1
(
4
)) and suppose
x^{\prime}\ast x\ast x^{\prime\prime}
.
By Lemma
2.5

x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}\in x^{\ast}
, and by compatibility of
x^{\ast}
we have
x^{\prime}\ast x^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus,
x
is transitive.

To conclude, we prove two implications between parts 4 and 5:

•

Suppose
x^{\ast}
is a semifilter.
By equivalence of parts 3 and 1 of this result,
x
is transitive, and so
using Lemma
2.5

x^{\prime}\ast x^{\ast}
if and only if
x^{\prime}\in x^{\ast}
.
By Lemma
1.2
,
x^{\ast}
is maximal.

•

Clearly, if
x^{\ast}
is a maximal semifilter then it is a semifilter.
∎

3
The compatibility system
F^{\ast}

1
Basic definitions and results

Definition 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and suppose
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
(
F
may be a semifilter, but the definition does not depend on this).
Define
F^{\ast}
the
compatibility system
of
F
by

F^{\ast}=\{x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid x\ast F\}

Unpacking Notation
2.3
(
1
), and combining with Definition
2.1
, we can write:

F^{\ast}=\{x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid x\ast F\}=\{x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid\forall x{\in}F.x^{\prime}\ast x\}=\bigcap\{x^{\ast}\mid x\in F\}.

Lemma
3.2
presents one easy and useful example of Definition
3.1
:

Lemma 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\ \liff\ }c}O^{\prime}\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)^{\ast}\hfil\ \Longleftrightarrow&\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\Longrightarrow O^{\prime}\between O\\
O^{\prime}\not\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)^{\ast}\hfil\ \Longleftrightarrow&\exists O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\land O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O.\end{array}

Proof.

We just unpack Definitions
4.4
and
3.1
.
∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then

F^{\ast}
is up-closed.

Proof.

This is just from Definition
3.1
and monotonicity of
\ast
(Lemma
2.3
(
1
)).
∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then:

1.

If
x\in F
then
F\subseteq x^{\ast}
.

2.

As a corollary,
F\subseteq F^{\ast}
.

Proof.

Suppose
x\in F
.
By compatibility of
F
(Definition
1.1
(
4
)),
x^{\prime}\ast x
for every
x^{\prime}\in F
.
It follows from Definition
2.1
that
F\subseteq x^{\ast}
.
The corollary is immediate from Definition
3.1
.
∎

We can use Lemma
3.4
and Definition
3.1
to give a more succinct rendering of Lemma
1.2
:

Corollary 3.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

F
is maximal.

2.

F^{\ast}=F
.

3.

F^{\ast}\subseteq F
.

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 just repeats Lemma
1.2
using Definition
3.1
.
To prove equivalence of parts 2 and 3 we use use Lemma
3.4
(2).
∎

2
Strong compatibility: when
F^{\ast}
is a semifilter

Proposition
2.6
relates good properties of
x
(transitivity) to good properties of its compatibility system
x^{\ast}
(e.g. being compatible).
It will be helpful to ask similar questions of
F^{\ast}
.
What good properties are of interest for
F^{\ast}
, and what conditions can we impose on
F
to guarantee them?

Definition 3.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe.
Then:

1.

Call
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}

strongly compatible
when
F^{\ast}
is nonempty and compatible.

2.

Call
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)

strongly compatible
when every abstract point (completely prime semifilter)
P\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is strongly compatible.

Remark 3.7
.

For the reader’s convenience we unpack Definition
3.6
.

1.

By Definition
1.1
(
4
),
F^{\ast}
is compatible when
x\ast x^{\prime}
for every
x,x^{\prime}\in F^{\ast}
.
Combining this with Definition
3.1
and Notation
2.3
,
F^{\ast}
is compatible when
x\ast F\ast x^{\prime}
implies
x\ast x^{\prime}
, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Thus,
F
is strongly compatible when

\forall x,x^{\prime}{\in}\bm{\mathsf{X}}.\ x\ast F\ast x^{\prime}\Longrightarrow x\ast x^{\prime}.

2.

(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is strongly compatible when every abstract point
P\in\mathsf{Point}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is strongly compatible in the sense just given above.

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Recall from Definition
3.4
(
•
‣
3.4
) and Lemma
3.5
that
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
is a semiframe.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

The point
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is hypertransitive in the sense of Definition
5.4
.

2.

The semifilter
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\subseteq\mathsf{Open}
is strongly compatible in the sense of Definition
3.6
.

Proof.

Remark
3.7
notes that the condition in Definition
5.4
is precisely the condition for
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
to be strongly compatible.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Given Lemma
3.8
, the reader might ask why we do not just call a strongly compatible semifilter ‘hypertransitive’.

There is a case for doing so, but caution is required:
strong compatibility of semiframes is not
quite
the same thing as hypertransitivity of points.
Every point
p
generates a semifilter
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
, but there may be more semifilters than there are points, and this makes the strong compatibility condition subtly different from the hypertransitivity condition.
We shall see the effects of this in Lemma
5.3
(2), and in Theorem
5.4
(see Remark
5.5
for a brief discussion), and then again in Definition
8.5
where we define a notion of
strongly compatible semitopology
(essentially: all of its semifilters are strongly compatible), which is not the same thing as the space being hypertransitive (essentially: all of its points are hypertransitive).

Therefore, we maintain a terminological distinction:
points
are hypertransitive,
semiframes
are strongly compatible.
The notions are related, but not quite the same thing.

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and suppose
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is nonempty.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

F^{\ast}
is a semifilter.

2.

F^{\ast}
is compatible.

3.

F
is strongly compatible.

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 2 and 3 is just Definition
3.6
.
For equivalence of parts 1 and 2 we prove two implications:

•

Suppose
F^{\ast}
is a semifilter.
Then it is compatible by assumption in Definition
1.1
(
5
).

•

Suppose
F^{\ast}
is compatible.
It is up-closed by Lemma
3.3
, and nonempty by Lemma
3.4
(2) (since
F
is nonempty).
Thus, by Definition
1.1
(
5
)
F^{\ast}
is a semifilter.
∎

Lemma 3.11
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and suppose
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then it is not necessarily the case that
F^{\ast}
is a semifilter.

This non-implication holds even in strong well-behavedness conditions: that
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is spatial and
F
is an abstract point (a completely prime semifilter).

Proof.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Let
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=(\{0,1,2\},\{\varnothing,\{0\},\{2\},\bm{\mathsf{P}}\})
, as illustrated in the top-left semitopology in Figure
1
.
Take
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)=(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
(which is spatial by Proposition
2.4
) and set
F=\mathit{nbhd}(1)=\{0,1,2\}
.
Then
\mathit{nbhd}(1)^{\ast}=\{\{0\},\{2\},\{0,1,2\}\}
, and this is not compatible because
\{0\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{2\}
.
2
2

2

1
is also a
conflicted
point; see Example
1.2
(
1
). This is no accident: by Lemma
5.6
(
2
) if
p
is conflicted then it is not hypertransitive, and by Lemma
3.8
it follows that
\mathit{nbhd}(p)^{\ast}
is not compatible.

∎

Remark 3.12
.

Lemma
3.11
gives an example of a semifilter
F
that is not strongly compatible (i.e. such that
F^{\ast}
is not a semifilter).
Note that in this example both the space and
F
are well-behaved.
This raises the question of finding sufficient (though perhaps not necessary) criteria for strong compatibility.
We conclude with one such criterion, which will be useful later:

Figure 1
:
Strongly compatible filter that contains no transitive element

Proposition
3.13
bears a family resemblance to Theorem
2.6
(if a point has a topen neighbourhood then it is regular):

Proposition 3.13
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then:

1.

If
F
contains a transitive element then
F
is strongly compatible.

2.

The converse implication need not hold: it may be that
F
is strongly compatible yet
F
contains no transitive element.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
x\in F
is transitive.
By Lemma
3.10
it would suffice to show that
F^{\ast}
is compatible (Definition
1.1
(
4
)).
So consider
y\ast F\ast y^{\prime}
.
Then
y\ast x\ast y^{\prime}
and by transitivity
y\ast y^{\prime}
.
Thus
F^{\ast}
is compatible.

2.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
We take, as illustrated in Figure
1
,

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{{\shortminus}2,{\shortminus}1,0,1,2\}
and

•

we let
\mathsf{Open}
be generated by
\{i,i{+}1\}
for
{\shortminus}2\leq i\leq 1
(unordered pairs of adjacent numbers).

Write
A=\{{\shortminus}1,0\}
and
B=\{0,1\}
and let
F
be the up-closure of
\{A,B\}=\{\{{\shortminus}1,0\},\{0,1\}\}
.
Note that
A
and
B
are not transitive (i.e. not topen).
The reader can check that
F^{\ast}=F
(e.g.
\{1,2\}\not\in F^{\ast}
because
\{1,2\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{{\shortminus}1,0\}\in F
), but
F
contains no transitive element.
∎

4
Semiframe characterisation of community

Remark 4.1
.

We saw the notion of
\mathit{K}(p)
the
community
of a point in Definition
1.4
(
1
).
In this Subsection we construct an analogue to it in semiframes.
We will give two characterisations: one in Definition
4.5
, and another in Proposition
4.7
.

We will mostly be interested in Definition
4.2
when
F
is a semifilter, but the definition does not require this:

Definition 4.2
.

Suppose if
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
and
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then define
F^{c}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
,
F^{\ast c}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
, and
x^{\ast c}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
by

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}F^{c}=&\bigvee\{y\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid y\not\in F\}\\
F^{\ast c}=&(F^{\ast})^{c}\\
x^{\ast c}=&(x^{\ast})^{c}.\end{array}

Remark 4.3
.

We unpack the definitions of
F^{\ast c}
and
x^{\ast c}
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}l}F^{\ast c}=&(F^{\ast})^{c}&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.cast}}\\
=&\bigvee\{y\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid y\not\in F^{\ast}\}&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.cast}}\\
=&\bigvee\{y\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid\neg(y\ast F)\}&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.X.ast}}\\[8.61108pt]
