x^{\ast c}=&(x^{\ast})^{c}&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.cast}}\\
=&\bigvee\{y\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid y\not\in x^{\ast}\}&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.cast}}\\
=&\bigvee\{y\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid\neg(y\ast x)\}.&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.x.ast}}\end{array}

Lemma
4.4
will be useful, and gives some intuition for
(\text{-})^{c}
and
(\text{-})^{\ast c}
by unpacking their concrete meaning in the special case of a semiframe of open sets of a semitopology:

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
Then:

1.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)^{c}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|p|
.

2.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)^{\ast c}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus p_{\between}
.

3.

O^{\ast c}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|O|=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

It is a fact of Definition
1.2
that
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|p|=\bigcup\{O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\not\in O^{\prime}\}
.
By Proposition
2.1
(
2
)
p\not\in O^{\prime}
if and only if
O^{\prime}\not\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
.

2.

It is a fact of Definition
6.1
, which is spelled out in Lemma
8.2
(
3
), that
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus p_{\between}=\bigcup\{O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}\mid\exists O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\land O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O\}
.
By Lemma
3.2

\exists O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\land O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O
precisely when
O^{\prime}\not\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)^{\ast}
.

3.

By Definitions
4.2
and
3.1
we have

O^{c}=(O^{\ast})^{\ast c}=\bigcup\{O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}\mid O^{\prime}\not\in O^{\ast}\}=\bigcup\{O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}\mid O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O\}.

The result then follows by routine reasoning on closures (Definition
1.2
).
∎

Definition 4.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then define
\mathit{k}(F)\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
the
abstract community
of
F
by

\mathit{k}(F)=(F^{\ast c})^{\ast c}\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}.

(For a more direct characterisation, see Proposition
4.7
.)

Proposition 4.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then

\mathit{k}(\mathit{nbhd}(p))=\mathit{K}(p).

In words: the abstract community of the abstract point
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
in
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
, is identical to the community of
p
.

Proof.

We reason as follows:

k
(
nbhd
(p))
=

(
nbhd
(p)^∗c)^∗c

Definition
4.5

=

(
P
∖p_≬)^∗c

Lemma
4.4
(
2
)

=

interior
(
P
∖(
P
∖p_≬))

Lemma
4.4
(
3
)

=

interior
(p_≬)

Fact of sets

=

K
(p)

Definition
1.4
(
1
)

∎

We can also give a more direct characterisation of the abstract community from Definition
4.5
:

Proposition 4.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then

\mathit{k}(F)=\bigvee\{x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}\mid x^{\ast}\subseteq F^{\ast}\},

and
\mathit{k}(F)
is greatest such that
\mathit{k}(F)^{\ast}\subseteq F^{\ast}
.

Proof.

We follow the definitions:

(F^∗c)^∗c
=

⋁{x∈
X
∣¬(x∗F^∗c)}

Remark
4.3

=

⋁{x∈
X
∣¬(x∗⋁{y ∣¬(y∗F)})}

Remark
4.3

=

⋁{x∈
X
∣¬∃y∈
X
. (x∗y ∧¬(y∗F))}

Definition
2.1
(
3
)

=

⋁{x∈
X
∣∀y∈
X
. y∗x ⟹y∗F}

Fact of logic

=

⋁{x∈
X
∣x^∗⊆F^∗}

Definitions
2.1
&
3.1

To see that
\mathit{k}(F)
is greatest such that
\mathit{k}(F)^{\ast}\subseteq F^{\ast}
, we note from Lemma
2.2
that

k
(F)^∗= ⋃{x^∗∣x∈
X
, x^∗⊆F^∗} .
∎

5
Semiframe characterisation of regularity

We now have enough to generalise the notions of quasiregularity, weak regularity, and regularity from semitopologies (Definition
1.4
parts
5
,
4
, and
3
) to semiframes:

Definition 5.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.

1.

Call
F

quasiregular
when
\mathit{k}(F)\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.

Thus, there exists some
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
such that
x^{\ast}\subseteq F^{\ast}
.

2.

Call
F

weakly regular
when
\mathit{k}(F)\in F
.

3.

Call
F

regular
when
\mathit{k}(F)\in F
and
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive.

Lemma
5.2
does for semiframes what Lemma
1.7
does for semitopologies:

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then:

1.

If
F
is regular then it is weakly regular.

2.

If
F
is weakly regular then it is quasiregular.

(The converse implications need not hold, and it is possible for
F
to not be quasiregular:
it is convenient to defer the proofs to Corollary
6.3
.)

Proof.

The proofs are easy:
If
\mathit{k}(F)\in F
and
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive, then certainly
\mathit{k}(F)\in F
.
If
\mathit{k}(F)\in F
then by Lemma
2.1
(
2
)
\mathit{k}(F)\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then:

1.

If
F
is quasiregular and strongly compatible then
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive.

2.

The converse implication need not hold: it is possible for
F
to be quasiregular and
\mathit{k}(F)
to be transitive, yet
F
is not strongly compatible.

3.

If
F
is weakly regular and
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive then
F
is strongly compatible.

4.

If
F
is weakly regular, then
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive if and only if
F
is strongly compatible.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
F
is quasiregular and strongly compatible.

By quasiregularity
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}\neq\mathit{k}(F)
.
By Proposition
4.7

\mathit{k}(F)^{\ast}\subseteq F^{\ast}
.
By strong compatibility
F^{\ast}
is a semifilter and so in particular
F^{\ast}
is compatible.
It follows from Proposition
2.6
(
1
&
4
) that
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive, as required.

2.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Let
(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{Open})
be the real numbers with their usual topology, and let
(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
be the topology generated by
\mathsf{Open}\cup\{\{0\}\}
— in words: we add
\{0\}
as an open set.

Let
F
be the semifilter of all
\mathsf{Open}
-open neighbourhoods of
0
.

F^{\ast}
is the set of
\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}
-open sets that intersect every
\mathsf{Open}
-open neighbourhood of
0
.
This is not compatible, because it contains
(0,)
(the set of numbers strictly less than
0
) and
(,0)
(the set of numbers strictly greater than
0
), and these do not intersect.
Using Proposition
4.7
, we calculate that
\mathit{k}(F)=\{0\}
; this is transitive because it is a singleton set.

So
F
is quasiregular,
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive, yet
F
is not strongly compatible.

3.

Suppose
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive and suppose
F
is weakly regular, so
\mathit{k}(F)\in F
.
By Proposition
3.13

F
is strongly compatible.

4.

From parts
1
and
3
of this result, noting from Lemma
5.2
that if
F
is weakly regular then it is quasiregular.
∎

Theorem 5.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then
F
is regular if and only if
F
is weakly regular and strongly compatible.
We can write this succinctly as follows:

Regular = weakly regular + strongly compatible.
3
3

3

Compare this slogan with the version for semitopologies in Theorem
2.2
.

Proof.

Suppose
F
is weakly regular and strongly compatible.
By Lemma
5.3
(
4
)
\mathit{k}(F)
is transitive, and by Definition
5.1
(
3
)
F
is regular.

For the converse implication we just reverse the reasoning above.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

In Theorem
5.8
we characterised regularity of points in terms of quasiregularity and being hypertransitive.
In view of Lemma
3.8
we might expect Theorem
5.4
to read ‘regular = quasiregular + strongly compatible’.
But this is false, as per the discussion in Remark
3.9
and the counterexample in Lemma
5.3
(2).
Thus, the semiframes results are subtly different from those governing point-set semitopologies.

6
Semiframe characterisation of (quasi/weak)regularity

The direct translation in Definition
5.1
of parts
5
,
4
, and
3
of Definition
1.4
, along with the machinery we have now built, makes Lemma
6.1
easy to prove:

Lemma 6.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Recall from Definition
4.4
and Proposition
2.1
(
1
)
that
\mathit{nbhd}(p)=\{O\in\mathsf{Open}\mid p\in O\}
is a (completely prime) semifilter.
Then:

1.

p
is quasiregular in the sense of Definition
1.4
(
5
)
if and only if

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is quasiregular in the sense of Definition
5.1
(
1
).

2.

p
is weakly regular in the sense of Definition
1.4
(
4
) if and only if
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is weakly regular in the sense of Definition
5.1
(
2
).

3.

p
is regular in the sense of Definition
1.4
(
3
) if and only if
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is regular in the sense of Definition
5.1
(
3
).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
p
is quasiregular.
By Definition
1.4
(
5
)
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
.
By Proposition
4.6

\mathit{k}(\mathit{nbhd}(p))\neq\varnothing={\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\mathsf{Open}}
.
By Definition
5.1
(
1
)
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is quasiregular.

The reverse implication follows just reversing the reasoning above.

2.

Suppose
p
is weakly regular.
By Definition
1.4
(
4
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
By Definition
4.4

\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
.
By Proposition
4.6

\mathit{k}(\mathit{nbhd}(p))\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
as required.

The reverse implication follows just reversing the reasoning above.

3.

Suppose
p
is regular.
By Definition
1.4
(
3
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
.
By Definition
4.4
and Proposition
4.6

\mathit{k}(\mathit{nbhd}(p))\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
.
By Proposition
4.6
and Lemma
1.4

\mathit{k}(\mathit{nbhd}(p))
is transitive.

The reverse implication follows just reversing the reasoning above.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then

•

p
is quasiregular / weakly regular / regular in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
in the sense of Definition
1.4

if and only if

•

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is quasiregular / weakly regular / regular in
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
in the sense of Definition
5.1
.

Proof.

We consider just the case of regularity; quasiregularity and weak regularity are no different.

Suppose
p
is regular.
By Definition
1.4
(
3
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
.
It follows from Lemma
1.4
that
\mathit{K}(p)
is transitive in
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
, and from Proposition
2.1
(
2
) that
\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
.
It follows from Proposition
4.6
that
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is regular in the sense of Definition
5.1
(
3
).
∎

Corollary 6.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
F\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{X}}
is a semifilter.
Then the converse implications in Lemma
5.2
need not hold:
F
may be quasiregular but not regular, and it may be weakly regular but not regular, and it may not even be quasiregular.

Proof.

It suffices to provide counterexamples.
We easily obtain these by using Proposition
6.2
to consider
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
for
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
as used in Lemma
1.7
(
3
&
4
).
∎

7
Characterisation of being intertwined

This Subsection continues Remark
2.4
.

The notion of points being intertwined from Definition
6.1
(
1
) generalises in semiframes to the notion of semifilters being compatible:

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathit{nbhd}(p)\ast\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathit{nbhd}(p)\mathrel{\between}\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime}).

For clarity and precision we unpack this.
The following are equivalent:

1.

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
in the semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
(Definition
6.1
(
1
)).

In words: the point
p
is intertwined with the point
p^{\prime}
.

2.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)\ast\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
in the semiframe
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
(Notation
2.3
(
3
)).

In words: the abstract point
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is compatible with the abstract point
\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
.

3.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)\mathrel{\between}\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
in the semitopology
\mathsf{St}(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
(Definition
6.1
(
1
)).

In words: the point
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is intertwined with the point
\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We unpack definitions:

•

By Definition
6.1
(
1
)
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
when for every pair of open neighbourhoods
p\in O
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
we have
O\between O^{\prime}
.

•

By Notation
2.3
(
3
)
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\ast\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
when for every
O\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
and
O^{\prime}\in\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
we have
O\ast O^{\prime}
.

By Proposition
2.1
(
2
) we can simplify this to:
p\in O
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
implies
O\ast O^{\prime}
.

•

By Definition
6.1
(
1
) and Theorem
1.4
,
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\mathrel{\between}\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
when:
for every pair of open neighbourhoods
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\in\mathit{Op}(O)
and
\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})\in\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
we have
\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
.

By Proposition
2.1
(
2
) we can simplify this to:
p\in O
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
implies
\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})
.

By Propoposition
3.3
(
2
) we can simplify this further to:
p\in O
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
implies
O\ast O^{\prime}
.

But by definition, the compatibility relation
\ast
of
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
is
\between
, so
O\ast O^{\prime}
and
O\between O^{\prime}
are the same assertion.
The equivalences follow.
∎

The property of being intertwined is preserved and reflected when we use
\mathit{nbhd}
to map to the soberified space:

Corollary 7.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
if and only if
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\mathrel{\between}\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

Proof.

This just reiterates the equivalence of parts 1 and 3 in Lemma
7.1
.
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

It may be that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is unconflicted (meaning that it contains no conflicted points), but the semitopology
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
contains a conflicted point.

2.

It may further be that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is unconflicted and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is such that
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is conflicted in the semitopology
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

We can summarise the two assertions above as follows:

1.

Soberifying a space might introduce a conflicted point, even if none was originally present.

2.

Soberifying a space can make a point that was unconflicted, into a point that is conflicted.
4
4

4

If we stretch the English language, we might say that soberifying a space can conflictify one of its points.

Proof.

It suffices to provide counterexamples.

1.

Consider the right-hand semitopology in Figure
1
; this is unconflicted because every point is intertwined only with itself.
The soberification of this space is illustrated in the right-hand semitopology in Figure
2
.
Each of the extra points is intertwined with the two numbered points next to it; e.g. the extra point in the open set
A
— write it
\bullet_{A}
(in-between
3
and
0
) — is intertwined with
0
and
3
; so
3\mathrel{\between}\bullet_{A}\mathrel{\between}0
.
However, the reader can check that
3\mathrel{\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}}0
.
Thus,
\bullet_{A}
is conflicted.

2.

We define
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
by:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=({\shortminus}1,1)
(real numbers between
{\shortminus}1
and
1
exclusive).

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by:

–

All open intervals that do not contain
0
; so this is open intervals
(r_{1},r_{2})
where
{\shortminus}1\leq r_{1}<r_{2}\leq 0
or
0\leq r_{1}<r_{2}\leq 1
.

–

All of the open intervals
({\shortminus}1/n,1/n)
, for
n\geq 2
.

The reader can check that:

•

Points in this semitopology are intertwined only with themselves.

•

The soberification includes four additional points, corresponding to completely prime semifilters
{\shortminus}1/0
generated by
\{({\shortminus}1/n,0)\mid n\geq 2\}
and
{+}1/0
generated by
\{(0,1/n)\mid n\geq 2\}
, and to the endpoints
{\shortminus}1
and
i{+}1
.

•

{\shortminus}1/0
and
{+}1/0
are intertwined with
0
, but are not intertwined with one another.

Thus,
0
is conflicted in
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
but not in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

Proposition
7.3
may seem surprising in view of Corollary
7.2
, but the key observation is that the soberified space may add points to the original space.
These points can add conflicting behaviour that is ‘hidden’ in the completely prime semifilters of the original space.

Thus, Proposition
7.3
shows that the property of ‘being unconflicted’
cannot
be characterised purely in terms of the semiframe of open sets — if it could be, then soberification would make no difference, by Theorem
1.4
(
3
).

There is nothing wrong with that, except that this is a paper about semiframes.
We can now look for some other condition — but one having to do purely with open sets — that might play a similar role in the theory of (weak/quasi)regularity of semiframes, as being unconflicted does in theory of (weak/quasi)regularity of semitopologies.

We already saw a candidate for this in Theorem
5.4
:
strong compatibility
.
We examine this next.

8
Strong compatibility in semitopologies

Remark 8.1
.

Note that:

1.

Theorem
5.4
characterises ‘regular’ for semiframes as ‘weakly regular + strongly compatibile’.

2.

Theorem
2.2
characterises ‘regular’ for semitopologies as ‘weakly regular + unconflicted’.

We know from results like Lemma
6.1
and Corollary
7.2
that there are accurate correspondences between notions of regularity in semiframes and semitopologies.
This is by design, e.g. in Definition
5.1
; we designed the semiframe definitions so that semiframe regularity and semitopological regularity would match up closely.

Yet there are differences too, since Theorem
5.4
uses strong compatibility, and Theorem
2.2
uses being unconflicted.
What is the difference here, and why does it arise?

One answer is given by Proposition
7.3
, which illustrates that the condition of ‘unconflicted’ (which comes from semitopologies) does not sit comfortably with the ‘pointless’ semiframe definitions.
This raises the question of how strong compatibility (which comes from semiframes) translates into the context of semitopologies; and how this relates to being (un)conflicted?

We look into this now; see Remark
8.8
for a summary.

Lemma 8.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is hypertransitive (Definition
5.4
).

2.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is strongly compatible.

3.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)^{\ast}
is compatible.

4.

For every
O^{\prime},O^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
, if
O^{\prime}\ast\mathit{nbhd}(p)\ast O^{\prime\prime}
then
O^{\prime}\between O^{\prime\prime}
.

(Above,
O^{\prime}\ast\mathit{nbhd}(p)
follows Notation
2.3
(
1
) and means that
O^{\prime}\between O
for every
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}
, and similarly for
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\ast O^{\prime\prime}
.)

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 is just Lemma
3.8
.
Equivalence of parts 2 and 3 is Definition
3.6
(
1
).
For the equivalence of parts 3 and 4, we just unpack what it means for
\mathit{nbhd}(p)^{\ast}
to be compatible (see Remark
3.7
).
∎

Lemma
8.3
shows that the situation outlined in Proposition
7.3
(2) cannot arise if we work with a strongly compatible point instead of an unconflicted one …

Lemma 8.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is hypertransitive in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

2.

\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is hypertransitive in
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
(Notation
1.3
).

Proof.

Note that:

•

From Lemma
8.2
,
p
is hypertransitive in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
when for every
O^{\prime},O^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
,

(\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.p\in O\Longrightarrow O^{\prime}\between O\between O^{\prime\prime})\quad\text{implies}\quad O^{\prime}\between O^{\prime\prime}.

•

From Definition
4.3
(
2
) and Lemma
8.2
,
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is hypertransitive in
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
when for every
\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime}),\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime\prime})\in\mathsf{Opens}(\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}))
,

\hskip-10.00002pt(\forall O{\in}\mathsf{Open}.\mathit{nbhd}(p)\in\mathit{Op}(O)\Longrightarrow\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})\between\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime\prime}))\quad\text{implies}\quad\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime\prime}).

Now by Proposition
2.1
(
2
),
\mathit{nbhd}(p)\in\mathit{Op}(O)
if and only if
p\in O
, and
by Corollary
2.2

\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime})\between\mathit{Op}(O)
if and only if
O^{\prime}\between O
, and
\mathit{Op}(O)\between\mathit{Op}(O^{\prime\prime})
if and only if
O\between O^{\prime\prime}
.
The result follows.
∎

…but, the situation outlined in Proposition
7.3
(1)
can
arise, indeed we use the same counterexample:

Lemma 8.4
.

It may be that every point in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is hypertransitive, yet
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
contains a point that is not hypertransitive.

Proof.

The same counterexample as used in Proposition
7.3
(1) illustrates a space
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
such that every point in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is hypertransitive, but
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
contains a point that is not hypertransitive.
We note that
\bullet_{A}
(the extra point in-between
3
and
0
) is not hypertransitive, because both
B
and
D
intersect with every open neighbourhood of
\bullet_{A}
, but
B
does not intersect with
D
.
∎

The development above suggests that we define:

Definition 8.5
.

Call a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})

strongly compatible
when
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
is strongly compatible in the sense of Definition
3.6
(
2
).

The proof of Proposition
8.6
is then very easy:

Proposition 8.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is strongly compatible in the sense of Definition
8.5
.

2.

\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is strongly compatible in the sense of Definition
8.5
.

3.

\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is strongly compatible in the sense of Definition
3.6
(
2
).

4.

\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is hypertransitive in the sense of Definition
5.4
.

Proof.

We unpack Definition
8.5
and note that strong compatibility of
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is expressed purely as a property of its semiframe of open sets
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
.
By Theorem
1.4
(
3
), the semiframe of open sets of
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is isomorphic to
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
, via
\mathit{nbhd}^{\text{-}1}
.
Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 follows.

By Notation
1.3
and Remark
1.2
(
3
), the points of
\mathsf{Soberify}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
are just abstract points of
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
.
Equivalence of parts 2 and 3 follows.

Equivalence of part 4 with the other parts follows using Lemmas
8.2
and
8.3
.
∎

Recall from Definition
1.4
(
7
) that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
being (weakly) regular means that every point in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is (weakly) regular.
Recall from Definition
8.5
that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
being strongly compatible means that
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)=\mathsf{Fr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is strongly compatible in the sense of Definition
3.6
(
2
).
We can now prove an analogue of Theorems
2.2
and
5.4
:

Theorem 8.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is regular.

2.

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is weakly regular and strongly compatible.

Proof.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is regular, meaning that every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is regular.

By Theorems
2.2
and
5.8
every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is weakly regular and hypertransitive.
(So by Lemma
8.2
every
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
is strongly compatible, and by Lemma
8.3
also hypertransitive.)
The definition of weak regularity for a space in Definition
1.4
(
7
) is pointwise, so it follows immediately that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is weakly regular.

But, the definition of strong compatibility for a space in Definition
8.5
is on its semiframe of open sets, which may include abstract points not only of the form
\mathit{nbhd}(p)
.
It therefore does not follow immediately that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is strongly compatible; Lemma
8.4
contains a counterexample.

We can still prove that
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is strongly compatible — but we need to do a bit more work.

Unpacking Definition
8.5
, we must show that
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
is strongly compatible.
Unpacking Definition
3.6
(
2
), we must show that every abstract point in
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
is strongly compatible.

So consider an abstract point
P\subseteq\mathsf{Open}
.
By Corollary
3.3

\bm{\mathsf{P}}
has a topen partition
\mathcal{T}
, which means that: every
T\in\mathcal{T}
is topen; the elements of
\mathcal{T}
are disjoint; and
\bigcup\mathcal{T}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Now
\bigcup\mathcal{T}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\in T
by Definition
1.1
(
7
) and Lemma
2.1
(
1
), so by Definition
1.1
(
2
) there exists at least one (and in fact precisely one)
T\in\mathcal{T}
such that
T\in P
.
Now
T
is a transitive element in
\mathsf{Open}
, so by Proposition
3.13

P\subseteq\mathsf{Open}
is strongly compatible as required.
∎

Remark 8.8
.

We summarise what we have seen:

1.

The notions of (quasi/weak)regularity match up particularly nicely between a semitopology and its soberification as a semiframe (Proposition
6.2
).

2.

We saw in Proposition
7.3
that the notions of (un)conflicted point and unconflicted space from Definition
1.1
(
2
) are not robust under forming soberification (Notation
1.3
).
From the point of view of a pointless methodology in semitopologies — in which we seek to understand a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
starting from its semiframe structure
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
— this is a defect.

3.

A pointwise notion of strong compatibility exists; by Lemma
8.2
it is actually hypertransitivity from Definition
5.4
.
This is preserved pointwise by soberification (Lemma
8.3
),
but soberification can still introduce
extra
points, and it turns out that the property of a space being pointwise hypertransitive is still not robust under soberification because the extra points need not necessarily be hypertransitive; see Lemma
8.4
.

4.

This motivates Definition
8.5
, and then Proposition
8.6
becomes easy.
Our larger point (no pun intended) is the Definition and its corresponding Proposition are natural,
and also
that the other design decisions are
less
natural, as noted above.

Perhaps somewhat unexpectedly, ‘regular = weakly regular + strongly compatible’ then works pointwise
and
for the entire space; see Theorem
8.7
.
Thus Definition
8.5
has good properties and is natural from a pointless/semiframe/open sets perspective.

Chapter 16
Graph representation of semitopologies

A substantial body of literature exists studying social networks as connected graphs.
A semitopology has the flavour of a social network, in the sense that it models voting and consensus on a distributed system.

It is therefore interesting to consider representations of semitopologies as graphs.
We will consider two ways to do this:

1.

We can map a semitopology to the intersection graph of its open sets.
We discuss it in Subsection
1
.
This works well but loses information (Remark
1.16
).

2.

We can use a
subintersection
relation between sets.
We discuss this in Subsection
2
.

1
From a semitopology to its intersection graph

We start with a very simple representation of
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
obtained just as the
intersection graph
of
\mathsf{Open}
(Definition
1.1
).
This is not necessarily the most detailed representation (we spell out why, with examples, in Remark
1.16
), but it is still simple, direct, and nontrivial:

1
The basic definition

Definition 1.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Define its
intersection graph
\mathsf{IntGr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
by:

•

The nodes of
\mathsf{IntGr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
are nonempty open sets
O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\neq\varnothing}
.

•

There is an edge
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
between
O
and
O^{\prime}
when
O\between O^{\prime}
.

Remark 1.2
.

1.

The notion of the
intersection graph
of a set of sets is standard.
1
1

1

See e.g. the
Wikipedia page on intersection graphs
(
permalink
).

The notion used in Definition
1.1
is slightly different, in that we exclude the empty set.
This technical tweak is mildly useful, to give us Lemma
1.4
.

2.

If
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
\mathsf{IntrGr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is its intersection graph in the sense of Definition
1.1
, then
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
is a synonym for
O\between O^{\prime}
.
But, writing
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
suggests that we view
O
and
O^{\prime}
as nodes.

Notation 1.3
.

For the rest of this Section we assume a fixed but arbitrary

G=\mathsf{IntGr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})

that is the open intersection graph of a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

We start with an easy lemma:

Lemma 1.4
.

O\leftrightarrow O
always (the graph
G
is reflexive).

Proof.

From Definition
1.1
, noting that nodes are
nonempty
open sets
O\in\mathsf{Open}
, and it is a fact of sets intersection that
O\between O
when
O
is nonempty.
∎

2
The node preorder
\leq

Definition 1.5
.

Write
O\leq O^{\prime}
when for every
O^{\prime\prime}
,
if
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime\prime}
then
O^{\prime}\leftrightarrow O^{\prime\prime}
.
In symbols:

O\leq O^{\prime}\quad\text{when}\quad\forall O^{\prime\prime}.O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime\prime}\Longrightarrow O^{\prime}\leftrightarrow O^{\prime\prime}.

We note that
\leq
from Definition
1.5
is a preorder (reflexive and transitive relation):

Lemma 1.6
.

1.

\leq
is reflexive:
O\leq O
.

2.

\leq
is transitive: if
O\leq O^{\prime}\leq O^{\prime\prime}
then
O\leq O^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

By routine calculations.
∎

Lemma 1.7
.

1.

If
O\leq O^{\prime}
then
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
.

2.

It is not in general the case that
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
implies
O\leq O^{\prime}
(but see Proposition
1.12
(1&2)).

In symbols we can write:
\leq\subseteq\leftrightarrow
and
\leftrightarrow\not\subseteq\leq
in general.
2
2

2

It gets better: see Lemma
1.9
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
O\leq O^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
1.4

O\leftrightarrow O
, and it follows (since
O\leq O^{\prime}
) that
O^{\prime}\leftrightarrow O
as required.

2.

It suffices to give a counterexample.
Consider the semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
where
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
O=\{0,1\}
,
O^{\prime}=\{1,2\}
, and
O^{\prime\prime}=\{0\}
, as illustrated in Figure
1
.
Then
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
but
O\not\leq O^{\prime}
since
O\leftrightarrow\{0\}
but
O^{\prime}\not\leftrightarrow\{0\}
.
∎

Figure 1
:
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
but
O\not\leq O^{\prime}
(Lemma
1.7
(2))

Remark 1.8
.

Suppose
O\leq O^{\prime}
, so that by Lemma
1.7
also
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
.
We can illustrate Definition
1.5
in the style of a categorical diagram —

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\leq}

— expressing that
O\leq O^{\prime}
holds when every arrow out of
O
factorises through
O^{\prime}
.

Lemma 1.9

(
\leq
generalises
\subseteq
)
.

We have:

1.

If
O\subseteq O^{\prime}
then
O\leq O^{\prime}
.

2.

The converse implication need not hold:
O\leq O^{\prime}
does not necessarily imply
O\subseteq O^{\prime}
.
3
3

3

So to sum up this and Lemma
1.7
:
\subseteq\subseteq\leq\subseteq\leftrightarrow
, and the inclusion may be strict:
\subseteq\subsetneq\leq
in general.

Proof.

1.

A fact of sets.

2.

It suffices to give a counterexample.
Set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\{0\},\{0,1\}\}
.
This generates a very simple graph
G
as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0,1\}}

The reader can check that
\{0,1\}\leq\{0\}
, but
\{0,1\}\not\subseteq\{0\}
.
∎

3
Transitive elements

Definition 1.10
.

Call
T\in G

transitive
when for every
O,O^{\prime}\in G
we have that

O\leftrightarrow T\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}\quad\text{implies}\quad O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}.

In pictures:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces O^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists}

Lemma 1.11
.

Suppose
T\in G
is a node.
Then the following are equivalent:

•

T
is transitive in the sense of Definition
2.1
(
1
).
4
4

4

Equivalently,
T
is topen (Definition
2.1
(
2
)), since every node in
G
is a nonempty open.

•

T
is transitive in the sense of Definition
1.10
.

Proof.

Just unfolding Definitions
1.1
and
2.1
.
∎

Lemmas
1.7
and
1.9
suggest that
\leq
generalises subset inclusion
\leq
.
In this light, Proposition
1.12
makes transitive elements look like singleton sets:

Proposition 1.12
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

T
is transitive.

2.

\forall O.(T\leftrightarrow O\Longrightarrow T\leq O)
.

3.

\forall O.(T\leftrightarrow O\Longleftrightarrow T\leq O)
.

Proof.

The lower equivalence just follows from Lemma
1.7
.

For the top equivalence, we prove two implications:

•

The top-down implication.

Suppose
T
is transitive and suppose
T\leftrightarrow O
.
To prove
T\leq O^{\prime}
it suffices to consider any
O^{\prime}
and show that
T\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
implies
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
.

But this is just from transitivity and the fact that
\leftrightarrow
is symmetric:
O\leftrightarrow T\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
implies
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
.

•

The bottom-up implication.

Suppose for every
O
, if
T\leftrightarrow O
then
T\leq O
, and suppose
O\leftrightarrow T\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
.
Because
T\leq O
and
T\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
, we have
O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime}
as required.
∎

Corollary 1.13
.

1.

If
T
is transitive then
T
is
\leq
-least.
That is:

O\leq T\quad\text{implies}\quad T\leq O.

2.

The converse implication does not hold: it is possible for
T
to be
\leq
-least and not transitive.

Proof.

1.

Suppose
T
is transitive and
O\leq T
.
By Lemma
1.9
(3)
O\leftrightarrow T
and by Proposition
1.12
(2)
T\leq O
.

2.

It suffices to provide a counterexample.
Consider the semitopology illustrated in Figure
2
.
It is a fact that
A
is not transitive,
yet
A
is
\leq
-least:
A\not\leq B
(because
B\leftrightarrow D
yet
A\not\leftrightarrow D
) and similarly
A\not\leq C
, and
A\not\leq D
(because
A\not\leftrightarrow D
).
∎

Definition 1.14
.

Suppose
O,O^{\prime}\in G
.
Define
O\approx O^{\prime}
when
O\leq O^{\prime}\land O^{\prime}\leq O
, and in this case call
O
and
O^{\prime}

extensionally equivalent
.
It is easy to see from Definition
1.5
that

O\approx O^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow\forall O^{\prime\prime}.(O\leftrightarrow O^{\prime\prime}\Longleftrightarrow O^{\prime}\leftrightarrow O^{\prime\prime}).

Corollary 1.15
.

1.

If
T
and
T^{\prime}
are transitive (Definition
1.10
) then the following are equivalent:

T\leq T^{\prime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad T^{\prime}\leq T\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad T\leftrightarrow T^{\prime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad T\approx T^{\prime}.

2.

As a corollary, if
T
and
T^{\prime}
are transitive then
T\leftrightarrow T^{\prime}
if and only if
T\approx T^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The left-hand equivalence is from Corollary
1.13
(since
T
and
T^{\prime}
are transitive).
The middle equivalence is from Proposition
1.12
.
The right-hand equivalent follows from the left-hand and middle equivalences using Definitions
1.5
and
1.14
.

The corollary just repeats the right-hand equivalence.
∎

Remark 1.16

(Intersection graph loses information)
.

The proof of Corollary
1.15
(2) is not hard but it tells us something useful: the intersection graph identifies intersecting topens, and thus identifies a topen with the (by Corollary
5.3
) unique maximal topen that contains it.

Consider a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and its corresponding intersection graph
\mathsf{IntGr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, and consider some regular point
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
Recall from Theorem
2.6
that
\mathit{K}(p)
is the greatest topen (transitive open) neighbourhood of
p
.
Putting Corollary
1.15
(2), Theorem
2.6
, and Lemma
4.3
together, we have that
\mathit{K}(p)
— when considered as a node in the intersection graph of open sets — is extensionally equivalent to each of its topen subsets, and also (equivalently) to any topen set that it intersects with.

So, if we were to build a functor from intersection graphs back to semitopologies, by forming a notion of abstract point and mapping a node to the set of abstract points that contain it, then Corollary
1.15
tells us that this will map all connected transitive nodes down to a single point.
Thus, our intersection graph representation from Definition
1.1

loses information
.

It is easy to generate examples of this kind of information loss.
The following clearly different semitopologies give rise to isomorphic intersection graphs, namely: the full graph on three points, representing three pairwise intersecting nonempty open sets.

1.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}=\bigl\{\varnothing,\ \{0\},\ \{0,1\},\ \{0,1,2\}\bigr\}
.

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}^{\prime}=\bigl\{\varnothing,\ \{0,1\},\ \{1,2\},\ \{0,1,2\}\bigr\}
.

See Figure
2
; the intersection graph isomorphism is illustrated on the right (where we equate
\{0\}
with
\{1,2\}
).
The left-hand and middle examples in the figure are of intersecting topens, consistent with Corollary
1.15
(2).

Whether this behaviour is a feature or a bug depend on what we want to accomplish — but for our purposes of modelling networks, we may care to also consider a representation that retains more information.
In the next Subsection we consider a slightly more elaborate graph representation, which is more discriminating.

Figure 2
:
Semitopologies with isomorphic intersection graphs (Remark
1.16
)

2
From a semiframe to its subintersection graph

Remark 2.1
.

In Remark
1.16
we gave a natural representation of a semitopology as its intersection graph.
We noted in Corollary
1.15
that this identifies open sets up to a notion of extensional equivalence
\approx
given in Definition
1.14
, and because topen sets are extensionally equivalent if and only if they intersect by Corollary
1.15
, the intersection graph representation of semitopologies identifies two topens precisely when they intersect.

This is not wrong — intersection topen sets
are
extensionally equivalent, after all — but suppose we want to retain a bit more detail.
How can we proceed?

1
The subintersection relation
\ltimes

Remark 2.2
.

Notice that the notion of semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
from Definition
3.1
is based on
two
structures on
\bm{\mathsf{X}}
:

•

a semilattice relation
\leq
, and

•

a compatibility relation
\ast
.

Correspondingly, our notion of semitopology observes
two
properties of open sets: whether
O
is a subset of
O^{\prime}
, and whether
O
intersects
O^{\prime}
.

We can ask whether these two structures can be obtained from a single relation.
The answer is yes (if we are also allowed to observe equality): we can combine
\leq
and
\ast
into a single relation and so obtain a graph structure, without the loss of information we noted of intersection graphs.
The definition is as follows:

Definition 2.3
.

1.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
X\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
5
5

5

We will be most interested in the case that
P
is the set of points of a semitopology, but the definition does not depend on this.

Define
X^{c}
the
complement of
X
by
X^{c}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus X
.

2.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
X,Y\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Define a relation
X\ltimes Y
, read ‘
X

properly subintersects

Y
’, as follows:

X\ltimes Y\quad\text{when}\quad X\between Y\land X^{c}\between Y.

When
X\ltimes Y\lor X=Y
, we say that
X

subintersects

Y
.

3.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and
x,y\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Define a relation
x\ltimes y
, and say that
x

properly subintersects

y
, when

x\ltimes y\quad\text{when}\quad x\ast y\land y\not\leq x.

Example 2.4
.

Set
P=\{0,1,2\}
.
Then:

1.

\{0\}
properly subintersects
\{0,1\}
, because
\{0\}\between\{0,1\}
and
\{0,1,2\}\setminus\{0\}=\{1,2\}\between\{0,1\}
.
Similarly,
\{1\}
properly subintersects
\{0,1\}
.

2.

\{0,2\}
properly subintersects
\{0,1\}
, because
\{0,2\}\between\{0,1\}
and
\{0,1,2\}\setminus\{0,2\}=\{1\}\between\{0,1\}
.

3.

\{2\}
does not subintersect
\{0,1\}
, because
\{2\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{0,1\}
.

4.

\{0,1\}
does not subintersect
\{0\}
or
\{1\}
, because
\{2\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{0\}
and
\{2\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{1\}
and
\{2\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{0,1\}
.

5.

\{0,1\}
subintersects itself, but does not properly subintersect itself because
\{0,1,2\}\setminus\{0,1\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{0,1\}
.
More generally
X
subintersects itself by definition, but it does not properly subintersect itself (think:
X\subsetneq Y
vs.
X\subseteq Y
).

Remark 2.5

(One property, and three non-properties)
.

It is easy to show that
X\ltimes Y
is positive (covariant) in its second argument: if
X\ltimes Y
and
Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}
then
X\ltimes Y^{\prime}
.
However,
X\ltimes Y
is neither positive nor negative in its first argument, and it does not commute with intersection in its right argument.

Take
X,Y\subseteq\{0,1,2,3\}
.
Then:

1.

It is not the case that
X\ltimes Y\land X\subseteq X^{\prime}
implies
X^{\prime}\ltimes Y
.

Take
X=\{0\}
and
Y=\{0,1\}
and
X^{\prime}=\{0,1\}
.

2.

It is not the case that
X\ltimes Y\land X^{\prime}\subseteq X
implies
X^{\prime}\ltimes Y
.

Take
X=\{0,1\}
and
Y=\{1,2\}
and
X^{\prime}=\{0\}
.

3.

It is not the case that
X\ltimes Y\land X\ltimes Y^{\prime}
implies
X\ltimes(Y\cap Y^{\prime})
.

Take
X=\{0,1\}
and
Y=\{1,2\}
and
Y^{\prime}=\{1,3\}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
X,Y\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

X\ltimes Y
in the sense of Definition
2.3
(
2
).

2.

X\between Y\land Y\not\subseteq X
.

In other words:
X\ltimes Y
for
X
and
Y
considered as sets as per Definition
2.3
(
2
), precisely when
X\ltimes Y
for
X
and
Y
considered as elements in the semiframe
(\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}),\subseteq,\between)
as per Definition
2.3
(
3
).

Proof.

Routine, using the fact of sets that
Y\between X^{c}
if and only if
Y\not\subseteq X
.
∎

Corollary 2.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a spatial semiframe and
x,y\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
.
Then

x\ltimes y\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\mathit{Op}(x)\ltimes\mathit{Op}(y).

Proof.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a spatial semiframe.
By Proposition
1.4
(
2
&
1
)

x\ast y
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)\between\mathit{Op}(y)
, and
x\leq y
if and only if
\mathit{Op}(x)\subseteq\mathit{Op}(y)
.
We use Lemma
2.6
.
∎

2
Recovering
\leq
and
\ast
from
\ltimes

Remark 2.8
.

We can recover
\subseteq
and
\between
from
\ltimes
.
We can also recover
\leq
and
\ast
.
We consider the construction for semiframes and
\leq
and
\ast
, because it is the more general setting; the proofs for the concrete instance of
\subseteq
and
\between
are identical:

Proposition 2.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
is a semiframe and suppose
x,y\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.
Then:

1.

x\ast y
if and only if
x=y\lor x\ltimes y\lor y\ltimes x
.

2.

x\leq y
if and only if
x=y\lor(x\ltimes y\land\neg(y\ltimes x))
.

Proof.

We consider each implication in turn:

•

We show that
x\ast y
implies
x=y\lor x\ltimes y\lor y\ltimes x
.

Suppose
x\ast y
.
By antisymmetry of
\leq
, either
x=y
or
y\not\leq x
or
x\not\leq x
.
The result follows.

•

We show that
x=y\lor x\ltimes y\lor y\ltimes x
implies
x\ast y
.

By reversing the reasoning of the previous case.

•

We show that
x=y\lor(x\ltimes y\land\neg(y\ltimes x))
implies
x\leq y
.

Suppose
x=y\lor(x\ltimes y\land\neg(y\ltimes x))
.

If
x=y
then
x\leq y
and we are done.
If
x\neq y
then we unpack Definition
2.3
(
3
) and simplify as follows:

x\ltimes y\land\neg(y\ltimes x)\Longleftrightarrow x\ast y\land y\not\leq x\land(\neg(x\ast y)\lor x\leq y)\Longleftrightarrow\\
x\ast y\land y\not\leq x\land x\leq y\Longrightarrow x\leq y

•

We show that
x\leq y
implies
x=y\lor(x\ltimes y\land\neg(y\ltimes x))
.

Suppose
x\leq y
.
By assumption
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
, so by Lemma
2.3
(
2
)
x\ast y
.
If
x=y
then we are done; otherwise by antisymmetry
y\not\leq x
and again we are done.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

It follows from the above that a semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
can be presented as a graph
\mathsf{Gr}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
such that:

•

Nodes of the graph are elements
x\in\bm{\mathsf{X}}
such that
x\neq{\boldsymbol{\bot}}_{\bm{\mathsf{X}}}
.

•

There is an edge
x\to x^{\prime}
when
x\ltimes x^{\prime}
in the sense of Definition
2.3
(
3
) — that is, when
x\ast x^{\prime}\land x^{\prime}\not\leq x
.

Similarly we can present a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
as a graph
\mathsf{Gr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
such that:

•

Nodes of the graph are nonempty open sets
\varnothing\neq O\in\mathsf{Open}
.

•

There is an edge
O\to O^{\prime}
when
O\ltimes O^{\prime}
in the sense of Definition
2.3
(
2
) — that is, when
O\between O^{\prime}\land O^{c}\between O^{\prime}
.

These presentations are equivalent in the following sense: if we start from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and consider it as a semiframe
(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between)
(which is spatial by Proposition
2.4
) and then map to a graph, then we get the same graph as if we just map direct from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to the graph.
In symbols we can write:

\mathsf{Gr}(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})=\mathsf{Gr}(\mathsf{Open},\subseteq,\between).

By Proposition
2.9
, the mapping from
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
to
\mathsf{Gr}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
loses no information; we can view the graph as just a different way of presenting the same structure.

Remark 2.11
.

Although
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
and
\mathsf{Gr}(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
are in one-to-one correspondence as discussed in Remark
2.10
, the representations suggest different notions of morphism.

•

For a semiframe
(\bm{\mathsf{X}},\leq,\ast)
, the natural notion of morphism is a
{\leq}/{\ast}
-preserving map, in a suitable sense as defined in Definition
2.1
(
1
):
x\leq x^{\prime}
implies
g(x)\leq g(x^{\prime})
, and
g(x)\ast g(x^{\prime})
implies
x\ast x^{\prime}
.

•

For a graph
(\bm{\mathsf{G}},\ltimes)
, the natural notion of morphism is some notion of
\ltimes
-preserving map, and this is
not
necessarily the same as a
{\leq}/{\ast}
-preserving map, because
\ltimes
uses
\not\leq
.

If we still want to preserve notions of lattice structure and semifilter, then we look at how
\leq
is defined from
\ltimes
in Proposition
2.9
, and see that it uses both
\ltimes
and
\neg\ltimes
, and so in this case we may want a notion of morphism such that
x\ltimes x^{\prime}

if and only if

g(x)\ltimes g(x^{\prime})
.
Looked at from the point of view of
\leq
and
\ast
, this suggests that
x\leq x^{\prime}
if and only if
g(x)\leq g(x^{\prime})
, and
x\ast x^{\prime}
if and only if
g(x)\ast g(x^{\prime})
.

Investigating the design space here is future work.
See also Remark
3.3
.

Part 3
Logic and computation

Chapter 17
Three-valued logic

1
Three-valued logic, valuations, and continuity

Remark 1.1

(Setting the scene)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then two foundational questions of semitopologies, as we apply them, are:

1.

Determine whether
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
.
That is, determine whether every open neighbourhood of
p
intersects with every open neighbourhood of
p^{\prime}
.

2.

Determine whether a function
f
on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, whose values are known at
p
and
p^{\prime}
, can be continuously extended to a continuous function on the whole space.

Both of these questions are hard to answer if we just look at continuous functions from
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to the discrete space
\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{t}}\}
.
For example: the only continuous maps to
\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{t}}\}
from the top-left semitopology in
Figure
1
(
\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2\}
and
\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\{0\}
and
\{2\}
) are
\lambda p.{\mathbf{t}}
and
\lambda p.{\mathbf{f}}
.
This is so uninformative that it does not even distinguish between that semitopology and a space with just one point.

An answer from topology is to work with the Sierpiński space that we mentioned in Example
8.4
.
As is known, continuous functions in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathsf{Sk}
biject with
\mathsf{Open}
.
1
1

1

The
Wikipedia page
has a brief but clear description (
permalink
).

However, and perhaps surprisingly, this is still not enough!
As we noted in Remarks
1.7
and in Subsection
2
a semitopology is not
just
its collection of open sets.
We also need to know how they intersect.

It turns out that what we need is the semitopology
{\mathbf{3}}
, as illustrated in Figure
1
.
We can think of this as
\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{t}}\}
augmented with a third truth-value
{\mathbf{b}}
(for ‘both’), or as two Sierpiński spaces glued end-to-end.
Intuitively, a map
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
can encode whether a point is in some
O
, in some
O^{\prime}
, or in both — and this gives us the expressivity to express semitopological properties of interest.

As a domain of truth-values,
{\mathbf{3}}
will let us use logic to describe properties of semitopologies, including e.g. being intertwined (see Subsection
1
).
This matters in particular because logic is a portal to computation: if we can state something in logic, then we can compute it — using e.g. proof-search or a SAT solver.
And indeed, we have a prototype implementation of our logic, which inputs predicates in our logic and outputs syntax that can be checked in a real SAT solver
[
Los24
]
.

Thus the logical material that follows below, while a prototype, is still a substantive step in the direction of turning semitopologies into a practical and applicable specification and computational framework.

1
The semitopology
{\mathbf{3}}

Figure 1
:
The semitopology
{\mathbf{3}}
/ the set of truth values of three-valued logic

Definition 1.2
.

1.

Define a semitopology
({\mathbf{3}},\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbf{3}})
, as illustrated in Figure
1
, by

{\mathbf{3}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{f}}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbf{3}}=\{\varnothing,\{{\mathbf{t}}\},\{{\mathbf{f}}\},\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\},{\mathbf{3}}\}.

Closed sets of
({\mathbf{3}},\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbf{3}})
are

\mathsf{Closed}_{\mathbf{3}}=\{{\mathbf{3}},\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\},\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\},\{{\mathbf{b}}\},\varnothing\}.

We may write
({\mathbf{3}},\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbf{3}})
just as
{\mathbf{3}}
.

2.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set.
Call a function
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
a
valuation
.

3.

Call a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}

continuous
when
f
is continuous as a map of semitopologies from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to
({\mathbf{3}},\mathsf{Open}_{\mathbf{3}})
, as per Definition
2.1
(
2
) (inverse image of an open set is an open set).

Remark 1.3
.

{\mathbf{3}}
is essentially just the top-left example in Figure
1
, where in that Figure we identify
0
with
{\mathbf{f}}
,
1
with
{\mathbf{b}}
, and
2
with
{\mathbf{t}}
.

Lemma 1.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is continuous.

2.

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
and
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
are open sets.

3.

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
are closed sets (see also Definition
4.5
).

Proof.

By Corollary
1.12
, a
f
is continuous when the inverse image of every open set is open, and also when the inverse image of every closed set is closed.

It follows from Definition
1.2
(
1
) that
\{\{{\mathbf{t}}\},\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\}
is a subbasis for the open sets,
2
2

2

By which we mean a set that, together with
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, generates all open sets by forming arbitrary, possibly empty, unions.
and
\{\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\},\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\}
is a subbasis for the closed sets.
3
3

3

By which we mean a set that, together with
\varnothing
, generates all closed sets by forming arbitrary, possibly empty, intersections.

The result follows by routine calculations.
∎

2
Indicator functions and characteristic sets

Definition 1.5
.

Suppose that:

•

(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.

•

O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}\in\mathsf{Open}
and
C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}\in\mathsf{Closed}
.

•

O_{\mathbf{t}}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O_{\mathbf{f}}
and
C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}\cup C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
O_{\mathbf{t}}\subseteq C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}
.

Then define
indicator functions

\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}},\ \delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}},\ \delta_{C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}

as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}r@{\ }l}\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(p)=&\begin{cases}{\mathbf{t}}&p\in O_{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus(O_{\mathbf{t}}\cup O_{\mathbf{f}})\\
{\mathbf{f}}&p\in O_{\mathbf{f}}\end{cases}&\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(p)=&\begin{cases}{\mathbf{t}}&p\in O_{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&p\in C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}\setminus O_{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}\end{cases}\\[21.52771pt]
\delta_{C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(p)=&\begin{cases}{\mathbf{t}}&p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&p\in C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}\cap C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}\end{cases}\end{array}

Lemma 1.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose

O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}\in\mathsf{Open},\quad C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}\in\mathsf{Closed},\quad O_{\mathbf{t}}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O_{\mathbf{f}},\quad C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}\cup C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}=\bm{\mathsf{P}},\quad\text{and}\quad O_{\mathbf{t}}\subseteq C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}.

Then the indicator functions

\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}},\ \delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}},\ \delta_{C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}}\ \in\ \bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}

from Definition
1.5
are all continuous from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
to
{\mathbf{3}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
1.4
it suffices to check that the inverse images of
\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
and
\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
are open, or that the inverse images of
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
are closed.
This follows by construction in Definition
1.5
, noting from Lemma
1.9

that a set is open/closed if and only if its complement is closed/open.
∎

Definition 1.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
Then define its
characteristic sets

\mathit{char}_{OO}(f),\mathit{char}_{OC}(f),\mathit{char}_{CC}(f)\in\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\times\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}).

as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\mathit{char}_{OO}(f)=&(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\})\\
\mathit{char}_{OC}(f)=&(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}\})\\
\mathit{char}_{CC}(f)=&(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\})\end{array}

Remark 1.8
.

The pair of sets
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
will be particularly useful.
Later on in Definition
4.5
we will give them names —
f^{\vDash}
and
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
— and study their properties.

Lemma 1.9
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is continuous.
Then we have:

1.

\mathit{char}_{OO}(f)=(O_{{\mathbf{t}}},O_{{\mathbf{f}}})\in\mathsf{Open}\times\mathsf{Open}
and
O_{\mathbf{t}}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O_{\mathbf{f}}
.

2.

\mathit{char}_{OC}(f)=(O_{{\mathbf{t}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}})\in\mathsf{Open}\times\mathsf{Closed}
and
O_{\mathbf{t}}\subseteq C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}
.

3.

\mathit{char}_{CC}(f)=(C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}})\in\mathsf{Closed}\times\mathsf{Closed}
and
C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}\cup C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

By Corollary
1.12
the inverse image of an open set under a continuous function is open and that of a closed set is closed.
The conditions
O_{\mathbf{t}}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O_{\mathbf{f}}
,
O_{\mathbf{t}}\subseteq C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}
, and
C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}\cup C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
come from the fact that
\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
,
\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\subseteq\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
, and
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\cup\{{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{f}}\}={\mathbf{3}}
.
∎

Proposition 1.10
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then
\delta_{\text{-}}
and
\mathit{char}(\text{-})
from Definitions
1.5
and
1.7

determine bijections between:

1.

Continuous valuations
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to{\mathbf{3}}
.

2.

Ordered pairs of disjoint (possibly empty) open sets:

(O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}})\in\mathsf{Open}\times\mathsf{Open}
such that
O_{\mathbf{t}}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O_{\mathbf{f}}
.

3.

Ordered pairs of an open set contained in a closed set:

(O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}})\in\mathsf{Open}\times\mathsf{Closed}
such that
O_{\mathbf{t}}\subseteq C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}
.

Note that
|O_{\mathbf{t}}|\subseteq C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}
by construction, but we do not require an equality.

4.

Ordered pairs of closed sets whose union is
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
:

(C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}})\in\mathsf{Closed}\times\mathsf{Closed}
such that
C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}\cup C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

From Lemmas
1.6
and
1.9
and by routine computations on sets.
∎

Corollary 1.11
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

The following are equivalent:

(a)

O\in\mathsf{Open}
.

(b)

There exists a continuous valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
such that
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}=O
.

(c)

There exists a continuous valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
such that
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}=O
.

2.

The following are equivalent (and see also Definition
4.5
):

(a)

C\in\mathsf{Closed}
.

(b)

There exists a continuous
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
such that
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}=C
.

(c)

There exists a continuous
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
such that
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}=C
.

Proof.

Routine using Proposition
1.10
.
For example, we can map
O
to
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}}O_{\mathbf{f}}}(O,\varnothing)
.
∎

2
Three-valued truth-tables

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c}\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&\underline{{\mathbf{b}}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}\\[30.1388pt]
\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&\underline{{\mathbf{f}}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}\\[30.1388pt]
\begin{array}[]{cc}\Box p\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\Diamond p\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{B}p\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}\end{array}

Above, the vertical axis of a table indicates values for
p
; the horizontal axis (if nontrivial) denotes values for
q
.

Figure 2
:
Truth-tables for three-valued logic (Definition
2.1
)

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\qquad}r@{\ }l}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p=&p\\
p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q)\\
p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q)\\
p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q=&({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}q\\
p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}{\mathbf{f}}=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p\\
p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q=&(p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(q{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p)=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q)\\
p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q=&(\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}q=(\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}q\\
p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}q=&(p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(q{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}p)\\
\Box p=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\mathbf{f}}\\
\Diamond p=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p={\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\mathbf{f}})=(p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\mathbf{f}}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\mathbf{f}}\\
\mathsf{B}p=&\Diamond(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p)=\Diamond p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\Diamond{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p=\Diamond p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Box p\\
\Box\Diamond p=&\Diamond p\\
\Diamond(p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q)=&(\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\Diamond q\\
\mathsf{M}(p\circ q)=&\mathsf{M}p\circ\mathsf{M}q\qquad\quad(\mathsf{M}\in\{\Box,\Diamond\},\ {\circ}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\wedge}},{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\})\end{array}

Figure 3
:
Some truth-table equivalences (Lemma
2.2
)

Having three truth-values gives us a great deal of extra structure over the two-valued case.
In this Subsection we survey the connectives that will be useful to us later.

1
Truth-tables of connectives

Definition 2.1
.

We define

1.

unary functions
Q\in\{{\boldsymbol{\neg}},\Box,\Diamond,\mathsf{B}\}
where
Q:{\mathbf{3}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
, and

2.

binary functions
Q\in\{{\boldsymbol{\wedge}},{\boldsymbol{\vee}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}},\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\}
where
Q:({\mathbf{3}}\times{\mathbf{3}})\to{\mathbf{3}}

by the truth-tables in Figure
2
.

Lemma 2.2
.

The truth-tables in Figure
2
are related as per the equalities in Figure
3
, where for the purposes of these equations,
p
and
q
are considered to range over elements of
{\mathbf{3}}
.

Proof.

By checking truth-tables.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

Definition
2.1
and Figure
2
and Lemma
2.2
and Figure
3
are elementary, but they express some useful observations:

1.

Figure
2
presents truth-tables for propositional connectives in a three-valued paraconsistent logic.
4
4

4

See
Wikipedia
(
permalink
).

There is nothing particularly unusual here within the genre of three-valued logic: we have three truth-values, and the connectives do what they do.

2.

{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
in Figure
2
are least upper bound and greatest lower bound operators on
{\mathbf{3}}
considered as a simple lattice with
{\mathbf{f}}\leq{\mathbf{b}}\leq{\mathbf{t}}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
inverts the lattice order.
More on this in Subsection
2
.

3.

The equivalence
p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}q
from Figure
3
characterises

\supset

as a
material implication

[
Edg20
]
(
permalink
).
Similarly,
p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q=(p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(q{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q)
is a
material equivalence
.

The symbols

\supset

and

\equiv

for material implication and equivalence follow the (now standard) notation used in
[
WR10
, page 7]
.
5
5

5

The phrase
material implication
goes back to Russell in 1903, so far as we are aware, specifically to
this passage
(
permalink
).

4.

\Box p
and
\Diamond p
are de Morgan duals, as per the equivalences
\Box p={\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
and
\Diamond p={\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
in Figure
3
.

5.

The equivalence

\mathsf{M}(p\circ q)=\mathsf{M}p\circ\mathsf{M}q\quad(\mathsf{M}\in\{\Box,\Diamond\},\ {\circ}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\wedge}},{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\})

is a little unexpected; it means in particular that we have these two properties:

\Box(p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q)=\Box p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Box q\quad\text{and}\quad\Diamond(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q)=\Diamond p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\Diamond q.

If we think of
\Box
and
\Diamond
as modalities, as our notation suggests, and look at these two properties in terms of Kripke structures
[
BRV01
]
, then it is not hard to see that they are characteristic of Kripke frames in which each world sees precisely one world.
6
6

6

Our denotation for
\Box
and
\Diamond
uses truth-values in
{\mathbf{3}}
, not Kripke structures. We are just pointing out where such axioms can and do arise in Kripke semantics for modalities.

6.

We take a moment to compute the equivalence
\Diamond(p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q)=(\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\Diamond q
from the other equivalences:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\Diamond(p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q)=&\Diamond({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q)=(\Diamond{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Diamond q=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Box\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Diamond q=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Diamond q\\
(\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\Diamond q=&({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Diamond q=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Diamond q\end{array}

2
Conjunction and disjunction

Definition 2.4
.

Suppose
V\subseteq{\mathbf{3}}
is a set of truth-values.
Define
\bigwedge V
and
\bigvee V
to be the least upper bound and greatest lower bound of
V
in
{\mathbf{3}}
considered as a lattice with
{\mathbf{f}}<{\mathbf{b}}<{\mathbf{t}}
.

As noted in Remark
2.3
(
2
), the truth-tables for
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
in Figure
2
also compute least upper bounds and greater lower bounds, so that
v{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}v^{\prime}=\bigwedge\{v,v^{\prime}\}
and
v{\boldsymbol{\vee}}v^{\prime}=\bigvee\{v,v^{\prime}\}
.
We may elide the difference between
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
-the-binary-operator and its generalisation to
\bigwedge
henceforth, and similarly for
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
and
\bigvee
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Suppose
V\subseteq{\mathbf{3}}
is a set of truth-values.
Then:

1.

\bigwedge V\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
if and only if
V\subseteq\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

2.

\bigwedge V={\mathbf{f}}
if and only if
{\mathbf{f}}\in V
.

3.

\bigvee V\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
if and only if
V\between\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

4.

\bigvee V={\mathbf{f}}
if and only if
V\subseteq\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
.

Proof.

A fact of Definition
2.4
.
∎

3
Implication(s)

Remark 2.6

(Two implications)
.

Figure
2
has two implication operators;

\supset

(material implication) and also
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
(we underline the differences in the truth-tables in Figure
2
).

Having multiple implication connectives is in a paraconsistent logic is typical: e.g. the authors of
[
AAZ11
]
note the existence of
sixteen
possible implications just in a three-valued logic
[
AAZ11
, note 5, page 22]
.

We chose to include

\supset

and
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
as primitive, from the sixteen implications available because — as we shall see — these connectives will be especially useful.
See — just for example — Proposition
4.14
(
3
), Corollary
4.15
, Definitions
1.1
and
4.2
, and Figure
2
.

We make some simple observations:

Lemma 2.7
.

For
v,v^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}
we have:

1.

v{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\neg}}v^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}v

2.

It is not necessarily the case that
v{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}v^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\neg}}v^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}v
.

In words: the contrapositive rule is valid for

\supset

, but not for
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
.

Proof.

We compute and compare truth-tables; differences are underlined:
