∎

Remark 2.8
.

We noted in Figure
3
and Lemma
2.2
that

p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q=(\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}q.

A minimal set of propositional connectives for our logic could leave out
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
— and then perhaps leave out

\supset

in favour of
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
, and arrive at (say)
\{{\mathbf{f}},{\boldsymbol{\vee}},{\boldsymbol{\neg}},\Diamond\}
.
More discussion of
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
and

\supset

is in Subsection
4
.

3
Predicates

Notation 3.1
.

For the rest of this paper fix a countably infinite set of
variable symbols
\mathsf{Var}
.
We will use variable symbols to construct our syntax in Definition
3.2
.

Definition 3.2
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is any set; we will always assume this is disjoint from
\mathsf{Var}
.
Define the syntax
\mathsf{Pred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
of
predicates over
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
by the grammar in Figure
4
.

In this syntax:

1.

We may call
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
a
propositional atom
or
propositional constant
.
When we give our syntax a denotation, it will get assigned a truth-value in
{\mathbf{3}}
.

2.

Variable symbols
x\in\mathsf{Var}
range over the propositional atoms.

3.

\forall

and

\exists

are quantifiers.
They bind variable symbols.
We treat predicates up to
\alpha
-equivalence henceforth.

4.

We define the
free variables of a predicate
as usual:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l}\mathit{fv}(v)=&\mathit{fv}(p)=\varnothing&\mathit{fv}(\mathsf{Q}\phi)=&\mathit{fv}(\phi)\\
\mathit{fv}(\phi\mathsf{Q}\phi^{\prime})=&\mathit{fv}(\phi)\cup\mathit{fv}(\phi^{\prime})\\
\mathit{fv}({\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi)=&\mathit{fv}(\phi)=\mathit{fv}({\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi)&\mathit{fv}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi)=&\mathit{fv}(\phi){\setminus}\{x\}=\mathit{fv}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi)\end{array}

Note that
\mathit{fv}(\phi)
does not include the propositional constants in
\phi
; we only include the free variables.

5.

We call a predicate
closed
when it has no free variables.
We write
\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
for the set of closed predicates in
\mathsf{Pred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
, in symbols:

\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})=\{\phi\in\mathsf{Pred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})\mid\mathit{fv}(\phi)=\varnothing\}.

Note that a closed predicate
\phi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
may still mention propositional constants from
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\phi::=&(v:v\in{\mathbf{3}})\mid(x:x\in\mathsf{Var})\mid(p:p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}})\\
\mid&(\mathsf{Q}\phi:\mathsf{Q}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\neg}},\Box,\Diamond,\mathsf{B}\})\\
\mid&(\phi\mathsf{Q}\phi^{\prime}:\mathsf{Q}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\wedge}},{\boldsymbol{\vee}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}},\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\})\\
\mid&{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi\mid{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi\\
\mid&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi\mid{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi\end{array}

Figure 4
:
Predicate syntax

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[v]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}=&v&v\in{\mathbf{3}}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[p]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}=&f(p)&p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{Q}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}=&Q\,{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}&\mathsf{Q}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\neg}},\Box,\Diamond,\mathsf{B}\}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi\mathsf{Q}\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}=&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\,Q\,{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}&\mathsf{Q}\in\{{\boldsymbol{\wedge}},{\boldsymbol{\vee}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}},\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\}\\[8.61108pt]
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}=&\bigwedge\{{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\mid f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}\}&\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}=&\bigvee\{{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\mid f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}\}&\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}=&\bigwedge\{{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}=&\bigvee\{{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}\end{array}

Figure 5
:
Denotation of a predicate
\phi
with respect to a valuation
f

Definition 3.3

(Denotation)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology on a set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation (Definition
1.2
(
2
)), and suppose
\phi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a closed predicate over
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
(Definition
3.2
(
5
)).

Then define
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}
inductively as in Figure
5
, where:

1.

The unary and binary connectives (
\{{\boldsymbol{\neg}},\Box,\Diamond,\mathsf{B}\}
and
\{{\boldsymbol{\wedge}},{\boldsymbol{\vee}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}},\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\}
) are interpreted using the tables in Figure
2
, as discussed in Remark
3.4
.

2.

\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]
is obtained from
\phi
by replacing (substituting)
x
with
p
throughout.

Remark 3.4
.

For each unary and binary connective
Q
considered in Figure
2
there is a corresponding unary or binary syntax connective
\mathsf{Q}
in the syntax of Figure
4
.
(This is no different from how in the usual two-valued propositional logic, the symbol ‘
\land
’ can represent both a function
\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{B}\to\mathbb{B}
and also a predicate connective.)

Each
\mathsf{Q}
is interpreted as the corresponding
Q
when we define the denotation in Figure
5
.

Remark 3.5
.

1.

The denotation of the connectives in
\{{\boldsymbol{\neg}},\Box,\Diamond,\mathsf{B}\}
and
\{{\boldsymbol{\wedge}},{\boldsymbol{\vee}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}},\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\}
is straightforward: we look up the input(s) in the corresponding truth-table in Figure
2
, and return the corresponding output; see Remark
3.4
.

2.

The modalities
\Box
,
\Diamond
, and
\mathsf{B}
are not continuous as functions from
{\mathbf{3}}
to
{\mathbf{3}}
; e.g.
\lambda v.\Box v
is not continuous at
{\mathbf{b}}
.
Likewise
\lambda v.v{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\mathbf{f}}
is not continuous.

3.

{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi
takes a conjunction (greatest lower bound) over the truth-values of
\phi
for every possible assignment of truth-values from
{\mathbf{3}}
to the propositional atoms in
\phi
.

Intuitively,
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
universally quantifies over all possible valuations; if we view valuations as possible worlds, then
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
is a box-style modality.

4.

{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi
is the de Morgan dual: it takes a disjunction (least upper bound) over the truth-values of
\phi
for every possible assignment of truth-values from
{\mathbf{3}}
to the propositional atoms in
\phi
.

Intuitively,
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
existentially quantifies over all possible valuations; if we view valuations as possible worlds, then
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
is a diamond-style modality.

5.

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi
takes a conjunction over the truth-values of
\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
So

\forall

quantifies universally over propositional atoms.

6.

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi
takes a disjunction over the truth-values of
\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
So

\exists

quantifies existentially over propositional atoms.

4
Validity

1
The definition

Definition 4.1

(Validity)
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation and
\phi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a closed predicate.

1.

Define
f\vDash\phi
by

f\vDash\phi\quad\text{when}\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}.

In this case we call
\phi

valid
in
f
.

2.

We may call
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
the set of
designated values
, following a terminology from paraconsistent logic — these are the
valid
truth-values.

Note that
{\mathbf{t}}
is valid, as we would expect, but so is
{\mathbf{b}}
.

3.

If
f
does not matter to calculating
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}
(e.g. because
\phi
mentions no variable symbols or predicate atoms) then we may write
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}
just as
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
, and
f\vDash\phi
just as
\vDash\phi
.

4.

Suppose
\Phi\subseteq\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a set of predicates.
Write

f\vDash\Phi\quad\text{when}\quad\forall\phi{\in}\Phi.f\vDash\phi,

and say that
\Phi
is
valid
in
f
.

5.

Write
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash\phi
when
f\vDash\phi
for every valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
and similarly for
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash\Phi
.

6.

Write
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash\phi
when
f\vDash\phi
for every valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, and similarly for
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash\Phi
and
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash\Phi
.

Definition 4.2
.

A
sequent
is a pair of finite sets of predicates
\Phi\vdash\Psi
.

We define a notion of
sequent validity
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash(\Phi\vdash\Psi)
by

\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash(\Phi\vdash\Psi)\quad\text{when}\quad\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\Phi){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}({\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Psi).

Remark 4.3
.

Recall from Remark
2.3
(
3
) that

\supset

is a material implication.
Yet
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
can also claim to be an implication operator, and unlike

\supset

it satisfies Modus Ponens.
We examine this in Propositions
4.12
and
4.14
.

In Definition
4.1
(
2
) we noted that
{\mathbf{t}}
is valid, but so is
{\mathbf{b}}
.
We can make this formal with a small lemma:

Lemma 4.4
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation and
\phi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
Then

f\vDash\phi\quad\text{if and only if}\quad f\vDash\Diamond\phi.

Proof.

Simply because
\Diamond v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
if and only if
v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
, for every
v\in{\mathbf{3}}
.
∎

2
f^{\vDash}
and
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
: the designated sets of a valuation

In this easy Subsection we continue Remark
1.8
and follow a basic exercise in unpacking the definitions.
The only extra ingredient we have now, relative to the results surrounding Remark
1.8
, is that we can interpret an element of
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
as ‘valid’:

Definition 4.5
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.
Then define the
designated set
f^{\vDash}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and the
neg-designated set
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
of the valuation
f
by:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}f^{\vDash}=&f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\\
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}=&f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\end{array}

Remark 4.6
.

1.

We call
f^{\vDash}
the designated set of
f
because this is the set of
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
such that
f(p)
is a
designated value
(i.e. is
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
), as per Definition
4.1
(
2
).

2.

Similarly,
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
is the set of
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
such that
f({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p)
is a
designated value
— note that this is
not the same thing
as the set of
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
such that
f(p)
is not a designated value, because
\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
is not the same thing as
\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
.

3.

The pair
(f^{\vDash},f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}})
characterises
f
in the sense that

\mathit{char}_{CC}(f)=(f^{\vDash},f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad f=\delta_{C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(f^{\vDash},f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}),

where
\mathit{char}
is from Definition
1.7
, and
\delta
is from Definition
1.5
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a continuous function on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.
Then both
f^{\vDash}
and
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
are closed sets.

Proof.

By Definition
1.2
(
1
)
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
are closed sets in
{\mathbf{3}}
, and by
Corollary
1.12

the inverse image of a closed set under a continuous function is closed.
∎

The notation in Definition
4.5
is justified by the following very easy lemma:

Lemma 4.8
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.
Then:

1.

p\in f^{\vDash}
if and only if
f\vDash p
.

2.

p\in f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
if and only if
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
.

Proof.

Direct from Figure
2
, Definitions
4.5
and
4.1
(
1
), and the case for
f\vDash p
in Figure
5
.
∎

3
Validity of conjunction and quantification

The
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
and

\forall

are ‘obviously’ a conjunction and universal quantification respectively, but we still need to check that the truth-tables work and the notion of validity
\vDash
interacts correctly with them.
We do this and find that there are no surprises:

Lemma 4.9
.

\bm{\mathsf{P}}

.

.

.

(

)

∎

.

Suppose

is a set and

4
Logical implications

Remark 4.11
.

Continuing Remarks
2.3
(
3
) and
4.3
, Figure
2
has truth-tables for two binary connectives expressing notions of ‘implications’:

\supset

and
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
.
We will need both:

1.

{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
expresses logical implication (see Lemma
4.13
(
3
) and Proposition
4.14
(
3
)).

2.

\supset

helps us express the property of ‘being intertwined’ in (see Lemma
1.3
and
1.6
).

Both implications interact nicely with
{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
:

Proposition 4.12
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is any set and
p,q,r\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.
Then:

1.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q){\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}r]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}(q{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}r)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}
.

2.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}r]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(q{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}r)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}
.

Proof.

We simplify using the equivalences in Figure
3
(we could also just check truth-tables, of course):

(p
∧
q)

\supset

r =

¬
(p
∧
q)
∨
r =
¬
p
∨¬
q
∨
r

p

\supset

(q

\supset

r) =

¬
p
∨¬
q
∨
r

(p
∧
q)
⇒
r =

¬
◇(p
∧
q)
∨
r =
¬
(◇p
∧
◇q)
∨
r =
□
¬
p
∨
□
¬
q
∨
r

p
⇒
(q
⇒
r) =

¬
◇p
∨

¬
◇q
∨
r = □
¬
p
∨
□
¬
q
∨
r .

∎

Lemma 4.13
.

Suppose
v,v^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}
.
Then:

1.

If
v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
implies
v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
, then
v{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

2.

It is possible that
v{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
, but
v^{\prime}\not\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

3.

v{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
if and only if
v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\Longrightarrow v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

We check the truth-table for

\supset

in Figure
2
and prove the contrapositive: if
v{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v^{\prime}={\mathbf{f}}
, then
v={\mathbf{t}}\land v^{\prime}={\mathbf{f}}
, and so
v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
does not imply
v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

2.

We set
v={\mathbf{b}}
and
v^{\prime}={\mathbf{f}}
and note from the truth-table for

\supset

in Figure
2
that
{\mathbf{b}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}{\mathbf{f}}={\mathbf{b}}
.

3.

We prove two implications:

•

For the right-to-left implication, we
prove the contrapositive.
Suppose
v{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}v^{\prime}={\mathbf{f}}
.
We check the truth-table for
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
in Figure
2
and see that
v^{\prime}={\mathbf{f}}
and
v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
Thus
v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
implies
v^{\prime}\not\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
, and it is
not
the case that
v\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
implies
v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

•

For the left-to-right implication, there are three sub-cases:

–

If
v^{\prime}={\mathbf{b}}
or
v^{\prime}={\mathbf{t}}
then
v^{\prime}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and there is nothing to prove.

–

If
v={\mathbf{f}}
then there is nothing to prove.

–

Suppose
v^{\prime}={\mathbf{f}}
and
v\in\{{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}}\}
.
We check the truth-table for
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
in Figure
2
and see that the result holds.
∎

Proposition 4.14
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation and
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
Then:

1.

If
f\vDash\phi
implies
f\vDash\phi^{\prime}
, then
f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}
.

2.

It is possible that
f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}
and
f\vDash\phi
but
f\not\vDash\phi^{\prime}
.

3.

f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi^{\prime}
if and only if
f\vDash\phi\Longrightarrow f\vDash\phi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.1
(
1
),
f\vDash\phi
when
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
, and similarly for
f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}
,
f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi^{\prime}
, and
f\vDash\phi^{\prime}
.
By Figure
5

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi\circ\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\circ{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}
for
\circ\in\{{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}},{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\}
.
We use Lemma
4.13
.
∎

We can pull the results in this Subsection together as follows:

Corollary 4.15
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation and
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
Then:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow&f\not\vDash\phi\lor f\vDash\phi^{\prime}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\Longrightarrow{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\\[8.61108pt]
f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow&f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\lor f\vDash\phi^{\prime}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\Longrightarrow{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}.\end{array}

Proof.

We reason as follows:

•

That
f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi^{\prime}
if and only if
f\not\vDash\phi\lor f\vDash\phi^{\prime}
just rephrases Proposition
4.14
(
3
).

•

That
f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}
if and only if
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\lor f\vDash\phi^{\prime}
follows from the characterisation
p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p){\boldsymbol{\vee}}q
of

\supset

as material equivalence in Figure
3
, and from Lemma
4.9
(2).

•

The rest of the equivalences just unpack the definition of validity from Definition
4.1
(
1
).
∎

5
Logical equivalence

Lemma
5.1
is closely related to Proposition
4.14
, though it is easiest to give a direct proof:

Lemma 5.1
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is a set and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation and
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
are closed predicates.
Then

f\vDash\phi\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad f\vDash\phi\ \Longleftrightarrow\ f\vDash\phi^{\prime}.

Proof.

A direct proof like that in Proposition
4.14
(
3
) is straightforward.
Or, we can note the equivalence
p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}q=(p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(q{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}p)
from Figure
3
, and use Proposition
4.14
(
3
) and Lemma
4.9
.
∎

Corollary 5.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f\vDash p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p^{\prime}
.

2.

p\in f^{\vDash}\Longleftrightarrow p^{\prime}\in f^{\vDash}
.

3.

p\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\Longleftrightarrow p^{\prime}\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
.

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 follows from Lemmas
5.1
and
4.8
(a direct calculation from the truth-table in Figure
2
is also straightforward).

Equivalence of parts 2 and 3 follows noting from Definition
4.5
that
f^{\vDash}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
so that

p\in f^{\vDash}\Longleftrightarrow p\not\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}

and

p^{\prime}\in f^{\vDash}\Longleftrightarrow p^{\prime}\not\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
.
∎

Corollary 5.3
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is any set and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation, and suppose
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Pred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
and
\mathit{fv}(\phi)\cup\mathit{fv}(\phi^{\prime})\subseteq\{x\}
, so that
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.(\phi\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime})
.

2.

f\vDash\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]\Longleftrightarrow f\vDash\phi^{\prime}[x{{:}\text{=}}p]
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

3.

\{p\mid f\vDash\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]\}=\{p\mid f\vDash\phi^{\prime}[x{{:}\text{=}}p]\}
.

4.

\{p\mid{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\}=\{p\mid{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}[x{{:}\text{=}}p]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\}
.

5.

\{p\mid{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={\mathbf{f}}\}=\{p\mid{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}[x{{:}\text{=}}p]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={\mathbf{f}}\}
.

Proof.

Equivalence of parts
1
and
2
is from Lemmas
5.1
and
4.9
.
Equivalence of parts
2
and
3
is a fact of sets.
Equivalence of parts
3
and
4
is immediate from Definition
4.1
(
1
).
Equivalence of parts
4
and
5
follows since
{\mathbf{3}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
.
∎

6
A sequent system

In this short subsection we develop a sequent system for our logic that is sound and complete with respect to validity.

The system here follows the style of the simple and elegant sequent system presented in
[
WM16
]
.
There are important differences: they consider a four-valued purely propositional logic, whereas we have a three-valued logic but with far richer connectives including modalities and quantifiers.
So the sequent system here can be viewed as an elaboration of a simplification of the one in
[
WM16
]
.

But in a way, the details do not matter.
What matters is that a legitimate notion of derivation is shown to exist, so that we can talk meaningfully about derivability and proof-search.

Definition 6.1
.

For this Subsection, we fix some finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
of propositional constant, as per Definition
3.2
(
1
).

Definition 6.2
.

1.

Define
tags
{\mathit{tb}}
,
{\mathit{ff}}
,
{\mathit{fb}}
, and
{\mathit{tt}}
by:

{\mathit{tb}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\quad{\mathit{ff}}=\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\quad{\mathit{fb}}=\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\quad{\mathit{tt}}=\{{\mathbf{t}}\}

We will let
\mathtt{a}
and
\mathtt{b}
range over tags.

2.

A
tag-sequent
\Sigma
is a finite set of pairs
\mathtt{a}:\phi
where
\mathtt{a}
is a tag and
\phi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a closed predicate (Definition
3.2
(
5
)).

3.

Define a
\mathit{neg}
operation on tags such that
\mathit{neg}(t)=\{{\boldsymbol{\neg}}x\mid x\in t\}
.
Spelling this out:

\mathit{neg}({\mathit{tb}})={\mathit{fb}}\quad\mathit{neg}({\mathit{fb}})={\mathit{tb}}\quad\mathit{neg}({\mathit{ff}})={\mathit{tt}}\quad\mathit{neg}({\mathit{tt}})={\mathit{ff}}

4.

The
derivable tag-sequents
are defined inductively by the sequent rules in Figure
6
.

Note that Figure
6
presents a subsystem of the logic, but this is fine because we can derive rules for
\Diamond
,
\mathsf{B}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x
,
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
,

\supset

,

\equiv

,
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
,

\Leftrightarrow

, and
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
,
as per the following equivalences:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l}\Diamond\phi=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi&\mathsf{B}\phi=&\Diamond\phi{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Box\phi&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\\
\phi{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}=&({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi){\boldsymbol{\vee}}\phi^{\prime}&\phi{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}=&(\phi{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(\phi^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi)&\phi{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\phi^{\prime}=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi^{\prime}))\\
\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi^{\prime}=&(\Diamond\phi){\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\phi^{\prime}&\phi\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}=&(\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(\phi^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi)&{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\end{array}

5.

Write
\vdash\Sigma
for the judgement ‘
\Sigma
is derivable’.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\big((\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b})\in\{({\mathit{tb}},{\mathit{ff}}),({\mathit{fb}},{\mathit{tt}})\big)$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=98.56073pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf(Ax)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 20.2944pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\phi,\mathtt{b}:\phi$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash\Sigma,\mathit{neg}(\mathtt{a}):\phi$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=63.29686pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\neg}}LR)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 10.48877pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi$}}}}\\[21.52771pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\phi\quad\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\psi\quad(\mathtt{a}{\in}\{{\mathit{tb}},{\mathit{tt}}\})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=141.67737pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}R)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 45.03322pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\phi{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\phi,\mathtt{a}:\psi\quad(\mathtt{a}{\in}\{{\mathit{fb}},{\mathit{ff}}\})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=114.79968pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}L)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 31.59438pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\phi{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi$}}}}\\[21.52771pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash\Sigma,{\mathit{tt}}:\phi\quad(\mathtt{a}{\in}\{{\mathit{tb}},{\mathit{tt}}\})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=96.86357pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf(\Box R)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 25.32771pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\Box\phi$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash\Sigma,{\mathit{ff}}:\phi,{\mathit{fb}}:\phi\quad(\mathtt{a}{\in}\{{\mathit{fb}},{\mathit{ff}}\})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=117.54411pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf(\Box L)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 35.66798pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\Box\phi$}}}}\\[21.52771pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash\mathtt{a}:\phi\quad(\mathtt{a}{\in}\{{\mathit{tb}},{\mathit{tt}}\})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=82.69142pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf({\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,R)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 9.60277pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,(\phi)$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\not\vdash\mathtt{a}:\phi\quad((\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}){\in}\{({\mathit{tb}},{\mathit{ff}}),({\mathit{tt}},{\mathit{fb}})\})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=142.57468pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf({\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,L)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 39.5444pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{b}:{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,(\phi)$}}}}\\[21.52771pt]
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]\quad(\mathtt{a}{\in}\{{\mathit{tt}},{\mathit{tb}}\},\text{every $p$})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=156.55257pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}R)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 45.70343pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:\phi[x{{:}\text{=}}p]\quad(\mathtt{a}{\in}\{{\mathit{fb}},{\mathit{ff}}\})$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=121.56021pt\hbox{\kern 3.00003pt${\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}L)}}$}}}\hbox{\kern 28.20726pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\Sigma,\mathtt{a}:{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\phi$}}}}\end{array}

Remark 6.3
.

The derivation rule
{\mathrm{\bf({\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,L)}}
is unusual because it has
\not\vdash
above the line.
This is still a well-defined inductive definition, by induction on the syntax of the tag-sequent.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose
\phi,\psi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
and suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
We check properties corresponding to soundness and completeness of the rules in Figure
6
:

1.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf(Ax)}}
with
(\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b})=({\mathit{tb}},{\mathit{ff}})
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tb}}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{ff}}
is a fact.

2.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf(Ax)}}
with
(\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b})=({\mathit{fb}},{\mathit{tt}})
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{fb}}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tt}}
is a fact.

3.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\neg}}R)}}
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in x
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\mathit{neg}(x)
.

4.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}R)}}
with
\mathtt{a}={\mathit{tb}}
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tb}}\land{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tb}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tb}}
.

5.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}R)}}
with
\mathtt{a}={\mathit{tt}}
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tt}}\land{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tt}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tt}}
.

6.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}L)}}
with
\mathtt{a}={\mathit{fb}}
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{fb}}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{fb}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{fb}}
.

7.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}L)}}
with
\mathtt{a}={\mathit{ff}}
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{ff}}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{ff}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{ff}}
.

8.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf(\Box R)}}
with
x\in\{{\mathit{tb}},{\mathit{tt}}\}
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={\mathbf{t}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Box\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={\mathbf{t}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Box\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tb}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Box\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{tt}}
.

9.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf(\Box L)}}
with
x\in\{{\mathit{fb}},{\mathit{ff}}\}
.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{fb}}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{ff}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\neq{\mathbf{t}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Box\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={\mathbf{f}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Box\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{ff}}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Box\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in{\mathit{fb}}
.

10.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf({\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,R)}}
with
\mathtt{a}
.

If
\mathtt{a}\in\{{\mathit{tb}},{\mathit{tt}}\}
then
\forall f^{\prime}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f^{\prime}}\in\mathtt{a}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\mathtt{a}
.

11.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf({\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,L)}}
with
\mathtt{a}

If
\mathtt{a}\in\{{\mathit{fb}},{\mathit{ff}}\}
then
\exists f^{\prime}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f^{\prime}}\in\mathtt{a}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\mathtt{a}
.

The case of
{\mathrm{\bf({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}R)}}

Definition 6.5
.

Suppose
\phi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
and
\mathtt{a}
is a tag and
\Sigma
is a tag-sequent.
Then:

1.

Write
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathtt{a}:\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}
when
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}\in\mathtt{a}
.

2.

Write
\vDash\Sigma
when for every valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
, there exists some element
\mathtt{a}:\phi
in
\Sigma
such that
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathtt{a}:\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}
.

3.

When
\vDash\Sigma
holds, we call
\Sigma
a
valid tag-sequent
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Suppose
\Sigma
is a tag-sequent.
Then we have:

1.

Soundness:
If
\vdash\Sigma
then
\vDash\Sigma
.

2.

Completeness:
If
\vDash\Sigma
then
\vdash\Sigma
.

Thus,
\Sigma
is derivable if and only if it is valid.

Proof.

We prove both by by a simultaneous induction on the syntax of tag-sequences:
7
7

7

An induction on derivations will not work, because of the
\not\vdash
in
{\mathrm{\bf({\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,L)}}
observed in Remark
6.3
.

1.

By routine checking, unpacking Definition
6.5
and using the relevant clause of Lemma
6.4
from left-to-right.

2.

By routine checking, unpacking Definition
6.5
and using the relevant clause of Lemma
6.4
from right-to-left.
∎

Chapter 18
Axiomatisations

1
Theory arising from a witness function

Definition 1.1
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
(Definition
2.2
(
1
)).

1.

Define the
axioms arising from
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
to be sets of predicates in
\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=&\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}q\bigr){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}p\\
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)=&\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q\bigr){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p\\[8.61108pt]
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}=&\bigwedge\{\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}\\
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}=&\bigwedge\{\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}\end{array}

2.

We can collect these axioms into a conjunction
\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
, which we call the
theory arising from
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
:

\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})=\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}\ {\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\ \mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}.

3.

We may omit the
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
annotation where this is unimportant or understood, writing (for example)
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
just as
\mathsf{closedAx}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
.

Remark 1.2
.

We can read
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
and
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
above as asserting that

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}|\subseteq&f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\quad\text{and}\\
|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}|\subseteq&f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}.\end{array}

The proofs below implicitly reflect this intuition, and make it formal.

Lemma 1.3
.

Suppose that:

•

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

•

p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathit{B}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
blocks
p
(Definition
2.4
).

•

f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation and
f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
.

Then:

1.

(\forall q{\in}\mathit{B}.f\vDash q)
implies
f\vDash p
.

2.

(\forall q{\in}\mathit{B}.f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q)
implies
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Recall from Definition
1.1
that

\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}q\bigr){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}p,

and
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
appears in
\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
by Definition
1.1
.

Choose one
w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
(by construction in Definition
2.2
(
1
) at least one such exists) and consider the disjunction
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}q
for
w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
in
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
.
We assumed that
\mathit{B}
blocks
p
, so by assumption in Definition
2.4

\mathit{B}\between w
.
We also assumed that
f\vDash q
for every
q\in\mathit{B}
, so in particular
f\vDash q
for some
q\in w
, and thus by Lemma
4.9

f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}q
.

Now this holds for
every

w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
, so by Lemma
4.9

f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}q
.
We assumed
f\vDash\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
, and by Proposition
4.14
(
3
)
f\vDash p
follows.

2.

The reasoning to show that
\forall q{\in}\mathit{B}.f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q
implies
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
is precisely similar to the previous case, using
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)=\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q\bigr){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
.
∎

Remark 1.4
.

Note a curious thing:

•

Definition
2.5
(
3
) characterises closed sets in the witness semitopology using a single property
p\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$!$}}\hskip 0.2pt}_{\hskip-1.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}}P
.

•

Definition
1.1
contains
two
axioms for each point
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
:
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
and
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
.

We can look at the definition of
p\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$!$}}\hskip 0.2pt}_{\hskip-1.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}}P
in Definition
2.4
(
1
), and we see that
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
visibly imitates it — but what is
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
there for?

A formal answer to this question is that the proof of Proposition
2.3
— which characterises those valuations
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that are continuous on the witness semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
— requires both axioms.

This formal answer raises a semi-formal question: why
should
it require both axioms?
Because Definition
1.1
(axiomatisation of continuity) has to work harder than Definition
2.5
(
3
) (definition of open/closed sets).
Defining an open (or a closed) set requires two truth-values (whether a point is in or not in that set) whereas the logic has three values and correspondingly a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
can return
{\mathbf{t}}
,
{\mathbf{f}}
, or
{\mathbf{b}}
.
This makes it a more complex entity than just an open (or closed) set.

In fact we saw in Remark
4.6
(
3
) that a valuation
f
can be thought of as a
pair
of closed sets
\mathit{char}_{CC}(f)=(f^{\vDash},f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}})=(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\})
; see Definition
1.7
and the surrounding discussion.
In this view, axiom
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
controls the behaviour of
f^{\vDash}
, and
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
controls the behaviour of
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
.

2
Axiomatisation of continuity

Recall from Definition
1.1

\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
the theory arising from
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and
suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.
Then

•

if
f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
then

•

f
is continuous on the witness semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
from Definition
2.5
(
3
).

Proof.

Suppose
f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
.

To prove that
f
is continuous, it suffices by Lemma
1.4
to show that
f^{\vDash}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
are closed sets in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.
We consider each in turn:

•

We show that
f^{\vDash}
is closed.
1
1

1

We cannot just use Lemma
4.7
to deduce this, because that Lemma requires us to know that
f
is continuous, but this is what we are trying to prove.

Following Definition
2.5
(
2
),
f^{\vDash}
is closed in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
when for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathit{B}\subseteq f^{\vDash}
, if
\mathit{B}
blocks
p
(Definition
2.4
), then
p\in f^{\vDash}
.

Suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and suppose
\mathit{B}\subseteq f^{\vDash}
for some blocking set for
p
, which by Lemma
4.8
means precisely that
f\vDash\mathit{B}
.
By assumption
f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(p)
, and it follows from Lemma
1.3
that
f\vDash p
, and so by Lemma
4.8

p\in f^{\vDash}
as required.

•

We show that
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
is closed.

This is just like the previous case, but using
\mathsf{closedAx}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
.
∎

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and
suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.

Then if
f
is continuous on the witness semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
, then
f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
.

Proof.

Suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is continuous.
By Definition
4.5

f^{\vDash}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
, and
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
are closed.

We consider
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and show that
f\vDash\mathsf{closedAx}(p)
and
f\vDash\mathsf{closedAx}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
.
By Proposition
4.14
(
3
) it would suffice to show that if
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}q
then
f\vDash p
, and if
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q
then
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
.

•

Suppose
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}q
.

Using Lemmas
4.9
and
4.8
this means precisely that there exists a blocking set
\mathit{B}
for
p
such that
\mathit{B}\subseteq f^{\vDash}
.
We noted above that
f^{\vDash}
is closed, so that by Definition
2.5
(
2
) it follows from
B\subseteq f^{\vDash}
that
p\in f^{\vDash}
, and so that
f\vDash p
as required.

•

Suppose
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q
.

By reasoning on
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
precisely similar to the previous case, we deduce that
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
.
∎

Proposition 2.3
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and
suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
(Definition
1.1
).

2.

f
is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

In words we can write:

\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
axiomatises continuity over the witness semitopology.

Proof.

The top-down implication is Lemma
2.1
.
The bottom-up implication is Lemma
2.2
.
∎

Corollary 2.4
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witnessed function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vDash\phi\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi.

Proof.

By Definition
4.1
(
6
),
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vDash\phi
holds when
f\vDash\phi
for every
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.
By Propositions
2.3
and
4.14
(
3
) and Lemma
4.9
, this is precisely what
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi
expresses.
∎

Remark 2.5

(Alternative axiomatisation)
.

An equivalent set of axioms to
\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
from Definition
1.1
is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\mathsf{openAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=&\Box p\ {\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{q\in w}\Box q\\
\mathsf{openAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)=&\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p\ {\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\ {\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{q\in w}\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q\\
\mathsf{OpenAx}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})=&\bigwedge\bigl\{\mathsf{openAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\bigr\}\ {\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\ \bigwedge\bigl\{\mathsf{openAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\bigr\}\end{array}

These axioms characterise that
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
and
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
are open sets as per Definition
2.4
(
2
), so by Lemma
1.4
this characterises continuity of
f
, which is also what
\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
does: thus we obtain a lemma that

f\vDash\mathsf{OpenAx}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\quad\text{if and only if}\quad f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}).

We leave filling in the details as an exercise to the reader.

Note a
false
argument for
false
axioms: it is not enough to claim that

p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{q\in w}q
is just the contrapositive of
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
, and
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
is just the contrapositive of
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{q\in w}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q
, because by Lemma
2.7
we know that
{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}
does not satisfy the contrapositive property.

We use
\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
because it is simpler than
\mathsf{OpenAx}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})
, in the sense that the latter requires modalities.
This is because Definition
4.1
(
1
) uses the set
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
as designated values, and
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
is a
closed
set in the semitopology on
{\mathbf{3}}
from Definition
1.2
(
1
).
Thus, it is slightly more natural to use our logic to express topological properties in terms of closed sets.

3
Quantifying over continuous valuations, within the logic

1
Basic properties

Notation 3.1
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
\phi\in\mathsf{Pred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
Define
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi
and
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi
by:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi=&{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,(\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi)\\
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi=&{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,(\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi)\end{array}

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and
suppose
\phi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
(Definition
3.2
(
5
)).
2
2

2

‘Closed’ means no free variables.
\phi
might still mention propositional atoms
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Suppose further that
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
Then:

1.

f^{\prime}\vDash{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi
if and only if
f\vDash\phi
for every continuous
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))\to{\mathbf{3}}
.

2.

f^{\prime}\vDash{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi
if and only if
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vDash\phi
.

3.

f^{\prime}\vDash{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi
if and only if
f\vDash\phi
for some continuous
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))\to{\mathbf{3}}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Following Definition
4.1
(
1
), Figure
5
, and Notation
3.1
, it is routine to check that

f^{\prime}\vDash{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi
precisely when

\forall f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}.f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi.

By Propositions
4.14
(
3
) and
2.3
this is equivalent to
insisting that
f\vDash\phi
for every continuous
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))\to{\mathbf{3}}
, as required.

2.

From part
1
of this result, using Definition
4.1
(
6
).

3.

By reasoning exactly similar to that used in part
1
.
∎

Lemma
3.3
will be helpful later; see for instance Lemma
1.5
:

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
\phi\in\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
Suppose
f,f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
are valuations.
Then

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}f\vDash{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi\Longleftrightarrow&f^{\prime}\vDash{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi,\quad\text{and}\\
f\vDash{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi\Longleftrightarrow&f^{\prime}\vDash{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,\phi.\end{array}

In words: the valuation in which we evaluate
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,(\text{-})
and
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,(\text{-})
is not relevant.

Proof.

Routine from Proposition
3.2
.
∎

2
Valuation quantification treated as a modality

Fix a witness function
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
.
We saw in Proposition
2.3
and Corollary
2.4
that continuity on the witness semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
can be captured in our logic as an axiom
\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
(Definition
5.2
(
2
)).

We can view
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,
and
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,
as a pair of modalities, where

•

{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,
behaves like a box modality, quantifying over all possible worlds, where possible worlds are valuations
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that are continuous over
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
, and

•

{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,
behaves like a diamond modality, quantifying over the existence of some possible world.

Just for this Subsection, we write

∀
Val
_wf

as

and
∃
Val
_wf

as

and we fix a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

It is routine to check that

and

satisfy standard modal axioms, including:

1.

Necessitation: If
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vDash\phi
then
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vDash{\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}\phi
.

2.

Distribution / K:
f\vDash{\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}(\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}\phi^{\prime}
.

3.

Reflexivity / T:
f\vDash({\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}\phi){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\phi
.

4.

4:
f\vDash({\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}\phi){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}{\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}\phi
.

5.

B:
f\vDash\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{${\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}$}\phi

6.

5:
f\vDash\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{${\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}$}\phi{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}\rotatebox[origin]{45.0}{${\hskip 0.5pt\framebox{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.7852,0.7852}\rule{5.69054pt}{5.69054pt}}\hskip 1.0pt}$}\phi
.

We recognise this as the well-known modal logic S5, which is equal to Necessitation + K + T + 5.
3
3

3

In the presence of the other axioms, axiom 5 is equivalent to axioms 4 and B.

S5 is characteristic of Kripke structures in which the accessibility relation is an equivalence relation.
The proofs are easy, and it is nice to find S5 in our logic.

What may be interesting to highlight here is that
although our logical syntax from Figure
4
assumes
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
and
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
(without
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
), what actually interests us in this paper is to use this syntax to construct a family of modalities
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,
and
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,
; one for each witness function
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
and corresponding judgement-form
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vDash(\text{-})
.

So, it is the
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
-indexed modal structure
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,
and
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,
obtained by quantifying over all continuous valuations on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
, rather than the raw syntax of
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
and
{\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
, that — for this paper, at least — we are using.

Chapter 19
Logical characterisation of intertwined-ness & regularity properties

We now set about characterising semitopological properties inside our logic.
This is a first step to building a framework within which a participant in a distributed network can reason about network properties (or at least, about those parts that the participant can access information about).

1
Logical characterisation of being intertwined

Remark 1.1
.

In this Subsection we proceed in three steps:

1.

Lemma
1.2
gives characterisations of points being interwined using closed sets, rather than the open sets used in Definition
6.1
(
1
).

2.

Lemma
1.3
uses this characterisation to express
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
as a logical judgement
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
.

3.

Notation
1.4
and Proposition
1.6
use a
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt}\,
binding to internalise this judgement as a predicate
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,(p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime})
.

Lemma 1.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
.

2.

For every
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
, if
p\in O
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
then
O\between O^{\prime}
(as per Definition
6.1
(
1
)).

3.

For every
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
, if
p\not\in C
and
p^{\prime}\not\in C^{\prime}
then
C\cup C^{\prime}\neq\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

4.

For every
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
, if
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
then
p\in C
or
p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.

5.

For every
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
, if
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
then
p,p^{\prime}\in C
or
p,p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We check the assertions are equivalent by routine manipulations.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 was observed already in Definition
6.1
(
1
).

By Lemma
1.9
part 3 just restates part 2 using closed sets instead of open sets.
Part 4 is then just the contrapositive of part 3.

We now prove equivalence of parts 4 and 5 (the more interesting part is to show that part 4 implies part 5):

•

Assume that for every
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
, if
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
then
p\in C\lor p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.

Suppose
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
and
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
By assumption
p\in C\lor p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
; suppose
p\in C
(the case of
p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
is symmetric).

Now
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
so
p^{\prime}\in C
or
p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.
If
p^{\prime}\in C
then we have
p,p^{\prime}\in C
and we are done.
Otherwise, we have
p^{\prime}\not\in C
and thus
p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
— and we have
p^{\prime}\not\in C
and thus by our assumption (since
C^{\prime}\cup C=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p^{\prime}\not\in C
) also
p\in C^{\prime}
.

Thus we have
p,p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
and we are done.

•

Assume for every
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
, if
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
then
p,p^{\prime}\in C\lor p,p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.

Suppose
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
and
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Clearly
p,p^{\prime}\in C
implies
p\in C
and
p,p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
implies
p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
, so
p\in C\lor p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
is immediate.
∎

Lemma 1.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
.

3.

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}p^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\vee}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime})
.

4.

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash(p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}p^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}p^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 2, 3, and 4 is routine from the equivalences in
Figure
3
and Lemma
2.2
(or just by checking truth-tables from Figure
2
).

Equivalence of parts 1 and 3 is just by unfolding definitions and checking truth-tables, but we spell out the details.
We consider two implications:

•

Suppose
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

We wish to show that
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}p^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\vee}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime})
.

Consider a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, and consider
f^{\vDash}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
from Definition
4.5
.
These are closed sets in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
by Lemma
4.7
.

We note that
f^{\vDash}\cup f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and so by Lemma
1.2
(
1
&
5
) (since
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
)
p,p^{\prime}\in f^{\vDash}
or
p,p^{\prime}\in f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
.
It follows using Lemma
4.9
that
f\vDash(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}p^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\vee}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p)
.

Now
f
was an arbitrary continuous valuation on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
, so the result follows by Definition
4.1
(
6
).

•

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}p^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\vee}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime})
.

Consider
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Closed}
such that
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
; by Lemma
1.2
(
1
&
5
) it would suffice to show that
p,p^{\prime}\in C
or
p,p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.

We define
f=\delta_{C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}},C_{{\mathbf{f}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(C,C^{\prime}):\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
from Definition
1.5
, so
f(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C)={\mathbf{t}}
and
f(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C^{\prime})={\mathbf{f}}
and
f(C\cap C^{\prime})={\mathbf{b}}
, and
C=f^{\vDash}
and
C^{\prime}=f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}
.
This is well-defined since
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and continuous by Lemma
1.6
.
Thus,
f\vDash(p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}p^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\vee}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime})
.

By Lemma
4.9
, we see that at least one of
f\vDash p\land f\vDash p^{\prime}
or
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p\land f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime}
must hold.
By Lemma
4.8
, either
p,p^{\prime}\in C
or
p,p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
, as required.
∎

Notation 1.4
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and
suppose
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then define a predicate
p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
by

p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}\ =\ {\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,(p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}).

We make an elementary observation:

Lemma 1.5
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
f,f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
are any valuations.
Then

f\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad f^{\prime}\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}.

In words:
f\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
— the validity of
p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
— depends on
p
,
p^{\prime}
, and
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
.
It does not depend on the ambient valuation
f
.

Proof.

Direct from Lemma
3.3
.
∎

Proposition 1.6
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
(Definition
6.1
(
1
)).

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
.

3.

f^{\prime}\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
(
f^{\prime}
does not matter, as per Lemma
1.5
).

Proof.

We prove two equivalences:

•

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 is just Lemma
1.3
(1&2), for the witness semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

•

To prove equivalence of parts 1 and 3, we unpack Notation
1.4
and use Proposition
3.2
(
1
) to see that

f^{\prime}\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
when
f\vDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
for every
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.
We use Lemma
1.3
.
∎

Remark 1.7
.

By Lemmas
3.3
and
1.5
, the valuation
f^{\prime}
is not used to evaluate
f^{\prime}\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
in Proposition
1.6
.
The valuation
f^{\prime}
is there because the validity judgement
‘
f^{\prime}\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
’ from Definition
4.1
(
1
) requires us to provide a valuation.
1
1

1

This happens in first-order logic too, for example with
f\vDash x{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}y\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}y{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}x
.

The reader might ask why in the statement of Proposition
2.3
(3) we do not replace

•

the judgement
f^{\prime}\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
(Definition
4.1
(
1
)) with

•

the judgement
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
(Definition
4.1
(
5
)).

Because this is a different result.
It is the difference between proving

\forall f^{\prime}.(A\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}B)
— which permits us to replace
A
with
B
, given some
f^{\prime}
— and

A\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\forall f^{\prime}.B
, which does not.

Corollary
1.8
will be useful later.
It shows how to characterise more sophisticated inclusions and equalities between
f^{\vDash}
and
p_{\between}

(
f^{\vDash}
is from Definition
4.5
;
p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x
is from Definition
6.1
(
2
)):

Corollary 1.8
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and
suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.
Then:

1.

f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x)
if and only if
f^{\vDash}=p_{\between}
.

2.

f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x)
if and only if
f^{\vDash}\subseteq p_{\between}
.

3.

f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}x
if and only if
p_{\between}\subseteq f^{\vDash}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Using Corollary
5.3
,
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x)
holds when

\{p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid f\vDash p^{\prime}\}=\{p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid f\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}\}.

By Definition
4.5
, the left-hand side of this equality is just
f^{\vDash}
.

By Proposition
1.6

f\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}
if and only if
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
, so
the right-hand side of this equality is
\{p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}\}=p_{\between}
(Definition
6.1
(
2
)).

The result follows.

2.

Using Proposition
4.14
(
3
) and Definition
4.5
,
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x)
holds when

f^{\vDash}\subseteq\{p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\mid f\vDash p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p^{\prime}\}.

We use Proposition
1.6
just as we did in part 1 of this result.

3.

Precisely as for part 2 of this result.
∎

2
Logical characterisation of topological indistinguishability

By Proposition
1.6
,
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,(p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime})
characterises being intertwined in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.
But recall that Figure
2
features two connectives for logical equivalence:

\equiv

and

\Leftrightarrow

.
In this Subsection we briefly consider the natural question: what does
{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,(p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p^{\prime})
characterise?
The answer is very simple: this is topological indistinguishability.

Recall from Definition
4.6
the notion of
topological indistinguishability

p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}
.
This has a beautiful logical characterisation:

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}
.

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This result looks like it should have a trivial proof.
Not so: the proof is simple, but not trivial.
We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}
and consider a continuous
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.
By assumption

p\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\Longleftrightarrow p^{\prime}\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
, so by Corollary
5.2
(1&3)
f\vDash p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p^{\prime}
as required.

•

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p^{\prime}
and consider some
O\in\mathsf{Open}
.
We set
f=\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(\varnothing,O)
— using
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(O,\varnothing)
will not give us what we need — so
f
maps a point to
{\mathbf{f}}
if it is in
O
, and to
{\mathbf{b}}
otherwise.
By Corollary
5.2
(1&3),
p\in O\Longleftrightarrow p^{\prime}\in O
as required.
∎

Definition 2.2
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then define a predicate
\mathsf{TopIndis}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p,p^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
by:

\mathsf{TopIndis}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p,p^{\prime})\ =\ {\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,(p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p^{\prime}).

Proposition 2.3
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p\mathrel{\mathring{=}}p^{\prime}
in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf})\vDash p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p^{\prime}
.

3.

f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{TopIndis}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p,p^{\prime})

(By Lemma
3.3
, the ambient valuation
f^{\prime}
