is not used in evaluating
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{TopIndis}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p,p^{\prime})
. See Remark
1.7
.)

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 is just Lemma
2.1
.
Equivalence of parts 2 and 3 is direct from Proposition
3.2
(
2
).
∎

3
Logical characterisation of
f^{\vDash}
having a nonempty open interior

To characterise (weak/quasi-)regularity, which we do below, we will need to logically characterise when
f
takes value
{\mathbf{t}}
at some point
p
, and when
f^{\vDash}
has a nonempty open interior and so is a closed neighbourhood.
These properties are closely related, and we study them in this Subsection:

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is continuous and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

f\vDash\Box p
if and only if
p\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
.

2.

If
f\vDash\Box p
then
p\in\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash})
.

3.

If
f\vDash\Box p
then
f^{\vDash}
is a closed neighbourhood of
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
(a closed set with
p
in its open interior; Definition
4.1
(
2
)).

4.

The converse implication to part
3
need not necessarily hold: it is possible that
p\in\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash})
yet
f\not\vDash\Box p
— later on in Corollary
2.7
we will identify those valuations for which the reverse implication does hold, as being a special and interesting class of
extremal
valuations.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Following Definition
4.1
(
1
) and the rules in Figures
5
and
2
,
f\vDash\Box p
when
f(p)={\mathbf{t}}
.
It is a fact that
f(p)={\mathbf{t}}
when
p\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
.

2.

Suppose
f\vDash\Box p
.
By part
1
of this result,
p\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
.
By Corollary
1.11
(
1
)
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\in\mathsf{Open}
, and by construction

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\subseteq f^{\vDash}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
, so
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\subseteq\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash})
.
Thus we have

p\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\subseteq\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash})

as required.

3.

By Lemma
4.7

f^{\vDash}
is a closed set.
The result now just follows from part
2
of this result.

4.

Consider the
singleton semitopology
\mathsf{One}=\{\ast\}
with
\mathsf{Open}=\{\varnothing,\mathsf{One}\}
.
Note that
\{\ast\}
is a closed neighbourhood, with open interior
\{\ast\}
.
Define
f:\mathsf{One}\to{\mathbf{3}}
by
f(\ast)={\mathbf{b}}
.
Then
f
is continuous and
f^{\vDash}=\{\ast\}
, but
f\not\vDash\Box\ast
.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation that is closed on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.
Then:

1.

f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
if and only if
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\neq\varnothing
.

2.

If
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
then
f^{\vDash}
is a closed neighbourhood.

3.

It is possible for
f^{\vDash}
to be a closed neighbourhood, yet
f\not\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
.
By Lemma
4.10
this is if and only if there exists some
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
such that
f\vDash\Box p
.
By Lemma
3.1
(1)
p\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
, so that
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\neq\varnothing
.

The reverse implication follows just reversing this reasoning.

2.

Suppose
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
.
By Lemma
4.7

f^{\vDash}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
is a closed set, and by part 1 of this result it has a nonempty open interior.

3.

See the counterexample in Lemma
3.1
(
4
).
∎

Corollary 3.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
C\subseteq\mathsf{Closed}
is a closed set.
Define

f=\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(\mathit{interior}(C),C)

and note from the construction of
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(\mathit{interior}(C),C)
in Definition
1.5
) that
f^{\vDash}=C
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

C
is a closed neighbourhood (a closed set with a nonempty open interior; Definition
4.1
(
3
)).

2.

\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(O,C)\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
.

Proof.

By an easy argument from the results so far:

•

Suppose
C
is a closed neighbourhood, meaning by Definition
4.1
(
3
) that
\mathit{interior}(C)\neq\varnothing
.
Choose
p\in\mathit{interior}(C)
and note that
f(p)={\mathbf{t}}
.
By Proposition
3.2
(
1
),
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
as required.

•

Suppose
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
.
By Proposition
3.2
(
2
),
f^{\vDash}
is a closed neighbourhood.
∎

4
Logical characterisation of being quasiregular

By Definition
1.4
(
5
), a point is quasiregular when its community is nonempty.
We express this as follows:

Definition 4.1
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
We define a predicate
\mathsf{QuasiRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
by:

\mathsf{QuasiRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\ =\ {\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x\land{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x).

Proposition 4.2
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is quasiregular in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

2.

There exists a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
such that

f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})\quad\text{and}\quad f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x\quad\text{and}\quad f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x.

3.

f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Quasiregular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.

(By Lemma
3.3
, the ambient valuation
f^{\prime}
is not used in evaluating
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{QuasiRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
. See Remark
1.7
.)

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 2 and 3 is just by unpacking Definition
4.1
and using Proposition
3.2
(
3
) and Lemma
4.9
.

Note from Proposition
4.9
that
p
is quasiregular when
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood (Definition
4.1
(
3
)).
We prove equivalence of parts 1 and 2 by proving two implications:

•

Suppose
p
is quasiregular, so that
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood and
\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
.

We set
f=\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(\mathit{K}(p),p_{\between}):\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
; so
f
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
on
\mathit{K}(p)=\mathit{interior}(p_{\between})
— which is nonempty since we assumed that
p_{\between}
is a closed neighbourhood — and
{\mathbf{b}}
on
\mathit{boundary}(p_{\between})=p_{\between}\setminus\mathit{K}(p)
, and
{\mathbf{f}}
on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus p_{\between}
.
Then:

–

By Lemma
1.6

f
is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
, so by Proposition
2.3

f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
.

–

By Proposition
3.2
(
1
) (right-to-left direction, since
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}=\mathit{K}(p)\neq\varnothing
)
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
.

–

By Corollary
1.8
(1)
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x
holds when
f^{\vDash}=p_{\between}
.
But we defined

f=\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(\mathit{K}(p),p_{\between})
, so this is a fact.

•

Suppose
f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
and
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
and
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x
.

By Proposition
2.3

f
is continuous from
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
to
{\mathbf{3}}
.
Write
C=f^{\vDash}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
By Proposition
3.2
(
1
) (left-to-right direction)
C
is a closed neighbourhood.
By Corollary
1.8

C=p_{\between}
.
Thus
p
is quasiregular.
∎

5
Logical characterisation of being weakly regular

By Definition
1.4
(
4
), a point is weakly regular when it is an element of its own community.
We express this as follows:

Definition 5.1
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
We define a predicate
\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
by:

\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\ =\ {\mathit{\exists\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,(\Box p\land{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.x\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Leftrightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x)).

Proposition 5.2
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is weakly regular in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

2.

f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.

(By Lemma
3.3
, the ambient valuation
f^{\prime}
is not used in evaluating
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
. See Remark
1.7
.)

Proof.

We unpack what
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
means.
By Proposition
3.2
(
3
) this means that there exists a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
that is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
such that

f^{\vDash}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
is a closed neighbourhood of
p
(by Lemma
3.1
(
3
)), and
f^{\vDash}
is equal to
p_{\between}
(by Corollary
1.8
).
But by Proposition
4.10
(
2
) this precisely means that
p
is weakly regular; the equivalence follows.
∎

6
Logical characterisation of being unconflicted

By Definition
1.1
(
2
), a point is unconflicted when the ‘intertwined with’ relation is transitive at that point.
We express this as follows:

Definition 6.1
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
We define a predicate
\mathsf{Unconflicted}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
by

\mathsf{Unconflicted}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\ =\ {\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime},x^{\prime\prime}.(x\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}p\,{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\,p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime\prime})\Longrightarrow x^{\prime}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime\prime}.

Proposition 6.2
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is unconflicted in the witness semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

2.

f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Unconflicted}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.

(By Lemma
3.3
, the ambient valuation
f^{\prime}
is not used in evaluating
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Unconflicted}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
. See Remark
1.7
.)

Proof.

We unpack what
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Unconflicted}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
means.
Using Lemma
4.10
, Proposition
4.14
(
3
), and Lemma
4.9
,

f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Unconflicted}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
if and only if

∀p’,p’’∈
P
.(f⊨p’

\between

_

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}

p ∧f⊨p

\between

_

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}

p”) ⟹f⊨p’

\between

_

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}

p” .

We simplify with Proposition
1.6
to obtain

∀p’,p’’∈
P
.(p’
≬
p ∧p
≬
p”) ⟹p’
≬
p” .

But as per Definition
1.1
(
2
), this is precisely what being unconflicted means.
∎

7
Logical characterisation of being regular

Remark 7.1
.

We will consider two characterisations of being regular in Definition
7.2
:

1.

By Theorem
2.2
a point is regular when it is weakly regular and unconflicted.
This is expressed by
\mathsf{Regular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
in Definition
7.2
.

2.

By Theorem
6.2
a point
p
is regular when it is weakly regular and furthermore
p_{\between}
is a minimal closed neighbourhood.
This is expressed by
\mathsf{Regular}^{\prime}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
in Definition
7.2
.

We translate both into our logic.

Definition 7.2
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
We define predicates
\mathsf{Regular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
and
\mathsf{Regular}^{\prime}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
by:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\mathsf{Regular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=&\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{Unconflicted}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\\
\mathsf{Regular}^{\prime}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=&\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.x^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime})){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.((p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}x^{\prime})\end{array}

Proposition 7.3
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is regular in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

2.

f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Regular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.

(By Lemma
3.3
, the ambient valuation
f^{\prime}
is not used in evaluating
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Regular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
. See Remark
1.7
.)

Proof.

The argument is routine from the machinery we have build:

1.

By Theorem
2.2
,
p
is regular when it is weakly regular and unconflicted.

2.

By Lemma
4.9
and Propositions
5.2
and
6.2
,
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Regular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
holds when
p
is weakly regular and unconflicted.

The result follows.
∎

Proposition 7.4
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and suppose
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is any valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p
is regular in
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.

2.

p\in\mathit{K}(p)
and
p_{\between}
is a minimal closed neighbourhood.

3.

f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Regular}^{\prime}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.

(By Lemma
3.3
, the ambient valuation
f^{\prime}
is not used in evaluating
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Regular}^{\prime}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
. See Remark
1.7
.)

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 just repeats Theorem
6.2
.
We now consider equivalence of parts 2 and 3.
We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{Regular}^{\prime}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.

Unpacking Definition
7.2
and using Lemma
4.9
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}f^{\prime}\vDash&\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\quad\text{and}\\
f^{\prime}\vDash&{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.x^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime})){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.((p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}x^{\prime})\end{array}

By Proposition
5.2

p
is weakly regular, which by Proposition
4.10
(3) means that
p_{\between}
is a minimal closed neighbourhood of
p
.

We will now show that
p_{\between}
is a minimal closed neighbourhood (not just of a minimal closed neighbourhood of
p
).
Suppose
C\subseteq p_{\between}
is some closed neighbourhood, and write
O=\mathit{interior}(C)\neq\varnothing
.
Let
f=\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(O,C)
from Definition
1.5
, so
f(p)={\mathbf{t}}
when
p\in O
, and
f(p)={\mathbf{b}}
when
p\in C\setminus O
, and
f(p)={\mathbf{f}}
when
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C
.
By Lemma
1.6

f
is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
.
It follows from Proposition
3.2
(
1
) that

f\vDash({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.x^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime})){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.((p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}x^{\prime}).

Now
C
is a closed neighbourhood so by Corollary
3.3
(since
f=\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},C_{{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{b}}}}(O,C)
)
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
.
By assumption
C\subseteq p_{\between}
so by Corollary
1.8
(2)
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.x^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime})
.
Then by Proposition
4.14
(
3
) it follows that
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}x^{\prime}
, and by Corollary
1.8
(3)
p_{\between}\subseteq C
.

Thus
C\subseteq p_{\between}
implies
p_{\between}\subseteq C
, and so
p_{\between}
is a minimal closed neighbourhood as required.

•

Suppose
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
and
p_{\between}
is a minimal closed neighbourhood.

The reasoning is essentially by reversing the argument of the previous case, but we spell out the details.

By Definition
1.4
(
4
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)
means precisely that
p
is weakly regular, and by Proposition
5.2
it follows that
f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)
.

By Propositions
3.2
(
1
) and
4.14
(
3
) and Corollary
1.8
(2), to prove

{\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.x^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime})){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.((p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}x^{\prime})

it suffices to show for every
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}({w\hskip-1.8ptf}))
, if
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
and
f^{\vDash}\subseteq p_{\between}
, then
p_{\between}\subseteq f^{\vDash}
.

So suppose
f\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x
, so that
f^{\vDash}
is a closed neighbourhood by Proposition
3.2
(
2
), and suppose
f^{\vDash}\subseteq p_{\between}
.
By minimality of
p_{\between}
it follows that
p_{\between}\subseteq f^{\vDash}
.

We use Lemma
4.9
to deduce

f^{\prime}\vDash\mathsf{WeaklyRegular}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\ {\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\ {\mathit{\forall\hskip-2.0ptVal}}_{\hskip 0.0pt{w\hskip-1.8ptf}}\,({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x.\Box x){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.x^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}(p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime})){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x^{\prime}.((p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\between$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.55}{${w\hskip-1.8ptf}$}}}x^{\prime}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}x^{\prime})

as required.
∎

Chapter 20
Computational complexity & logic programming

We now consider some computational aspects of semitopologies, specifically the following two questions:

1.

What is the computational complexity of deciding whether two points are intertwined?

2.

What is a suitable notion of logic programming over
{\mathbf{3}}
?

Many excellent treatments of logic programming over two-valued logic are available
[
MNPS91
,
Lif08
,
Lif19
]
.
There is some literature on the complexity of checking satisfiability of many-valued propositional logics; for example
[
H0̈3
,
Han11
]
consider propositional many-valued logics, and
[
Vid21
]
considers the modal case.
We are not aware of any comprehensive surveys.

1
Translation of SAT to intertwinedness problem

Definition 1.1
.

Suppose
\psi
is a Boolean logic proposition over some set of
propositional atoms

\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
.
So
\psi
is built from propositional atoms
q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
, with connectives
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\top}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\neg}},

{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
.
Suppose without loss of generality that
\psi
is in conjunctive normal form, so

\psi=\bigwedge_{i\in I}\bigvee_{j\in i}a_{ij}q_{ij}

where

1.

I
is an indexing set and each
i\in I
is itself an indexing set of
j\in i
,

2.

each
q_{ij}\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
is a propositional atom, and

3.

a_{ij}
indicates an
arity
, which is either
positive arity
(meaning that
q_{ij}
is unnegated) or
negative arity
(meaning that
q_{ij}
is negated).

Notation 1.2
.

We may call the combination
a_{ij}q_{ij}
of an arity and a propositional atom a
literal
.
We may call the literal

positive
when its arity is positive (so
a_{ij}q_{ij}
is just a propositional atom
q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
), and

negative
when its arity is negative (so
a_{ij}q_{ij}
is a negated proposition atom
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q
).

Remark 1.3
.

Definition
1.4
below is a little fiddly to write out.
The reader might like to look at it together with Proposition
1.5
, since that Proposition motivates the design of this Definition.
Intuitively in Definition
1.4
:

1.

Points on the right are intended to be assigned ‘true’, and points on the left are intended to be assigned ‘false’.

2.

A conjunctive restriction is represented by a point with one witness-set, which may have several elements.

3.

A disjunctive restriction is represented by a point with several witness-sets, each of which contains just one element.

Details of how this works are in Proposition
1.5
.

Definition 1.4
.

We fix some data to help us map a proposition
\psi
from Definition
1.1
to a witness function in the sense of Definition
2.2
(
1
), as follows:

1.

Fix symbols
\mathit{left}
and
\mathit{right}
.

2.

For each
i\in I
, fix a symbol
\mathit{right}_{i}
.

3.

For each
q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
, fix symbols
{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}
and
{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}
, and fix symbols
\mathit{left}_{q}
and
\mathit{right}_{q}
.

4.

Define a set of
points

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{\mathit{left},\mathit{right}\}\cup\{\mathit{right}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\cup\bigcup_{q{\in}\bm{\mathsf{Q}}}\{{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}},{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}},\mathit{left}_{q},\mathit{right}_{q}\}.

5.

Define a function
m
from literals (unnegated or negated propositional atoms; see Notation
1.2
) to points
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, such that

m(q)={\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\quad\text{and}\quad m({\boldsymbol{\neg}}q)={\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}.

6.

Define a witness function
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
as follows:

(a)

For each
q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
, let
{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}
and
{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}
have witness-sets

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}({\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}})=\{\,\{{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\}\,\}\quad\text{and}\quad{w\hskip-1.8ptf}({\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}})=\{\,\{{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\}\,\}.

So each of
{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}
and
{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}
has just one witness-set, which is singleton itself.

(b)

For each
q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
, let
\mathit{left}_{q}
and
\mathit{right}_{q}
have witness-sets

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(\mathit{left}_{q})=\{\{{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\},\ \{{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\}\}={w\hskip-1.8ptf}(\mathit{right}_{q}).

So each
\mathit{left}_{q}
has two singleton witness-sets, and so does
\mathit{right}_{q}
.

(c)

Let
\mathit{left}
have just one witness-set, given by

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(\mathit{left})=\bigl\{\ \{\mathit{left}_{q}\mid q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}\}\ \bigr\}.

(d)

Let
\mathit{right}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
have just one witness-set, given by

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(\mathit{right})=\bigl\{\ \{\mathit{right}_{q}\mid q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}\}\cup\{\mathit{right}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\ \bigr\}.

(e)

For each
i\in I
, let
\mathit{right}_{i}
have witness-sets given by

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(\mathit{right}_{i})=\bigl\{\ \{m(a_{ij}q_{ij})\}\mid j\in i\bigr\}.

So for each
j\in i
, the singleton set
\{m(a_{ij}q_{ij})\}
is a witness-set of
\mathit{right}_{i}
.

Proposition 1.5
.

Suppose that:

•

\psi
is a Boolean logic proposition over propositional atoms
\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
in conjunctive normal form, in the sense of Definition
1.1
.

•

{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is the witness function derived from
\psi
as per Definition
1.4
.

Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\psi
is 2-satisfiable in (the usual) two-valued Boolean logic, as per Definition
2.2
(1).

2.

\mathit{left}\mathrel{\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}}\mathit{right}
.

Proof.

We prove two implications.

•

Suppose
\mathit{left}\mathrel{\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}}\mathit{right}
, so there exist a disjoint pair of open neighbourhoods

\mathit{left}\in L\in\mathsf{Open}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathit{right}\in R\in\mathsf{Open}.

Now by the construction of the witness semitopology in Definition
2.5
(
3
)
and by the design of
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
in Definition
1.4
, we can make observations as follows:

–

By Definition
1.4
(
6d
)
\mathit{right}\in R
has only one witness-set, so by Definition
2.5
(
3
) that witness-set must be a subset of
R
.
It follows that
\mathit{right}_{q}\in R
for every
q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
, and
\mathit{right}_{i}\in R
for every
i\in I
.

–

By Definition
1.4
(
6c
)
\mathit{left}\in L
has only one witness-set, so by Definition
2.5
(
3
) that witness-set must be a subset of
L
.
It follows that
\mathit{left}_{q}\in L
for every
q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}
.

–

By Definition
1.4
(
6b
) each
\mathit{right}_{q}\in R
has just two witness-sets, so (as for the cases above) at least one of those witness-sets must be a subset of
R
.
Therefore

{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\in R\lor{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\in R.

By similar reasoning on the
\mathit{left}_{q}
we have

{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\in L\lor{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\in L.

But we assumed
L
and
R
are disjoint, so we conclude that

({\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\in R\land{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\in L\land{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\not\in L\land{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\not\in R)\ \lor\ ({\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\in L\land{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\in R\land{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\not\in R\land{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\not\in L).

–

Finally, by Definitions
1.4
(
6e
) and
2.5
(
3
), for each
i\in I
it follows from
\mathit{right}_{i}\in R
that there exists some
j\in i
such that
\{m(a_{ij}q_{ij})\}\subseteq R
, thus that
m(a_{ij}q_{ij})\in R
.

We can now take our satisfying assignment
f
to be such that
f(q)={\mathbf{f}}
when
{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\in L
, and
f(q)={\mathbf{t}}
when
{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\in R
.

Intuitively, this interprets
q
as true when its positive literal
{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}
is in
R
, and it interprets
q
as false when its positive literal
{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}
is in
L
.

•

Suppose we have a satisfying assignment
f:\bm{\mathsf{Q}}\to\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{t}}\}
.
Then we set

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}L=&\{{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\mid q\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\}\cup\{{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\mid q\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\}\cup\{\mathit{left}_{q}\mid q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}\}\cup\{\mathit{left}\}\quad\text{and}\\
R=&\{{\text{{+}}\mathtt{q}}\mid q\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\}\cup\{{\text{{-}}\mathtt{q}}\mid q\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\}\cup\{\mathit{right}_{q}\mid q\in\bm{\mathsf{Q}}\}\cup\{\mathit{right}_{i}\mid i\in I\}\cup\{\mathit{right}\}.\end{array}

Intuitively, this puts the positive and negative literals that are false on the left, and the positive and negative literals that are true on the right.
By the same reasoning as above, it is routine to check that
L
and
R
are a disjoint pair of open sets.
∎

Theorem 1.6
.

The problem of determining whether two points are intertwined in a finite semitopology, is NP complete in general.

Proof.

Proposition
1.5
exhibits a reduction of SAT into an intertwinedness problem, and the reduction clearly runs in polynomial time (indeed, it is linear).
∎

Remark 1.7
.

Theorem
1.6
shows that checking intertwinednessness is NP complete in general.
However, this is not the last word about how hard intertwinedness is to check in practice, since there may be practical use cases where the problem is easier.

For example, suppose we have a semitopology
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
of which we know that it contains a strongly transitive open set
K
.
This may (for example) arise as a kernel atom (Definition
1.2
(
1
)) of core participants who for historical reasons are considered reliable, whose witness functions have been relatively stable, and of which we can prove (using our sequent system from Subsection
6
, or by model-checking the witness function restricted just to those participants) that they are intertwined, consensus-equivalent, and hypertwined with each other (Definition
6.6
).
1
1

1

English has many terms for such a
K
: clique, cabal, eminences, founding committee, in-crowd, and so on. This is a fairly typical situation in real life. For example, a secret to getting along in an organisation is to find out which small clique of well-connected members actually makes the decisions and gets things done — this may or may not line up with any formal hierarchy of the organisation. And, such a group tends in practice to be stable over time (sometimes infuriatingly so).
This is the magic of mathematics: we may not be able to cure bureaucracy, but at least we can build a topological model of it!

Then to check whether the space
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is intertwined (meaning by Notation
6.4
that
all
points in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
are pairwise intertwined), it suffices to compute
|K|
.
By Lemma
3.5
,
|K|=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
if and only if
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
is intertwined.

By the algorithm given in Remark
4.14
, the closure
|K|
can be computed efficiently.

2
Horn satisfiability over
\mathsf{Bool}
and
{\mathbf{3}}

1
The (standard) HORNSAT algorithm for Boolean logic

We start with a brief but precise introduction to Horn clause theories and an algorithm to check their satisfiability in Boolean (two-valued) logic:

Notation 2.1
.

A
literal
is a possibly negated propositional atom:
p
or
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
.
We call
p

positive literal
and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
a
negative literal
.
A
Horn clause
is a possibly empty disjunct of positive and negative literals, that contains at most one positive literal.
For example:

•

{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime\prime}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q
and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p^{\prime\prime}
are Horn clauses.

•

p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q
is not a Horn clause, because it contains two positive literals.

•

\varnothing
the empty disjunct is a Horn clause.
We may call this
empty Horn clause
, and (slightly abusing notation) we may write this as
{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
.

•

p
and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
— which are clauses consisting of a single literal — are Horn clauses.
We may call a Horn clause consisting of a single literal a
unit clause
.

Call a finite set of Horn clauses a
Horn clause theory
.

Definition 2.2
.

1.

Call a propositional theory (which need not necessarily consist only of Horn clauses)
2-satisfiable
when there is a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
that makes all the clauses true in two-valued propositional logic.

2.

Call a propositional theory
3-satisfiable
when there is a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that makes all the clauses either
{\mathbf{b}}
or
{\mathbf{t}}
in
{\mathbf{3}}
.

HORNSAT is the computational problem of deciding whether a Horn clause theory over two-valued logic is 2-satisfiable.
This can be solved in linear time
[
DG84
]
:

Remark 2.3

(HORNSAT algorithm)
.

An overview of the algorithm is as follows:

1.

If the set contains a positive unit clause
p
, then:

(a)

delete every clause that contains that literal in positive form, except for
p
itself, and

(b)

delete
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
from any of the remaining clauses.

2.

Repeat the previous step until no further changes to the theory occur.

3.

If the resulting theory contains an empty Horn clause then fail; otherwise succeed.

If we succeed, then we can read a satisfying assignment from the result by mapping
p
to
{\mathbf{t}}
for every unit clause
p
that remains, and mapping every other
p^{\prime}
to
{\mathbf{f}}
.

2
A proposal for HORNSAT over
{\mathbf{3}}

We now sketch a proposal for Horn clause programming in
{\mathbf{3}}
, adapting Notation
1.2
(Horn clause theories) and Remark
2.3
(the HORNSAT algorithm) to the three-valued case.

The syntax and algorithm in Definition
2.4
and Remark
2.6
are just pencil-and-paper prototypes, but they are simple and elegant and uncover no obvious difficulties, which suggests that they could be implemented and that more advanced systems could also be practical, like those discussed in
[
MNPS91
,
Lif08
,
Lif19
]
but based on
{\mathbf{3}}
instead of
\mathsf{Bool}
.

Definition 2.4

(Horn clause theories and HORNSAT over
{\mathbf{3}}
)
.

Fix a set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

1.

(a)

A
boxed positive literal
is a proposition of the form
\Box p
, for
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

(b)

An
unboxed positive literal
is a proposition of the form
p
, for
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

(c)

A
positive literal
is a boxed or unboxed positive literal.

2.

(a)

A
boxed negative literal
is a proposition of the form
\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
, for
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

(b)

An
unboxed negative literal
is a proposition of the form
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
, for
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

(c)

A
negative literal
is a boxed or unboxed negative literal.

3.

A
literal
is a positive or negative literal.

4.

A
3Horn clause
is a possibly empty disjunct of literals, at most one of which is positive:

•

When a 3Horn clause contains a positive literal, we call this the
head
of the Horn clause.

•

When a 3Horn clause is empty, we call it
unsatisfiable 3Horn clause
.

•

When a 3Horn clause is a singleton, we call it a
unit clause
.

5.

Echoing Notation
2.1
, call a finite set of 3Horn clauses a
3Horn clause theory
.

Remark 2.5
.

Useful classes of propositions fit into the 3Horn clause syntax of Definition
2.4
.
In particular, it follows from the equivalences in Figure
3
that:

•

p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q
is equivalent to
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q
, which we can express as a Horn clause
\{{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p,q\}
.

•

p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q
is equivalent to
({\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q){\boldsymbol{\wedge}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}q{\boldsymbol{\vee}}p)
, which we can express as a pair of Horn clauses
\{{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p,q\}
and
\{p,{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q\}
.

•

We can express the invalidity
\nvDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q
as
\{\Box p,\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q\}
, because (checking Figure
2
)
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\supset$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{f}={\mathbf{f}}
precisely when
f(p)={\mathbf{t}}
and
f(q)={\mathbf{f}}
.

•

p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}q
is equivalent to
\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q
.

•

(p_{1}{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\dots{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}p_{n}){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}p
is equivalent to
\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p_{1}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\dots\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p_{n}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}p
.

Note from Definition
4.1
(
1
) and Figure
5
that:

f\vDash\Box p
precisely when
f(p)={\mathbf{t}}
;

f\vDash p
precisely when
f(p)\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
;

f\vDash\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
precisely when
f(p)={\mathbf{f}}
; and

f\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
precisely when
f(p)\in\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

Remark 2.6

(Three-valued HORNSAT)
.

We now propose an algorithm to check satisfiability of Horn clause theories in
{\mathbf{3}}
— which as per Definition
2.2
(2) is the problem of deciding whether there exists a
three-valued
valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
that makes all the clauses valid (have truth-value equal to
{\mathbf{b}}
or
{\mathbf{t}}
) in
{\mathbf{3}}
as per the three-valued truth-tables in Figure
2
.

It is based on the algorithm for two-valued logic from Remark
2.3
.
Rules are applied in decreasing order of priority, and are repeated as often as they continue to act to change the theory:

1.

Suppose the theory contains a unit boxed positive literal
\Box p
(so intuitively, a satisfying assignment must satisfy
f(p)={\mathbf{t}}
).
Then:

(a)

We remove all other clauses that contain
p
or
\Box p
, because these clauses are now satisfied.

(b)

We delete all literals of the form
\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
,
because these literals cannot be satisfied.

(c)

We delete all literals of the form
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
,
because these literals cannot be satisfied.

2.

Suppose the theory contains a unit positive literal
p
(so for a satisfying assignment,
f(p)\neq{\mathbf{f}}
).
Then:

(a)

We delete all other clauses that contain a literal
p
(but
not
those that contain
\Box p
), because these clauses are now satisfied.

(b)

We delete all literals of the form
\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
from all clauses,
because these literals cannot be satisfied.

3.

If the theory contains an unsatisfiable (empty) Horn clause, then we fail.
Otherwise, we succeed.

If we succeed, then we can read a satisfying assignment from the result by

•

mapping
p
to
{\mathbf{t}}
if a unit clause
\Box p
remains,

•

mapping
p
to
{\mathbf{b}}
if a unit clause
p
remains (in this case, from the form of the rules a unit clause
\Box p
cannot remain), and

•

mapping
p
to
{\mathbf{f}}
otherwise.

Chapter 21
Extremal valuations

1
Definition of an extremal valuation

Remark 1.1
.

Intuitively, a valuation is
extremal
when it is continuous and it returns as many
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}
values as possible (is as definite as possible), and conversely when it returns as few
{\mathbf{b}}
values as possible (is no more ambivalent than necessary); the precise definition is in Definition
1.2
.

For example, there are four extremal valuations from
{\mathbf{3}}
to itself:

\lambda v.{\mathbf{t}},\quad\lambda v.{\mathbf{f}},\quad\lambda v.v,\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda v.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}v.

Conversely,
\lambda v.\Box v
is not extremal because it is not continuous.

\lambda v.{\mathbf{b}}
is continuous but not extremal.
The function mapping
{\mathbf{t}}
to
{\mathbf{t}}
,
{\mathbf{b}}
to
{\mathbf{b}}
, and
{\mathbf{f}}
to
{\mathbf{b}}
is continuous but not extremal.

It turns out that extremal valuations are rather useful.
If we think in terms of agreement, an extremal valuation represent a system state where algorithms have run and succeeded as much as they can; if a participant is still returning the ambivalent truth-value
{\mathbf{b}}
then this is because they must, and not just because they have not yet made up their mind.

Definition 1.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
f,f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
are continuous valuations.

1.

Call the elements
{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\in{\mathbf{3}}

definite
, and call
{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbf{3}}

ambivalent
(because
{\mathbf{b}}
is short for ‘both’).

2.

If
f(p)
is ambivalent / definite then call
f

ambivalent
/
definite
at
p
.

It is easy to check from Definition
4.1
(
1
) that
f
is definite at
p
when
f(p)\nvDash\mathsf{B}p
and when
f\vDash\Box p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
.

3.

Define
\mathit{definite}(f)
by

\mathit{definite}(f)=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}.

Thus,
\mathit{definite}(f)
is the set of points at which
f
is definite.
Note a nice characterisation of this using Definition
4.5
as

\mathit{definite}(f)=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus(f^{\vDash}\cap f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}})=(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus f^{\vDash})\cup(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}).

4.

Define a partial order
f\leq f^{\prime}
on valuations by

f\leq f^{\prime}\quad\text{when}\quad f|_{\mathit{definite}(f)}=f^{\prime}|_{\mathit{definite}(f)}.

In words:
f\leq f^{\prime}
when
f^{\prime}
agrees with
f
whenever
f
has a definite value (however,
f^{\prime}
may be definite at other values at which
f
is ambivalent).

5.

Call
f
a
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
-extremal valuation
when

(a)

f
is continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and

(b)

f
is
\leq
-maximal amongst valuations that are continuous on
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
.

If the semitopology is clear from context, we may just call
f
an
extremal valuation
.

6.

We will write

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash^{{}^{\hskip-3.0pt\mathsf{X}}}\phi

when
f\vDash\phi
for every extremal valuation.

Remark 1.3
.

There is a slight wrinkle to the terminology here:

•

In Definition
1.2
(
2
) we let a valuation on
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
be any function
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
; so if we need it to be continuous then we have to say ‘
f
is a
continuous
valuation’.

•

In Definition
1.2
(
5
) we let an extremal valuation be a
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
-continuous valuation that is
\leq
-maximal.

We never need to say ‘
f
is an extremal
continuous
valuation’, because if
f
is extremal then it is assumed continuous.
The notion of being extremal assumes continuity; since without it being extremal just means being a function in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
.

2
Topological characterisation of extremal valuations

In this Subsection we characterise extremal valuations in terms of regular open/closed sets.
The key technical observation powering this Subsection is Lemma
2.4
.

We note a standard fact from topology relating inverse images, closures, and interiors
[
Eng89
, Proposition 1.4.1(v’&vi)]
.
It is also valid in semitopologies:

Lemma 2.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
and
(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime},\mathsf{Open}^{\prime})
are semitopologies and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
is continuous and
P^{\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}
.
Then:

1.

|f^{\text{-}1}(P^{\prime})|\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}|P^{\prime}|
.

2.

\mathit{interior}(f^{\text{-}1}(P^{\prime}))\supseteq f^{\text{-}1}(\mathit{interior}(P^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

For part 1, write
p^{\prime}=f(p)
and
P=f^{\text{-}1}(P^{\prime})
.
Suppose
p\in|P|
, so by Lemma
5.3

\mathit{nbhd}(p)\between P
.
Now consider
O^{\prime}\in\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
; it would suffice to show that
O^{\prime}\between P^{\prime}
.
Write
O=f^{\text{-}1}(O^{\prime})
.
Note that
O\in\mathsf{Open}
by continuity and
O\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
by construction.
It follows that
O\between P
, and so that
O^{\prime}\between P^{\prime}
as required.

For part 2, just use part 1 and take complements, noting that
f^{\text{-}1}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}\setminus P^{\prime})=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus f^{\text{-}1}(P^{\prime})
and by Lemma
2.1
that
\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}\setminus P^{\prime})=\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}\setminus|P^{\prime}|
and
\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}\setminus P^{\prime})=\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime}\setminus|P^{\prime}|
.
∎

Corollary 2.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a continuous valuation.
Then:

1.

|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and

|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

2.

The inclusions may be strict.

Proof.

It is a fact that
|\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
|\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|=\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
The inclusions follow from Lemma
2.1
.

To show that the inclusions may be strict, it suffices to provide an example.
Consider the semitopology in Figure
1
(top-right example) with
f(0)={\mathbf{f}}
,
f(1)={\mathbf{b}}
, and
f(2)={\mathbf{t}}
.
The reader can check that this is continuous and

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\subsetneq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}

and

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\subsetneq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
∎

Remarkably, extremal valuations are characterised
precisely
by the property that the inclusions of Corollary
2.2
are equalities, as we shall now see:

Lemma 2.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a continuous extremal valuation.
Then

|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\quad\text{and}\quad|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}.

Proof.

By Corollary
2.2

|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}

and

|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
Now suppose
p\not\in|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|
(the case of
{\mathbf{f}}
is precisely similar).
So there exists
O\in\mathit{nbhd}(p)
such that
O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
.
There are now two subcases:

•

Suppose
O\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
.

Then
p\not\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and we are done.

•

Suppose
O\not\subseteq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
.

Then we obtain a strictly more definite continuous valuation as
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\cup O)
.
By extremality of
f
, this is impossible.
∎

The nontrivial technical content of this Subsection is here:

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a continuous valuation.
Then

|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\ \land\ |f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\quad\text{implies}\quad\text{$f$ is extremal}.

Proof.

We prove the contrapositive.
Suppose
f
is not extremal, so that there exists a continuous valuation
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
such that
f\lneq f^{\prime}
.
So there is a
p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
at which
f^{\prime}
is definite and
f
is not; suppose
f^{\prime}(p^{\prime})={\mathbf{t}}
(the case that
f^{\prime}(p^{\prime})={\mathbf{f}}
is exactly similar).

Since also
f^{\prime}
is continuous, by Definition
4.4
and Remark
4.5
there exists an
O^{\prime}\in\mathit{nbhd}(p^{\prime})
such that
O^{\prime}\subseteq(f^{\prime})^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
.
From this it follows using Definition
1.2
(
1
) that
p^{\prime}\not\in|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|
.
Now we assumed
f(p^{\prime})={\mathbf{b}}
, so that
p^{\prime}\in f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
is a fact.
Thus,
|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|\neq f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
as required.
∎

Proposition 2.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a continuous valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is extremal.

2.

|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.

3.

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\subseteq|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|
and
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\subseteq|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|
.

It is a fact that
|\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and
|\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|=\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
in
{\mathbf{3}}
, so we can say:

A continuous valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is extremal precisely when inverse images commute with closures.

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 is just Lemmas
2.3
and
2.4
.
Part 2 certainly implies part 3, and part 3 combined with Lemma
2.1
implies part 2.
∎

It is easy and natural to dualise Proposition
2.5
:

Corollary 2.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a continuous valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is extremal.

2.

\mathit{interior}(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\})=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}

and

\mathit{interior}(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\})=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
.

3.

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}\supseteq\mathit{interior}(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\})

and

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\supseteq\mathit{interior}(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\})
.

It is a fact that
\mathit{interior}(\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\})=\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
and
\mathit{interior}(\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\})=\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
in
{\mathbf{3}}
, so we can say:

A continuous valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is extremal precisely when inverse images commute with interiors.

Proof.

We take complements in Proposition
2.5
and dualise using Lemma
2.1
(just as we did to derive part 2 of Lemma
2.1
from part 1).
∎

In Lemma
3.1
(
4
) we noted that
f\vDash\Box p
implies
p\in\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash})
, but the reverse implication need not hold.
Rather nicely, it turns out that extremal valuations are characterised
precisely
as those valuations such that the reverse implications hold, for both
\Box p
and
\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
:

Corollary 2.7
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a continuous valuation.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

f
is extremal.

2.

p\in\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash})\Longleftrightarrow f\vDash\Box p

and

p\in\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}})\Longleftrightarrow f\vDash\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
,
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

3.

p\in\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash})\Longrightarrow f\vDash\Box p

and

p\in\mathit{interior}(f^{\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}})\Longrightarrow f\vDash\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
,
for every
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.

Proof.

This just rephrases Corollary
2.6
using Lemma
3.1
(
1
) and Definition
4.5
.
∎

3
Maximal disjoint pairs of open sets

We now develop a different view of extremal valuations, based on maximal elements in the poset of disjoint pairs of open sets.
We start by introducing some notation and recalling an easy sets bijection:

Notation 3.1
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
are sets and suppose
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{pow}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}^{\prime})
are sets of subsets.
Then write
\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
for the set of disjoint pairs in
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
In symbols:

\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\{(P,P^{\prime})\mid P\in\mathcal{P},\ P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\ P\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}P^{\prime}\}.

In particular, if
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology then
\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
is the set of disjoint pairs of open sets.

Remark 3.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Recall the
indicator functions

\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}
from Definition
1.5
and the
characteristic sets

\mathit{char}_{OO}
from Definition
1.7
, and recall from Proposition
1.10
that

1.

\mathit{char}_{OO}
maps a continuous valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
to a disjoint pair

\mathit{char}_{OO}(f)=(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\})\in\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}

and

2.

\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}
maps
(O,O^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
to the continuous valuation that maps
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
to
{\mathbf{t}}
if
p\in O
, and to
{\mathbf{f}}
if
p\in O^{\prime}
, and to
{\mathbf{b}}
if
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus(O\cup O^{\prime})
, and

3.

\mathit{char}_{OO}
and
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}
are inverse and biject between valuations and pairs of disjoint open sets.

We now note that
\mathit{char}
and
\delta
can also be viewed as maps of posets:

Definition 3.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

Extend the subset inclusion ordering
\subseteq
to disjoint pairs of open sets in
\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}

componentwise
as follows:

(O_{1},O_{1}^{\prime})\leq(O_{2},O_{2}^{\prime})\quad\text{when}\quad O_{1}\subseteq O_{1}^{\prime}\ \land\ O_{2}\subseteq O_{2}^{\prime}

for
(O_{1},O_{1}^{\prime}),(O_{2},O_{2}^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
.

2.

Call
(O,O^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}

maximal disjoint
when it is
\leq
-maximal amongst pairs of disjoint open sets.

Lemma 3.4
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then:

1.

If
f,f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
are continuous valuations then the following are equivalent:

•

f\leq f^{\prime}
in the sense of Definition
1.2
(
4
).

•

\mathit{char}_{OO}(f)\leq\mathit{char}_{OO}(f^{\prime})
in the sense of Definition
3.3
(
1
).

2.

The following are equivalent for
(O_{1},O^{\prime}_{1}),(O_{2},O^{\prime}_{2})\in\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
:

•

(O_{1},O^{\prime}_{1})\leq(O_{2},O^{\prime}_{2})
in the sense of Definition
3.3
(
1
).

•

\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(O_{1},O^{\prime}_{1})\leq\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(O_{2},O^{\prime}_{2})
in the sense of Definition
1.2
(
4
).

Proof.

By routine calculations from the definitions.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology.
Then
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}
and
\mathit{char}_{OO}
determine poset isomorphism between

•

continuous valuations ordered by
\leq
(Definition
1.2
(
4
)) and

•

\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
ordered by
\leq
(Definition
3.3
(
1
)).

Proof.

Direct from Lemma
3.4
.
∎

The maximal elements of
\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
have some useful characterisations:

Proposition 3.6
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
(O,O^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

(O,O^{\prime})
is maximal disjoint.

2.

O=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime})
and
O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
.

3.

O
is regular (Definition
7.2
) and
O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
.

4.

O^{\prime}
is regular and
O=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime})
.

5.

O
and
O^{\prime}
are regular and
O=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime})
and
O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
.

Proof.

Equivalence of parts 1 and 2 is just an easy corollary of Lemma
1.2
.
We spell out the details:

•

Suppose
(O,O^{\prime})
is maximal disjoint.

Then
O^{\prime}
is a greatest open set that is disjoint from
O
, meaning equivalently that
O^{\prime}
is a greatest open set contained in
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
, so by Lemma
1.2

O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
.
Similarly,
O=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime})
.

•

Suppose
O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
and
O=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime})
.

Consider a disjoint pair of open sets
(O^{\prime\prime},O^{\prime\prime\prime})\geq(O,O^{\prime})
, meaning by Definition
3.3
(
1
) that
O^{\prime\prime}\supseteq O
and
O^{\prime\prime\prime}\supseteq O^{\prime}
.
Then
O^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime}
so that
O^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime})=O
, so that
O^{\prime\prime}=O
.
Similarly,
O^{\prime\prime\prime}=O^{\prime}
.
Since
(O^{\prime\prime},O^{\prime\prime\prime})
was arbitrary,
(O,O^{\prime})
is a maximal disjoint pair of open sets.

To prove equivalence of parts 2 and 3 we reason as follows:

•

Suppose
O=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime})
and
O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
.

We reason as follows using Lemma
2.1
(
3
):

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}O=&\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O^{\prime})&\text{Assumption}\\
=&\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O))&O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)\\
=&\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|O|))&\text{Lemma~\ref{lemm.closure.interior}(\ref{item.closure.interior.complement.closure})}\\
=&\mathit{interior}(|O|)&\text{Fact of sets}\end{array}

•

Suppose
O
is regular (so
O=\mathit{interior}(|O|)
) and
O^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
.

We just reverse the reasoning of the previous case.

To prove equivalence of parts 3 and 4 follows by the same reasoning, on
O^{\prime}
.
Equivalence of parts 1 and 5 then follows easily.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Putting Proposition
3.6
and Corollary
3.5
together, we see that the following items of data are in natural correspondence:

1.

Extremal valuations
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.

An extremal
f
corresponds to the (by Corollary
3.5
) maximal disjoint pair
(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\})
, and (by Proposition
3.6
)
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
is a regular open.

2.

Maximal pairs
(O,O^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
.

A maximal
(O,O^{\prime})
corresponds to the (by Corollary
3.5
) extremal
valuation
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(O,O^{\prime}):\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
, and (by Proposition
3.6
)
O
is regular.

3.

Regular open sets
O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}
.

A regular open set
O
corresponds to the (by Proposition
3.6
) maximal disjoint
(O,\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O))
and to the (by Corollary
3.5
) extremal valuation
\delta_{O_{{\mathbf{t}}},O_{{\mathbf{f}}}}(O,\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O))
.

We sum this up in a small table in Figure
1
.

\begin{array}[]{c@{\quad}c@{\quad}c}f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to{\mathbf{3}}&(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\})&f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\\[4.30554pt]
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(O,O^{\prime})&(O,O^{\prime})\in\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}&O\\[4.30554pt]
\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}},O_{\mathbf{f}}}(O,\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O))&(O,\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O))&O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}\end{array}

In row 1
f:(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})\to{\mathbf{3}}
is an extremal valuation; in row 2
(O,O^{\prime})
is a maximal disjoint pair of open sets; in row 3
O
is a regular open set.

Figure 1
:
Correspondence between extremal valuations, maximal disjoint pairs of open sets, and regular open sets

We conclude with a small lemma noting that extremal valuations always exist.
A standard argument using Zorn’s lemma is possible, but we give a more direct argument using closures and regular open sets:

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a continuous valuation.
Then there exists an extremal valuation
f^{\prime}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
such that
f\leq f^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Write
O=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}
and
O^{\prime}=f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}
.
Note from Remark
3.2
that
O
and
O^{\prime}
are open and disjoint.
Write
R=\mathit{interior}(|O|)
.
Note by construction that
O\subseteq R
, and from Lemmas
7.3
and
7.8
that
R
is open, regular, and disjoint from
O^{\prime}
.

Finally, write
R^{\prime}=\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus R)
.
By construction
O^{\prime}\subseteq R^{\prime}
and using Proposition
3.6
,
(R,R^{\prime})
is maximal disjoint above
(O,O^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{Open}\otimes\mathsf{Open}
.
We set
f^{\prime}=\delta_{O_{{\mathbf{t}}}O_{{\mathbf{f}}}}(R,R^{\prime})
.
By Corollary
3.5

f^{\prime}
is extremal and (since
O\subseteq R
and
O^{\prime}\subseteq R^{\prime}
)
f\leq f^{\prime}
.
∎

4
Logical characterisation of extremal valuations

Remark 4.1
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, and recall from Subsection
2
and Proposition
2.3
that a valuation
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is continuous in the witness semitopology from Definition
2.5
(
3
) if and only if
f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
, for
\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
the theory arising from
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
in Definition
1.1
.
Recall that
\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
has for each
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
the following pair of axioms:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=&\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}q\bigr){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}p\quad\text{and}\\
\mathsf{closedAx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)=&\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q\bigr){\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p.\end{array}

Definition 4.2
.

Suppose
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to\mathcal{W}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
is a witness function on a finite set
\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Continuing Definition
1.1
:

1.

Define the
extremal axioms arising from
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
to be sets of predicates in
\mathsf{ClosedPred3}(\bm{\mathsf{P}})
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)=&p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}\Box q\bigr)\\
\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\bigl({\boldsymbol{\wedge}}_{w\in{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)}{\boldsymbol{\vee}}_{q\in w}\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q\bigr)\\[8.61108pt]
\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}=&\bigwedge\{\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}(p)\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}\\
\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}=&\bigwedge\{\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)\mid p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\}\end{array}

2.

We collect these axioms into a large conjunction
\mathsf{AxEx}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
, which we call the
extremal theory arising from
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
:

\mathsf{AxEx}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})=\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})\ {\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\ \mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}\ {\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\ \mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}.

We may omit the
{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
annotation where this is unimportant or understood, writing (for example)
\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
just as
\mathsf{closedAxEx}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}(p)
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is a valuation.
Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

f
is an extremal valuation.

2.

f\vDash\mathsf{AxEx}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
.

Proof.

We prove two implications:

•

Suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is an extremal valuation.

By assumption
f
is continuous, so by Proposition
2.3

f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
.
It remains to show that
f\vDash\mathsf{AxEx}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
.
From Proposition
2.5
we have that

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\subseteq|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|

and

f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\subseteq|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|
.
But this is precisely what
\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}
and
\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
express, so we are done.

•

Suppose
f\vDash\mathsf{AxEx}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
.

Then
f\vDash\mathsf{Ax}(\hskip 0.0pt{{w\hskip-1.8ptf}})
so that by Proposition
2.3

f
is continuous.
Also,
f\vDash\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}\land f\vDash\mathsf{closedAxEx}_{w\hskip-1.8ptf}^{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
expresses precisely that
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\subseteq|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\}|
and
f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\subseteq|f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\}|
, so by Proposition
2.5

f
is extremal as required.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

We note in passing that we can use the equivalences in Figure
3
to rewrite the extremal axioms from Definition
4.2
into a more clausal form as follows:

•

p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\bigwedge_{i}\bigvee_{j}q_{ij}

becomes

\Box p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\bigwedge_{i}\bigvee_{j}q_{ij}
.

•

{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\Rightarrow}}\bigwedge_{i}\bigvee_{j}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q_{ij}

becomes

\Box{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p{\boldsymbol{\vee}}(\bigwedge_{i}\bigvee_{j}q_{ij})
.

5
Extremal valuations and regular points

We can think of Proposition
5.1
(which considers continuous functions to
{\mathbf{3}}
) as generalising Theorem
2.3
(which regards partially continuous functions, but to a discrete space):

Proposition 5.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is an extremal valuation and
T\in\mathsf{Topen}
and
p,p^{\prime}\in T
.
Then:

1.

f(p)=f(p^{\prime})
.

2.

f(p)\neq{\mathbf{b}}
.

Proof.

Write
(O,O^{\prime})=\mathit{char}_{OO}(f)=(f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{t}}\},f^{\text{-}1}\{{\mathbf{f}}\})
, and note from Remark
3.7
(
1
) that this is a maximal pair of disjoint open sets.
We consider each part in turn:

1.

If
p\in O
then
T\between O
and by Proposition
3.2

T\subseteq O
, so that
p^{\prime}\in O
.
By the same reasoning
p^{\prime}\in O
implies
p\in O
, and similarly
p\in O^{\prime}
if and only if
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
.
Thus

p\in O\Longleftrightarrow p^{\prime}\in O\quad\text{and}\quad p\in O^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}.

It follows that
f(p)=f(p^{\prime})
as required.

2.

Suppose
f(p)={\mathbf{b}}
; we will arrive at a contradiction.
By assumption
p
has a topen neighbourhood
T
and it follows using Theorem
2.6
(
5
&
2
) that
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
.

Using part 1 of this result (for the topen
\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
) we know that
f(p^{\prime\prime})={\mathbf{b}}
for every
p^{\prime\prime}\in\mathit{K}(p)
.
But then
\mathit{K}(p)
is an open set that is disjoint from both
O
and
O^{\prime}
, contradicting their maximality.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then:

1.

If
p
is regular then every extremal valuation is definite at
p
.

2.

The converse implication need not hold: it is possible for every extremal valuation to be definite at
p
, yet
p
is not regular.

Proof.

For part 1, suppose
p
is regular, so that by Definition
1.4
(
3
)
p\in\mathit{K}(p)\in\mathsf{Topen}
, and suppose
f:\bm{\mathsf{P}}\to{\mathbf{3}}
is an extremal valuation.
By Proposition
5.1
(2) (taking
p^{\prime}=p
)
f(p)\neq{\mathbf{b}}
, so by Definition
1.2
(
2
)
f
is definite at
p
.

For part 2, it suffices to provide a counterexample.
Consider the semitopology in Figure
2
, so:

•

\bm{\mathsf{P}}=\{0,1,2,3\}
and

•

\mathsf{Open}
is generated by
\bigl\{\{0,1\},\,\{1,2\},\,\{2,3\},\,\{3,4\}\bigr\}
.

The reader can check that extremal valuations are constant and definite on
\{0,1\}
and
\{2,3\}
, or on
\{0,3\}
and
\{1,2\}
— for instance we can map
\{0,1\}
to
{\mathbf{t}}
and
\{2,3\}
to
{\mathbf{f}}
.
However, by Lemma
6.7

no points in this space are regular
(see the discussion in Example
6.8
(
3
)).
∎

6
Characterisations of intertwinedness properties

1
Studying
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
and
p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}

Lemma 6.1
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\forall O,O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}.(p\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime})\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}
(meaning by Definition
6.1
(
1
) that
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
).

2.

\forall O,O^{\prime}{\in}\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}.(p\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime})\Longrightarrow O\between O^{\prime}
.

Proof.

This just repackages Corollary
7.9
.
∎

Lemma
6.2
gives us yet another view of being intertwined; we will also use it in Definition
6.6
:

Lemma 6.2
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
.

2.

\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}.p,p^{\prime}\in|O|\lor p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.

3.

\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}.p,p^{\prime}\in|O|\lor p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.

4.

\forall C\in\mathsf{Closed}.p,p^{\prime}\in C\lor p,p^{\prime}\in|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C|
.

5.

\forall C\in\mathsf{Closed}_{\mathit{reg}}.p,p^{\prime}\in C\lor p,p^{\prime}\in|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C|
.

Proof.

For the equivalence of parts
1
and
2
we prove two implications:

•

Suppose
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
.

Write
C=|O|
and
C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.
By Lemma
1.6

C
is closed, and by Lemma
1.9
so is
\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.
By construction and Lemma
1.3
(
2
)
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
, so by Lemma
1.2
(
5
)
p,p^{\prime}\in C\lor p,p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
.

•

Suppose
p\mathrel{\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}}p^{\prime}
,
so there exist
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O^{\prime}
.

Because
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O
, we know from Definition
1.2
(
1
) that
p^{\prime}\not\in|O|
.
Also,
p\not\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
(because
p\in O
).
It follows that
p,p^{\prime}\in|O|
is impossible, and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
is impossible.

For the equivalence of parts
1
and
3
it suffices to show that part
2
implies part
3
, and that the negation of Lemma
6.1
(2) implies the negation of part
3
:

•

Suppose
\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}.p,p^{\prime}\in|O|\lor p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.

Then certainly
\forall O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}.p,p^{\prime}\in|O|\lor p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
, because every regular open set is also an open set.

•

Suppose we have
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}
such that
p\in O\land p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
and
O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O^{\prime}
.

Since
p\in O
, we have by Lemma
1.3
(
2
) that
p\in|O|
, and by construction that
p\not\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.
Since
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O
, we have that
p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
, and by Definition
1.2
(
1
) that
p^{\prime}\not\in|O|
.

We prove equivalence of parts
1
and
4
as follows:

•

Suppose
p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}
.

Suppose
C\in\mathsf{Closed}
.
Write
C^{\prime}=|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C|
.
By Lemma
1.6

C^{\prime}
is closed and
by Lemma
1.3
(
2
)
C\cup C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
It follows by Lemma
1.2
(
5
) that
p,p^{\prime}\in C\lor p,p^{\prime}\in C^{\prime}
, so we are done.

•

Suppose
p\mathrel{\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}}p^{\prime}
,
so there exist
p\in O\in\mathsf{Open}
and
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Open}
such that
O\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O^{\prime}
.

Write
C=|O|
and
C^{\prime}=\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.
Because
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}\mathbin{\cancel{\between}}O
, we know from Definition
1.2
(
1
) that
p^{\prime}\not\in C
.
Also,
p\not\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
(because
p\in O
).
Now using Lemma
2.1
(
3
&
2
) we have that

|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus|O||\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.closure.interior}(\ref{item.closure.interior.complement.closure})}}{{=}}|\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)|=|\mathit{interior}(C^{\prime})|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.closure.interior}(\ref{item.closure.interior.closed})}}{{\subseteq}}C^{\prime}.

So if
p\not\in C^{\prime}
then also
p\not\in|\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C|
.
It follows that
p,p^{\prime}\in C
is impossible, and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus C
is impossible, so we are done.

Equivalence of parts
3
and
5
follows by a routine argument from regularity (Definition
7.2
) using Corollary
7.6
and Lemma
2.1
.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Suppose
(\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open})
is a semitopology and
p,p^{\prime}\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}

2.

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}

3.

\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash^{{}^{\hskip-3.0pt\mathsf{X}}}p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
(Definition
1.2
(
6
))

Proof.

Parts 1 and 2 just repeat Lemma
1.3
(1&2).
For equivalence of parts 2 and 3 we prove two implications:

•

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
.
Then certainly
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash^{{}^{\hskip-3.0pt\mathsf{X}}}p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
, since every extremal valuation is a continuous valuation.

•

Suppose
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\nvDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
.
By the equivalence of parts 1 and 2 of this result and by Lemma
6.2
, there exists an
O\in\mathsf{Open}_{\mathit{reg}}
such that
p\not\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
— so
p\in O
— and
p^{\prime}\not\in|O|
— so
p^{\prime}\in\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O)
— or
p^{\prime}\in O
and
p\in\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O
.
We consider the former case; the proof for the latter is precisely similar.

We set
f=\delta_{O_{\mathbf{t}}O_{\mathbf{f}}}(O,\mathit{interior}(\bm{\mathsf{P}}\setminus O))
.
By Remark
3.7
this is extremal, and by construction
f(p)={\mathbf{t}}
and
f(p^{\prime})={\mathbf{f}}
so that
f\nvDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Lemma
6.2
and Proposition
6.3
are interesting not just for what they are, but for the design space that they suggest:

1.

Given that we have

•

two turnstiles (
\vDash
and
\vDash^{{}^{\hskip-3.0pt\mathsf{X}}}
),

•

two notions of logical equivalence (

\equiv

and

\Leftrightarrow

), and also

•

the
\Box
modality,

when Proposition
6.3
notes that these are equivalent

p\mathrel{\between}p^{\prime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}\vDash^{{}^{\hskip-3.0pt\mathsf{X}}}p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}p^{\prime},

this only draws attention to the rest of design space in which they are embedded (below, we omit
\bm{\mathsf{P}},\mathsf{Open}
for compactness):
1
1

1

The design space is even larger than this, of course. We have multiple modalities, and Remark
2.6
notes that there are actually sixteen different implications in three-valued logic. But we have to start somewhere.
